CHAPTER 4: TRAFFIC PATTERNS AND LANDINGS
4.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
This chapter describes aircraft handling associated with Harvard II VFR pattern
procedures, which normally include the traffic pattern, overhead break, final turn, land-
ing transition, landing, and the low approach and Go Around. Additional patterns flown
are the straight-in, which includes the approach phase, and closed pattern, both of which
are derived from the normal patterns. All of the approaches can be flown in any flap con-
figuration. To illustrate this, this manual lists final approach speeds as “100/105/110”
KIAS. The numbers represent the minimum allowable airspeeds prior to round-out and
are in the sequence of LDG flap, T/O flap, and flapless configurations respectively.
2.
Some airfields that you will operate from, over your career, do not permit the typ-
ical military traffic patterns as flown during your training; therefore, the patterns men-
tioned above can easily be modified to adapt to other established procedures. Regardless
of this, published VFR pattern profiles and procedures ensure the orderly flow of the
maximum number of aircraft, with the minimum congestion, around an aerodrome and
runway. The traffic pattern profiles, normally published in Wing Flying Orders (WFOs)
at military airfields, will include a ground track, altitude, and airspeed.
4.2
TRAFFIC PATTERN PROCEDURES
3.
For clarity, this publication defines the specific VFR pattern profiles as follows:
a.
Traffic Pattern. The normal profile flown as defined in WFOs up to but not
including the initial point. A portion of the traffic pattern will also be used to
reposition the aircraft for a straight-in approach;
b.
Overhead Break. The profile flown, in relation to the runway from the initial
point, up to, but not including, the perch point;
c.
Final Turn. The profile flown from the perch point where descent is com-
menced with the intent to land, up to, but not including, the landing transition;
d.
Straight-in Approach. The profile flown from base leg up to, but not includ-
ing, the landing transition;
47

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 2 =====
e.
Landing Transition. The segment of flight immediately following the final
turn or approach where the airspeed is purposely reduced to final approach
speed in preparation for landing up to and including touchdown; and
f.
Closed Pattern. The profile flown following a take-off or low approach used
to reposition the aircraft for a final turn. The closed pattern is normally com-
menced from the upwind end of the runway.

NOTE
Speeds listed in Table 4.1 do not take into considerations AOA type ap-
proaches and/or environmental factors such as wind gusts and/or wind
shear. The responsibility rests with the pilot in command whether to fly
a greater airspeed to compensate for adverse weather conditions.
NOTE
At all times while manoeuvring when configured, you shall strive to
maintain the ideal approach speed but not below threshold crossing speed
plus 10 KIAS as depicted in Table 4.1.
4.3
PRE-DESCENT CHECK
4.
Complete the Pre-Descent Check at an appropriate time during return to base. The
check should be done before or shortly after beginning descent. Apply good judgement
and ensure the necessary items are completed as the situation dictates. When checking
your fuel, allow approximately 40 pounds for each traffic pattern/straight-in and landing,
and 15 pounds for each closed pattern and landing. Plan for an adequate reserve on
landing to comply with flying orders.
5.
Ensure that you have received the latest information available concerning your
landing airfield (ATIS, Tower frequency, MF, ATF). At a controlled aerodrome (i.e.
Moose Jaw) you must state your intentions to ATC and receive landing instructions
prior to entering controlled airspace. At an uncontrolled airport specific procedures are
detailed in GPH 204. From the information received, you can then plan your entry for
the type of approach intended.
4.4
THE STRAIGHT-IN APPROACH
6.
Although the usual VFR procedure used in your daily flying is the overhead break,
there may be times when the use of a straight-in approach is desirable. There are also
many civilian airfields that may not allow the overhead break; therefore, a variation of
the straight-in approach will have to be used.
4.5
JOINING FOR THE STRAIGHT-IN APPROACH
7.
The primary method of joining for the straight-in approach is via the extended base
leg of the approach IAW published procedures. Entry for the approach shall be made in
such a manner as to avoid “cutting off” other aircraft, conforming as closely as possible
to the published ground track.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 4 =====
8.
When joining for the straight-in, plan your descent to be established on the ex-
tended track of the base leg, on altitude and airspeed at least 1 NM from the published
traffic pattern to ensure effective lookout. Traffic that is already established in the traffic
pattern normally has the right of way. Base leg altitude is normally 1000 ft AGL or as
published in local orders. Base leg altitude at Moose Jaw is 2700 ft MSL (approximately
800 ft AGL) in order to de-conflict with normal pattern traffic. Base leg airspeed is be-
tween 120 and 147 KIAS (approximately 25% torque). If any conflict exists during the
rejoin, take positive action to avoid the conflict and re-position the aircraft for another
rejoin attempt.
4.6
BASE LEG
9.
Once established on the published base leg, ensure that the airspeed is below 147
KIAS. Lower the landing gear and select flaps to T/O for a normal straight-in approach.
Adjust power to maintain an ideal airspeed of 120 KIAS (approximately 35% torque)
and complete the pre-landing check. Trim the aircraft throughout. The 45 degree head-
ing change on the entry leg is designed to make the turn to final easier to judge. Using
30-45 degrees of bank, turn to roll onto the 45 degree leg and transmit to tower (e.g.
“APOLLO 4, LEFT/RIGHT BASE, STRAIGHT-IN”). See Figures 4.2 and 4.3. Com-
mence a 30-45 degree bank turn in order to align the aircraft on the extended runway
centreline. A small amount of power may be required during these turns in order to
maintain 120 KIAS.
Figure 4.2: Straight-In RWY 29L

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 5 =====
Figure 4.3: Straight-In RWY 11R
4.7
APPROACH
10.
Maintain base leg altitude and apply sufficient crab to track the extended runway
centreline in preparation for descent. Upon intercepting a 3 degree glide path commence
descent with the speed stabilized at 120 KIAS (approximately 25% torque). Trim out the
control pressures. Apply the principals of PAT/APT in order to intercept and maintain
the 3 degree glide path. Constantly cross-check the runway aim point and the airspeed
throughout the descent (when you cross-check the aim point on approach, spend a few
moments to determine if there is a trend, such as the glide path becoming steeper or
shallower). If a deviation occurs, adjust the power and/or attitude as required to correct
back to the ideal glide path and/or airspeed and then readjust to maintain. Pitch and
power changes should be relatively small while correcting in order to minimize the
possibility of chasing the parameters or inducing excessive torque changes. Continue
to trim out the control pressures while making corrections. Additionally, it is important
to maintain an effective lookout by scanning up and to the left/right. This will provide
situational awareness of possible PFL or final turn traffic.
WARNING
Never reduce the PCL to idle until landing is assured. Idle torque will
cause rapid airspeed reductions or generate excessive rates of descent
and engine spool up time is quite slow. Should you suddenly need more
power, it will be more readily available if the PCL is above idle.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 6 =====
11.
Remember to crab as required to track the extended runway centerline throughout
the approach. Corrections should be made with minimal bank and assisted with rudder
as required to minimize the effects of adverse yaw.
WARNING
Do not exceed 15 units of AOA while manoeuvring during any portion
of the approach.
12.
As you get closer to the runway, maintain the descent while attempting to pass
through an imaginary window at ¾ NM from the runway threshold on a 3 degree glide
path, which is approximately 2100’–2200’ MSL in Moose Jaw. This window is a com-
mon reference point regardless of the type of VFR approach flown. This window is the
point at which the landing transition is initiated.
4.8
THE LANDING TRANSITION
13.
The landing transition described herein is based upon a normal straight-in ap-
proach and it commences as the aircraft passes through the window. Initiate the landing
transition with the selection of LDG flap and allow the airspeed to slow to the final ap-
proach speed of 100 KIAS. Adjust the attitude as required to maintain the runway aim
point throughout the configuration change and subsequent speed reduction. A slight
power reduction may also be required to assist with the speed reduction. If landing is to
be made with T/O flap, then a more pronounced power reduction will be required to get
the speed to reduce to the T/O flap final approach speed of 105 KIAS. The decision to
select LDG flap will be dependent upon the wind and or training requirements.
14.
The use of the Harvard II’s split flaps on final generally has a twofold effect:
a.
T/O Flap. A very slight increase in lift which effectively lowers the stall
speed and allows for a slower final approach speed along with an increase in
drag; and
b.
LDG Flap. A marked increase in drag and, to a much lesser extent, a further
increase in lift, allowing for an even slower final approach speed. The aircraft
attitude remains relatively constant through the entire range of flap travel.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 7 =====
CAUTION
Large changes in pitch and significant power reductions should be avoided
on short final as they can induce high rates of descent and potential hard
landings.
If you are significantly above the glide slope on short final, do not contin-
uously steepen glideslope in order to maintain the aim point. If glides-
lope control has been mismanaged, the safest action is to adjust the aim
point further down the runway or go around.
4.9
THE ROUND-OUT/FLARE
15.
The actual landing begins when the airspeed is purposely reduced below final ap-
proach speed. The round-out/flare is the transition from the final approach speed to the
landing attitude and is normally accomplished using a combination of attitude and PCL
adjustments. Cross the aim point on speed with the control surfaces trimmed. Initiate
a round-out at approximately 10–15 ft above the runway by applying gentle back pres-
sure to the control column to check the descent and retard the PCL to idle once landing
is assured. Smoothly change the aircraft’s attitude to the landing attitude and continue
gradually increasing the attitude as the airspeed decreases to prevent the aircraft from
sinking. A slow continuous increase in back pressure is required to hold the aircraft just
above the runway. When you have attained a slightly nose up attitude, hold this attitude
constant and allow the main wheels of the aircraft to settle onto the runway. Momentary
actuation of the stick shaker may occur just prior to touchdown. Airspeed will dissipate
in the flare and touchdown will normally occur at 80–90 KIAS. During normal con-
ditions, the touchdown should occur approximately 300–400 ft down the runway. See
Figure 4.4
Figure 4.4: Final Approach and Landing

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 8 =====
NOTE
Do not hesitate to advance the PCL to maintain final approach speed
until the initiation of the flare.
4.10
THE TOUCHDOWN
16.
The touchdown should be on the main wheels with the nose-wheel off the runway.
At the point of touchdown the aircraft is not fully stalled but is settling because of
a gradual reduction in lift. After the main wheels are firmly on the runway, gently
lower the nose-wheel before the decreasing airspeed causes a loss of elevator control.
With sufficient runway available, once the airspeed has decreased below the minimum
stall speed for landing configuration, the brakes may be tested by applying light toe
pressure to the brakes to determine if the brakes are serviceable. Although braking may
be used above 80 KIAS, great caution must be used to avoid locking the brakes. The
proper braking technique involves an application of positive and steady brake pressure
while easing aft on the control column and maintaining any applicable crosswind aileron
input. This action will dissipate your speed and achieve a controlled deceleration to taxi
speed. The Harvard II is prone to nose-wheel shimmy. If this condition is encountered,
use sufficient aft pressure on the control column to minimize weight on the nose-wheel.
The rudder effectiveness will decrease as airspeed decreases therefore you must transfer
to the brakes for directional control before the rudder becomes ineffective. If required,
nose-wheel steering should only be engaged after the aircraft has slowed to taxi speed.
On completion of the after-landing roll, exit the runway in compliance with local flying
orders.
WARNING
The likelihood of a blown tire resulting from brake lock is greater during
increased crosswind conditions due to one wheel experiencing less force
on it than the other. If sufficient runway length is available, delaying
testing brakes until below the minimum stall speed for landing configu-
ration is recommended. Remember, a blown tire can occur due to brake
lock even at relatively low speeds.
17.
Directional Control on Landing. The following factors tend to influence direc-
tional control on landing:
a.
Wind. A strong crosswind causes the aircraft to turn into the wind. A gust
of wind may cause a wing to rise, thus producing an ‘into wind’ turning
moment.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 9 =====
b.
Tires. If you land with a flat tire, or if a tire bursts during the after-landing
roll, the braking efficiency is no longer equal and this will cause the aircraft
to swing towards the side with better braking.
18.
During the landing, directional control is of vital importance and can be easily
achieved. There are four controls that help you to maintain direction:
a.
Rudder. The rudder serves the same purpose as in the air — it changes the
aircraft’s yaw. On the ground, this yaw is directional control, thus the rudder
is used to initiate, increase, or stop a turn. The effectiveness of the rudder
varies directly with the speed of the aircraft, and becomes ineffective at low
speed. As the speed decreases, the brakes must be used to assist in directional
control.
b.
Ailerons. The ailerons are used to balance the lift produced by the wings.
During the landing roll, moving the control column to one side reduces lift
on that wing thereby increasing the downward force on the main gear on that
side. This added force is effective in preventing the aircraft from turning into
wind. This is a common technique used for countering a crosswind.
c.
Brake. It is possible to maintain direction with brakes alone. However,
brakes are not normally used for directional control, unless the rudder is
not sufficiently effective. The use of brakes can overcome the more violent
swings, such as those from a flat tire.
d.
Nose-Wheel Steering. With the nose-wheel on the ground, nose-wheel steer-
ing gives you positive directional control. Because of its sensitivity you
should not engage the nose wheel steering until the aircraft has been slowed
down to taxi speed.
CAUTION
Do not use nose-wheel steering during the after-landing roll
above taxi speed, except when there is brake failure, the run-
way is wet or icy, or the aircraft has a flat tire.
e.
Vision. Occasionally, you will round-out too low, too high, or too quickly.
Usually, these errors in judgment are a result of sight fixation. If you focus
your eyes on a point that is too close to the aircraft, the tendency is to round-
out high; if you look too far away, the tendency is to round-out low. You
should sit erect with your head in a natural position. During the round-out,
while you are bringing the aircraft into the landing attitude, vary your visual
ground coverage to a moderate distance ahead of the aircraft (600–1000 ft)
and take in both sides of the runway. Change your focus slowly. Your in-
structor will demonstrate several approaches and landings and will point out
when to start the round-out. Try to take in the whole picture and you will
find, after some practice, that a certain point looks right.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 10 =====
4.11
LANDING IRREGULARITIES
19.
The following lists the common problems associated with landings. In all cases,
recovery actions are listed, however anytime on final approach or during the landing
phase, a timely decision to Go Around is often the wisest course of action.
20.
Ballooning. If you round-out too quickly or with too high an airspeed, the sudden
increase in lift may cause the aircraft to climb before it touches the runway. If you
do not recognize that you are high and continue to hold the landing attitude, there is
a danger of stalling and dropping the aircraft onto the runway. This could result in
structural damage to the landing gear. Normally the recovery action from ballooning is
to go around. However, if the error is slight and you have sufficient airspeed, add power
and complete another round-out for landing. In this case, set PCL to IDLE after the
main wheels are on the ground.
21.
Bounce. If you should round-out too late, the aircraft may land and then bounce
back into flight. In the event of a bounce, if the airspeed is sufficient that you are in no
danger of stalling, hold the aircraft level until it starts to sink, round-out again, and then
land. Remember that you can use power to assist the second round-out and to cushion
the touchdown.
22.
Round-out Too High. If you should round-out too high and are in no danger of
stalling, hold the aircraft level until it starts to sink, round-out again, and then land.
Remember that you can use power to assist the second round-out and to cushion the
touchdown.
23.
Crosswind Effect. Avoid skipping or drifting in a crosswind. The dihedral wing
of the Harvard II is particularly sensitive to effects of a crosswind. You must ensure that
you use the ailerons to prevent the aircraft from drifting sideways and possibly skipping
(see Figure 4.5). Maintain crosswind inputs throughout the landing.
CAUTION
Never try to salvage a landing from a bad approach or from a poor land-
ing. If you are in doubt as to the quality of your approach or landing,
GO AROUND.
24.
Porpoising. Porpoising is a series of bounces from the main wheels to the nose
wheel and back. It is usually caused by the nose-wheel contacting the ground before
the main gear, or when the pilot over-controls during a correction for a bounce on land-
ing. When the landing is attempted at a higher than normal speed, an abrupt backward
or forward movement of the control column, at the point of touchdown, may induce
porpoising. The combined reaction time of the pilot and control response is such that
incorrect control movements may aggravate the porpoising and increase the magnitude
of the bounce. This hazardous condition must be corrected immediately to prevent loss

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 11 =====
Figure 4.5: Aircraft Skip on Runway
of control, structural damage, or both.
The corrective action is to select PCL to MAX and neutralize the controls. Complete a
go around after the aircraft is safely airborne.
Fuel, weather, or other considerations may preclude the pilot from completing a go
around. In such cases, the corrective action for a porpoise is to neutralize the controls
until the aircraft starts to sink. Attempt to touch down on the main wheels by moving
the control column progressively rearward. Do not pump the control column. Early
recognition and recovery are essential.
4.12
CROSSWIND APPROACHES AND LANDINGS
25.
Drift becomes readily apparent during the landing transition. There are two com-
monly used techniques to counter the effect of crosswind: the wing-down method and
the combination method.
26.
Wing-Down Method. Lower the upwind wing sufficiently to eliminate drift, and
simultaneously apply enough opposite rudder to keep the longitudinal axis in line with
the centerline of the runway (see Figure 4.6). As the speed of the crosswind varies,
so does the angle of bank required to keep the aircraft on the centerline of the runway.
This is intentional cross-control. Because the wind may vary with altitude, continual
small adjustments may be required to keep the aircraft lined up with the runway. During

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 12 =====
Figure 4.6: Correcting for Crosswind
Figure 4.7: Normal Crosswind Landing
the round-out and float period as the airspeed is decreasing, gradually increase the up-
wind aileron and opposite rudder to keep straight until the aircraft lands on the upwind
main wheel. Maintain the upwind aileron and sufficient rudder to keep straight as the
downwind main wheel lowers to the runway (see Figure 4.7). When both wheels are
firmly on the ground, maintain directional control with the same control inputs: rudder
to maintain directional control and aileron into wind to maintain the centerline. As the
aircraft slows, increase control deflection as required and then switch to brakes as the
rudder becomes ineffective.
27.
Combination Method. Crab to eliminate drift on the final approach and transfer
to the wing-down method during the round-out and float period. This method requires
more finesse and better depth perception than the wing-down method, but is the pre-
ferred method. Once established on final, crab into wind to compensate for drift. The
wings should be level and the nose should be pointing towards the upwind side of the
centerline. During the approach, you may have to make minor adjustments to compen-
sate for variations in the wind. As you begin the round-out, smoothly apply rudder to
line up the longitudinal axis of the aircraft with the centerline of the runway, and simul-
taneously apply upwind aileron to lower the wing and prevent drift as you complete the

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 13 =====
landing.
28.
The following rules of good airmanship apply, particularly to crosswind landings:
a.
Land on the runway centerline in order to leave room to correct for a swing
in either direction and to allow for directional control errors during the ap-
proach; and
b.
Never land while the aircraft is crabbing because severe stress is imposed on
the landing gear. The wing-down method is to be utilized for all crosswind
landings.
NOTE
Take-off flap or flapless landings should be considered during
strong, gusty crosswind conditions.
4.13
TOUCH-AND-GO LANDINGS
29.
You will perform more touch-and-go than full-stop landings during basic training.
Two types of touch-and-go landing techniques will be discussed. In the first type, the
nose-wheel is lowered to the runway, and in the second type, the nose-wheel is held off
the runway. In either case, crosswind technique is maintained throughout the touch-and-
go.
30.
Lowering Nose-Wheel. Complete a normal landing. As the nose-wheel touches,
smoothly advance the PCL to MAX. Be ready to compensate with rudder for the torque
of the engine. Raise the nose to the take-off attitude which will vary slightly according
to the amount of flap used, and allow the aircraft to fly off the runway. Do not re-
trim the aircraft during the after-landing roll. Initially, the nose-up trim applied on final
approach has little effect, but as the airspeed increases, you will have to re-trim. When
the aircraft is well clear of the ground, raise the landing gear, and at 110 KIAS raise the
flaps. Re-trim for the climb and complete the Post-Take-Off Check before attaining 147
KIAS.
31.
Nose-Wheel Held Off. When the main wheels touch down, smoothly advance
the PCL to MAX. Be ready to compensate for the torque of the engine with rudder to
maintain directional control. Hold the takeoff attitude by maintaining continuous back
pressure, and allow the aircraft to fly off the ground. When the aircraft is well clear
of the ground, raise the landing gear and, at 110 KIAS, raise the flaps. Re-trim for the
climb and complete the Post-Take-Off Check prior to 147 KIAS.
4.14
THE TRAFFIC PATTERN
32.
The Harvard II traffic pattern is similar to that flown by high performance military
aircraft. During your basic training, the traffic pattern is used to position/reposition

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 14 =====
the aircraft at initial for the overhead break. The traffic pattern is normally flown at
1500 ft AGL or as otherwise defined by local operating procedures. In Moose Jaw the
Harvard II traffic pattern is flown at 3000 ft MSL (approximately 1200 ft AGL) to de-
conflict with Hawk traffic. The Harvard II pattern is flown at an airspeed of 220 KIAS
(approximately 65% torque) which provides sufficient airspeed to glide the aircraft to
the runway from most places in the pattern in the event of an engine failure. Carry out
a circuit check on the downwind leg of every pattern. This check is to be completed
abeam the mid-point of the runway in use.
33.
Use 60 degree bank turns throughout the pattern, except for the last turn to initial,
when the bank should range from 45–60 degrees, as required to line up on the extended
runway centreline. The 45 degree heading change on the entry leg is designed to make
the turn to initial easier to judge. Maintain a vigilant lookout throughout the traffic
pattern, but even more so prior to the turns. Traffic normally joins the traffic pattern
via one of the extended legs; therefore, the greatest potential for conflict exists in the
corners of the pattern.
4.15
JOINING THE TRAFFIC PATTERN
34.
The primary method of joining the traffic pattern is via one of the extended legs of
the published traffic pattern (See Figure 4.8). Entry to the traffic pattern shall be made in
such a manner as to avoid “cutting off” other aircraft, conforming as closely as possible
to the published pattern.
35.
When joining the traffic pattern, ensure that the aircraft is established on the ex-
tended rejoin track, at pattern altitude and airspeed at least 1 NM from the published
traffic pattern to ensure effective lookout prior to entry. Remember that aircraft already
established in the traffic pattern normally have the right of way. If any conflict exists
during the rejoin, take positive action to avoid the traffic and reposition the aircraft for
another rejoin attempt.
4.16
STRAIGHT-IN APPROACHES FROM THE TRAFFIC PATTERN
36.
As was previously mentioned, a portion of the traffic pattern will be used to repo-
sition the aircraft for a straight-in approach if desired. Fly the normal traffic pattern
ground track and speeds until abeam the approach end of the runway in use. Reduce the
PCL to approximately 20% torque and commence a descent at 220 KIAS to 300’ below
normal traffic pattern altitude. Once level, leave the PCL at the reduced torque setting,
allowing the aircraft to decelerate. Continue on the downwind leg until approximately
1 NM beyond the base leg of the OHB traffic pattern. Commence an energy depleting
45 degree angle of bank turn, and roll out on base leg. This will minimize any delays
configuring the aircraft as it should roll out on the base leg below the 147 KIAS max
gear extension speed. Once established on base leg for the straight-in, continue with the
procedures described in Section 4.6.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 15 =====
Figure 4.8: Commonly used VFR Rejoin References
37.
Accurate altitude control and lookout is critical while approaching the base leg
turn point. Considering the altitudes flown in Moose Jaw, other Harvard II traffic may
be joining the traffic pattern with just 300 ft of altitude separation above you.
4.17
THE OVERHEAD BREAK
38.
The overhead break begins at initial. Transmit your position and intentions to the
tower (i.e. APOLLO 4, INITIAL LEFT/RIGHT STOP). Apply crab as required to track
along the extended runway centreline and maintain traffic pattern altitude. See Figure
4.10. Moreover, to maintain an effective lookout, scanning up and to the left/right will
provide situational awareness of possible PFL traffic.
39.
The Break. The break is a 180-degree level turn used to place the aircraft down-
wind and reduce the airspeed in preparation to initiate the pre-landing check (See Figure
4.10). At a point approximately 2000 ft past the runway threshold, assuming average
wind conditions (10 kts headwind component), smoothly roll into a 60-degree bank turn
in the appropriate direction, reduce PCL to IDLE and trim the aircraft as necessary.
During the final portion of the turn, or while rolling out, advance the PCL slightly to
get the engine out of the idle setting. This will minimize the engine spool up time when
the power is later needed. Roll out on downwind, correcting for known wind conditions
if required. Ideally, the roll out will position the aircraft parallel to the runway prior to
reaching the point abeam the runway threshold.
40.
Downwind. Maintain traffic pattern altitude, carry out the pre-landing check and
select flaps to T/O. Power up to arrest the rate at which the airspeed is decreasing (ap-
proximately 35% torque). Remember to constantly re-trim as the airspeed and configu-

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 16 =====
Figure 4.9: Ground Track and References of Common Harvard II Sequences on the
Outer Runway
ration changes.
41.
Landing gear and flap extension must not be delayed to compensate for extending
the downwind leg. Allow the speed to decrease to an ideal speed of 120 KIAS. If
extending, maintain approximately 35% torque in order to maintain speed.
Figure 4.10: OHB Pattern and References RWY 29L
4.18
EFFECT OF WIND ON THE OVERHEAD BREAK
42.
If there is a strong headwind, delay the break so that the increased ground speed
on downwind will not carry the aircraft too far before commencing the final turn. Con-
versely, for a light headwind, the break is performed earlier. Do not vary the angle

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 17 =====
Figure 4.11: OHB Pattern and References RWY 11R
of bank during the overhead break to compensate for a crosswind. If there is a strong
crosswind component, crab into wind once you have rolled out on downwind. Adjust
the ground track on downwind if required to compensate for a stronger crosswind in
preparation for the final turn.
NOTE
The effect of wind will increase with a reduction of airspeed. More com-
pensation for wind may be required to maintain track downwind after
rolling out from the break than on downwind in the traffic pattern.
4.19
THE FINAL TURN
43.
Unlike the final approach, final turns are flown in the intended landing configu-
ration (normally LDG flap). Under normal wind conditions, commence the final turn
abeam the window, as previously described (see Figure 4.12 and 4.13). The point at
which the final turn commences is called the perch. Select your desired flap setting
prior to commencing the final turn. The intent is to fly a continuous descending 180
degree turn from the perch point to the window. Smoothly roll into a turn of up to 45
degrees of bank while lowering the attitude of the aircraft to commence a descent. Set-
ting an attitude that corresponds to approximately one third sky and two thirds ground
when looking out the front windscreen will approximate the initial attitude for the final
turn with LDG flap selected. Rudder is required while rolling into the turn to counteract

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 18 =====
Figure 4.12: OHB and FT RWY 29L
any adverse yaw and also assists in lowering the nose during the roll in. The attitude
described earlier will help you maintain 120 Kts during the final turn. Don’t hesitate
to apply attitude correction to fix an airspeed error. Power setting will control the rate
of descent (approximately 20% torque at the ideal perch point with LDG flap selected).
The minimum allowable speed during the final turn is the threshold crossing speed plus
10 kts (See Figure 4.14). It is important to remember that prior to commencing the final
turn an effective lookout is important to ensure that no potential conflict exists. Scan-
ning along the extended runway centerline will provide situational awareness of traffic
conducting a straight-in approach.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 19 =====
Figure 4.13: OHB and FT RWY 11R
WARNING
Do not exceed 15 units of AOA while manoeuvring during any portion
of the final turn.
The nose of the aircraft should never be allowed to rise up to, or above,
the horizon during the final turn, especially if at or near the minimum
manoeuvring airspeed.
If, at any time during the final turn, any symptoms of an approaching
stall are encountered, immediately carry out the recovery procedures as
detailed in Chapter 5.
44.
Approximately halfway around the turn, as required by local flying orders, request
landing clearance. When the tower clears you to land, confirm that the landing gear is
down and locked.
45.
Ideally, fly the final turn so that the aircraft rolls out on the extended runway centre-
line while passing through the window (¾ NM from the runway threshold). Adjust the
attitude and power to maintain a 3-degree glide path and commence the final approach.
The reduced power setting required to maintain 120 KIAS in the final turn (compared

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 20 =====
Figure 4.14: Approximate Land Flap Final Turn Attitude
to the straight-in) will allow the airspeed to naturally reduce to final approach speed.
Continue with the landing transition, as described in Section 4.8. Figure 4.15 illustrates
the pilot’s view at the window if on glide path, low on glide path, and high on glide path.
46.
Your primary cross-check during the final turn and subsequent alignment with the
runway should alternate between the EASI, the runway sight picture, and the window.
Apply the principals of APT/PAT throughout the final turn. If a deviation occurs, adjust
the power and/or attitude as required to correct back to the ideal sight picture and/or
airspeed and then re-adjust to maintain. Adjustments may require a modification of
just one, or both, variables to effect the desired change. Trim the aircraft to remove all
control pressures.
47.
If the aircraft appears to be overshooting the runway centreline, avoid increasing
the G-forces, which would in turn, increase the AOA. When the error is small, increase
the bank up to a maximum of 45 degrees to decrease the radius of turn, or continue
the turn and make an “S” turn to line up with the centreline of the runway. When the
error is too large – go around (when time permits, advise the tower controller of your
intentions). If the aircraft is undershooting the centreline of the runway, decrease the
bank slightly to increase the radius of the turn. If you are forced to delay the perch,
anticipate requiring more power and a shallower nose attitude to capture the extended
runway centerline on a 3 degree glide path.
48.
Final turns are easily adjusted for any flap setting. When planning to land at a flap
setting other than landing flap from the normal perch point, ensure that the intended
landing configuration is selected prior to commencing the final turn. The final turn
speed remains the same regardless of the configuration. Because of the reduced drag,
lower torque settings and slightly higher attitudes will be required with less flap selected.
Remember that the minimum allowable speed during the final turn is the final approach

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 21 =====
Figure 4.15: Above and Below Glideslope Examples
speed plus 10 kts.
4.20
EFFECT OF WIND ON THE FINAL TURN
49.
As previously mentioned in Section 4.18, the break point is varied longitudinally
in order to deal with the effects of varying wind conditions. The same is true for the
final turn. However, the perch point can be moved both laterally to compensate for a
crosswind, and longitudinally to compensate for varying headwinds. Certain conditions
may require both movements of the perch point.
4.21
LOW APPROACH
50.
A low approach is a planned procedure carried out because you do not wish to
land from a particular approach. There will also be many instances during training
where ATC will direct you to fly a low approach. In any case, do not allow the airspeed
to decrease below 120 KIAS.
51.
A low approach is flown at a minimum altitude of 200 ft AGL. Execute a low
approach by leveling the aircraft at the desired altitude and adding sufficient torque
to maintain 120 KIAS (approximately 35% torque, or approximately 55% torque if
configured with LDG flap). Do not reposition the flaps. Maintain a ground track either

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 22 =====
Figure 4.16: Sample OHB and Perch Point Wind Adjustments
on the extended runway centreline or offset to one side of the runway, depending on
traffic and ATC direction. A low approach is always followed by a go around.
4.22
GO AROUND
52.
A Go Around is carried out when you have decided to discontinue an approach as
a result of a poor approach, conflicting traffic, instructions by ATC, or upon completion
of a low approach.
53.
Use the following procedure when carrying out a go around:
a.
Advance the PCL to MAX anticipating rudder inputs due to the torque of the
engine;
b.
Simultaneously raise the nose to establish the climb attitude of approximately
5–7 degrees nose up; and
c.
When clear of the ground and climbing, raise the gear and, above 110 KIAS,
retract the flaps. Carry out the post-take-off check.
54.
If you carry out a go around on final, parallel the runway, watch for conflicting
traffic, advise the tower of your intentions and rejoin the circuit as you would after
take-off.
NOTE
As a preventive measure to minimize flap over-speeds during go arounds,
aircrew shall adhere to the sterile cockpit concept until the PTO check
is complete.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 23 =====
4.23
BREAK-OUT (TRAFFIC PATTERN OR CIRCUIT)
55.
If you elect to break out from the traffic pattern or circuit, commence a climbing
turn to clear the traffic patterns. Ensure the post-take-off check is complete, depart on a
track approximately 45 degrees to the traffic pattern flow and then advise tower of your
intentions while maintaining a vigilant lookout. Level off at an appropriate altitude and
follow local procedures. When well clear of the traffic pattern, rejoin as described for
the intended approach.
4.24
CLOSED PATTERN
56.
The closed pattern is a climbing 180-degree turn used to reposition the aircraft on
downwind after a low approach or touch-and-go. It uses a minimum amount of fuel
(approximately 15 lbs) and is commonly used to maximize practice of the final turn and
landing.
Figure 4.17: Closed Pattern
57.
After completing the post-take-off check, request clearance from the tower for
a closed pattern. Maintain a shallow climb and allow the airspeed to increase to a
minimum of 140 KIAS. When cleared for a closed pattern, commence a climbing turn
towards downwind using up to 20 degrees of pitch and 45–60 degrees of bank. The bank
angle can be allowed to increase to a maximum of 90 degrees, depending on the rate of
climb. When 200–300 ft below pattern altitude, coordinate the reduction of power,

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 24 =====
pitch, and bank to roll out on downwind at approximately 140 KIAS (approximately
20% torque). It is important to remember that prior to commencing the closed pattern,
an effective lookout is important to ensure that no potential conflict exists. Scanning up
and to the left/right will provide situational awareness of other traffic.
58.
Once established on downwind, check your spacing from the runway. Because
of the relative tightness of a closed pattern you may have to crab out on downwind to
attain the spacing achieved following a normal overhead break. Complete the circuit
check and call downwind to receive your sequencing. When approximately abeam the
wind-adjusted overhead break point, initiate the pre-landing check in preparation for the
final turn.
4.25
FLAPLESS APPROACH AND LANDING
59.
Throughout your basic training you will practice approaches with various flap set-
tings. Additionally, mechanical failure or loss of hydraulics may require the use of an
emergency approach without the use of flaps. The reduced lift and the reduced drag
without flaps adversely affects aircraft performance. The reduced lift dictates a higher
landing speed, which in turn dictates a longer after-landing roll. The reduced drag makes
it more difficult to slow the aircraft from approach speed to the final approach speed.
60.
The propeller will still be very effective, even at lower torque settings associ-
ated with flapless approaches. The approach speed when flapless is 120 KIAS and
the Threshold Crossing Speed is 110 KIAS.
4.26
STRAIGHT-IN FLAPLESS
61.
The procedures to set up for a straight-in flapless approach are the same as for a
straight-in approach. When configuring the aircraft on base leg, set approximately 30%
torque and maintain a minimum of 120 KIAS while manoeuvring the aircraft. Upon
intercepting the glide path (three degrees), set approximately 20% torque and lower the
nose slightly to commence the descent while maintaining 120 KIAS. The attitude of the
aircraft on final is slightly higher than for a normal straight-in approach. During the
approach, glide path maintenance is performed in the same manner as for a straight-in.
62.
As the aircraft approaches the window, reduce the PCL slightly and allow the speed
to reduce to flapless final approach speed of 110 KIAS. The speed reduction should be
planned to have the speed stabilized on short final just prior to commencing the round-
out. Aim to cross the aim point on speed with the control surfaces trimmed. Coordinate
PCL and pitch attitude to maintain proper airspeed and rate of descent. Retard the PCL
to IDLE once the landing is assured. Due to the higher attitude the amount of flare will
be slightly less than for a LDG flap landing. Momentary actuation of the stick shaker
may occur just prior to touchdown. Airspeed will decrease in the flare and touchdown
will normally occur just above the normal landing speed. As soon as the main wheels

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 25 =====
are firmly on the runway, lower the nose-wheel and apply the brakes as required when
below 80 KIAS to slow the aircraft to taxiing speed.
63.
Compared with a landing using flaps, the aircraft:
a.
lands at a higher speed;
b.
has a slightly higher nose attitude at touchdown;
c.
has a longer after-landing roll; and
d.
has a tendency to over-rotate during a flapless touch-and-go landing.
CAUTION
If you wish to do a flapless landing after the flaps have been extended, go
around and return for another approach. Never retract the flaps during
final approach because the subsequent loss of lift will increase the AOA
and may cause the aircraft to stall or sink rapidly.
4.27
GENERAL SAFETY CONSIDERATIONS
64.
Do not hurry to raise the landing gear when you perform a go around from a
bad landing or from ballooning as the aircraft may touch down a second time. Keep
the aircraft straight and the landing attitude constant as you increase the power. The
increasing speed and power will cushion the second touchdown, if there is one. Do not
move the control column forward rapidly to level off after the aircraft balloons; adjust it
smoothly and do not raise the flaps before 110 KIAS as early flap retraction will cause
the aircraft to sink. Finally, be sure to complete the post-take-off check.
65.
When there are other aircraft ahead of you in the pattern, anticipate the possibility
of turbulence. Plan your spacing so that there is sufficient time for the turbulence to
subside. Always keep an appropriate distance between your aircraft and the aircraft in
front. If you encounter jet wash or propeller wash, do not become alarmed. Use firm
control movements to correct the attitude during these deviations. Apply full power and
go around if necessary.
66.
Remain alert at all times and do not make mechanical responses to situations.
Particular attention should be given to ensure that the maximum speeds for selection of
landing gear and flaps are not exceeded.
WARNING
Do no retract ancillaries if overstress, over-speed or damage is suspected
or known. Retracting ancillaries under these circumstances may cause
further damage which may result in the requirement for a gear up land-
ing or an ejection.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 26 =====
4.28
OTHER DIRECTIVES/ADVISORIES FROM MOOSE JAW ATC
67.
“Fly through.” Tower will direct you to “fly through” after your call at initial
if a traffic conflict exists, usually due to a conflicting PFL. Continue to the departure
end of the runway then turn crosswind to rejoin the traffic pattern. You must watch for
traffic established on downwind. Traffic on downwind has the right of way and you
must break out if a conflict exists. A break out from this position is simply a climb
straight ahead above the pattern altitude while maintaining situational awareness of the
conflicting aircraft.
68.
“Low approach left of left/right of right/in between.” This direction is often
given to straight-in traffic that is conflicting with a PFL or someone sequenced for a
final turn (ATC prioritizes final turns over straight-ins). It is a simple modification to
where they want you to position the aircraft during the low approach relative to the
runway. Sometimes they will also include the direction to “move over now” as they see
an impending conflict developing where you would essentially be flying loose formation
with another aircraft on approach. To exit this situation, you may be required to delay
your go around until the aircraft who was cleared for the touch-and-go starts accelerating
away from you. Alternatively, if you feel you are ahead of the conflicting aircraft, it may
be better to start your go around at the runway threshold and positively move ahead of
them. In all cases, ensure clearance from traffic before moving back to the extended
runway centerline.
69.
“Watch for traffic.” ATC may at times use this phrase to alert you to a potential
conflict. If in the traffic pattern and unable to visually locate the traffic, then a break
out might be prudent. If you have the right of way but the conflicting traffic is not
manoeuvring to avoid you, then traffic avoidance becomes your responsibility.
70.
“Simulated Traffic.” Aircraft conducting practice forced landings from the traffic
pattern will zoom and proceed toward the runway threshold via low or final key.
71.
“PFL high to low.” ATC is indicating that there is traffic proceeding from high key
(usually over the threshold at 5000–6000’ MSL) to low key (1 NM abeam the threshold
at roughly 3700’ MSL).
72.
“Cleared the option.” ATC is offering you the option of carrying out a touch and
go, low approach, stop and go, or full stop landing.
73.
“Break departure end.” ATC is clearing you for an OHB but, due to traffic, they
are directing you to delay the break until the departure end of the runway.
74.
“Call in the break.” ATC may make this request to assist them with sequencing
other traffic.
75.
“Early left/right.” Staff may occasionally request an early turn to downwind or
to the flying area. In these cases, they must receive clearance from ATC then initiate a
climbing turn to crosswind before reaching pattern altitude and airspeed.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 27 =====
76.
“Rejoin via Whiskey/Echo.” At times, aircraft will rejoin from the area via the
extended downwind leg instead of the base leg. It is for this reason that you must
lookout before turning downwind from crosswind. Whiskey is a waypoint located on
the extended downwind of runway 29L. Echo is a waypoint located on the extended
downwind of runway 11R.
77.
“Continue with the gear.” This generally occurs when there is traffic ahead on
the runway conducting a stop and they are expected to clear the runway in time for
you to complete a touch-and-go. Your response should be “[call sign] continuing 3
green”. At this point, you are not cleared for a touch and go or landing. Unless you
subsequently receive clearance for a touch and go or landing you are expected to conduct
a low approach and go around.
78.
“Extend.” After requesting a closed pattern, ATC may direct you to extend your
departure leg before issuing a clearance for the closed pattern. This delay is to ensure
appropriate spacing is available with other traffic.
79.
“Unable, rejoin.” Should ATC be unable to accommodate a closed pattern you
will be directed to rejoin. You shall continue straight ahead and follow the standard
traffic pattern.
80.
“Slide over to the inner.” At times, your instructor will request to transition from
the outer runway to the inner runway to expedite recovery. This may be executed from
a final turn or a straight in approach.
81.
“Break right/left to the inner/outer.” Similar to the slide over, a break in the
opposite direction for the given runway (inner or outer) can be used by staff, if cleared
by ATC, to move from one runway to the other.
4.29
UNCONTROLLED AERODROME OPERATIONS
82.
Operations at uncontrolled aerodromes in the Harvard 2 are flown using a square
circuit similar to that flown by light aircraft. All operations at uncontrolled airports
including joining and departing the circuit, conducting multiple circuits or an inspection
pass, as well as all RT shall be carried out IAW GPH 204A or the FAA PHAK (Pilot’s
Handbook of Aeronautical Knowledge). Be mindful that procedures differ slightly in
the US compared to Canada.
83.
Plan to join the circuit and be established downwind at an airspeed that will al-
low for configuration once abeam the approach end of the runway. Remember that the
downwind spacing will be slightly further out from the runway than for a normal circuit.
When the runway threshold is at approximately the rear 45 degree position, commence
a turn to base leg. Upon intercepting a 3 degree glide path, initiate a descent (approxi-
mately 25% torque). Commence the turn to final as required, and fly a normal approach
and landing transition or low approach.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 28 =====
Figure 4.18: Joining Uncontrolled Airport Traffic Pattern – Standard Left Hand Circuit

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 29 =====
CHAPTER 5: STALLS AND SLOW FLYING
5.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
The sequences covered in this chapter are included to familiarize you with the
symptoms and recovery from stalls and the characteristics and feel of the aircraft at low
airspeeds.
2.
Theory of Stalls. The term “stalling” describes the condition in which the lift from
the wings can no longer support the weight of the aircraft. Normally, the airflow over the
wings is smooth, with some minor turbulence towards the trailing edge. As the angle-
of-attack is increased beyond the optimum angle, the airflow begins to break up and
becomes progressively more turbulent, and the area of turbulence thickens and spreads
towards the leading edge. Greater angles-of-attack produce even more turbulence, until
a point is reached beyond which there is a sudden loss of a large percentage of the
total lift. This angle is known as the “critical angle” or “stalling angle”. The indicated
airspeed at which the wings stall is known as the “stalling speed”. An aircraft can stall at
any airspeed, in any attitude, and at any torque setting, provided that the critical angle is
exceeded. The most important factors affecting the indicated stalling speed are weight,
torque, flap position, and load factor.
3.
Another phenomenon occurs as the angle-of-attack increases: the Centre of Pres-
sure (C of P) moves steadily forward until the stalling angle is reached; then it moves
sharply back.
4.
The Harvard II aircraft is equipped with an Angle of Attack (AOA) system. This
system is flap compensated and provides a linear indication on the gauge of zero lift
AOA through maximum lift AOA at the stall, 0 to 18 units. AOA information is valid
for all combinations of weight, configuration, and steady state bank angles.
5.
There are four important things you must learn about stalls. You must be able to:
a.
recognize the symptoms of an approaching stall;
b.
recognize the characteristics of the stall itself;
c.
take the correct recovery action; and
d.
take action to prevent stalls.
75

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 30 =====
5.2
THE SYMPTOMS AND CHARACTERISTICS OF THE STALL
6.
Once you recognize the symptoms of a stall, you know that the aircraft is ap-
proaching a critical condition of flight requiring fast, positive, corrective action. Table
5.1 has been prepared to help you to interpret the aircraft’s stalling symptoms.
Symptom
Method of Recognition
Nose High, Low Airspeed
This warning is most noticeable when the aircraft is about to
stall from the level-flight attitude. Individually, a nose-high atti-
tude or a low airspeed does not constitute a symptom of a stall,
but when they occur together, the aircraft will stall if you fail to
take recovery action.
Sloppy Controls
In the Harvard II, all three controls are effective down to the
stall. However, their effectiveness decreases as the speed de-
creases. This symptom is not always present when the aircraft
is stalled at high speed.
“Stick Shaker”/High AOA
A stall-warning unit has been fitted to provide an artificial warn-
ing by imparting a vibration to the control column as the wings
approach the critical angle. The stick shaker is activated at 15.5
units AOA which equates to 5-10 kts prior to the stall under 1
“G” flight (the aircraft will stall at 18 units AOA).
Aerodynamic Buffet
A stall-warning unit has been fitted to provide an artificial warn-
ing by imparting a vibration to the control column as the wings
approach the critical angle. The stick shaker is activated at 15.5
units AOA which equates to 5–10 kts prior to the stall under 1
G flight (the aircraft will stall at 18 units AOA).
Mush
Mush is a sensation of sinking caused by the high angles of
attack associated with stalling.
Table 5.1: Stall Symptoms
7.
During normal operations recovery action is taken on recognition of the first symp-
tom of an approaching stall. If the aircraft is flown beyond the symptoms with no cor-
rective action, the aircraft will stall. Any of the characteristics in Table 5.2 indicate that
the aircraft has stalled.
5.3
STALL RECOVERY
8.
The aim in stall recoveries is to recover with a minimum loss of altitude or perfor-
mance. The following is the standard stall recovery procedure for the Harvard II aircraft;
it is valid for all types of stalls:
a.
release sufficient back pressure to un-stall the wings;
b.
simultaneously select PCL to MAX;

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 31 =====
Characteristic
Method of Recognition
Nose Drop
When the critical angle of attack is exceeded, there is a possibility
of the nose dropping due to the rapid rearward movement of the
centre of pressure. The stall may also be recognized by a loss of
nose authority and by an increase in aerodynamic buffet.
Wing Drop
There is a possibility of encountering wing drop during all stalls.
This condition occurs if one wing stalls prior to the other wing for
reasons such as turbulence, uncoordinated flight, etc.
Loss of Altitude/Sinking
When the aircraft has stalled, the lift produced is no longer able
to sustain the desired condition of flight. This is characterized by
a sinking sensation and a loss of altitude.
Table 5.2: Stall Characteristics
c.
apply rudder to counteract adverse yaw from any wing drop which may have
occurred during the stall and to counteract torque from selecting PCL to
MAX;
d.
level the wings with aileron (if necessary);
e.
raise the nose to minimize altitude loss and establish a climb as soon as pos-
sible;
f.
once a positive rate of climb is confirmed select landing gear UP and flaps to
T/O, and
g.
above 110 KIAS, select flaps UP and complete the post-take-off check.
NOTE
Maintain a positive rate of climb until completion of the post-take-off
check.
9.
When releasing back pressure, the amount of initial control column movement
need only be sufficient to un-stall the wings. If you apply too much forward movement,
you will lose too much altitude; if you apply too little, you will not un-stall the wings.
With the aid of your instructor and continued practice, you will learn just how much
control movement to use.
10.
When easing out of the dive, if you raise the nose too rapidly, the AOA may in-
crease to 18 units resulting in the aircraft entering a secondary stall; if you raise it too
slowly, the aircraft will lose excessive altitude. Apply sufficient back pressure to main-
tain just outside the stick shaker while raising the nose, momentary activation of the
stick shaker may occur. Raise the nose to the slow flight attitude as this will provide a
positive rate of climb while allowing the aircraft to accelerate out of the stall range.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 32 =====
11.
When power is applied, the aircraft is accelerated in the direction of the thrust; the
effect is proportional to the power used. Therefore, on the recovery, provided that the
power is applied early enough, you may be able to keep the nose above the straight-
and-level position, thus preventing excessive altitude loss. Power is the most important
factor when considering altitude loss in a stall recovery. This can be illustrated if you
do two stall recoveries, one with and one without power, and compare the amount of
altitude lost in each. Rudder will be required to counteract the torque of the engine. Use
only sufficient rudder to eliminate the torque effect and maintain balanced flight.
12.
Normally, you will initiate recovery action as soon as any of the stall symptoms
become evident. However, during dual practice, you may allow the stall to fully develop
so that you can learn and understand the whole recovery procedure. If there is a wing
drop, do not attempt to raise the dropped wing with aileron until you have first un-stalled
the wings, as this could aggravate the situation. Do not use rudder to raise the dropped
wing; this could cause the aircraft to enter a spin.
13.
Your instructor will demonstrate all types of stalls and will explain the degree to
which the controls are used for recovery from each type. As you become more proficient
you will recover from stalls in any attitude, and at various airspeeds and power settings.
5.4
THE PRE-STALL, SPIN, AEROBATIC (PSSA) CHECK
14.
Before you practice stalls, do the Pre-Stall, Spin and Aerobatics (PSSA) Check.
This check is outlined in the checklist, but because the flight manual does not elaborate on
the check it is detailed in Table 5.3. If the time interval between manoeuvres is short,
only the items of the PSSA check that change quickly need be checked. These would
include, as a minimum: the aircraft fuel state, a confirmation that the area and altitude
are still suitable, and lookout (a FAAL check).
5.5
CLEAN STALL
15.
Entry. To enter the clean stall, complete the PSSA check and during your lookout,
reduce the PCL to IDLE. Maintain altitude as the airspeed decreases by increasing the
back pressure on the control column to increase the pitch attitude and re-trim as required.
If the speed brake was used to decelerate, ensure it is retracted before the stall in case
an unintentional spin is encountered. The stick shaker operates at 5–10 kts above the
clean stalling speed of approximately 80–85 KIAS, depending on the all-up-weight of
the aircraft. Stick shaker will occur at 15.5 units AOA and the aircraft will stall at 18
units.
16.
Recovery. When the aircraft stalls, use the standard stall recovery procedure.
17.
Power on Stalls. After becoming proficient at recovering from a powered-off
clean stall, your instructor will have you practice the clean stall at various torque settings
