5.6
HIGH-SPEED STALL
18.
When the critical angle is exceeded, an aircraft will stall at any attitude, airspeed,
or torque setting. Load factor (G) increases the stalling speed. There will be noticeable
buffet as the aircraft approaches the critical angle during a high-speed stall.
19.
Entry. To enter this stall from a wings-level attitude, complete a PSSA check, set
PCL to IDLE, reduce airspeed with speed brake, and then, at approximately 140 KIAS,
retract the speed brake. Increase back pressure rapidly to make the aircraft stall at a
speed higher than the 1 G stalling speed. During the stall the aircraft will sink, even
though the nose has been raised above the horizon.
20.
To practice high-speed stalls in a turn, reduce PCL to IDLE and at approximately
140 KIAS, put the aircraft into a steep turn, and tighten the turn to induce a stall. Be
prepared for a possible flick roll during this stall as one wing may stall before the other.
21.
Recovery. When the aircraft stalls, carry out the standard stall recovery procedure.
22.
The procedures necessary to recover from high-speed stalls vary with the circum-
stances of the stall. When practicing high-speed stalls at 140 KIAS with the power at

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 34 =====
idle, always carry out the standard stall recovery procedure. In many advanced aero-
batics and turning manoeuvres, it is sufficient to release just enough back pressure to
prevent stick shaker operation and to continue with the manoeuvre.
5.7
FINAL TURN STALL
23.
During the final turn, the aircraft is at an altitude which allows you little time to
recover from a stall; therefore, you must be able to recognize the symptoms of a stall
and take immediate recovery action. For this reason, proficiency in this type of stall
recovery is extremely important.
24.
A final turn stall may be caused by:
a.
lowering flaps in the turn without lowering the nose, thus causing the airspeed
to decrease;
b.
using excessive G in an attempt to correct a steep nose-down attitude or to
prevent overshooting the runway centerline;
c.
neglecting to lower flap prior to turning final and allowing the airspeed to
decrease;
d.
setting PCL improperly; or
e.
any combination of the above.
25.
Entry. To enter this stall, complete the PSSA check and position yourself on
a simulated downwind position. Maintain altitude and when airspeed is below 147
KIAS, lower the landing gear and set T/O flap. Set torque to stabilize at 120 KIAS
(approximately 35%) and carry out the pre-landing check as per the normal downwind
procedure. Trim as required throughout. At a simulated perch point, start a final turn.
During or prior to the turn you can simulate any situation (outlined above) by using
various flap, angles of bank, wing loading, and PCL setting. The nose attitude of the
aircraft can be adjusted as required to accomplish the desired scenario.
CAUTION
Care must be exercised when using wing loading (G) to induce the fi-
nal turn stall as there is a smaller margin of error available to prevent
overstressing the aircraft in the landing configuration.
26.
Recovery. When the aircraft stalls, carry out the standard stall recovery procedure.
27.
The final turn stall should be practiced in both directions so that you are aware of
the characteristics of the stall and recovery from either direction. Prompt recovery action
is of prime importance to ensure a successful recovery from a final turn stall. Should
you experience the symptoms of a stall on the actual final turn, recover immediately and
then consider your position relative to other traffic.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 35 =====
5.8
THE LANDING ATTITUDE STALL
28.
A stall in the landing attitude allows very little time for the recovery as it would
happen during a critical phase of flight at very low altitudes and at low airspeeds. Further
to this, the aircraft is fully configured with low power settings. You must therefore be-
come adept at recognizing the symptoms of the impending stall and applying immediate
recovery action.
29.
Entry. To enter this stall, complete the PSSA Check and select PCL to IDLE.
Maintain altitude as the airspeed decreases by adjusting pitch attitude and trim through-
out. When airspeed is below 147 KIAS, carry out the pre-landing check and continue
to allow the airspeed to decrease until the aircraft stalls. The stall characteristics are
similar to a clean stall, except that the stalling speed is lower (68–76 KIAS).
30.
Recovery. When the aircraft stalls, use the standard stall recovery procedure.
31.
You will practice entering landing attitude stalls with power on as well as power
off. Due to the spool up time required when the PCL is at idle, you will find that the
altitude loss is considerably greater.
32.
You should also practice the landing attitude stall in various configurations, such as
with half flap or no flap, so that you can see the characteristics of the stall and recovery
from these configurations.
5.9
SLOW FLIGHT
33.
Slow flight acquaints you with the handling characteristics of the aircraft at low
airspeeds and illustrates the importance of smooth control coordination. This manoeu-
vre enables you to experience aircraft operation in the region of reverse command and
the inherent characteristics of flight there. While operating in this region, you will find
that large power changes have a relatively small effect on aircraft performance. Slow
flight also exposes you to the control pressures and flight conditions usually encountered
during the early stages of a go around or touch-and-go.
34.
Entry. To enter slow flight complete the PSSA check and, when entering from
higher airspeeds, retard the PCL to IDLE. Maintain altitude throughout the manoeuvre
by raising the pitch attitude as airspeed decreases and trim throughout. At approximately
150 KIAS, set 10%–20% torque, and when below 147 KIAS, complete the pre-landing
check. As the AOA approaches 15 units, smoothly apply sufficient power to maintain
level flight (35–40% torque). A momentary encounter with stick shaker may occur.
Your prime reference to maintain level flight during this manoeuvre is the horizon. You
may have to make slight PCL and pitch changes to maintain altitude and AOA.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 36 =====
35.
Your instructor will demonstrate the decreased effectiveness of the controls at low
speeds. Notice that the controls have to be moved farther than normal to get effective
control response and that the pressures are light.
36.
Lookout. Because of the relatively high nose attitude, forward visibility is de-
creased; therefore, you must maintain a good lookout before and during slow flight.
37.
Once you have mastered slow flight straight-and-level, you will practice turns to
enhance your lookout and improve your low airspeed aircraft handling. At low airspeed,
a shallow angle-of-bank will result in a relatively high rate-of-turn. Use only gentle
turns, as the aircraft will stall at comparatively low bank angles. A slight increase in
torque is necessary during the turn to maintain AOA.
38.
Recovery. Recover from slow flight using the following procedure:
a.
maintain slow flight attitude;
b.
set PCL to MAX;
c.
establish a positive rate of climb then select gear UP and flaps T/O;
d.
at 110 KIAS select flaps UP and complete the post-take-off check.
5.10
STALL SEQUENCE
39.
To facilitate the practice of all the stalls and slow flight, a stall sequence should
be developed. Plan your sequence to allow a logical and efficient transition between
manoeuvres. The stall sequence normally consists of slow flight, a landing attitude
stall, a final turn stall, and a high speed stall.
40.
Carry out the PSSA check prior to commencing your sequence. Plan your se-
quence to remain within the area cleared and maintain a conscientious lookout. You
should not need to do another PSSA check between manoeuvres. Your instructor will
help you develop an efficient stall sequence.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 37 =====
Figure 5.1: Slow Flight Stall Sequence

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 38 =====
CHAPTER 6: UNUSUAL FLIGHT ATTITUDES AND SPIN-
NING
6.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
During your training you will operate the aircraft near the edge of its flight enve-
lope. Sequences flown near the edge of the flight envelope include stalls, spins, unusual
attitudes, basic aerobatics, advanced aerobatics, and advanced formation fluid manoeu-
vring. If you follow the recommended procedures and techniques described in this
Manual, you will safely achieve the aim of each training sequence and remain within
aircraft limits. Should you find yourself in an unusual flight attitude or a situation where
the aircraft does not respond as expected to your control inputs you must carry out the
procedures described in this chapter to ensure your safety and the continued operation
of the aircraft. The same applies if you experience a G Induced Loss of Consciousness
84

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 39 =====
(G-LOC) or an Almost Loss of Consciousness (A-LOC).
WARNING
In situations with low indicated airspeeds and extreme nose-high at-
titudes, if the aircraft does not respond immediately and in a normal
sense to application of flight controls, reduce the PCL to IDLE, neutral-
ize flight controls, and carry out the remainder of the departure from
controlled flight checklist as required. This will also apply to situations
of G-LOC and A-LOC until such time that you are able to take further
recovery actions.
WARNING
If an inadvertent departure from controlled flight is encountered while
inverted or with power on, engine damage should be suspected and un-
usual engine operation, low oil pressure, and/or CHIP annunciator illu-
mination may be experienced. The mission shall be terminated and, if
possible, the aircraft shall be recovered via a PEL. After landing notify
maintenance and the engine shall be inspected by qualified personnel.
WARNING
If the aircraft is not in a recognized, recoverable attitude by 6000’ AGL:
EJECT
6.2
NOSE-HIGH UNUSUAL ATTITUDE
2.
Symptoms are:
a.
lack of horizon references, windscreen predominantly sky;
b.
altitude increasing; and
c.
airspeed decreasing.
3.
Recovery. Once it is confirmed that the aircraft is in a nose-high attitude, use the
following recovery procedure:
a.
PCL to MAX while applying sufficient rudder to counteract the torque of the
engine. This minimizes a further loss of airspeed during recovery;

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 40 =====
b.
roll towards the nearest horizon and maintain slight positive G. Avoid exces-
sive back pressure because of the danger of stalling the aircraft. Do not allow
the aircraft to roll beyond the inverted position. (Increasing the aircraft’s
bank angle towards the nearest horizon provides the shortest route towards
the horizon and will also change the aircraft lift vector so that at 90 degrees of
bank there is no lift holding the aircraft above the horizon. In fact, when the
bank angle exceeds 90 degrees the lift vector is then being applied towards
the horizon thereby expediting the recovery); and
c.
roll out as the nose passes through the horizon and the airspeed is increasing
and flyable. You may also reference the AOA gauge throughout the recovery.
4.
If you have to recover from a vertical attitude, the roll to the horizon is not nec-
essary. Continuous back pressure is all that is needed to reach the horizon, from which
point you can roll wings level. Remember that you can only use this technique when
your attitude is at or near the vertical.
5.
Your instructor will have you practice recoveries from nose-high attitudes initially
at a relatively high airspeed, then at gradually reduced speeds as you gain proficiency.
Always remember to bring the nose of the aircraft to the horizon by the shortest route.
6.3
NOSE-LOW UNUSUAL ATTITUDE
6.
Symptoms are:
a.
windscreen predominantly or all ground;
b.
altitude decreasing; and,
c.
airspeed increasing.
7.
Recovery. Once it is confirmed that the aircraft is in a nose-low attitude, use the
following recovery procedure:
a.
PCL to IDLE and extend the speed brake (if required);
b.
simultaneously level the wings; and
c.
recover from the dive while minimizing altitude loss, being careful not to
overstress the aircraft.
8.
When recovering from nose-low attitudes, remember to level the wings before you
ease out of the dive. It is possible to exceed the rolling G limitations of the aircraft if
back pressure is increased while rolling wings level. Also, leveling the wings quickly

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 41 =====
prior to applying back pressure will position the lift vector towards the horizon which
will make maximum use of the extra lift produced by increasing the G on the aircraft.
NOTE
During nose-low unusual attitudes where the airspeed is very low and
the dive attitude is shallow, full power may be used to minimize altitude
loss.
6.4
POST RECOVERY ACTIONS
9.
Upon completion of the recovery actions from either unusual attitude, establish the
aircraft in a stable flight condition with a medium power setting and retract the speed
brake (if required). Conduct an effective lookout to assess potential conflicting traffic
and confirm airspace. In order to assess the cause and/or results of the unusual attitude,
carry out the following actions:
a.
Assess human factors: conduct a check of yourself and how you feel both
physically and mentally, including a check of OBOGS (both), oxygen con-
nections (both), G-suit test, and cabin pressurization;
b.
Assess aircraft general condition in order to ascertain that the engine is op-
erating properly and there was no over-speed, overstress or other structural
damage;
c.
Conduct a post-take-off check to ensure all ancillaries were retracted and
suffered no damage; and
d.
Analyze the situation by asking the question “Why did this occur?” The
information garnered from this self-examination will prove beneficial in de-
termining the next logical course of action.
6.5
SPINS
10.
In the early days of flying, a spin was considered a highly dangerous manoeuvre.
Today, spins and spin recoveries are routine exercises in many aircraft.
11.
Intentional spinning is to be performed dual only. Spins are practiced so that you
may learn to recognize the instrument, physical, and visual indications of a spin and also
to allow you to practice the spin recovery procedure. Basically, the Harvard II is a stable
aircraft which has to be grossly mishandled to enter a spin and will recover rapidly when
the correct recovery action is taken. You will learn to recognize the symptoms of a spin
and, more importantly, how to prevent the aircraft from entering an inadvertent spin.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 42 =====
12.
The theory of spinning is detailed in Aerodynamics, Volume 1, Manual of Train-
ing, A-12-050-001/PT-001. The characteristics of spins and recovery procedures are
described in the following paragraphs.
6.6
SYMPTOMS AND RECOGNITION OF A SPIN
13.
Incipient Stage. This is the transitory period between a stall and a fully developed
spin; it may be the result of a failure to recover from a stall.
14.
Usually, the warning symptoms are obvious enough for you to recognize the in-
cipient stage in time to recover before the aircraft goes into a fully developed spin.
Immediately centralizing the controls (primarily the release of back pressure to un-stall
the wings) results in recovery from an incipient spin to a stabilized condition of flight.
15.
Spin Characteristics. If you were to become disoriented or did not recognize the
symptoms of an incipient spin, you could get into a fully developed spin. The aircraft
will typically stabilize with the nose 60 degrees below the horizon and the pitch moment
will be slightly oscillatory. This pitching motion will dampen out after two to three
rotations at which point the spin will have reached a near steady state condition. The
aircraft will complete one revolution approximately every 3 seconds with an altitude
loss of 400 to 500 ft per turn. The turn needle will be totally deflected in the direction of
the spin. The AOA indicator will be near the 12 o’clock position and the airspeed will
stabilize at approximately 120 KIAS.
16.
Ailerons have a pronounced effect on the spin. With the ailerons held neutral, or
in the direction of the spin, roll and yaw become noticeably oscillatory, especially in a
spin to the right. If ailerons are deflected opposite to the direction of the spin, the roll
and yaw oscillations are dampened out.
17.
To recap, when the aircraft is stabilized in an upright spin, the instruments give the
following indications:
a.
Altimeter: shows a rapid descent;
b.
Turn-and-Slip Indicator: turn pointer is fully deflected in the direction of
the spin and the ball is close to the center;
c.
Airspeed Indicator: stabilizes at approximately 120 KIAS;
d.
AOA Indicator: stabilizes near the 12 o’clock position; and
e.
Vertical Speed Indicator: shows a maximum rate of descent.
6.7
SPIN RECOVERY
18.
The pilot will initiate the recovery with an action aimed at opposing the autorota-
tion and reducing the incidence to un-stall the wings. There is a standard spin recovery,

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 43 =====
based on rudder correcting the yaw and elevator un-stalling the wings. Torque aggra-
vates a spin and causes the aircraft to lose more altitude on recovery; therefore, it is
necessary to reduce the PCL to IDLE. If the flaps and landing gear are extended, clean
up the aircraft.
19.
The recovery from any inadvertent spin in the Harvard II is:
a.
carry out the Inadvertent Departure From Controlled Flight red page re-
sponse; and
NOTE
If the control column is not centralized during the recovery (ap-
proximately two thirds forward of its total fore–aft distance) a
secondary spin may develop when opposite rudder is applied.
b.
if the aircraft remains in an upright spin, apply anti-spin controls as follows:
(1)
apply full rudder opposite to the turn pointer;
(2)
move control column progressively forward of the neutral (centralized)
position with ailerons neutral (centralized);
(3)
when spinning stops, centralize the controls;
(4)
level the wings; and
(5)
smoothly recover aircraft to level flight.
NOTE
Avoid abrupt forward control column movement to prevent over-
stressing the aircraft with negative G.
20.
Recovery action should be positive, and a firm grip should be maintained on the
controls because there will be some elevator and rudder buffet. Do not apply aileron at
any time in the spin: if the ailerons are deflected when the aircraft enters a spin, return
them to the centralized position. Spin rotation will cease abruptly with the aircraft in
a steep nose down attitude within 1½ turns after applying spin recovery control inputs.
Centralize the rudder as soon as the spinning stops to prevent the aircraft from entering
a spin in the opposite direction. Care must be taken not to raise the nose too quickly;
quick reference to the AOA gauge will assist you in easing out of the resultant dive.
Should you feel the stick shaker or experience any of the other stall symptoms, reduce
the amount of back pressure on the control column and continue recovery. Failure to do
this may precipitate another spin.
6.8
THE PRE-STALL, SPIN, AEROBATIC (PSSA) CHECK
21.
Before putting the aircraft into an intentional spin, complete the PSSA Check as
outlined in the checklist. Plan the spin to have the recovery complete before 10,000’

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 44 =====
MSL or 6000’ AGL, whichever is higher. For planning purposes, altitude loss during
a six-turn spin will be approximately 4500’ from entry until recovery is completed and
level flight is attained. This is the only flight manoeuvre that is taught in the basic
syllabus in which the pilot has no control over the aircraft until the recovery has been
initiated. It is imperative that the lookout be all encompassing. To this end, lookout
turns will be in the form of wingovers or clearing turns which allow for better visibility
of the area directly below the aircraft.
WARNING
Intentional spins below 10,000’ MSL are prohibited due to the high
stresses on the propeller, which occur during the spin manoeuvre with
the propeller RPM below 80%.
6.9
SPIN ENTRY
22.
On completion of the PSSA check, enter the spin without delay. Reduce the PCL
to IDLE during the clearing turns; if the speed brake was used to decelerate, ensure it is
retracted before the stall. Maintain a level to shallow climbing flight attitude until a stall
characteristic is met, at which point apply full rudder in the direction of the intended
spin and simultaneously move the control column fully aft. To ensure an expeditious
entry following the clearing turns, do not allow the aircraft to descend prior to the stall.
23.
Power-off spin entry is characterized by roll and yaw in the direction rudder is ap-
plied, resulting in a barrel-roll-under manoeuvre to a near level attitude after completing
the first turn. Wing drop, opposite to the direction of the spin, may be experienced if the
up-going wing is stalled early by applying aileron to increase bank during spin entry.
If the spin is entered using the correct technique, the aircraft will enter smoothly into a
stabilized spin.
24.
A spin which is entered intentionally for practice purposes does not require appli-
cation of the Inadvertent Departure from Controlled Flight red page response. This is a
planned event used to gain experience at recognizing a spin and practicing the anti-spin
techniques. Check the instruments to ensure that the aircraft has stabilized in a fully
developed spin. If the airspeed is high or increasing above 150 KIAS, recover immedi-
ately. During the spin, hold the control column fully back without applying aileron and
maintain full rudder in the direction of the spin until commencement of the recovery
procedure.
25.
To recover from an intentional spin:
a.
Centralize the controls;
b.
Pause and Assess; and

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 45 =====
c.
Apply anti-spin controls as detailed in Section 6.7.
26.
To simulate a situation where you may have become disoriented or allowed a fully
developed spin to occur, your instructor will enter a stabilized spin and then give you
control. Your immediate action will be to apply the red page response for an Inadver-
tent Departure from Controlled Flight. If the spin is confirmed and the aircraft is still
spinning, carry out the remainder of the spin recovery procedure.
6.10
SAFETY FACTORS
27.
During the spin, sit erect and observe the ground through the windscreen. Do not
move your head unnecessarily: this may induce vertigo and/or airsickness.
28.
Your instructor will tell you when to recover from a spin by using the single word
recover. Should the intercom fail during a spin, your instructor will stir the stick in order
to take control and recover from the spin.
29.
Spins must be fully recovered by 6000’ AGL. If the aircraft does not recover by
6000’ AGL — EJECT!
30.
While the aircraft is spinning, you have little control over it until you begin recov-
ery action. You must observe the following rules when you practice spins:
a.
do the PSSA check before starting the exercise;
b.
clear the area well and enter the spin without delay; and
c.
spins are to be only practiced while dual.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 46 =====
CHAPTER 7: BASIC AEROBATICS
7.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
The purpose of teaching aerobatics is to help you to develop a more sensitive
“feel” while you are controlling the aircraft, and to improve your ability to coordinate
the use of controls during varying airspeeds and attitudes. Learning to fly the following
basic aerobatic manoeuvres will improve your overall flying ability and increase your
confidence. In addition, it will lead to greater familiarity with all attitudes of flight, and
will allow the aircraft to be flown closer to its maximum capabilities. Emphasis during
aerobatics should be on smoothness and continuity, not merely on pre-determined entry
and exit airspeeds.
7.2
G AWARENESS
2.
During any mission where manoeuvres exceeding 3 G will be flown, the following
exercise shall be performed. After clearing the area, set PCL to MAX, accelerate to a
minimum of 220 KIAS, commence a 3 G turn through 90 degrees, roll out, unload to ½
G for 5 seconds, reverse, and commence a 4 G turn through 180 degrees. The aircraft
is then turned 90 degrees back to the original heading using a 3 G turn. If the situation
dictates (airspace, weather, etc.) the last turn may be completed in either direction, but
is normally flown back to the original heading. This exercise can be completed at any
time during the mission prior to commencing high G manoeuvres and is performed in
order to confirm the aircrew’s physiological readiness. If you are not feeling well (i.e.
grey/black out, experience nausea, etc.), inform your instructor immediately.
7.3
THE PRE-STALL, SPIN, AEROBATIC (PSSA) CHECK
3.
Before any aerobatic manoeuvres are carried out, the PSSA Check must be com-
pleted. When checking the fuel state, ensure any imbalance is less than 50 lbs. If the
time interval is short between aerobatic manoeuvres, only the items of the PSSA check
which change quickly need be checked. These would include, as a minimum, the aircraft
fuel state, a confirmation that the area and altitude are still suitable, and lookout.
92

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 47 =====
4.
Before conducting any aerobatic manoeuvre, the aircraft should be trimmed in all
axes to a selected speed, normally the optimum entry speed of the manoeuvre. This trim
setting should not be re-adjusted during aerobatics as this will ensure that the control
pressures will feel the same every time a particular aerobatic manoeuvre is performed.
5.
The lookout required to clear the area will vary according to the complexity of the
aerobatic manoeuvre. Although the area should be cleared in all directions, special em-
phasis should be placed on those directions in which the aircraft will be moving. Look-
out continues throughout each exercise; again, the emphasis is placed in the direction
that the aircraft is traveling. A good lookout is extremely important during aerobatics
since the aircraft is manoeuvring through a large airspace.
6.
During aerobatics, a good awareness of flight attitude, airspeed, and especially alti-
tude must be maintained in order not to violate published minima. Looping manoeuvers
typically use approximately 3000’ of vertical airspace.
7.
Power settings for individual aerobatics will vary slightly with altitude, temper-
ature and aircraft weight/configuration changes. These changes are negligible in the
Harvard II and PCL set to MAX is used for most basic aerobatics. See Table 7.1 for
suggested power settings and airspeeds.
Manoeuvre

Table 7.1: Basic Aerobatics Suggested Power Settings and Airspeeds
7.4
THE AILERON ROLL
8.
An aileron roll (Figure 7.1) is an aerobatic manoeuvre in which the aircraft is rolled
through 360 degrees around its longitudinal axis. This manoeuvre is accomplished by
use of ailerons.
9.
The aileron roll is commenced with an entry airspeed of 230 KIAS, with PCL
set to MAX.The nose is then smoothly raised through the reference to a pitch attitude
of approximately 20 degrees. Once this attitude has been reached the back pressure is

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 48 =====
Figure 7.1: Aileron Roll
released and aileron deflection is smoothly applied. When the desired rate of roll is
attained the aileron deflection is held constant. The nose may drop through the hori-
zon during the roll. The termination of the roll is anticipated by reducing the aileron
deflection so that the ailerons are neutral as the wings-level attitude is reached. Slight
back pressure may be required to return the aircraft to level flight. Minor coordination
of rudder inputs may be required to maintain balanced flight throughout the roll.
NOTE
The aircraft will tend to “barrel” very slightly during a roll in either
direction: do not attempt to prevent this with forward control pressure.
7.5
THE LOOP
10.
The loop (Figure 7.2) is a 360-degree turn in the vertical plane, the elevators being
the prime control.
11.
In order to commence a loop, set PCL to MAX, attain 230 KIAS and align the air-
craft with (or parallel to) a reference line with the wings level. Back pressure is applied
on the control column to maintain approximately 3–4 G until the aircraft approaches
the vertical. As the nose is raised, ensure the wings are kept level with aileron. As the
airspeed decreases, continued rearward movement of the control column is required to

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 49 =====
Figure 7.2: Loop
maintain sufficient back pressure to keep the nose moving at a constant rate. As the
airspeed decreases further, the G will bleed off, but sufficient back pressure should be
maintained to cross the top of the loop with slight positive G. As the aircraft approaches
the top of the loop, the opposite horizon is used as a reference. Because of the torque of
the engine, the aircraft will want to move off heading during the loop. Coordinate rudder
inputs to maintain balanced flight and keep the aircraft from tracking off the reference
line throughout the loop. If the aircraft has strayed from the desired track, alignment
may be regained by applying aileron in the direction of the reference line and leveling
the wings when track has been regained.
12.
After the nose passes through the horizon the back pressure is increased. As the
airspeed increases, continued forward movement of the control column will be required
to maintain a constant rate of pitch change. Additionally, as the speed builds up, antic-
ipate removing rudder inputs required to keep alignment. To complete the loop, adjust
back pressure to achieve 230 KIAS.
13.
Because the elevator is the prime control in a loop, the control of back pressure
determines the quality of the loop. If too much back pressure is used initially, the angle-
of-attack will increase too rapidly and the aircraft may high-speed stall. If too little back
pressure is used on pull-up, there will not be enough momentum to carry the aircraft
across the top of the loop, and the aircraft may stall. The nose should be kept moving
at a constant rate. Too much back pressure during the recovery may precipitate a high-
speed stall.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 50 =====
7.6
THE CUBAN EIGHT
14.
The Cuban Eight (Figure 7.3) is a manoeuver that combines portions of the loop
and the roll.
Figure 7.3: Cuban 8
15.
The entry is identical to the loop up to the point when the aircraft reaches the
inverted position. At this point the approaching horizon is checked and a reference
point is picked 45 degrees below the horizon. As the nose nears this point, the back
pressure is released and a half-roll is commenced around this point. To prevent the nose
of the aircraft from barreling off the reference point during the half-roll, it is flown as a
point roll at approximately ½ G. Coordinated rudder input may be required to maintain
alignment during the roll. As the wings approach level, the aileron deflection is reduced
to neutral and rudder inputs for the roll are removed. Back pressure is then applied to
achieve a constant rate pull-out to attain 230 KIAS as the nose passes through the level
flight attitude. To complete the manoeuvre the entire procedure is repeated, this time
rolling in the opposite direction to recover to level flight on the original heading at 230
KIAS.
7.7
THE CLOVER LEAF
16.
The cloverleaf (Figure 7.4) is a manoeuvre in which portions of the loop and roll
are combined.

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 51 =====
Figure 7.4: Clover Leaf
17.
Entry for the clover leaf is similar to the loop. With the wings level, back pressure
is applied to maintain 2.5–3.5 G. When the nose is at a pitch attitude of approximately
60 degrees, a reference point, which is 90 degrees off the entry direction, is chosen on
the horizon. Back pressure is then maintained and aileron pressure is applied to start a
roll in the direction of the chosen reference point. The aileron deflection is regulated to
maintain a constant rate of roll so that the wings are level when the aircraft is inverted
and aligned with the selected reference point. As the speed slows, coordinated rudder
input will be required to counteract the engine torque. Once aligned with the 90 degree
reference point, the aileron deflection is then reduced to neutral, and back pressure is
increased to recover the aircraft to level flight at 230 KIAS similar to the last half of a
loop. Sufficient back pressure should be maintained to cross the top of the manoeuvre
with slight positive G.
7.8
HESITATION ROLL
18.
The hesitation roll (Figure 7.5) is merely an aileron roll with a hesitation at the
inverted position. As in the aileron roll, the aircraft tends to barrel very slightly. Do not
attempt to prevent this.
19.
The first half of the manoeuvre is exactly the same as for the aileron roll. As

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 52 =====
Figure 7.5: Hesitation Roll
the aircraft approaches the inverted position, the aileron pressure is relaxed so that the
ailerons are neutral as the aircraft reaches the inverted position. Coordinated rudder
input may be required to counteract the engine torque. Following a pause, the roll is
then continued in the same direction and the aircraft is recovered to the wings-level
upright position on the same reference line as when the roll was entered.
20.
If the hesitation roll is used to increase airspeed, the length of the hesitation is
increased and the nose of the aircraft is allowed to drop at the inverted position. Due to
the difference in airspeed, control column pressures may have to be adjusted to maintain
the same rate-of-roll during the roll to the upright position. The hesitation is very useful
as a linking manoeuvre during multiple aerobatics.
7.9
ROLL OFF THE TOP
21.
The Roll Off the Top (Figure 7.6) consists of the first half of a loop followed by
a half-roll back to level flight. The result is a considerable gain in altitude along with a
180-degree change of direction.
22.
The aircraft is set up for a Roll off the top by setting MAX torque, attaining 250
KIAS and aligning the aircraft with a suitable reference line. Back pressure is applied to
set approximately 4 G. The higher entry airspeed allows the G to be maintained longer,

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 53 =====
Figure 7.6: Roll Off the Top
ensuring sufficient airspeed for the half-roll. As the airspeed decreases, continued rear-
ward movement of the control column is required to maintain sufficient back pressure
to keep the nose moving at a constant rate. Coordinated rudder will be required as the
speed bleeds off to counteract the torque of the engine. As the nose approaches the op-
posite horizon, the back pressure is released and coordinated aileron and rudder pressure
are applied to complete a half-roll back to level flight. To prevent the nose of the aircraft
from barreling off the reference line during the half-roll, it is to be flown at approxi-
mately ½ G. In order to recover to a straight-and-level attitude, the roll must be started
while the nose of the aircraft is still above the horizon. The speed will be approximately
120 KIAS upon completion of the manoeuvre.
7.10
HALF ROLL PULL THROUGH
23.
The Half Roll Pull Through (Figure 7.7) is a manoeuvre in which the aircraft is
rolled to the inverted position, and then pulled through as in the last half of a loop. The
result is a loss of altitude, a gain in airspeed, and a 180-degree change of direction.
24.
A half roll pull through is started at an airspeed of 120 KIAS, with the aircraft
aligned to a suitable reference line and the wings level. The nose is then raised to a pitch
attitude of approximately 20 degrees, back pressure is released and the PCL is reduced to
IDLE. Coordinated aileron and rudder pressure are then applied to complete a half-roll

===== SMM part 2 PDF page 54 =====
Figure 7.7: Half Roll Pull Through
to the wings-level inverted position. To prevent the nose of the aircraft from barreling
off the reference line during the half-roll, it is to be flown at approximately ½ G. The
inverted position is anticipated by smoothly relaxing the aileron pressure so that the
ailerons are neutral as the wings reach level. Without pausing in the inverted position,
back pressure is smoothly applied to attain a constant rate of pitch change and achieve
level flight at 230 KIAS. The back pressure during the first part of the pull-through
should be sufficient to avoid an excessive build-up of airspeed and consequent high G
loads. The speed-brake should be extended if an excessive altitude loss or airspeed gain
is anticipated. Power may be increased during the last part of the pull-through to enter
another manoeuvre, if desired.
7.11
MULTIPLE AEROBATICS
25.
To further improve your pilot ability and airmanship, and to save time during
practice, a number of individual manoeuvres may be combined into one continuous
sequence. The airspace involved is considerably greater than that required for single
manoeuvres; therefore, greater emphasis must be placed on clearing the area. The exer-
cise should be planned so that minimum altitude restrictions are respected and aircraft
remain in the area that was cleared.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 1 =====
26.
A good aerobatic sequence requires careful planning in selecting manoeuvres which
can be linked together easily. Power settings may be varied as required for each manoeu-
vre, or a constant (average) power setting may be used to maintain a smooth, continuous
sequence. Your instructor will assist you in planning a sequence that will fulfill these
goals.
27.
Repetition of a manoeuvre in a sequence is not good planning; however, the same
manoeuvre may be done in opposite directions. The important factors in multiple aer-
obatics is the development of good judgement, smoothness, and continuity. Each ma-
noeuvre should flow into the next. If your airspeed is not exactly where it should be for
the follow-on manoeuvre, avoid the temptation of hesitating between the manoeuvres to
make the adjustment. If the error is not too large, try to compensate for and correct it
during the follow-on manoeuvre.
7.12
SAFETY FACTORS
28.
The following speed restrictions apply for aerobatic manoeuvres:
a.
Rolling Manoeuvres. The minimum entry speed for complete rolling ma-
noeuvres is 180 KIAS. Normal entry speed for a complete rolling manoeuvre
is 230 KIAS.
b.
Looping Manoeuvres. The minimum entry speed for looping manoeuvres
is 200 KIAS. The maximum entry speed for basic looping manoeuvres is 250
KIAS. Normal entry speeds vary from 230–250 KIAS.
29.
The following safety considerations apply when manoeuvring in the extreme atti-
tudes achievable during aerobatics:
a.
ensure all aerobatic manoeuvring is conducted above the minimum altitudes
published in local directives;
b.
if at any time you become disoriented, discontinue the manoeuvre and carry
out the appropriate unusual attitude recovery;
c.
should the symptoms of a high speed stall be encountered during the ma-
noeuvre, releasing sufficient back pressure to eliminate the symptoms and
continuing with the manoeuvre is, normally, all that is required. If the stall
is severe, discontinue the manoeuvre and recover the aircraft to level flight;
and
d.
if another aircraft enters your planned manoeuvring airspace, discontinue the
manoeuvre and take appropriate action to avoid the conflicting traffic.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 2 =====
CHAPTER 8: BASIC INSTRUMENT FLYING
8.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
Although many aircraft fly above the weather, they often have to climb through
cloud after take-off or descend through cloud during an instrument approach before
landing. Pilots of aircraft that do not have a high altitude capability often fly many
hours within extensive areas of cloud and precipitation. This means that you must un-
derstand the fundamentals of instrument flying so that you can reach a high standard of
proficiency in preparation for future flying assignments.
2.
The Harvard II is equipped with an instrument package consisting of electronic
displays and a separate set of standby instruments. Before any instrument trip, a thor-
ough check of all flight instruments and navigation equipment must be carried out.
8.2
BASIC INSTRUMENT FLYING
3.
In clearhood exercises, the attitude of the aircraft is determined by reference to
the natural horizon and is checked occasionally by reference to the instruments. During
instrument flying, the horizon bar of the Electronic Attitude Direction Indicator (EADI)
replaces the natural horizon, and the relationship between the miniature aircraft and the
horizon bar presents a visual picture of the aircraft’s attitude.
4.
The aircraft control instruments are the EADI and the torque gauge. The aircraft
is controlled in exactly the same way during instrument flying as it is during clearhood
flying. This is accomplished by using the EADI as the reference for pitch and bank, and
the torque gauge as the reference for power.
5.
The aircraft performance instruments are the altimeter, the VSI, the ASI, the EHSI
and the turn-and-slip indicator. These instruments tell you what the aircraft is doing,
regardless of whether the aircraft’s attitude is controlled by reference to the natural
horizon, the EADI, or both.
6.
The aircraft navigation instruments show the position of the aircraft in relation to
a navigation facility or user defined waypoint. GPS, VOR and NDB are examples of
navigation instruments. Navigation instruments are discussed in Chapter 9.
102

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 3 =====
Figure 8.1: Control and Performance Instruments
8.3
EADI
7.
The EADI is the instrument used for attitude reference. Proper aircraft control
depends on your ability to hold an accurate constant attitude, to change that attitude
smoothly, and to know when and by how much to change the attitude. Any change in
the aircraft’s attitude is immediately indicated on the EADI by a corresponding change
of the aircraft symbol’s relation to the horizon bar. Small changes of attitude are easily
identified and controlled.
8.
Changing the position of the aircraft symbol in relation to the horizon bar makes
definite pitch attitude changes. Coarse adjustments to known pitch references will be
made using the pitch scale on the EADI (e.g. set 10 to 15° nose up for a normal climb).
Precise adjustments are made by reference to the thickness of the horizon bar and are
referred to as “bar widths”.
9.
The angle indicated between the aircraft symbol and the horizon bar shows the
direction of turn while the position of the bank pointer on the index scale shows the
number of degrees of bank. The bank index scale is graduated at 0, 10, 20, 30, 45 and
60 degrees of bank; therefore, by referring to the EADI, you can establish and maintain
desired amounts of bank.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 4 =====
Figure 8.2: EADI Attitude Indications
8.4
TORQUE
10.
The torque gauge is the control instrument for power and indicates the aircraft’s
current power level. It is used during instrument flight in the same manner as for clear-
hood flight. If a constant airspeed is maintained, the vertical speed resulting from power
changes can be based on the approximation of 50 fpm of vertical speed for each 1%
change in torque. If a constant altitude is maintained, changes in airspeed are based on
the approximation of 2 KIAS for every 1% change in torque.
8.5
PERFORMANCE INSTRUMENTS
11.
Because you must control the attitude and torque to get a desired performance, you
must know when a change is needed. The performance instruments show the results
of the present attitude and torque. When there is an undesirable indication, you must
change the attitude and/or torque.
12.
To know what to change (bank, pitch, or torque), remember that bank attitude
control is used to maintain a heading or a desired turn. Except during manoeuvres for
which there is a fixed torque setting, a combination of torque and attitude is required to
maintain a desired airspeed and altitude.
13.
The amount of change in attitude or torque required to achieve a desired perfor-
mance must be learned through experience. When the attitude must be changed, the
adjustment is made with reference to the EADI; when torque is changed, the adjustment
is made with reference to the torque gauge. After the adjustment, check the performance
instruments to see if the change is correct. If it is not correct, make a further adjustment
of attitude and/or torque.
8.6
CROSS-CHECK
14.
During instrument flying, you must divide your attention between the control, per-
formance, and navigation instruments. Proper division of attention and the sequence for

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 5 =====
checking the instruments varies from pilot to pilot and throughout a trip.
15.
The way in which instruments respond to changes of attitude and/or torque in-
fluences cross-check techniques. Attitude and torque indicators respond immediately
to changes, but the indications of the performance instruments may lag slightly behind
changes of attitude and/or torque. This lag is caused by aircraft inertia and by the oper-
ating principles and mechanisms of the instruments themselves, but it should not affect
the accuracy of your control over the aircraft.
16.
When you are controlling the attitude and torque properly, the indications on the
performance instruments will stabilize or change smoothly with a minimum of lag.
Smooth aircraft control is achieved by making appropriate adjustments using the con-
trol instruments and allowing the performance instruments to stabilize. Refinement of
the performance parameters is accomplished by repeating this process until the desired
performance is achieved. Do not allow any lag in the performance instruments to cause
you to make premature adjustments on the control instruments. Invariably, this leads to
useless “chasing” of instruments and is termed “over-controlling”.
17.
The EADI is probably the only instrument to which you may devote your attention
for any appreciable length of time. You may need several seconds to complete a routine
attitude change, such as entering a turn, and during this time you may have to devote
your attention exclusively to the EADI to ensure precise attitude control. The EADI
is the instrument that is checked the greatest number of times. A normal crosscheck
technique is to glance from the EADI to a performance instrument, back to the EADI,
to another performance instrument, back to the EADI, and so forth.
18.
This example of cross-check technique does not mean that you should not compare
the indications of one performance instrument against another. However, predominant
attention to the EADI is normal and desirable.
19.
The omission of some instruments during the cross-check is a common error. You
may find that you are inclined to omit a performance instrument from your cross-check,
although you are watching other performance instruments and the control instruments
properly. For example, you may become so engrossed with pitch-attitude control to
maintain an altitude, or a specific rate of climb or descent, that you fail to note an error
in heading. Also, the indications on some instruments are not as eye-catching as the
indications on others. A 4 degree heading error is not as noticeable as a 300 to 400 fpm
error in the rate of climb or descent; therefore, you must develop a habit of including all
the instruments in your cross-check.
8.7
BASIC MANOEUVRES
20.
Any instrument trip, no matter how long or complex, is simply a series of con-
nected, basic instrument manoeuvres. Thus, the more accurately you fly the basic ma-
noeuvres, the more accurately and safely you can complete your whole trip.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 6 =====
21.
The failure to regard an instrument trip as a series of basic instrument manoeuvres
leads to erratic aircraft control. The information received from navigation instruments,
a radar controller, etc., should be thought of as advice to perform a basic instrument
manoeuvre. Instrument approaches consist of a series of connected basic instrument
manoeuvres, such as a constant airspeed descent, a descending turn, changing airspeeds,
straight-and-level flight, and so on. By visualizing the next basic manoeuvre, you can
plan ahead and know exactly what cross-check technique and control input should be
used during the manoeuvre.
8.8
TRIM
22.
Proper trim technique is essential for smooth instrument flying. By relieving all
control pressures, it is easier to hold a constant attitude, thus ensuring smooth and pre-
cise control. Control pressures vary with airspeed and use of ancillaries (speed brake,
flaps, and landing gear). A small torque change does not affect the trim appreciably, but
the resulting change in airspeed requires a trim change. Constantly monitor the ball on
the turn and slip and trim out any pressures required to keep it centered. Remember the
Trim Aid Device (TAD) will not completely centre the ball.
23.
To trim the aircraft, apply sufficient control pressure to establish the desired atti-
tude, and then use the trim to relieve all control pressures, no matter how slight. Do
not attempt to change the aircraft’s attitude by means of trimming; this causes erratic
aircraft control.
8.9
STRAIGHT-AND-LEVEL FLIGHT
24.
In straight-and-level, un-accelerated flight, you must maintain a desired altitude,
heading and airspeed.
25.
The EADI is the primary instrument for selecting the attitude for straight-and-level
flight. The altimeter shows whether the attitude is the correct one. If it is correct, the
altitude should remain constant. If the aircraft loses altitude, raise the nose to increase
the pitch attitude; if the aircraft gains altitude, lower the nose to decrease the pitch
attitude. The magnitude of the pitch correction will depend on the rate and amount of
altitude deviation.
26.
Good judgment is required when you are deciding on the rate of correction re-
quired to reach a desired altitude. Do not make the corrections so rapidly that you
overshoot the desired altitude; likewise, do not prolong a correction unnecessarily. As
a general rule, the pitch attitude change should be such that the vertical speed in fpm is
approximately double the error in feet of altitude. For example, if there is a 100’ error
in altitude, make a correction of approximately 200 fpm. As a general rule, a 1 degree
change in pitch attitude equates to approximately 500 fpm of vertical speed.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 7 =====
27.
When there is a small attitude error, the altimeter often registers a slight change of
altitude before the VSI has had time to move. If you make a small pitch correction with
reference to the EADI, the altitude deviation may be corrected before the VSI has had
time to show an error.
28.
Do not over-control the pitch attitude because of oscillations of VSI indications
in rough air. You must always make sufficient reference to the EADI to ensure good
attitude control.
29.
To maintain heading, keep the wings level by referring to the EADI and cross-
check the heading and turn indicators. Setting the heading bug to the desired course on
the EHSI will make it easier to determine if you have strayed from your heading. If
the aircraft turns off heading, establish a specific angle-of-bank on the EADI to return
the aircraft to the desired heading at a controlled rate. A normal rate of correction is
to establish an angle-of-bank on the EADI equal to the number of degrees off heading.
Use a slightly larger bank angle when the aircraft is flying at high true airspeeds, but
keep the bank angle below 30 degrees.
30.
Small heading deviations are not readily noticeable. For this reason, you must
frequently cross-check the heading and turn indicators.
31.
When you are adjusting the torque to establish the airspeed for a particular phase
of flight, knowing the approximate torque setting is of value. As the airspeed approaches
the desired indication, refer to the torque gauge and establish the approximate setting.
A cross-check of the airspeed will indicate the need for subsequent small torque adjust-
ments. Make a point of learning and remembering the approximate torque settings that
are detailed below.

NOTE
The torque settings listed above are approximate and will vary slightly
according to temperature, weight, and the characteristics of the individ-
ual aircraft.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 8 =====
32.
When there is an error in airspeed in level flight, delay the torque adjustment until
you have checked the altimeter and VSI to see whether there is a need for a pitch change.
For example, if the aircraft is descending and the airspeed is slightly high, both can be
corrected by a pitch change.
33.
Straight and level airspeed changes are performed using the same procedures as in
the clearhood phase (Section 3.18); however, the attitude reference is the EADI instead
of the horizon. Due to the large effect of torque on the aircraft, the preferred method of
losing 20 KIAS or greater is to set desired torque and extending the speed brake instead
of setting the PCL to IDLE. This allows for a more controlled airspeed reduction as well
as having a lower probability of contributing to any disorientation.
NOTE
Aircraft performance may differ slightly depending on the aircraft weight,
temperature, altitude, etc.
Students are expected to use the rules of
thumb as a baseline, analyze, and apply appropriate corrections to reach
the required parameters.
8.10
TURNS
34.
Instrument turns are normally made at 30 degrees Angle-Of-Bank (AOB). How-
ever, as you gain experience, you will practice instrument turns at specific rates and
various bank angles.
35.
To enter a constant AOB turn, establish and maintain the desired bank angle by
referring to the bank scale of the EADI. Use light control pressure when making adjust-
ments to the bank and, throughout the turn, devote sufficient attention to the EADI to
keep the bank angle constant. Anytime you are turning to an assigned heading, use the
heading bug on the EHSI to identify the desired heading. Setting the heading bug can
be accomplished upon issuance of the desired heading just prior to rolling into the turn,
or it may be accomplished immediately after rolling out of the turn.
36.
With bank angles of 30 degrees or more, a change in pitch attitude is required to
compensate for the loss of vertical lift. You must anticipate the correction and change
the pitch as required to maintain level flight. As you raise the nose to maintain altitude,
increase the torque to compensate for the increased drag in order to maintain airspeed;
the amount of back pressure and torque required depend on the bank angle. To return to
straight-and-level flight, apply opposite control pressures to level the wings, and adjust
the pitch attitude and torque. Cross-check the altimeter and VSI. As the bank angle
decreases and the vertical lift changes, you will have to make a conscious effort to
release the back pressure and lower the nose to prevent the aircraft from climbing. The
bank should be applied at a constant rate and, ideally, the roll-in and rollout should be
at the same rate. Do not trim the aircraft during constant speed turns.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 9 =====
37.
In coordinated flight, an aircraft continues to turn as long as the wings are banked;
therefore, to recover on a specific heading, you must start the rollout before the aircraft
reaches the desired heading. The amount of lead varies with the AOB and the rate of
rollout.
38.
Any instrument turns with a bank angle greater than 45 degrees is considered to be
a steep turn. Although steep turns are seldom required in routine instrument flying, they
increase your ability to fly accurately on instruments. Flying techniques during a steep
turn are the same as for a normal turn, except that you must pay more attention to the
pitch attitude throughout the turn because of the larger changes necessary to maintain
altitude and airspeed. During the rollout, three actions must be performed simultane-
ously: you must decrease the bank, release the back pressure, and reduce torque. You
must use a rapid cross-check to roll the aircraft out smoothly at a constant altitude.
39.
Changing airspeed in turns is taught to improve your cross-check technique and to
foster accuracy of control. There are two methods of changing airspeeds in turns: one is
to make the change after the turn has been established and the other is to alter the torque
setting while the aircraft is being rolled into the turn. In either method, the procedure
for decreasing or increasing airspeed is the same as that listed in Section 3.18.
40.
Changing airspeed in a constant bank turn requires only an adjustment of pitch
attitude to maintain altitude. However, in a constant rate turn the procedure is more
difficult. Because the rate of turn depends on the angle-of-bank/airspeed relationship,
each time the airspeed is changed, you must adjust the bank angle if the rate of turn is
to remain constant. In order to hold a constant altitude during these airspeed changes,
you must vary the pitch attitude.
8.11
CLIMBS AND DESCENTS
41.
Climbing and descending manoeuvres are of two general types. One is a constant-
airspeed climb or descent in which a fixed torque setting is established. The pitch at-
titude is controlled to maintain the airspeed and whatever the vertical speed results is
accepted. The other type is a constant-rate climb or descent in which the torque and
attitude are adjusted to produce the required airspeed and vertical speed rate. Once
established, only minor adjustments of torque and attitude are required to maintain or
regain the desired rate and airspeed.
42.
Normally a climb is made at a reduced airspeed. On the entry to a climb, establish
the pitch attitude first on the EADI and, as the airspeed decreases to within 5 KIAS of
the desired speed, apply climbing torque and then re-trim. From information supplied
by the performance instruments, make minor adjustments on the EADI to maintain the
required airspeed. If the climb is to be made at cruising airspeed, adjust the torque and
attitude simultaneously and then re-trim if required.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 10 =====
43.
The level off from a climb is normally made at a higher airspeed than that of
the climb. When the aircraft approaches the desired altitude, adjust the pitch attitude
gradually to maintain the altitude. For leveling off, use a lead, in feet of altitude, equal
to 10 per cent of the vertical speed. Maintain climb torque until the airspeed is within 5
KIAS of the required speed, reduce to the required torque and then re-trim. Make minor
adjustments on the EADI and torque gauge. Should the level off be at the same or lower
airspeed than the climb, adjust the torque either simultaneously or before the aircraft
reaches the desired altitude, so that the aircraft is at the required airspeed as soon as
possible.
44.
A descent can be made at other than cruising speed. When descent is made at an
increased airspeed, lower the nose, maintain an approximate attitude on the EADI and,
as the aircraft approaches the desired airspeed, reduce torque to the required setting and
then re-trim. In a descent made at the same airspeed as the cruise, make these adjust-
ments simultaneously. In a descent made at a lower airspeed, first reduce the torque
and maintain altitude until the aircraft reaches the desired airspeed. As the airspeed
approaches within 5 KIAS of the desired speed, lower the nose and start the descent,
making minor adjustments on the EADI to maintain speed and re-trim.
45.
Descents can be made at any airspeed and configuration but there are two primary
IFR descent profiles used in the Harvard. The first is the Enroute Descent profile. Con-
figuration for this descent is 220 KIAS and 5 degrees nose down. This will require
approximately 30% torque, will generate a descent rate of approximately 2000 fpm,
and will result in approximately 1000’ of altitude loss every 2 NM (one to two). This
is the recommended descent profile during normal IFR operations. The second is the
Penetration Descent profile. Configuration for this descent is 220 KIAS and 10 degrees
nose down. This will require approximately 4–6% torque, will generate a descent rate
of approximately 4000 fpm and will result in approximately 1000’ of altitude loss ev-
ery 1 NM (one to one). This is the recommended descent profile during a high altitude
penetration approach, to minimize fuel consumption, or any other time when a faster
descent profile is required.
46.
The level off is a combination of pitch and torque adjustments so that the aircraft
reaches the desired airspeed as soon as possible after attaining the correct altitude.
47.
Constant-rate climbs and descents vary from constant-airspeed climbs and de-
scents in that, after the approximate rate of climb or descent has been established, small
adjustments to rate and airspeed are made by changes to torque and attitude. Usually,
neither is adjusted without a simultaneous adjustment to the other.
48.
The constant-rate climb entry is similar to that of a constant-airspeed climb, ex-
cept that the attitude varies according to the desired rate of climb. Once the aircraft is
established in the climb, include the VSI in the cross-check. Maintain a constant pitch
attitude on the EADI until the performance instruments stabilize; then check the VSI

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 11 =====
and ASI to ensure that you have achieved the desired rate and airspeed. If a correc-
tion is required, make minor adjustments on the control instruments and cross-check for
accuracy.
49.
The descent entry is similar to that described in Section 3.20, and once established,
the procedure is identical to that for the rated climb. Occasionally you may have to
establish the rate of descent before reducing the airspeed. Reduce the torque to well
below that required and establish and maintain the rate of descent by cross-checking the
VSI and the EADl. When the airspeed falls to the desired speed, re-adjust the torque.
50.
The technique used for climbing and descending turns is a combination of that for
climbs, descents, and constant-bank turns. You may start the climb or descent before the
turn, at the same time as the turn, or after the aircraft has entered the turn. Similarly, you
may complete the level off any time during a turn, except that the turn should always be
made onto a specific heading and the level off should be completed at a predetermined
altitude.
51.
After all the basic exercises are completed, twizzles will be introduced. A twiz-
zle is simply a combination of climbs, descents, and turns that are reversed at given
parameters. This exercise is to help improve the basic crosscheck. There are differ-
ent techniques to execute a twizzle. For example, initial parameters can be given as a
start and be changed while performing the exercise. Another technique will provide all
parameters before executing the twizzle, therefore the aircraft will remain in a certain
box.
8.12
INSTRUMENT TAKE-OFF
52.
To ensure a positive rate of climb as the aircraft becomes airborne, hold a pitch
attitude on take-off of approximately 5–7 degrees nose-up on the EADI and ensure that
you have a positive rate of climb by cross-checking the altimeter and VSI. Maintain a
wings-level attitude on the EADI, adjusting the bank slightly, as required, to maintain
heading. Raise the landing gear when the aircraft is safely airborne and raise the flaps
at 110 KIAS. While the airspeed is increasing to climbing speed, maintain the pitch
attitude and heading by referring predominantly to the EADI. Cross-check the VSI and
altimeter to prevent the aircraft from leveling off or entering a descent. As the airspeed
approaches climb speed, re-adjust the pitch attitude as required (approximately 10–15
degrees nose-up depending on speed) to maintain the climb speed, and then re-trim
while cross-checking the heading indicator. If required, and only when at a safe altitude,
commence a gentle turn to the departure heading/track.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 12 =====
8.13
GO AROUND
53.
A go around on instruments combines the techniques of leveling off from a descent
at a constant airspeed with those used for starting a normal climb.
54.
When carrying out a go around, set PCL to MAX. As the torque increases, adjust
the attitude on the EADI to approximately 5–7 degrees nose-up and re-trim. Cross-
check the VSI to ensure that the aircraft is climbing. Raise the landing gear and above
110 KIAS, raise the flaps. When the airspeed approaches 180 KIAS, adjust the attitude
to maintain 180 KIAS. If remaining in the radar square for another approach, adjust the
torque and attitude to maintain 180 KIAS and a minimum of 1000 fpm rate-of-climb
(approximately 65% torque). Climb to the published missed-approach altitude or the
altitude specified in your clearance. Level off, adjusting your torque to approximately
45%, and maintain 180 KIAS.
8.14
UNUSUAL ATTITUDES
55.
During instrument flying, deviations from desired attitude may occur and correc-
tive action must be taken immediately. In most instances, a simple attitude adjustment
will be the only corrective action required. However, if an unusual flight attitude is
encountered, corrective actions are the same as those used during clearhood unusual at-
titude recoveries. The EADI now becomes the horizon reference and the performance
instruments play a more critical role.
56.
The most probable cause of an unusual attitude during instrument flight is due
to the pilot’s focus of attention being diverted away from their instrument cross-check.
Items which can divert a pilot’s attention range from writing down a clearance to dealing
with a checklist response item. Proper use of trim will allow for short deviations from
the instrument cross-check; however, pilots must always be vigilant and ensure they do
not allow their attention to stray from the instruments for too long.
57.
Initially, performance instruments are used to identify an unusual attitude and to
confirm if the EADI is functioning properly. If it is determined that the EADI is op-
erating normally, it then becomes the primary reference during the recovery. If it is
determined that the EADI is not functioning properly, or if the EADI has a FAIL indi-
cation, the recovery will be flown using the standby instruments.
58.
Analysis of the flight attitude should simply use airspeed indication and/or ten-
dency, altimeter indications, VSI indications, turn indication, and G forces as needed to
confirm the condition of flight. Once the aircraft attitude is positively identified from
any combination of these indications, there is no need to delay recovery while checking
all the other indications. As for any unusual attitude recovery, the controls must be cen-
tralized until the attitude is confirmed. If, after checking all available flight indications,
you are still unable to determine your attitude of flight, you must maintain the controls

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 13 =====
centralized and closely monitor the altimeter until the attitude is recognized. If you
have not recovered from the unrecognized condition of flight by 6000’ AGL, EJECT!
The rules for recovering from an unusual attitude under instrument conditions are:
a.
centralize controls and analyze your flight attitude;
b.
carry out appropriate recovery actions for your condition of flight;
c.
attempt to determine the cause of the unusual attitude and take appropriate
corrective action. Transfer to the standby instruments if required; and
d.
advise ATC if the altitude/heading deviation warrants.
8.15
PRIMARY INSTRUMENT RECOVERY
59.
Nose-Low Unusual Attitude. Decreasing altitude and increasing airspeed char-
acterize the performance instrument indications of a nose-low unusual attitude. EADI
indications would be primarily black background with possible bank indications and red
recovery chevrons. Once it is confirmed that the aircraft is in a nose low attitude, set
PCL to IDLE and simultaneously level the wings on the EADI. If required, extend the
speed brake to prevent excess airspeed and/or loss of altitude. Apply sufficient back
pressure to return to level flight indications using the EADI.
60.
Nose-High Unusual Attitude. Increasing altitude and decreasing airspeed char-
acterize the performance instrument indications of a nose-high unusual attitude. EADI
indications would be primarily blue background with possible bank indications and red
recovery chevrons. Once it is confirmed that the aircraft is in a nose high attitude, apply
maximum continuous power to prevent a further decrease in speed. Roll the aircraft
towards the nearest horizon to a maximum of 90 degrees of bank on the EADI while
maintaining slight positive G throughout the recovery. As the aircraft symbol crosses
the horizon bar and the airspeed is increasing and flyable, roll the aircraft to a normal
level flight attitude.
8.16
STANDBY INTRUMENT RECOVERY
61.
Nose-Low Unusual Attitude. Decreasing altitude and increasing airspeed char-
acterize the performance instrument indications of a nose-low unusual attitude. If you
determine that the EADI is malfunctioning, transition to standby instruments, set PCL
to IDLE and simultaneously level the wings on the standby A/I cross referencing the
turn indicator and ball. If required, extend the speed brake to prevent excessive airspeed
and loss of altitude. Apply sufficient back pressure to return to level flight. When the
airspeed stops increasing and the altimeter no longer indicates a descent, the aircraft is
either at, or passing through, the level-flight attitude. Cross-check the standby A/I with
the other standby instruments and confirm its status, cage it if required.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 14 =====
62.
Nose-High Unusual Attitude. Increasing altitude and decreasing airspeed char-
acterize the performance instrument indications of a nose-high unusual attitude. If you
determine that the EADI is malfunctioning, transition to standby instruments and apply
maximum continuous power to prevent a further decrease in speed. Roll the aircraft to-
wards the nearest horizon to a maximum of 90 degrees of bank on the standby A/I while
maintaining slight positive G throughout the recovery. As the aircraft symbol crosses
the horizon bar and the airspeed is increasing and flyable, roll the aircraft to a normal
level flight attitude. Level the aircraft cross referencing the turn and slip and the altime-
ter. As with the nose low recovery, cross-check the standby A/I and confirm its status,
cage it if required.
NOTE
If the rate of roll is too fast, or the amount of back pressure is not suf-
ficient, the turn indicator will give erroneous indications. If allowed to
continue, the aircraft can roll to the inverted position before the turn
needle indicates wings level. An indication that you are inverted is the
turn indicator moving through the centre index in the opposite direction
to the roll. Should you get into this situation, continue to roll in the same
direction until the turn indicator reaches the opposite side of the dial and
returns to the centre of the index. Stop the roll at this point, as the wings
will be relatively level, and continue with the remainder of the recovery.
WARNING
Under extreme circumstances it may be necessary to reduce the PCL to
IDLE to avoid spinning the aircraft.
8.17
POST RECOVERY ACTIONS
63.
Carry out the same post recovery actions as for the clearhood flight procedures
including the need to advise ATC of a change of altitude (if different than clearance).
When analyzing the situation however, one must now determine the contributing factors
which led to the unusual attitude and determine the impact on the remainder of the
flight. For example, the loss of the EADI could require a substantial difference in how
to recover the aircraft to the landing field.
8.18
STANDBY INSTRUMENT FLIGHT
64.
Although instances of instrument failure are few, it is essential to know how to
control an aircraft when some of the flight instruments are unserviceable. If the EADI

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 15 =====
fails, transfer to the standby A/I. If the primary altimeter, ASI, or turn and slip fail trans-
fer your cross-check to the appropriate standby instrument. The standby instruments
are much smaller than their primary displays; however, their functions are similar to
their primary counterparts. If the standby A/I is determined to be unreliable as well, a
combination of performance instruments must replace the EADI and standby A/I (par-
tial panel). The turn-and-slip indicator and the EHSI replace the bank reference, and
a combination of the VSI and altimeter replace the pitch reference normally obtained
from the EADI. To a lesser extent, the ASI aids in determining your pitch attitude, but
airspeed changes are slower than VSI and altimeter changes. When level flight has been
approximated on the performance instruments, re-cage the standby A/I and determine if
is functioning properly. If so, continue with the standby A/I as your primary source of
attitude information, but continually monitor the performance instruments for any signs
of standby A/I degradation.
65.
In the event that both the EADI and the standby A/I are unreliable, flight must
be completed using the performance instruments as the primary instruments. In this
case, the turn and slip indicator becomes the primary means of keeping wings level and
maintaining heading. Altimeter and VSI become the primary references for maintain-
ing altitude or controlling climb and descent rates. If flying with primary reference to
performance instruments, trim is of vital importance for keeping aircraft control.
66.
If the heading indicator fails, you can maintain heading by keeping the “needle and
ball” centered. The aircraft heading can be checked by referring to the standby compass,
and heading changes can be approximated by making rated turns with reference to the
clock.
8.19
INSTRUMENT RECOVERY FROM A SPIN
67.
If you should inadvertently enter a spin from an unusual attitude while flying in
cloud, you should consider ejection rather than recovery. The decision depends on air-
craft altitude and the cloud base.
68.
The instrument indications of a spin are detailed in Section 6.6 (Symptoms and
Recognition of a Spin). In IMC, your instrument indications will be essential for rec-
ognizing and recovering from a spin. Once it is confirmed that the aircraft is in a spin
and you decide to recover rather than eject, use the spin recovery procedure outlined in
Section 6.7.
69.
The turn indicator may be slow to move from full deflection during the recovery,
particularly if you inadvertently apply positive G. Watch for this as the aircraft may go
into a spiral dive in the opposite direction. Monitor your altitude throughout and apply
the minimum altitude criteria for ejection.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 16 =====
CHAPTER 9: APPLIED INSTRUMENT FLYING
9.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
Applied instrument flying, as the name implies, is the practical application of the
basic instrument flying techniques you have learned. These procedures can be as simple
as flying straight and level while enroute or as complicated as performing a rated descent
while tracking an ILS on course to decision height at a specific altitude. All procedures
are covered in the various publications with most being detailed in the CFP 148, Man-
ual of Instrument Flying, A-0A-148-001/AG-001. Certain elaboration is required with
respect to the Harvard II.
9.2
THE FLYING TRAINING DEVICE
2.
At this stage of the basic flying course, you should be familiar with the attributes
and limitations of the Flying Training Device (FTD). Although there are some limita-
tions to the simulation of actual flight, you can effectively practice all of the instrument
exercises and develop a sound IFR cross-check. For maximum benefit, study the theory
of each exercise before attempting it in the FTD. Each topic is introduced in ground
school and is followed up, first in the FTD and later in the air. Conscientious study and
continual practice are two requirements for skillful instrument flying.
9.3
SYSTEMS MANAGEMENT
3.
Applied instrument flying is simply a series of basic IF sequences linked together
and sometimes performed simultaneously. The accomplished instrument pilot will have
mastered the basic skills and, based on their appreciation of the task to be performed,
will combine several basic manoeuvres in a well thought out sequence to successfully
complete the task. Whether on departure, enroute, arrival, or an approach, you should
have formulated a plan of action based on the available navigation aids and your expe-
rience level. The avionics package in the Harvard II is exceptional for a training aircraft
and requires a thorough understanding of the avionics systems and a well-developed air
picture. A gradual introduction of the available navigation aids will enable you to mas-
ter each taught sequence prior to attempting a subsequent and oftentimes more difficult
sequence. A standardized method of systems management will be introduced through-
116

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 17 =====
out the instrument phase to assist you in developing your air picture and prevent task
saturation.
4.
As a minimum, a suggested systems management set up would be:
a.
active navigation aids properly tuned and identified on the RMU;
b.
bearing pointers set on the EHSI as required. As a guide, the single needle
will generally be used for the active ground based navigation aid (VOR 1,
VOR 2) while the double needle, if used, is reserved for GPS;
c.
EFIS control head NAV source selector button used to set primary nav source
and if required, the desired course with the course select knob; and
d.
EHSI display format set as needed (HSI, MAP, or ARC).
5.
The GPS is a powerful tool when flying IFR. Knowledge of this system will be
gained through exposure and practice. It may be combined with any other approach aid
in any phase of flight to improve your air picture. It is especially useful as an “overlay”
when flying VOR or NDB procedures where distance, time to go, ground speed, and
track information is unavailable.
6.
The EHSI display format can also be used to enhance air picture through MAP and
ARC modes of operation.
9.4
IFR NAVIGATION
7.
The majority of your instrument cross-country flying will be done under IFR con-
trol; therefore, you must have a thorough knowledge of associated regulations. Trans-
port Canada (TC) is responsible for the control of all air traffic within the designated
airspace for airways and for civilian terminals, such as Toronto, Montreal, and Win-
nipeg. There are also military control areas that are under the jurisdiction of and regu-
lated by the Canadian Forces. Examples of such areas are Moose Jaw, Bagotville and
Cold Lake.
8.
Pre-Flight. The CFP 148 should be consulted in conjunction with route prepa-
ration, departure, enroute, and arrival planning. You will complete several exercises in
ground school to familiarize you with the many aspects of pre-flight planning.
9.
Fuel Management. Clearance routing may necessitate a departure from your
flight-planned route, which may affect your fuel figures. The ability to up-date a log
through the use of mental calculations and foresight is one of the requirements of a
competent cross-country pilot. Additionally, arrivals at busy terminals may include
a clearance for an approach procedure other than that planned. Normally, fuel con-
sumption is not affected to any great extent; however, fuel should be considered when
planning your descent.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 18 =====
10.
Flight Plan/Final Check. File an ICAO Flight Plan, or civilian equivalent, at
least 30 minutes before your estimated time of departure. Finally, check NOTAMs and
