the weather, and ensure that you are aware of the local departure procedures; many
aerodromes have published Standard Instrument Departures (SID). Copies of departure
procedures may be obtained from the Flight Planning Centre or may be included in GPH
200. You should have already studied applicable Standard Terminal Arrival Routings
(STAR) and approach charts for your destination and alternate.
9.5
DEPARTURE
11.
Under most circumstances, you must read back ATC clearances. Initially your
instructor will simulate clearances while operating VFR in the MTCA. As you gain
experience you will be expected to respond to all ATC clearances or instructions. If you
are not certain about any detail, query the controller before going airborne. Do not
hesitate to inform the controller that you cannot safely comply with the instructions. On
departure, be prepared to comply with noise abatement procedures. At Moose Jaw there
are several published local Standard Instrument Departures (SID) to ensure the orderly
flow of IFR traffic both to the local training area and enroute IFR. Each departure will
have specific systems management requirements to complete the departure safely and
comply with all ATC instructions.
12.
The acceptable departure airspeeds for the Harvard II are either 140 KIAS or 180
KIAS (approximately 10–15 degrees nose-up). For all departures remaining within the
IFR approach pattern (radar square) or the local flying area (MTCA), 180 KIAS will be
the preferred climbing profile as it provides the best forward visibility. For all depar-
tures to an enroute phase of flight, either climbing profile is acceptable. The climbing
profile selection shall be based on operational considerations: altitude to climb, fuel
burn, obstacles on departure, weather, etc.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 19 =====
9.6
ARRIVAL
13.
Many airfields in Canada are served by terminal radar facilities that expedite depar-
tures, enroute traffic, and arrivals within the terminal area. Radar vectors from ATC may
be used to position the aircraft on final approach for the runway-in-use. This eliminates
the need to carry out the full published approach procedure (provided you have been
cleared for a straight-in type approach). The final approach can be completed visually
or by using ILS, VOR/DME, VOR, GPS, or PAR.
NOTE
ATC may not have altitude-finding capability; therefore, the traffic re-
ported by ATC may be at any altitude (including yours). Should a radar
failure occur, normal procedural separation is established by ATC as
quickly as possible.
14.
Arrival procedures can be quite involved, requiring considerable anticipation of
approach requirements. Proper systems management to set up the cockpit will help ease
the transition into the approach phase. Normally, you will conduct an enroute descent
to an instrument approach.
15.
Plan your descent point using the Enroute Descent or Penetration Descent profile
as described in Chapter 8. Advise the appropriate ATC agency a few minutes prior to the
intended descent point. Many factors should be considered prior to initiating a descent
such as fuel, winds, turbulence, icing, destination weather, and approach planning, to
name a few.
16.
When planning your descent, remember to allow sufficient time for hand-over and
approach. Some considerations include:
a.
Where is the approach fix in relation to you?
b.
How will wind affect your descent?
c.
Is fuel a factor?
d.
Is this merely a step-down descent for an approach from the high structure?
17.
As you are descending, occasionally compare your altitude with the distance to go.
Sometimes during descent it may be necessary to modify the descent profile in order to
comply with ATC clearances. In all cases, ensure that you are level at the appropriate
altitude in sufficient time to execute the approach.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 20 =====
9.7
COURSE TRACKING
18.
When flying on a predetermined course, the objective is to fly a straight path to or
from the station with a drift correction that allows for wind. This technique is termed
tracking and the procedures used for tracking are the same for VOR, LOC, or GPS.
19.
In general, tracking on the EHSI in the Harvard II can be accomplished in one of
two ways:
a.
Firstly, the desired course can be selected on the Course Deviation Indicator
(CDI) with the aircraft then turned as required to maintain the CDI bar in
the center of the display. This is a simple yet very effective technique for
tracking as it gives immediate and easily deciphered information on your
position relative to the desired track.
b.
The second method is to use one of the system bearing pointers. Depending
on whether you are tracking towards or away from the station, set the head
or the tail of the needle on the desired track. If the needle moves away from
the desired track, turn the aircraft to correct to it. This is accomplished by
turning to place the head of the needle above the desired course if tracking
towards a station or placing the tail of the needle below the desired course if
tracking away from a station.
9.8
COURSE INTERCEPTION
20.
When flying IFR, you will periodically be required to intercept a predetermined
course and then track it towards or away from the station. This is done for a variety of
reasons ranging from intercepting a desired course away from a station after a departure
to intercepting an approach course into your arrival airport.
21.
There are several methods that can be utilized to position the aircraft on the de-
sired course. In order to remain predictable when flying IFR, and also to standardize
the interception process to facilitate judgment of intercept rates to desired tracks, the
following procedures will be used when intercepting radials in the Harvard II.
9.9
INBOUND INTERCEPTION
22.
Once given an intercept clearance, ensure you have the correct navigation aid set
up on your system bearing pointer. Find the desired track on the EHSI (remember that
if tracking a radial inbound, your desired track is the reciprocal of the radial) and move
from the desired track to the head of the needle. In order to establish an intercept, move
30 degrees beyond the heading pointer and use this as your intercept angle (intercept
angle not to exceed 90 degrees). Set the heading bug on this desired heading and turn
the aircraft to it via the shortest route. Set the CDI to the desired track, ensuring that it
is reading information from the proper nav source. As you approach the desired track

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 21 =====
Figure 9.1: Example: Tail (195°) to the desired radial (240°) + 45° = new heading 285°
Figure 9.2: Example: Desired track (060°) to the head of the needle (015°) + 30° = 345°
turn the aircraft in order to rollout on it, then track the desired course inbound using the
procedure described in Section 9.7.
9.10
OUTBOUND INTERCEPTION
23.
Once given an intercept clearance, ensure you have the correct navigation aid set
up on your system bearing pointer. Direct your attention to the tail of the needle, move

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 22 =====
around the compass rose to the desired track, then move a further 45 degrees around the
compass rose to determine your intercept heading. Set the heading bug on this desired
heading and turn the aircraft to it via the shortest route. Set the CDI to the desired track,
ensuring that it is reading information from the proper nav source. As you approach
the desired track turn the aircraft in order to rollout on it, then track the desired course
outbound using the procedure described in Section 9.7.
9.11
ARCING
24.
An arc, by definition, is a circumference around a designated reference point. It is
commonly used to transition an aircraft from an inbound or outbound radial to a radial
desired for an approach or departure and is flown by maintaining a constant distance
from the designated reference point.
Figure 9.3: Arcing left with no wind
25.
An arc is normally flown by using a series of short, straight legs which maintain
the aircraft within ½ NM from the desired DME. Arcing can be accomplished with
reference to either the head or tail of the needle. The following method details the
use of the head. Use of the tail requires simply the reverse of the head reference. To
maintain the arc, a series of heading changes will be required in order to maintain within
the desired DME. Under no winds, this is normally accomplished by placing the head of
the needle 5–10 degrees above the wing tip index and flying the corresponding heading
until the DME increases to within ½ NM above the desired DME. At this point, a turn
is performed to repeat the procedure. This pattern will have to be adjusted as required
to compensate for winds aloft.
26.
If you allow the aircraft to stray from the arc, corrections are accomplished in
the following manner. If inside the arc, place the head of the needle approximately 20
degrees below the wing tip index and fly back out to regain the arc. If outside the arc,

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 23 =====
Figure 9.4: Arcing left with wind from inside the arc (Left); and outside the arc (Right)
Figure 9.5: Correction of error inside the arc (Left); and outside of the arc (Right)
put the head of the needle approximately 30 degrees above the wing tip index and fly
back in to regain the arc. Remember that these do not take wind into account and may
need to be adjusted to compensate for strong wind conditions.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 24 =====
9.12
INTERCEPTING AN ARC FROM A RADIAL
27.
When planning to intercept an arc from a radial, one must first determine the di-
rection of the arc and what direction to turn in order to intercept it. The direction of the
arc is stated with reference to the point around which it is flown. For example, an arc to
the right is flown clockwise around the reference point. As a result, you will have to turn
in different directions to intercept the arc depending on whether you are intercepting it
inbound or outbound.
28.
When intercepting an arc from a radial, plan to commence a rate ½ turn at a dis-
tance equal to 1% of your true airspeed corrected for winds from the arc. This is calcu-
lated by taking the number of thousand feet MSL you are currently flying at, multiplying
it by 4, and adding this to your IAS. For example, if you are at 8,000’ MSL at 180 KIAS,
your TAS would be 212 KIAS (4 x 8 = 32 + 180 = 212). In this scenario, you would
commence a rate ½ turn to intercept the arc 2.1 miles from the arc DME in calm winds.
This distance will require correction for winds, with the amount of correction varying
with wind direction and velocity. This turn will place the aircraft on the arc upon com-
pletion of the turn.
29.
When flying the Harvard II, the lead distance can be easily determined by using
the groundspeed readout on the GPS. The groundspeed indication from the VOR on the
EHSI can also be used in this case because the aircraft is tracking on a radial. This speed
is an actual indication of the aircraft’s speed corrected for wind therefore using 1% of
the groundspeed will be very accurate.
9.13
INTERCEPTING A RADIAL FROM AN ARC
30.
When you are planning to intercept a radial from an arc, you must determine the
direction to turn in order to intercept the desired radial inbound or outbound. In order to
avoid over or undershooting the desired radial, a lead point will be calculated.
31.
When intercepting a radial from an arc, the same lead distance will be required as
for intercepting an arc from a radial. In order to determine distance, however, the DME
readout cannot be used. Distance is instead calculated using the number of radials per
NM at your current DME multiplied by the lead distance required for the turn. This
is accomplished by dividing your current DME into 60, and multiplying this with the
distance derived using the same formula as stated in 9.12. For example, if arcing at
15 miles at the same altitude and airspeed as stated in 9.12, commence a rate ½ turn 8
radials prior to the desired radial (60 / 15 = 4 x 2.1 (TAS) = 8.2). Again, the groundspeed
readout from the GPS can be used to replace the TAS calculation.
9.14
POINT TO POINT
32.
The theory and techniques on how to calculate and fly point to points are detailed
in Annex A of the CFP 148. In the Harvard II, point to points can be accomplished in

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 25 =====
several manners. Initially you will be taught to perform them using only the EHSI basic
navigation data page. This method is ideal for initial training as it requires situational
awareness of the position of the navigation aid used, the desired point, and your current
position. The knowledge you will gain performing point to points in this manner will
improve your situational awareness for the remainder of your flying career.
33.
After having learned the basic techniques to perform a point to point, you will
learn other techniques to help you improve your accuracy. For example, using the Map
page in VOR mode is a very effective technique for refining your parallel checks. This
technique provides an overhead perspective with your aircraft now placed in the centre
of your EHSI. By increasing the range until the VOR site is positioned on the display,
the course bar display can be changed to indicate the radial upon which your desired
point is situated. Use the range information provided to determine where your desired
point lies on the course bar display. This provides very accurate information for your
parallel checks used to determine the desired heading.
34.
The most accurate technique uses the GPS to build a track to take you from your
present position directly to the desired point. This technique will be shown to you during
your training. Although this technique is very effective, it must be stressed that setting
up the GPS will take your attention away from your primary instrument cross-check. If
aircraft control cannot be accurately maintained, do not use this method.
Figure 9.6: Sample Point to Point

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 26 =====
Figure 9.7: HSI Scaling
9.15
HOLDING
35.
Holding is used primarily as a delaying technique. Generally, in a radar environ-
ment, aircraft sequencing is accomplished by ATC through the use of assigned vectors
and airspeed control; however, situations may arise when holding is required, such as:
a.
non-radar environment where procedural control is in effect;
b.
during times of traffic congestion; or
c.
for practice purposes.
36.
There are strict rules that must be followed both on entry and during the hold.
Holding procedures are found in the GPH 204 and the CFP 148. Maximum hold entry
speeds shall be in according with the GPH 204; the holding speed for the Harvard II
during training is 150 KIAS. If required, the white diamond on the AOA gauge can be
flown in order to maximize endurance.
37.
Fuel Considerations. Prior to accepting a holding clearance it is important to
ensure that you have adequate fuel to maintain the hold and land with required fuel
reserves. Given the nature of the Harvard II’s endurance, under normal circumstances
a detailed fuel calculation prior to accepting a hold clearance during local training is
not required provided you maintain good situational awareness throughout the mission.
However, good airmanship would dictate that you calculate your fuel status enroute to
the hold.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 27 =====
Figure 9.8: Visualization of Standard and Non-Standard holds on EHSI
Figure 9.9: Hold Entry Standard (clockwise/right turns) and Non-Standard (to use these
images they must be rotated to match your visualized hold then the correct entry se-
lected)
9.16
APPROACH TRANSITIONS
38.
When setting up for an approach, there are several options on how to get from
where you are to the IAF for an approach. This is referred to as the approach transition
and applies from enroute flight, from the local flying area, or even on departure if an
emergency scenario requires that you return to the departure field. In all cases, you
must transition to the approach phase of flight.
39.
There are several types of approach transitions: vectored, point to point, proce-
dure turn, and RNAV transition. Simply stated, you can be guided to your IAF by your
ATC controller or through self-navigation. There are no hard and fast rules governing
airspeed or descent rates on transition. You will be responsible, however, to reach your

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 28 =====
IAF on altitude and at your airspeed in order to properly commence your approach pro-
cedure. In order to accomplish this, update your information throughout the transition
assessing how much distance you have left to cover and how much altitude you have to
lose.
9.17
PROCEDURE TURN
40.
A procedure turn is a method of reversing course on a low altitude instrument ap-
proach and is used to keep the approach procedure close to the aerodrome of intended
landing. Procedure turns are often flown at uncontrolled aerodromes where ATC is un-
able to provide radar vectors. A detailed description of low altitude procedure turns
can be found in the Manual of Instrument Flying. Airspeed during the procedure turns
(recommended 150 KIAS for the Harvard II) should be in compliance with existing in-
strument regulations. An approach that includes a procedure turn often has no DME
information, and distance-to-go is based on timing alone. Therefore, appropriate plan-
ning must be done to ensure that the aircraft arrives at MDA in time to complete a
normal landing transition if the runway is sighted.
9.18
IMC STABILIZED APPROACH CRITERIA
41.
The stabilized approach has been adopted to help prevent flight crews from trying
to salvage a mishandled approach that could lead to a flight safety hazard, incident, or
accident. To be stabilized on an IFR approach in IMC, all of the following conditions
must be achieved prior to, or upon reaching 1000’ AAE:
a.
in the approach configuration (landing gear down and flaps as required) with
pre-landing checks completed;
b.
indicated airspeed within +10/-5 KIAS from planned approach airspeed (nor-
mally 120 KIAS, or as briefed);
c.
rate of descent not in excess of 1000 fpm inside the FAF unless required by
the published approach descent profile and briefed by the flight crew;
d.
the aircraft within accepted parameters of the lateral and vertical flight path
for the IFR approach flown;
e.
only small changes in heading/pitch necessary to maintain the correct flight
path; and
f.
once visual the aircraft shall be in a position to carry out a standard 3º glide
path approach to the touchdown zone (no “duck under”) and still have suffi-
cient runway to land and stop the aircraft safely based on actual weather and
runway surface conditions.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 29 =====
Failure to meet the above criteria during IMC shall result in the completion of a missed
approach.
42.
Stabilized approach criteria should be employed to the maximum extent possible
during an emergency.
NOTE
Approaches in gusty conditions may be continued where airspeed and
descent fluctuations exceed the stabilized approach criteria if the excur-
sions are brief in duration, and in the judgment of the AC it is safe to
continue. If severe wind shear is encountered the approach shall be con-
sidered destabilized and a go around shall be initiated. Aircraft Com-
manders shall carefully consider stabilized approach requirements be-
fore accepting an ATC request for higher airspeeds in the final approach
segment of the approach. Unique approach conditions or abnormal con-
ditions requiring a deviation from the above elements of a stabilized ap-
proach require a special briefing.
9.19
NON-PRECISION APPROACHES
43.
During your training and your ensuing flying career, you will encounter several
types of non-precision approaches. Normally the aircraft will be positioned prior to the
FAF in order to achieve a stable descent to MDA that respects all intermediate min-
imum altitudes (safe) and achieves MDA with sufficient distance remaining to com-
plete a landing if required (effective). If no FAF exists (e.g. following a procedure
turn) then the aircraft should be configured prior to being established on the final ap-
proach course in order to achieve a stable descent to MDA. During training, strive to
achieve the IMC stabilized approach criteria described above. Possible routings to the
FAF (normally via the IAF) include radar vectors, point to point navigation, procedure
turn, GPS, or penetration descent. Non-precision approaches include VOR (full proce-
dure), VOR/DME, LOC/BCRS, LOC only, and GPS. Regardless of the type of approach
flown, non-precision procedures are similar for the final approach, landing transition,
and missed approach phases.
9.20
INTERMEDIATE AND FINAL APPROACH SEGMENTS
44.
Strive to cross the IAF below 147 KIAS in order to provide the flexibility to lower
the landing gear and flaps when required. Plan your descent profile (vertical path con-
trol, described below) in order to maintain above all intermediate minimum altitudes
(safe) and cross the FAF prepared to reach MDA with sufficient distance remaining to

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 30 =====
complete a landing if required (effective). During training, ensure that you are within the
following acceptable on course limits before any planned descent on the intermediate
segment:
a.
VOR Approach: inside a distance of 20 NM from the station, +/- 5 degrees
of the inbound course affords the aircraft a safe obstacle clearance. For those
approaches that require interception outside 20 DME, establishing the air-
craft on the inbound radial prior to descending will ensure a similar margin
of safety.
b.
Localizer approach: due to the increased sensitivity, the acceptable deflec-
tion for descent is as soon as the localizer becomes alive.
c.
GPS: where a change in track is anticipated, once the CDI automatically
changes to the subsequent segment track; or where little changing in track
is anticipated, once the distance to go commences to countdown for the next
segment.
45.
In order to achieve stabilized approach criteria, ensure that the aircraft is config-
ured, stabilized at 120 KIAS, and properly trimmed prior to the FAF. Inside the FAF,
monitor your VSI to ensure that you respect stabilized approach criteria (safe) and also
achieve MDA with sufficient distance remaining for a landing transition if required (ef-
fective). The anticipated rate of descent will vary with the approach flown and condi-
tions of the day. In most cases this will be 500–800 fpm. There are scenarios where
greater descent rates may be required; however, the rate of descent on final approach
should not be allowed to exceed 1000 fpm. Torque settings required for the descent
normally range from 20–30%.
46.
Upon reaching MDA, adjust torque and attitude to maintain altitude and 120 KIAS
until the MAP (approximately 35% torque and 2 degrees nose up). Strive to achieve and
maintain MDA. Deviations below MDA should not be tolerated and require a prompt
correction. The MAP is identified by DME, station passage, or timing depending on
the approach flown. Airspeed control is particularly important if your MAP is based on
timing.
9.21
VERTICAL PATH CONTROL
47.
Constant descent angle (CDA) information is supporting information depicted on
many non-precision approach procedures. You are responsible for how you choose
to use available CDA information during each particular non-precision approach. Al-
though techniques may vary, your plan for vertical path control must always respect
published minimum segment altitudes (safe) and strive to achieve MDA with sufficient
distance remaining to complete a landing if required (effective).
48.
The three main techniques for vertical path control during a non-precision ap-
proach are:

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 31 =====
a.
Step-Down: this technique involves a series of vertical descents and level-
offs at the minimum segment altitudes during the non-precision approach.
This technique makes minimal use of available CDA information. The step-
down technique is inherently unstable, requiring a higher workload, multiple
power and pitch changes, with more time at a minimum separation from ob-
stacles, and larger variations in rates of descent. The step-down technique
is not inherently unsafe but a premature descent or missed level-off could
prove catastrophic. Accordingly, the step-down technique is discouraged
when other methods are available. Even so, the step-down technique remains
valuable when, for example, the aim is to minimize time spent in cloud or
when the minimum FAF crossing altitude is close to the CDA altitude at the
FAF.
b.
Constant Descent Angle: this technique involves flying an approximately 3
degree flight path angle from an intermediate segment altitude to MDA, pass-
ing at or above any minimum approach altitudes. This method involves inter-
preting the CDA information depicted on the non-precision approach proce-
dure, including the distance/altitude table and rate of descent information. It
allows you to establish and maintain stabilized approach criteria throughout
the non-precision approach, with at most one level flight segment (at MDA
prior to the MAP).
c.
Stabilized Constant Descent Angle (SCDA): this technique involves using
the CDA information to mimic a precision approach. SCDA approaches are
characterized by removing the level-off at MDA, and proceeding directly to
either the landing or the missed approach as required.
49.
Both the step-down and constant descent angle techniques are acceptable and en-
couraged for use throughout the entirety of Harvard Phase 2 and Phase 3 training. You
are expected to demonstrate awareness of available CDA information and good judg-
ment in approach planning and technique selection.
50.
Using either technique, commence your descent as planned with an approximately
10% torque reduction and lower the attitude by one degree. Allow the VSI to stabilize.
Once stable, compare your actual versus planned rates of descent and adjust torque
slightly if required. Approaching the FAF, ensure that your altitude will pass between
the published minimum (safe) and CDA altitudes (effective) for the FAF. A slight power
change or a level-off may be required crossing the FAF, depending on your chosen
technique. Inside the FAF, continue to monitor the VSI to ensure both that the stabilized
approach criteria are respected (safe) and that MDA is achieved at or before the distance
corresponding to the last altitude on the distance/altitude in the table (effective). In
certain conditions, an approach reaching MDA slightly late can still be effective, but
during training you should plan using available CDA information.
51.
The SCDA technique is not recommended for Phase 2 and Phase 3 training, since
it involves a more challenging workload for undergraduate single pilot operations and

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 32 =====
removes the opportunity to practice and demonstrate MDA control. It also presents a
higher risk of an ineffective approach due to a potentially late arrival at MDA. Even so,
your instructor may introduce this technique at some point in your training for exposure
only.
52.
When flying the step-down or constant descent angle technique, you will normally
maintain MDA and proceed toward the MAP. Approaching MDA while flying the SCDA
technique, you must instead decide early whether to continue visual on the constant de-
scent angle to the runway or—without descending below MDA—commence the vertical
portion of the missed approach.
9.22
LANDING TRANSITION
53.
If the runway comes into view while you are on approach, it will normally not be
in the centre of the windscreen. As a result, ensure you scan the entire area ahead of you
when searching for the runway and be prepared to manoeuvre the aircraft in preparation
for landing. Maintain the MDA until a normal 3 degree sight picture is obtained as
outlined in Chapter 3. Any abrupt attitude changes to attempt to move the projected
touchdown point may produce high sink rates and thrust/lift problems at a critical time
and are, therefore, prohibited. VASIS or PAPI may be available and can be used as an
aid to position yourself on glide path. When using VASIS and PAPI, maintain glide path
indications to touchdown.
9.23
MISSED APPROACH POINT
54.
If you do not acquire runway environment by the MAP, or if the aircraft is not in
a position to carry out a safe landing, execute a missed approach. Perform the missed
approach by carrying out the go around procedure as detailed in Chapter 8. You must
comply with missed approach instructions issued by ATC or listed on your approach
plate. In certain conditions, it is appropriate to initiate the vertical portion of the missed
approach prior to the MAP, but the lateral portion always commences at the MAP. While
operating in VMC conditions, there may also be VFR restrictions imposed.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 33 =====
9.24
CIRCLING PROCEDURES
55.
Circling procedures are used to align the aircraft visually with the runway in use
when an instrument approach has been flown to another runway, or an approach has
been flown which is authorized for circling only (Figure 9.10). During the procedure
(as depicted in the Manual of Instrument Flying) maintain a minimum of 120 KIAS with
landing gear and T/O flap selected, keep visual contact with the runway while staying
within the visual manoeuvring area based on the aircraft category in accordance with
the GPH 204, and do not descend below circling MDA until landing is assured.
NOTE
Should you lose sight of the runway, initiate a missed approach for the
approach just flown, following the procedures outlined in the Manual of
Instrument Flying.
Figure 9.10: Recommended manoeuvring strategies for various final approach angles
9.25
VOR/DME APPROACH
56.
VOR/DME approaches allow the pilot to have continuous bearing and range in-
formation at all times. VOR is often combined with DME to provide lower landing
minima. During this approach, fixes, and MAPs are normally identified with a bearing
and DME from station. Possible routings to the FAF include:
a.
straight-in radial approach;
b.
arc approach; and
c.
high altitude penetration.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 34 =====
9.26
VOR APPROACH
57.
VOR approaches provide bearing information; however, without DME, position
from the airfield cannot be accurately determined except during station passage. For this
reason, a procedure turn will normally be flown in conjunction with a VOR approach and
MAP will normally be identified by station passage or by timings. A detailed description
of low altitude procedure turns is given in the CFP 148 or Manual of Instrument Flying.
Airspeed during procedure turns (recommended 150 KIAS) should be in compliance
with existing instrument regulations.
9.27
LOCALIZER-ONLY APPROACH (NO GLIDE PATH)
58.
Occasionally it may be necessary to fly a front-course ILS approach without glide
slope information. This is a non-precision approach. The cockpit set up is the same
as for an ILS; however, approach characteristics resemble a VOR/DME. The altitude to
which you may descend prior to the FAF may vary as described in the CFP 148. LOC-
only approaches may use DME, or timings for reference points on approach and for the
MAP. The primary difference with a LOC-only when compared with a VOR approach
is that the CDI is more sensitive on a LOC-only.
9.28
LOCALIZER BACK-COURSE APPROACH
59.
The localizer Back-Course or LOC (BC) approach is virtually identical to the
LOC-only approach. It is flown on the same localizer used on an ILS approach to the
opposing runway. For this reason, you must ensure that the published front course track
is set in the course-selector. This will result in the aircraft symbol being directional
relative to the CDI. As with a LOC-only there are several possible methods of deter-
mining your position and your MAP. These will be depicted on your approach plate.
Another similarity is the sensitivity of the course bar. The transmitter for the Localizer
is much closer to the aircraft on a back course therefore it will be more sensitive than
the LOC-only approach.
NOTE
As you approach the ground transmitter site, the CDI becomes increas-
ingly sensitive. When close to the missed-approach point a large-scale
deflection of the localizer may occur. You may fly an intercept heading
to regain the localizer track or, if the error is excessive, it is sometimes
prudent to execute a missed approach.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 35 =====
9.29
GPS APPROACH
60.
The GPS provides non-precision approach capability. GPS approaches are similar
to all other non-precision approaches; however, a GPS approach provides immediate
access to several items of useful information for your approach. These items of infor-
mation are available on other approaches but only through extrapolation from several
sources. For example, when flying the initial stages of the approach, a turn point is
predetermined by the GPS for the conditions of the day. By following the information
displayed, you can fly your turns to roll out accurately on the next approach track. Also,
the GPS display on your EHSI will provide you with your current distance to the next
approach point, and the time it will take to reach it. This information is invaluable in
updating your descent profiles and improves your overall awareness throughout your
approach.
61.
The GPS provides different modes: Enroute, Approach Arm, and Approach Ac-
tive. The modes are described in the KLN 900 manual. Of particular interest, the change
from approach arm mode to approach active mode is indicated by a change in colour
and is thus very useful. This takes place two miles prior to the FAF and acts as a vivid
reminder to configure the aircraft for final approach.
62.
During Phase II training, RNAV approaches shall be flown with the EHSI in HSI
mode and a bearing pointer active to the GPS. In this mode you will receive waypoint
turn anticipation when the CDI automatically changes to the subsequent leg track.
63.
When carrying out a missed approach, in order to receive positive track guidance
on the CDI to the MAHWP, the pilot must select Direct To and then Enter on the KLN
900.
9.30
PRECISION APPROACHES
64.
The accuracy of precision approaches allows the pilot to fly to lower landing
minima than employed for non-precision approaches. ILS and PAR are precision ap-
proaches that provide accurate azimuth and glide path information in order to position
an aircraft at Decision Height (DH) so that the pilot may be able to visually sight the
runway environment and land straight ahead.
9.31
INITIAL APPROACH FIX PROCEDURES
65.
The procedures for a precision approach are the same as those used for non-
precision approaches. Transitions to precision final can occur in several manners, sev-
eral of which utilize non-precision instruments and procedures. In all cases however the
general actions required from the IAF to the FAF are the same. These actions serve to
position the aircraft properly for the final approach phase.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 36 =====
66.
Strive to cross the IAF below 147 KIAS in order to provide the flexibility to lower
gear and flaps if required. (In some cases, you may find that the extra drag will assist
you in your descent to the next step down altitude on approach.) You can cross the IAF
and commence descent to the next step down altitude maintaining below 147 KIAS or in
some cases you can forego descent in order to intercept glide path from present altitude.
If descending, set a torque setting which allows for a controlled descent to your next
altitude.
67.
In all cases, strive to be level 2 NM prior to glide path interception. (In order to
determine the distance you will be from the field when you intercept glide path, use
the following formula: take your altitude above aerodrome elevation and divide by 300.
Since you lose 300 ft per NM on a 3 degree glide path, the answer to your equation will
be the number of NMs from the threshold of the runway at which you will intercept
glide path.) At this point, ensure your speed is below 147 KIAS, lower the landing gear
and select T/O flap. Set torque to approximately 35% and carry out the pre-landing
check. Stabilize the aircraft at 120 KIAS in level flight and ensure you are properly
trimmed in preparation for intercepting the glide path.
9.32
GLIDE PATH INTERCEPT AND FINAL APPROACH
68.
When intercepting glide path, adjust attitude and torque to maintain a final ap-
proach speed of 120 KIAS and a rate of descent to maintain glide path. A 3 degree
glide path with light winds requires a rate of descent of approximately 550 fpm which
requires 24–26% torque. The approximate attitude on the EADI to maintain glide path
is to place the fixed aircraft symbol bars on the horizon line. Minor changes to these
settings are required to compensate for conditions of the day. As you cross the Bea-
con/Marker, ensure checks are complete (i.e. 5-T check), as outlined in the CFP 148.
If applicable, check your altitude crossing the Beacon/Marker to ensure you are on the
correct glide path.
69.
On final approach, when making heading corrections, use small amounts of bank
(i.e. use a bank angle equal to the number of degrees of heading change, to a maxi-
mum of rate ½). Additionally the use of ARC mode on the EHSI may assist you in
maintaining the correct heading when past the FAF on an ILS, or when on final ap-
proach on a PAR. Small, coordinated pitch and power changes should be sufficient to
keep the aircraft on glide path and on speed during the descent. Remember that as for
the straight-in glide path learned during the clearhood phase, corrections to glide path
and/or airspeed require changes in attitude, torque, or a combination of both. Any ten-
dency to over-control can be eliminated by concentrating on maintaining the desired
attitude and cross-checking the performance instruments.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 37 =====
9.33
LANDING TRANSITION
70.
As you reach decision height, look up to determine if you can see the runway
environment. If the runway environment comes into view, line up with the centreline in
preparation for landing. Use a combination of ancillaries and/or torque to reduce your
speed for a normal landing as described in Chapter 3. A precision approach glide path
is projected from a point approximately 1000’ from the threshold of the runway. When
you see the runway and transition to a visual approach, do not move your aim point to
the threshold of the runway. Maintain the current glide path to the runway thus avoiding
a “duck under”.
9.34
MISSED APPROACH
71.
If you do not acquire the runway environment at Decision Height (DH) or if the air-
craft is not in a position to carry out a safe landing, execute a missed approach. Perform
the missed approach by carrying out the go around procedure as detailed in Chapter 8.
You must comply with missed approach instructions issued by ATC or listed on your
approach plate. While operating in VMC conditions, there may be VFR restrictions
imposed.
9.35
RADAR APPROACH
72.
In the rapidly changing and modernizing aviation environment, the radar approach
is being superseded by other precision approach aids. Radar approaches can still be
found at some Canadian military airfields. You will also encounter them at various
USAF and USN airfields and in many overseas countries.
73.
During a radar approach, a radar controller acts as your course and glide path
source of information. All clearances to step-down altitudes and information on glide
path intercept will also be passed on by your controller. Emphasis must be placed on
accurate heading control during a radar approach as the controller will continue to as-
sign headings assuming that you have maintained the last heading. Your controller will
indicate glide path errors and you must decide the amount of correction required based
on the information passed on to you. For example, if you are slightly above, small cor-
rections suffice; however, if you are told you are well above, more aggressive corrective
action will be required.
74.
A minimum fuel radar approach is used to conserve fuel. The controller will nor-
mally plan to have you intercept the final approach closer to the runway in order to
minimize fuel consumption. To do so they will vector you at a lower altitude so that
you will intercept the glide path in a normal manner but closer to the runway. Reduce

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 38 =====
to final approach speed once you are established on final approach. Upon glide path
interception, continue as for a normal radar final.
75.
No-compass radar approach procedures are explained in the CFP 148.
9.36
ILS APPROACH
76.
The ILS is a precision approach system that provides course and glide slope in-
formation to the pilot. It consists of a localizer, glide slope transmitter, and associated
marker beacons.
77.
The primary difference when flying an ILS approach as opposed to a non-precision
approach is the addition of glideslope information on your instrument displays. The
attention required to analyze the information and deciding on the corrective action re-
quired will take your attention away from your primary cross-check. You must learn to
compensate for this distraction and concentrate on your basic IF skills.
78.
When flying an ILS, ensure you continue to fly an attitude on your EADI. When
you check your EHSI to update your situation, do not immediately make adjustments.
Analyze your situation, and then look at your performance instruments to assess what
parameters were being maintained. At this point you can adjust your attitude and torque
as required to attain your new desired parameters on your performance instruments. You
can now determine if the desired parameters are correct by cross-checking your EHSI
and updating your navigation information. Continue to repeat this process throughout
the approach in order to maintain accurate aircraft control throughout.
79.
Possible routings to an ILS final include:
a.
radar vectors;
b.
point-to-point navigation; or
c.
a published transition procedure to an Intermediate Fix (IF).
NOTE
As the aircraft approaches the runway, the localizer beam narrows, which
has the effect of increasing tracking sensitivity on final. Knowing this,
you should select and fly appropriate headings and avoid chasing the
localizer.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 39 =====
9.37
RADAR SQUARE PATTERN
80.
The radar square pattern is a vectored transition that allows for multiple instrument
approaches. The procedure is described in the CFP 148. The size and shape of the
pattern may vary depending upon traffic; therefore, it is incumbent upon the pilot to
maintain a mental picture of their position in relation to the airfield and to plan their
transition to the final approach accordingly.
Figure 9.11: Typical radar square
81.
The radar square pattern will be used extensively during your training to perform
practice approaches. If departing into a radar square, accelerate normally to 180 KIAS
and climb to the assigned altitude. Fly the pattern at 180 KIAS until turning onto base
leg of the procedure. At this point, reduce torque to commence deceleration to the
airspeed desired for the IAF. During normal patterns, 20% torque works very well to
establish the aircraft on airspeed for the turn to final approach.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 40 =====
CHAPTER 10: VISUAL NAVIGATION
10.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
One of the most important skills required by a military pilot is the ability to navi-
gate the aircraft by visual reference to ground features. This conventional form of navi-
gation makes use of detailed maps, a plan of accurately flown pre-determined tracks and
groundspeeds, and heading and airspeed revisions made as required to reach the target
at a pre-determined time.
2.
During visual navigation training, you will be instructed in this method of navi-
gation while in the low-level environment. You will be also instructed in Mental Dead
Reckoning (MDR) navigation at a higher altitude to further broaden your skills. Al-
though the techniques and procedures taught on Phase Two may differ to varying de-
grees from those in the operational communities, they do teach a safe and efficient
method for navigation.
With these basics mastered, you can adjust to any tactical
method and perform future tasks and missions.
3.
In simple terms, visual navigation as taught in visual navigation can be broken
down into two parts; Navigation procedures and Map Reading principles. Navigation
procedures are a straight-forward ’checklist’ of habits that will act as the backbone or
foundation of a successful mission. Map Reading is a more challenging and fluid skill
that must develop with experience. While more important in the long run, Map Reading
skills take time to learn and a solid application of navigation procedures makes this
progression easier.
4.
The techniques and procedures applied to low-level navigation are also used during
the transit to the Hack, Return to Base (RTB) and MDR legs, which you will also be
taught during training.
5.
The primary reference for navigation training at FTS is A-GA-198-001/FP-001,
Manual of Pilot Navigation (MPN). It provides amplification of basic navigation proce-
dures. The procedures, as defined in the MPN, have been refined in this document and
are broken down into the following parts:
a.
Preparation and planning,
b.
Departure procedures,
140

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 41 =====
c.
Low-Level awareness,
d.
Navigation procedures,
e.
Map Reading principles,
f.
Mental Dead Reckoning, and
g.
Destination procedures.
6.
In addition, Emergency handling, specific to the low level environment, will be
covered.
10.2
PREPARATION AND PLANNING
7.
Pre-flight planning is a very important part of any low-level mission, since the suc-
cess of this type of mission depends on thorough preparation. You will be responsible
for preparing your maps and must approach pre-flight planning carefully and methodi-
cally.
8.
Map Preparation.
Phase Two navigation missions are planned entirely on a
1:500,000-scale map that includes the departure route, low level legs and RTB, oth-
erwise known as the Route Map. During the ground school portion of your training,
you will receive detailed instruction on the creation of the route map.
9.
All route maps will be prepared and conform to 1 Cdn Air Div – Tactical Standard
Operating Procedures (SOPs). Your navigation training will not employ any of the
tactical notations in the SOPs, but your maps should use the same notations, colours
and symbols for all non-tactical symbology. These SOPs have been developed so that if
you are tasked to prepare maps for other pilots flying a mission, a minimum amount of
route study will be required for them to interpret the plan. Also, if you are handed a set
of maps prepared by someone else you should be able to follow the routing successfully
with few if any questions about the plan. A mission planning guide is included in
Section 10.12 below and examples of route maps will be made available to you in your
appropriate mission planning areas.
10.
As a student you should strive to complete each mission planning exercise in a
maximum of two hours. Initially you may find this is insufficient time, but as your
experience builds, you will find that this is adequate time for preparation. Ensure that
you consult the most recent restrictions to determine if the routing should be changed. If
the standard routes conflict with a restriction, then your best judgment should be used to
resolve the conflict. As a guide, the following items should be indicated on your map(s):
a.
Departure Route. As basic route planning can occur well in advance of the
actual mission, routing from the take-off to the low level start point (Hack)
should be depicted for both runways. This routing will commence from a
position 5 NM from the point of brake release, which equates to 2 minutes.

===== SMM part 3 PDF page 42 =====
Figure 10.1: Sample Navigation Map With Markings
Plan your departure routing as to avoid high density traffic areas like the in-
ner/outer patterns including the rejoin lines. Avoid over-flying the city of
Moose Jaw below 5000’ MSL and in close proximity of the Moose Jaw Mu-
nicipal aerodrome.
b.
Low Level Legs. Notation and symbology will be depicted according to 1
Cdn Air Div SOPs and the examples contained in this chapter for the Route
Maps.
c.
RTB. The RTB will be depicted on the map from the target area directly back
to the base avoiding any obvious restrictions. Notation and symbology will
also conform to 1 Cdn Air Div SOPs.
d.
Heading. The calculated magnetic track for each leg is depicted on the map,
IAW the standardized symbology which can be found at the end of this chap-
ter. During your pre-flight planning, determine the maximum drift for the
route based upon your planned groundspeed and the forecast wind speed at
your planned altitude. The calculated headings, adjusted for wind, will be
depicted on your route map.

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 1 =====
e.
Altitude. The planned enroute altitude of any mission depends on many fac-
tors: sensitive citizens, restricted airspace and weather conditions being just
a few. In virtually all situations fuel considerations will be a factor in your
choice of altitudes. During your low-level training, fuel considerations have
been made and routes have been developed that should allow you to conduct
the mission in the medium to low-level environment from take-off to landing
and respect minimum VFR fuel reserves. Although 500 feet AGL sight pic-
ture will be taught during this phase, during the planning stage, check heights
may be annotated along the route to aid in altitude control enroute. Unless
otherwise directed, all low-level missions shall not be flown lower than 500
feet above all obstacles within 500 feet of track.
f.
Airspeed. Indicated airspeeds for each leg will be planned and depicted on
your route map. The aim of these airspeeds is to achieve a desired ground-
speed. Determine the no wind IAS to achieve this groundspeed based upon
the temperature at your planned altitude. You can expect that colder temper-
atures will require a higher indicated airspeed for a set groundspeed. Use this
calculated base speed, adjusted for wind, to determine the IAS for each leg.
For your Phase II training, all pre-planned portions, including transit to the
hack, low-level legs and RTB, will be planned for 240 Kts ground speed.
g.
Minimum Fuel. For the purpose of planning, 600 pounds per hour, or 10
pounds per minute will be used on low level missions. This figure is both
conservative in nature and one that offers ease of calculation, particularly
once airborne. More Accurate fuel flows can be found in the Harvard Quick
Reference Handbook (QRH) if required for more fuel critical missions.
h.
Once you have determined the fuel required for each leg, determine the mini-
mum fuel required at each turn point and hack to successfully complete your
mission. Start by planning to have 200 lbs of fuel at the “initial” point of the
traffic pattern at your destination. Then add the fuel required to transit from
the target to your destination at the altitude selected for the RTB to the 200
lbs required at initial. This gives you the minimum fuel figure to complete
the RTB. Keep working back in this way until you determine your minimum
fuel at the hack for your mission. Note these figures on your map in the
appropriate places.
i.
“BINGO” Fuel. A bingo fuel is depicted on your map at the point along
your route that is furthest from your destination, when measured in a straight
line. This fuel represents the minimum fuel at any point along your route that
is required to terminate the route and return straight to your destination. Fuel
consumption for this figure is normally based on low-level fuel consumption
at your planned enroute speed. This minimum fuel figure will also have the
200 lbs of fuel required at initial factored into it.

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 2 =====
10.3
LOW-LEVEL TRAINING RULES (TRS)
11.
Prior to any low-level navigation trip the Low-Level Training Rules (TR) shall be
briefed. The TRs will be briefed in detail by your instructor prior to flying your first
navigation mission. You must be prepared to brief the TRs at any time thereafter.
12.
Training Rules have been developed to ensure that all pertinent flight safety and
other related issues are reviewed immediately prior to conducting training missions in
certain regimes of flight. The TRs in Table 10.1 shall be briefed prior to every mission
where any part of the mission, aside from pattern work, is flown below 1000 ft AGL in
the Harvard;
NOTE
Table 10.1 includes all Low-Level Training Rules for the Harvard II.
Only those applicable to the mission need to be briefed (eg. Wingmen
considerations are not required when briefing single ship nav)
10.4
LOW-LEVEL NAVIGATION
13.
Low-level navigation in the Harvard is flown at a minimum altitude of 500 ft AGL
and minimum airspeed of 180 KIAS. Additional restrictions (e.g. Sensitive Citizens,
Poultry Farms etc.) shall be avoided in accordance with the FOM. Due to the low
altitude, there are many inherent dangers. Pilots must remain alert at all times, avoiding
any tendency to inadvertently descend.
14.
Considerations for low-level operations include:
a.
the field of view is reduced;
b.
landmarks can be difficult to identify;
c.
radio equipment has limited range;
d.
avoid becoming pre-occupied with routine “head in” cockpit tasks;
e.
fuel consumption is high;
f.
if you encounter turbulence that makes aircraft control difficult, lock your
harness and climb;
g.
there is an increased risk of bird strikes; and
h.
emergencies at low-level are particularly dangerous because of your proxim-
ity to the ground. During an emergency or birdstrike, your initial concern
should be to climb (zoom, etc.) while assessing the situation. This will pro-
vide you with a greater safety margin while taking appropriate corrective
action.

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 3 =====
WX Limits: Ceiling/Visibility Staff: 1000/3 Dual: 1500/3 Solo/TAC NAV: 2000/5, Dis-
cernible Horizon
The following shall be briefed (as applicable) when any part of the mission, aside from
pattern work, will be flown below 1000’ AGL:
•
Minimum Altitudes (Flying Orders)/ Minimum Airspeed 180 KIAS,
•
Flight path and Terrain Clearance are primary tasks,
•
Each pilot is responsible for flight path and terrain clearance,
•
Each pilot will fly at their own comfort level, but not below mins,
•
Wingmen will fly above Lead except where terrain dictates otherwise,
•
Enroute turns are level to slightly climbing, not to exceed 180 degrees,
•
Task saturation/ Channelized attention avoidance,
•
Depth perception over featureless terrain/ White-Out illusions,
•
Adjust minimum altitude for sun, weather, terrain or comfort level,
•
Sustained and instantaneous G capability,
•
Lookout responsibilities, sector prioritization, expected threat areas, canopy bow, line of
sight considerations,
•
Weather abort/ Inadvertant IMC, and
•
Emergency/ Bird strike reactions.
PULL UP, PULL UP: Everybody roll wings level and climb to 1000’ AGL or higher; as
required for terrain clearance. Anybody can make this call.

10.5
LOW LEVEL AWARENESS
15.
Navigation requires the effective integration of map reading and navigation proce-
dures. Just as you have seen in instrument flying, this integration occurs in the form of a
logical crosscheck. This crosscheck is referred to as the low-level crosscheck. There are
several items included in this crosscheck, such as terrain clearance, lookout, navigating
the aircraft, and cockpit checks. At times you will be required to prioritize items but
at all times terrain avoidance must take precedence over all other tasks in the low-level
environment. While the aforementioned basics remain constant, regardless of aircraft,
in future training and in operational flying, you will find that Low Level Awareness
training will be tailored to the specific performance of the platform flown. Where the
fighter force, for example, defines below 5000 feet AGL to be the “Low Level” environ-
ment, the flight characteristics and mission profile of Harvard II allow this limitation to
be lowered to 1000 feet AGL.
16.
Descent. When descending to the low-level environment, you must prioritize your
crosscheck on terrain clearance and lookout. Prior to descent, carry out an Ops Check,
check your harness is tight and secure all loose articles. Ensure you clear the area di-
rectly below you prior to initiating your descent then commence a controlled descent to
your desired altitude. Excessive rates of descent should be avoided, however prolonged
shallow descents will draw your crosscheck away from navigating for an extended pe-
riod of time. Plan on a positive rate of descent and concentrate only on lookout and
terrain clearance until you are level at your desired altitude. As you descend below
1000 ft AGL, ensure that the wings are level and commence a gradual pitch change to
reduce the rate of descent in order to level off at 500 ft AGL. Descending turns shall not
be conducted below 1000ft AGL.
17.
Terrain Clearance. The primary and most important task in the low-level cross-
check is flight path and terrain clearance. If you find that your attention to this task is
so great that you are incapable of completing any other tasks, then you are flying too
low. In the low-level environment, at 500 ft AGL in straight and level flight, you have
approximately 5 seconds of time to do other tasks between periods of cross-checking
terrain clearance. You must develop a sense for how long this is. By delaying your ter-
rain clearance for an extra second or two while completing some other task, you could
be entering an unrecoverable descent. You must develop the ability to keep track of
exactly how long you have been away from your terrain clearance task and return to it
when the time is up. This is a discipline that is essential to surviving in the low-level
environment.
18.
Maintaining height over hilly terrain requires anticipation and power adjustments
to maintain your airspeed. Delaying your climb to clear obstacles so that you “balloon”
over a point is not a desired technique as it hampers your forward visibility and normally
leads to excess altitude gain; it is also tactically unsound. The desired technique is to
determine what obstacles are ahead and plan to initiate your climb to attain your crossing
altitude prior to the obstacle. This allows you to cross the highest points in a level flight

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 5 =====
condition. This is termed “contour flying”. The power and attitude changes will vary
depending on the relative change of terrain.
19.
Turning. All turns in the low-level shall be level to slightly climbing, and shall
not exceed 180 degrees in heading change. The rationale in the low-level environment
is to spend as little time as possible in turns so that your primary low-level crosscheck
is allowed to continue with minimal interruption. When turning, you must modify your
crosscheck to focus exclusively on terrain clearance and lookout into the turn. You
must complete the turn prior to performing any other task. During the turn, you may
divert your attention for quick glances (maximum 1 second) at necessary items such as
your altimeter to ensure you are not descending You must be mindful of turning into
rising terrain and anticipate the use of contour flying techniques if required. Regardless
of the type of turn completed, there are three key points to remember while turning in
the low-level environment:
a.
Never descend;
b.
Never look behind your wing; and
c.
Never fixate on anything.
Descending during a low level turn is an unacceptable situation and you must be vigilant
to prevent its occurrence. Causes of a descent include: overbanking, insufficient “G” for
current bank angle, or poor horizon references in undulating terrain. Should you find
yourself in a descending turn, you must immediately take action to correct it. Change
your lift vector immediately by decreasing bank and increasing “G” as required to com-
mence a climb to regain lost altitude. Once lost altitude is regained, you can continue
with your turn in a normal manner.
20.
Collision Avoidance. While flight path and terrain clearance is considered the
most important factor in conducting safe and effective low-level navigation mission,
lookout becomes the next most important aspect. In Phase II, the approved Low-level
navigation routes will take place in airspace accessible to both military and civilian
traffic. Use of common radio frequencies for giving and receiving position reports will
greatly enhance your situational awareness, however, an effective lookout is paramount
in detecting and avoiding all other aircraft. Considering the relatively fast speed of the
Harvard II and the training and experience of many light aircraft operators, one should
never assume the other pilot is visual with you.
21.
Lookout. Lookout is the responsibility of each pilot and must be completed in
a very methodical way. The primary lookout sector (forward sector) is the main area
of focus and crosscheck outside of this sector should be no more than 5 seconds in
order to ensure ideal primary lookout and terrain clearance. Similar to the instrument
scan learned in IF where you always returned to the EADI, the lookout scan during
low-level navigation always returns to the primary sector. Confirm that there are no
obstacles, birds or traffic straight ahead near your aircraft, and then check in the distance

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 6 =====
Figure 10.2: Lookout Sectors
for upcoming terrain, threats and weather. Verify you are not descending and that your
heading, altitude and airspeed are accurate. You now have 3 to 5 seconds to clear one
of the secondary sectors mainly for traffic. Concentrate your lookout to just above and
just below the horizon. Leaning slightly forward and back, or tilting your head, will
help you see around the canopy bow. Return to the forward sector to repeat the process
of clearing the primary sector. Once complete, you have 3 to 5 seconds to scan the
other secondary sector. Remember that this lookout continues while incorporating your
navigation workflow, always returning to the primary sector.
10.6
TAKE-OFF AND DEPARTURE
22.
Before the takeoff, the additional task of cockpit organization is important to mis-
sion success. It is necessary to place route maps in the cockpit so that they stay organized
and ready for use. Remember that a poorly secured map can end up on the floor and it
is very hazardous to go searching for a lost map at 500 ft AGL.
23.
Good planning and a thorough route study will aid in ensuring that the take-off
and departure go smoothly. Since the Hack may be difficult to identify, accurate navi-
gation is imperative. Ensure your clock is ready to start at brake release and confirm the
accuracy of your heading system.
24.
Once airborne remember to conduct the normal Post-Take-Off and maintain a vig-
ilant lookout for other traffic, taking into consideration the rejoin paths of arriving air-
craft. Once clear of the high density traffic areas, conduct a Climb/Level Off Check.
25.
Enroute to the Hack. Locating and properly identifying the Hack is an important
key to the overall effectiveness of a navigation mission. Carry out a fuel check prior

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 7 =====
Offset: (preferred)
Racetrack
•
Identify hack early and turn, if re-
quired, to generate manoeuvring space.
•
Turn inbound to intercept an extended
desired-track line.
•
Fly visually towards hack and refine
H-A-A
•
Fly directly over the hack point.
•
Turn to a “downwind leg” on the re-
ciprocal of your desired track.
•
Turn to your desired track and fly to-
wards your hack and refine H-A-A.
Table 10.2: Hack Procedures
to the hack and ensure your quantity is equal to, or greater than, the MIN FUEL noted
on your map at the hack. It is useful to note the difference, or “delta”, in minimum
and actual fuel for future reference during the mission. Accurately fly the routing that
you have planned on your route map employing the proper map reading principles as
discussed later in this chapter. Once the Hack is positively identified, reset your clock
in preparation for crossing the Hack.
10.7
NAVIGATION PROCEDURES
26.
As discussed earlier in the introduction to this chapter, Navigation Procedures are
a set or list of all of the habits that make up the routine that will be used while navigating
in the low level environment. Think of these procedures as a checklist of activities to be
conducted whenever applicable.
Figure 10.3: Offset Hack
27.
Set Heading/Hack Procedures. Prior to your descent and during your hack pro-
cedures it is critical to maintain a vigilant lookout for other aircraft. Plan your descent
and manoeuvring to aid in crossing the hack on heading, airspeed and altitude. Your

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 8 =====
Figure 10.4: Racetrack Hack
descent profile must minimize manoeuvring while descending into the low level envi-
ronment. If unsatisfied with your alignment, conduct an orbit or racetrack pattern and
re-align the aircraft. Ensure that your focus is shared during this phase between terrain
clearance, collision avoidance and the set-up for the hack.
28.
Ideally the hack is identified early enough so you can employ the “offset” tech-
nique (Figure 10.3). This is simply defined as turning away from the hack to provide
manoeuvring space to turn back towards it with enough time and distance to more ac-
curately set heading, altitude and airspeed. It should not require a turn of more than
180 degrees from your first leg heading. The second technique used is called the “Race
Track” technique (Figure 10.4). It should only be used when the hack is identified late
and insufficient time is available to fly the Offset. The hack can then be overflown and
the aircraft manoeuvred to an ‘outbound’ type leg before turning back inbound to the
hack — much like a holding pattern.
29.
As you cross the hack, call “PREPARE TO HACK ... HACK NOW’ over the
intercom, ensuring that you start your timing when you are directly over the hack point.
Hacking even slightly late or early introduces a time error that you will have to eliminate
later. Conduct a “5T Check” to ensure all tasks are completed at the hack. An example
is as follows:
a.
TIME – is running;
b.
TURN – confirm correct heading (adjust if required);
c.
TRACK – conduct a Track Angle Departure Check;
d.
THROTTLE – confirm set for desired airspeed; and
e.
TALK – intercom/RT appropriate for dual/solo mission.
30.
Aircraft Control. In general terms, successful navigation entails precise control
of your heading, altitude and airspeed. This stable platform will be the foundation
on which your map reading skills will develop. As part of your routine, verbalizing

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 9 =====
“HEADING – ALTITUDE – AIRSPEED” will aid you in ensuring these parameters are
being accurately flown. Accurate setting of power and a solid trim technique in all axes
further aid this process.
31.
Heading. Fly your pre-determined heading, but monitor your Track (TK) as indi-
cated on the GPS. Adjust your heading as required throughout the route, to maintain the
desired track.
32.
Altitude. Another important skill in low-level training is the ability to visualize
and maintain a specific altitude above ground (minimum 500 ft AGL). Even in aircraft
configured with a radar-altimeter, sight picture will be used as a basic control the altitude
of the aircraft. You will have previously determined the hack- crossing altitude and
have a general idea of enroute altitudes. These altitudes may be a useful reference until
you learn to visualize what your required ‘500 foot sight-picture’ looks like. Use the
altimeter in conjunction with the sight picture to strive for the ideal altitude over the
entire mission.
33.
Several factors may affect safe execution of low-level navigation. Minimum alti-
tudes will be raised when the 500 foot sight-picture cannot be ascertained. These may
be encountered in the following situations:
a.
Sun – flying towards the sun, particularly in the early morning or late evening,
may cause a loss of depth perception and identification of ground features
and/or threats;
b.
Weather – transitions from bright sunlight, to dark shaded areas due to clouds,
low lying fog or precipitation may make the sight picture difficult to see, in
extreme circumstances this may require a weather abort;
c.
Featureless Terrain – most common following fresh snowfall in the winter-
time or over open water, ground features may be obscured and altitude can
be difficult determine. The worst case scenario may be encountered with
low clouds over snow covered ground. The low clouds eliminate shadows
and can make altitude impossible to determine by lookout. A White-out Il-
lusion occurs when the horizon is also obscured, giving the illusion of flying
in cloud;
d.
Terrain – steep terrain gradients may exceed aircraft performance;
e.
Any time safety of flight may be compromised such as task saturation or pilot
currency/comfort level.
34.
During the winter, with temperatures -30 degrees C and colder, altimeter errors
can be as much as 80 to 100 ft. These errors should be determined during pre-flight
planning and applied during the mission. To calculate this, refer to GPH 200 (Instrument
Approach Procedures) Flip, Temperature Correction Chart.
35.
Airspeed. Fly your pre-determined IAS for the planned groundspeed, but monitor
groundspeed as indicated on the GPS. Adjust your IAS as required throughout the route,

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 10 =====
Figure 10.5: Watch Map Ground
to maintain the desired ground speed. This will virtually eliminate any timing errors
almost immediately.
36.
Watch-Map-Ground. This workflow that is employed as a method of incorporat-
ing map reading skills into your navigation routine. With minute marks depicted on the
low-level map, you will always know your position along track with a reasonable degree
of accuracy by simply referring to the time on your stopwatch. In general, concentrate
on ground features that are close to track, and a minimum of 1 to 2 minutes (4 to 8 NM)
ahead of your actual position. This is accomplished by noting your current time, finding
this point on your map and noting the features at the appropriate distance ahead on the
map (Figure 10.5). Apply the principles of map reading in order to positively identify
the specific ground features. If you are unable to identify the anticipated features prior
to reaching them, determine your new position by referring to your watch, and check
the map for new features at an appropriate distance ahead of you. Do not continue to
look for the first feature because it will now be behind you. Depending upon the scale
of your map, there may be prolonged periods of time when there are no appropriate
features on your map. In these instances concentrate on accurate heading, airspeed and
altitude control and anticipate the next usable features as you approach them.
37.
Track Corrections. If you have determined that you are off track, visual correc-
tions to a confirmed ground feature are the primary method of regaining it. Attempt to
make an immediate visual correction in order to regain track prior to the ground feature
(or checkpoint) so that you cross it on heading and airspeed. If no features exist ahead,
then a timed correction can be employed. If you determine the distance you are off
track, apply the following formula, called the “Standard Closing Angle”. Correct for
drift. Make a further 10-degree correction to track and hold it for 1 .5 minutes for each
1 NM off track. Alternatively, a 15-degree correction to track can be flown. Hold it for 1
minute for each 1 NM off track. Don’t forget to turn back to desired track. This formula
is based on 240 kts groundspeed.
38.
Time Corrections. The minute marks on the low-level map are included specifi-

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 11 =====
cally as an aid to navigation at low-level. As you progress along the route a divergence
between actual and desired time may develop. Predetermined check points and turn
points are normally sufficient to ensure proper time maintenance. However, at any con-
firmed and identified ground feature the time can be noted and compared against the
ideal. Time errors can be caused by any of the following:
a.
not maintaining ideal groundspeed;
b.
not maintaining previously calculated/revised headings (causes track error
which can cause a time error); and
c.
winds different from the forecast.
39.
Anytime a timing error is confirmed, proceed as follow:
a.
Note the time error and assess the cause.
b.
Ensure that a correction to the indicated airspeed is made to stabilize the
error. When using the GPS, simply adjust your indicated airspeed to capture
240 Kts ground speed.
c.
Make an adjustment to the ground speed, of one knot for every second of
timing error. To avoid chasing ground speed, all adjustments should be based
on indicated airspeed.
d.
Ensure that you monitor your correction. After 4 minutes, reset your indi-
cated airspeed to achieve the desired ground speed.
40.
If the required airspeed increase/decrease is not practical (either too large a correc-
tion or insufficient time remaining), you may adjust the required airspeed change and
change the required time period by the same factor (i.e.: 100 seconds late, fly a 50-knot
increase for eight minutes).
41.
Monitor time corrections to ensure they are working and have been correctly ap-
plied. This can be confirmed by ensuring the time error decreases with the passage of
time (use of minute marks/suitable features).
42.
Turn point Procedures. Any turn radius depicted on your map should be based
on a 60-degree bank turn in a no-wind condition, but you may wish to compensate
for known winds by adjusting your bank angle not to exceed 60 degrees of bank. For
heading changes of 30 degrees or less 45 degrees of bank may be used, to prevent
the likelihood of overshooting the desired heading. Your new track commences from
the end of the turn radius or simply originates from the turn point for turns of smaller
duration. Despite this, it is still important that you cross the point on heading and
commence your turn.
43.
Before reaching each turning point, time permitting, conduct a cockpit check and
at a minimum confirm that you have the minimum fuel required. Determine the IAS and
heading for the next leg based upon your assessment of the winds. Immediately prior

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 12 =====
to the turn have a good lookout to clear the area for rising terrain, obstacles and traffic
with careful attention to the direction of the turn. At the turning point, note the time and
complete the turn to the new heading. IAW the Low Level Training Rules, ensure you
do not descend during the turn.
44.
Once the turn is complete, first execute a lookout in the primary sector, then fine
tune your heading, altitude, airspeed and complete a 5T Check, clear the area and then
continue on with your low-level crosscheck. If there is a time error carry out a time
correction as required.
45.
In any case where you are unable to positively identify the turn point, take in to
account any known timing error and execute the turn based upon clock timing.
46.
Track Angle Departure Checks. Following the hack and each turn point, as
prompted by the 5T Check, carry out a Track Angle Departure (TAD) Check. This
procedure is a visual confirmation (Map to Ground) that the aircraft is correctly aligned
for the next leg of the route. This is accomplished by using ground features near or
on track, after the Hack/Turn Point and confirming the aircraft is headed in the correct
direction. This check will quickly identify any misread heading, in-accurate heading
system, large change in forecast wind or similar situation. Any prominent and confirmed
feature or group of feature(s) will be effective. Smaller ground features closer to the
aircraft, larger objects off in the distance or the orientation relative to the section lines all
work well as a TAD Check. The accuracy of this check will depend upon the availability
of features and the pilot’s ability to pin-point the aircraft position. Strive in all cases to
complete the most accurate confirmation of track possible.
10.8
MAP READING FUNDAMENTALS
47.
Basic map reading is essential to accurate low-level visual navigation. Regard-
less of how accurate heading, altitude and airspeed control is during a mission, these
factors alone cannot ensure the aircraft in placed directly over the target. Where Nav-
igation procedures represent the framework or foundation, Map Reading is detailed or
“finishing” aspect of the combination.
48.
The Navigation Procedure WATCH-MAP-GROUND is intended to incorporate
map reading into your routine. The skill of Map Reading itself is essentially organized
into the four functions of: orientation, anticipation, confirmation and pinpointing. The
key to successful map reading is the correct application of these four fundamentals.
49.
Orient the enroute map so that you are always at the bottom of the map moving
upwards. This ensures the sequence of features will show up on the ground in the order
they were studied during your map preparation. This is the first step towards successful
anticipation.
50.
Anticipation of what you will see once airborne, starts the very first time that the
route is viewed in the planning phase of the mission. During your planning and route

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 13 =====
study you will begin to familiarize yourself with features along the route. Once air-
borne, using the procedure WATCH-MAP-GROUND, the MAP stage initiates the cycle
of what the next anticipated ground feature will be. While many large and obvious
features can be committed to memory, not all can be, and consulting the map will be
required many times throughout the mission in order to properly anticipate the finer de-
tails. Looking at features 1-2 mm ahead during the MAP phase as opposed to features at
your current time is also critical to anticipation. An excellent technique for anticipating
upcoming points on your route involves making use of obvious lead-in features. When
consulting the MAP, look for BIG, easily identifiable features that will offer a point on
the ground to commence your scan. From that point use, MEDIUM sized features that
will allow your eye to track to a SMALL feature that will assist in positively identifying
your position relative to your desired track. Your instructor will further explain using the
BIG – MEDIUM – SMALL technique when transitioning from the map to the ground.
51.
Confirmation is achieved by positively identifying your selected checkpoints.
This function is one of great importance and is a key in successful map reading. De-
termining with a high degree of certainty that the ground feature in question is indeed
the anticipated map feature, can be done in several manners. What is important in all
cases, is to determine first what is unique about the MAP feature and then, compare
this to the GROUND feature. Most larger features, such as lakes, can be confirmed
effortlessly by their sheer size and/or unique shape. Other less obvious points require
the identification of several points in the surrounding area for accurate confirmation. Do
not limit the search to the immediate vicinity of the point in question. Often ambigu-
ous points, such as a crossroads, can only be confirmed by looking some distance down
those roads. From a procedural prospective, you may find yourself repeating the stage
MAP and GROUND several times as you progress from larger to smaller features.
52.
Pinpointing is the most accurate stage of map reading and is achieved by deter-
