mining your actual position in relation to a confirmed ground feature. This allows you
to apply proper corrections to return to the ideal and is required in all case before em-
ploying a visual correction to track, as described earlier. As a general rule, having made
use of larger, easily identifiable features as a lead-in, the smaller, more detailed features
on your map will allow you to pin-point the actual point on the ground.
53.
At low-level, your forward progress over the ground appears to be much quicker
than at higher altitudes. Anticipate this and ensure that you always map read at least
one to two minutes ahead of your actual position. If at any time anticipated feature
cannot be found and/or confirmed, re-start the W-M-G cycle, anticipating a new feature.
One technique that may assist you in map reading is to follow progress along track
with your left thumb. (Avoid the temptation to “track-crawl”). This gives you a visual
indication of your approximate position and allows you to concentrate on features the
correct distance ahead of this position.
54.
In the low-level environment, ground features with vertical relief stand out promi-
nently. During your preflight route study, you determined the general relief characteris-

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 14 =====
tics of your route. You should also have noted specific topographic features along track
such as valleys, low ground, ridges, hills, lakes, rivers and streams. These features are
quite visible at low-level and should be used in conjunction with other features. Road-
ways and railroads running parallel to track often can be seen for long distances. The
opposite is the case when orientated perpendicular to track.
55.
Keep in mind that many changes may have occurred since the creation of the map.
Seasonal and/or long-term environmental changes can make the ground features look
significantly different than anticipated. Small towns may have disappeared or large
industrial facilities may have been constructed. Geographical relief tends to be reliable
due to its resistance to environmental change. Likewise is the point with the largest of
man-made features, such as dams, cities and highways.
56.
You must avoid the tendency to note significant features on the ground and then
trying to identify them on your map. Depending on the scale of map used, varying
amounts of detail may not appear on your map. Always read map-to-ground to eliminate
this problem.
57.
Exercise caution during the task of map reading, to ensure and that Terrain Clear-
ance and Collision Avoidance are not comprised.
58.
Low level navigation will be completed after flying over the target. After the
target, immediately climb away from low level environment using MAX PCL and turn
to your pre-determined heading to RTB. Altitude selection will be based on weather and
direction of flight.
10.9
DESTINATION PROCEDURES
59.
Arrival at Home Base. If you are returning to your own base, you will be quite
familiar with the common rejoins to the outer traffic pattern. Depending upon the di-
rection of your arrival from the low-level route, it may be most practical to rejoin via a
less common path, or possible even to rejoin for the inner traffic pattern. During your
pre-flight route study you should have planned your arrival to avoid restricted areas and
the IFR letdown area in use. Additionally, if rejoining to the inner pattern, familiarize
yourself with the Hawk traffic pattern procedures and take this into account as you re-
cover. As you approach your destination, listen to ATIS, squawk home base discrete,
and then contact ATC for clearance to enter the control zone. A vigilant lookout is es-
sential throughout the rejoin. As per the Clear-hood section, plan your rejoin to ensure
that the aircraft is established on the rejoin track, altitude and airspeed at least one mile

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 15 =====
prior to the published traffic pattern.
NOTE
During rejoins to the inner runway on the extended downwind leg, take
into account the crosswind position of traffic changing from outer to
inner pattern.
60.
Arrival at an Unfamiliar Airport. If you are landing at an unfamiliar airport
you must first familiarize yourself with all the pertinent aspects. Make particular note
of the airfield elevation, runways available and their length. Contact ATC to determine
the runway in use and any restrictions that may affect routing. If there is no published
circuit altitude, plan to fly the traffic pattern at 1000 ft AGL for a straight-in, or 1500 ft
AGL for an overhead pattern.
61.
Formulate an arrival plan, noting the significant ground features you will use to
navigate to the pattern, as the ground features will not be as familiar to you as those at
your home base. When the aerodrome is visual, reference to other traffic and the runway
will ensure a safe recovery.
62.
If the airfield is served by a RADAR terminal control facility, contact them for
traffic advisory prior to contacting the tower. Requesting radar vectors for a visual or
an instrument approach is the easiest method of ensuring compliance with local arrival
procedures.
10.10
MENTAL DEAD RECKONING
63.
Mental Dead Reckoning (MDR) skills are an asset to any pilot who is faced with
a situation requiring a diversion or deviation from track due to any unforeseen circum-
stance. Unlike the entire planned portion of your mission, where you have had ample
time to prepare and are expected to accurately execute the navigation, the MDR repre-
sents a more coarse form of navigation. The expectations change in that the goal has
become to simply execute the deviation. Navigation procedures remain important; how-
ever the roughness of headings and other calculations are compensated for by the more
accurate map reading permitted by the increase in altitude. The use of GPS will not be
available during MDR.
64.
While carrying out MDR calculations and flight, it is very important to maintain
control of the aircraft in level flight with reasonable accuracy and maintain a vigilant
lookout for other aircraft. The Hack should be completed within 5 minutes of being
given the MDR target; however, you should realize that an actual diversion may neces-
sitate an immediate heading alteration and that your priorities in preparation will change
to best suit each situation. Typically an MDR will be flown in the medium altitude lev-
els.

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 16 =====
Figure 10.6: MDR sample map
65.
Preparation. Having determined the destination of the MDR (and motivation),
the next task is to assess a track to be flown. A suitable feature either on or just off track
should be selected as a hack. If necessary, due to lack of good references, the hack can
be planned from a timing mark on the present planned leg. In any case, when picking
a hack, lengthy or significant diversions from planned track should be avoided. Once
the hack is selected, draw a line connecting the hack and target by either creasing the
map or using a pen or pencil. Finally, determine the magnetic track to be flown. On
aeronautical charts, the quickest method of doing this is to parallel the track through a
compass rose found on the map, using a pencil.
66.
To determine your fuel requirements, first measure the total distance to the diver-
sion point and then to destination. Next, determine the time enroute and fuel required to
reach your destination via the planned routing. You will require a minimum of 200 lbs
of fuel at the “initial” point, so be sure to include this in your fuel calculations. For the
Harvard II, the fuel and time calculations are based upon a 220KIAS /240 knots TAS
and a fuel flow of 10 pounds per minute. An example can be completed as follows:
a.
approximate the distance to your destination via the planned routing, i.e.: 40
NM (use the scale along the line of Longitude, one of your pre-planned legs
or other distance scale preplanned. i.e. a marked pen or log card);
b.
divide the total distance by the estimated groundspeed in miles/minute (240

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 17 =====
kts I 60 minutes = 4 NM/mm) to determine the enroute time, i.e.: 40/4 = 10
minutes; and
c.
multiply your time (in minutes) by the fuel consumption (in lbs/mm) to deter-
mine the enroute fuel, and then add the fuel required overhead the destination
to find the total fuel required, i.e. 10 minutes X 10 lbs/mm = 100 lbs + 200
lbs at initial + fuel from the MDR target to Initial. Compare this minimum
required to your current fuel state.
67.
The route to your destination may not be direct; therefore, you may have to make
separate calculations to find the time and fuel for each leg. Doing this assists you in
your log keeping and anticipation of turn points, as well as providing a check for fuel at
each turn point.
68.
Mark appropriate times on the map to assist you while navigating enroute. The
number of time checks required, will depend upon the length of the MDR leg. On
longer legs, in excess of sixty miles, ¼, ½, and ¾ marks are generally used. On shorter
legs, the ¼ mark tends to occur too close after the hack and does not serve any practical
use. If required, the ¾ can be used, but provided the ½ timing update was conducted, the
¾ tends only to pre-occupy you when anticipation of the target should be the priority. If
the anticipated enroute features suggest one third distance lines, this is another option.
69.
Depending on the situation, you may be required to start the MDR leg before
completing the entire planning process. Provided that you have a track line and have
given consideration to your fuel quantity, it is acceptable to hack and complete out the
remaining calculations enroute.
70.
Enroute. When reaching the MDR hack point, commence a turn to intercept
track and hack the clock. If required, climb or descend to an appropriate VFR cruising
altitude. Once established on track, carry out a 5T Check to ensure your planning and
navigation are accurate. Pay particular attention to the Track Angle Departure check to
verify the accuracy of your estimated track/heading.
71.
Initially, you will be required to fly your track as a heading, until you determine the
effects of the wind. Timely application of perceived wind conditions may be completed
at any time, including the planning stage of your MDR leg. Forecast upper winds of the
day will give you some sense of their velocity at your MDR altitude.
72.
Enroute medium level procedures are virtually identical to those used during low-
level navigation. Apply the procedures and establish a stable platform using HEADING
– ALTITUDE – AIRSPEED. By using WATCH-MAP-GROUND, you can monitor your
progress along the route. The most significant difference with the MDR is the distance
at which you can anticipate seeing ground features. Conversely, the altitude may hinder
finding closer features as they fall under the nose. This being said, it is important to look
much further ahead than in the low level. Applying the same sight-line logic as used
low-level, for MDRs flown at approximately 3000 AGL it is recommended to look at

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 18 =====
least 10-15 miles ahead of the aircraft. Confirmation and Pin-pointing remains the same
as low-level.
73.
Visual corrections to track can be made for minor deviations if errors are picked
up early. For larger deviations however, a revised track from your present position,
direct to the target should be determined and drawn on your map to expedite arrival.
74.
During the MDR leg, the goal is not to reach the target at a pre-calculated time.
The use of the clock and timing marks is simply to aid in anticipating the target. Strive
to maintain 220 KIAS (approx. 240 TAS) and, when pinpointing, determine a revised
ETA if required. For example, if your planned ETA was 8:00 and you arrive at your ½
distance line at 3:45, you would revise your ETA to 7:30.
10.11
PRECAUTIONARY PROCEDURES
75.
Below Minimum Fuel. The fuel figure that you have indicated in the minimum
fuel block on your map indicates the actual fuel you require in order to complete the
mission with a 200-lb reserve at the “initial” point. Normally, you will find you have
surplus fuel well above the minimum required at the hack, and this surplus or differential
will remain fairly constant throughout the trip.
76.
If this surplus begins to decrease significantly and/or you find you are below the
minimum fuel required at any point enroute, a premature recovery to base is likely re-
quired. Attempt to determine why the fuel state has deteriorated (whether because of
stronger than forecast winds, poor fuel management or a possible fuel leak) and deter-
mine your next course of action. A minor shortfall may only require an RTB at a higher
altitude, while a critical shortage would indicate an immediate climb for optimum range
and possibly a diversion to the nearest suitable airfield. For example, some routes may
take you close to Swift Current or Saskatoon, so these airfields should be considered.
77.
Uncertain of Position. If you follow correct navigation procedures and map read-
ing techniques, you should never become lost during low-level navigation. If, however,
you become uncertain of your position through a failure to identify several features
along track, follow the procedures below:
a.
maintain a steady heading;
b.
review previous navigation to include a crosscheck of the EHSI and standby
compass (to ensure accuracy), as well as to confirm if the correct heading and
airspeed have been flown;
c.
estimate your position on the map based on the airspeed and amount of time
you have flown since your last pinpoint. If you are still unable to position
yourself, try to identify a prominent feature by reading from the ground-to-
map;
d.
turn on ETA for the turn point if still unsure of position; and

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 19 =====
e.
once you have positively identified a feature along track, return to watch-
map-ground map reading technique and concentrate on reaching your next
pre-selected checkpoint or turn point. At low-level, a climb of 500 to 1000 ft
enhances your field of view.
78.
Lost. The most important factor to consider when lost is to admit it. You could
potentially be flying further from your destination and wasting valuable fuel reserves.
If you are lost, you should climb to a medium altitude and spend a maximum of five
minutes attempting to pinpoint your position. If unsuccessful, you should:
a.
climb as necessary, being aware of airspace implications and requirements
for an ATC clearance;
b.
employ all radio and navigation aids to RTB; and
c.
if necessary, declare an emergency and squawk mode 3, code 7700.
79.
Deteriorating Weather. All your navigation trips are planned for VFR flight.
However, with sudden changes in weather you could encounter low ceilings and poor
visibility on a navigation mission. The weather limits for the VFR flight are outlined in
the CF Flying Orders and Unit Orders.
80.
You should never continue a visual navigation trip into marginal weather condi-
tions. If the weather ahead appears unsuitable, make your decision to abort early and
RTB or divert to a known VFR alternate.
81.
You may encounter certain weather phenomena such as gradually lowering ceil-
ings, rain showers, or whiteout conditions in which you will lose sight of the ground
with little or no warning. DO NOT DESCEND to regain visual contact with the ground.
If inadvertent IMC is encountered at low level, consider this an emergency procedure.
a.
Immediately convert to instruments;
b.
Roll wings level, if turning, on the EADI;
c.
Select FULL power and establish a positive climb to get away from the low
level environment; and
d.
Climb to your route safety altitude.
CAUTION
Under no circumstances are you to descend below your route safety-
height while trying to maintain visual contact with the ground.
82.
If you are unable to continue VFR, contact an ATC agency for clearance to climb
and, if necessary, make an IFR recovery. Remember that at low altitude, or even at your
safety altitude, you may be unable to contact ATC without first climbing to an even
higher altitude.

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 20 =====
83.
On occasion, during an RTB or MDR leg, you may encounter localized weather
phenomena, i.e. isolated thunderstorms, that require you to alter heading but not neces-
sarily abort the mission. On such occasions you should employ the “dogleg” procedure
as described in Chapter 9 of the MPN. This procedure may be modified by the inclusion
of a leg parallel to the original track, or it may be adapted to fly from one leg to another
if the weather is unsuitable at a turn point.
84.
Good airmanship and judgment dictate the course of action to take; in any case,
make your decision early. A press-on regardless attitude in an area of descending ceil-
ings and poor visibility is not only foolish but could result in the loss of a pilot and
aircraft – experienced pilots in the past have committed this fatal error.
10.12
MISSION PLANNING GUIDE
85.
All low-level Enroute maps will be annotated as follows:
a.
Turn Points. Indicated by BLACK circles centered over the geographical
point.
They are sequentially numbered in GREEN. A small BLACK dot
will indicate the exact turn point (Figure 10.7).
Figure 10.7: Turn Point
b.
Hack. Shown by BLACK squares centered over easily identifiable geograph-
ical points, indicating the start or restart of timing. A small BLACK dot will
indicate the exact turn point (Figure 10.8).
Figure 10.8: Hack
c.
Target. Marked with a BLACK equilateral triangle centered over the geo-
graphic centre of the target. A small BLACK dot will indicate the exact turn
point(Figure 10.9).
d.
Track Line. Joins the turn points, IP and target in BLACK. The lines may be
offset with a 2 “G” turn radius; however, turns of less than 90 degrees should
not require any appreciable turn radius depicted on your map. Turn radii for
larger turns should be no more than ½ NM (Figure 10.10).

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 21 =====
Figure 10.9: Target
Figure 10.10: Track Lines
e.
Dog Box. Drawn in black and placed near the track at the beginning of each
leg, containing the information needed to fly the leg. This format should be
followed (Figure 10.11).
Figure 10.11: Dog Box
f.
Fuel Figures. Fuel figures are often shown in RED and include Bingo and
required enroute fuel (Figure 10.12).
Figure 10.12: Fuel Figures
g.
Distance to Go Marks. Drawn from the next turning point back on track at
10 NM intervals on the left side of track in BLUE (Figure 10.13).
Figure 10.13: Distance to Go Marks
h.
Timing Marks. Drawn in BLACK at each minute of elapsed time from the

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 22 =====
Hack. Accumulated time is shown to the nearest second at the right of the
hack, turn points, and target in BLACK (Figure 10.14).
Figure 10.14: Timings Marks
i.
Hazards. Dashed RED lines are used to highlight power lines. Single ob-
structions (i.e.: towers) are marked with a RED inverted cone. Normally
these hazards are marked on the map by the mapping software (Figure 10.15).
Figure 10.15: Hazards
j.
Restricted Areas/Points. Restricted areas will be outlined in RED and Red
diagonal lines will indicate restricted airspace. Restricted points/sensitive
citizens will be circled in RED (1 NM radius). Turkey I Fur Farms will be
circled in GREEN (2 NM radius). Airfields will be circled in BLUE (2 NM
radius) (Figure 10.16).
Figure 10.16: Restricted Areas
k.
Checkpoint Markings. The marking of checkpoints is optional on the low-
level route map. If used, a line perpendicular to the track line shall extend
from the checkpoint to the right side of the track line. The exact time for
the checkpoint shall be shown in a rectangular block. The checkpoint will
be annotated in such a way as to not obscure any required detail on the route
map (Figure 10.17).
l.
Hack Timings. Timings from departure to the hack shall be indicated on
either side of the track line with timings at the hack indicated on either side
of the hack box (Figure 10.18)
10.13
ROUTE STUDY
86.
Because of the reduced field of view at low-level, route study is very important.
As mentioned in the Map Reading Fundamentals, Anticipation begins during the route

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 23 =====
Figure 10.17: Checkpoints
Figure 10.18: Hack Timings
study. Route study is simply mentally flying the route before you are airborne. Consider
the angle from which you will observe features along the route and how they should
appear when you encounter them. You should emphasize the following points in your
route study:
a.
Departure Route. When planning your departure, ensure local restrictions
and other factors such as altitude, weather, traffic areas, initial track align-
ment, etc., are taken into considerations.
b.
The Hack. Study the hack closely and be able to identify it with little refer-
ence to the map. Pre-determine the altitude at which you cross the hack by
adding your proposed height above ground to the elevation at the hack as de-
termined from your map. To a large extent your hack procedures determine
how smoothly low-level navigation will proceed.
c.
Track Angle Departure Checks. At the hack and each turn point you are
required to carry out a “Track Angle Departure Check”. This is accom-
plished by comparing the track being flown with prominent features along
the planned track to confirm the aircraft is headed in the correct direction.
Prominent features to be used should be selected during the route study.
d.
Enroute Terrain. Determine the general relief characteristics along your
route such as flat, gently rolling or sharp hills. Consider natural features such
as lakes and rivers. Note that some may have seasonal variation or be more
difficult to see under the cover of winter snow.
e.
Forecast Winds. Assess the forecast wind direction in relation to your over-
all route. The aim is to obtain a general idea of how the forecast winds will

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 24 =====
affect your base speed and heading on each leg. You are making this deter-
mination based on forecast winds. Remember that the actual winds are often
not as forecast (either speed or direction). Therefore, you must have a solid
grasp on assessing the triangle of velocities and know how to adjust your
base speed and drift based upon actual wind velocity encountered en route.
f.
Altitude. It is quite difficult to maintain a specified altitude by visual ref-
erence with only limited experience; therefore, you may wish to determine
your desired altitude at the Hack and at appropriate points enroute on your
map (i.e.: where altitude changes 200 to 300 ft). It is quite simple to deter-
mine the required altitude by referencing spot altitudes or contour lines near
your track and adding your planned height above ground. Use these approx-
imate altitudes as a guide only as you strive to develop the appropriate sight
picture for the desired altitude. The sooner you accomplish this, the easier
your navigation will go.
g.
Checkpoints. Study each leg in order to identify potential checkpoints along
the route. The length of the leg and availability of unique features determines
how many potential checkpoints you may have. Refer to Chapter 12 of the
MPN for information on checkpoint selection. It is good practice to select
potential checkpoints approximately midway between turn points/target, but
not closer than 3 minutes before turn points and target. Checkpoints are used
to verify track and timing, and allow you to concentrate on acquiring the
target or turn point. The marking of checkpoints on the route map is not
mandatory; however, if you choose to do so, checkpoints shall be annotated
in accordance with the procedures outlined in Chapter 12 of the MPN. Study
the features for each potential checkpoint that make it unique. Plan to use
large, easily identifiable features to locate smaller, more exact features. Some
checkpoints may only be accurate for checking time (i.e. crossing a straight
road at 90 degrees). Other checkpoints may be accurate for determining track
only (i.e. next to a line-type feature). A good checkpoint is one that has more
than one feature associated in order to more precisely check track and time.
h.
Turning Points. Since you will be flying heads-up, you must be very familiar
with what you will see and how you will identify each turning point. Note
the features you will employ in carrying out a Track Angle Departure Check
(don’t be tempted to use only the ‘section lines’ as they are normally evident
in 4 directions).
i.
Target. Reaching the target is the ultimate goal of your low-level mission.
You must be aware of what distinct features to expect during the final run-in
as well as those at the target and its immediate environment. If available,
reconnaissance photos can be used for this.
j.
Return to Base (RTB). Your navigation mission does not end at the target.
An RTB plan is essential to successfully complete the mission. You must
familiarize yourself with the route and select an appropriate cruising altitude

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 25 =====
for the RTB. You should also consider your options for rejoining the traffic
pattern at your destination as detailed in the destination procedures. It should
be efficient, safe and comply with local flying orders.
87.
Do a final route study before commencing your trip, paying particular attention to
your departure plan and hack point features. Once satisfied that you are fully prepared,
fill out a flight plan and file it with ATC. One last check of weather and NOTAMS prior
to flight is suggested to ensure conditions have not changed since your initial briefing.
10.14
BRIEFING THE ROUTE
88.
In future flying training, your target run-in procedures will become more complex
depending on mission profile. Therefore, providing a detailed image of the target and a
target briefing will be introduced to you now.
89.
The detail on the low-level map you create is minimal and so a more detailed image
of the target will be required. In the future, this may be provided to you through recon-
naissance imagery. For now, you will use open source imagery (e.g. Google Earth).
The example below shows how to take the information from your map and develop the
TGT imagery and required information. The required information on the TGT imagery
product is as follows:
a.
Image: Ensure image includes target and any lead-in features that will be
useful in identifying the target.
b.
Orientation: orientate your snip of the Google image track up.
c.
Title: map title, target description, time on target.
d.
North reference: include in one of the upper corners.
e.
Fuel: include in red near the TGT.
f.
TGT triangle and track: include in a high contrast color.
g.
Dog Houses and ingress/egress tracks: include on the either side of track,
provided that it does not cover pertinent map features.
90.
During the mission briefing you will provide the instructor with a brief of the TGT
utilizing the TGT imagery. The brief shall cover at a minimum the target feature, the
direction (track) you will be arriving to the target from, your time on target, and your
egress heading.

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 26 =====
Figure 10.19: Example Target Brief Card

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 27 =====
CHAPTER 11: SYSTEMS NAVIGATION
11.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
Once you have learned to perform Visual Navigation, as described Chapter 10,
you will learn to incorporate GPS in your workflow. Systems navigation involves us-
ing GPS information to increase your capacity for other critical tasks in the low level
environment, such as lookout and aircraft control.
2.
You began navigation training concentrating on developing basic map reading
skills and sound navigation procedures. The Harvard II has some integrated onboard
systems (specifically GPS) which, when used in conjunction with these basic naviga-
tion skills, allow for extremely accurate navigation and timing. Once you have demon-
strated proficiency in the basics of visual navigation, you will begin to integrate the use
of the aircraft systems into your navigation skills. This type of navigation is commonly
referred to as Systems Navigation. Always remember that the visual navigation proce-
dures you have been introduced to form the basis for any systems navigation, regardless
of the type of aircraft you are flying or the mission you are accomplishing.
3.
The primary aircraft system in the Harvard II used to assist in visual navigation is
the GPS. The hack, turn points, and target can be pre-programmed into the GPS (250
user-defined points available). The route can be entered in sequence as a flight planned
route (25 flight planned routes available of up to 30 waypoints each), and timing rela-
tionships can be determined (ETE and actual timings). Extremely accurate information
will be presented to you throughout the trip.
4.
You have already learned GPS operations during your ground school lectures and
have applied them during some of your instrument flying. The procedures used for
visual navigation are no different, but these procedures will be thoroughly reviewed
prior to any systems navigation mission. Since there are 250 user-defined waypoints
available, all of the appropriate navigation points can easily be entered and stored in
the GPS under a predetermined identifier. As part of your pre-flight planning, have the
identifiers recorded on a log card in the appropriate sequence for the route so they can
be easily entered into one of the available flight plans in the GPS. It is a good idea to
169

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 28 =====
have the latitude and longitude, or radial/DME relationship from a known nav-aid for
each point recorded as well, just in case the point is not stored in the GPS’s database.
5.
Additional time on the ground with the aircraft running will be required to program
the route into the GPS. Plan for this accordingly. GPS programming can take place
during the taxi, but caution and vigilance should prevail whenever devoting time inside
the cockpit during ground operations, especially when moving.
11.2
ENTERING A FLIGHT PLAN (GPS)
6.
To create a flight plan, rotate the left outer knob to select the FPL pages. Rotate
the left inner knob to select the first available empty flight plan page. If no empty pages
are available, an existing flight plan must be deleted. Once a flight plan page is avail-
able, press the left CRSR button. The cursor will be positioned over the first waypoint
position. Enter the departure point first using the left outer and inner knobs to select and
then set the characters of the identifier. Once satisfied with the departure point, press
the ENT button to accept the first waypoint and the waypoint page will be displayed on
the right side. If the waypoint is incorrect, press the CLR button to delete the choice
and begin again. If a non-defined waypoint has been entered, the create waypoint page
will be displayed. Create the waypoint if required. To accept the waypoint, press the
ENT button. The cursor will move to select the second waypoint. Repeat the above
procedure for all waypoints for the flight plan, including the destination. Rotate the left
outer knob clockwise to scroll through the flight plan waypoints and select USE. Press
the left CRSR button to deactivate the cursor function and complete the flight plan.
7.
To select the numbered flight plan you have just programmed, use the left outer
knob to select the FPL pages, and then use the left inner knob to scroll through the flight
plan list to the desired flight plan. Press the left CRSR button and the cursor will appear
over the USE prompt. Press the ENT button to accept and activate the flight plan in
normal order. The selected flight plan is now FPL 0, the active flight plan.
8.
Setting the GPS for Systems Navigation (Normal Operations). Prior to take-
off, activate the desired flight plan as described above. There are a number of pages
in the GPS that can display the information required in order to integrate the GPS into
the navigation mission. When in MAP mode on the EHSI most of this information is
also displayed on the EHSI. During your training, the minimum amount of information
required for systems is as follows:
a.
groundspeed;
b.
active leg of the flight plan;
c.
desired track (DTK) for the active leg;
d.
actual track (TK);
e.
distance to go to the selected steer-to waypoint (DIS); and

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 29 =====
f.
time-to-go to the selected steer-to waypoint (ETE).
9.
Your instructor will point out various combinations of displays that will provide
the above information to you. Once the appropriate GPS pages are selected, select GPS
as the primary source on the EFIS control panel for display on the EHSI and select
MAP mode on the EHSI. This will provide GPS accurate steering information to each
waypoint as the flight plan progresses as well as show distance, time-to-go (TTG) and
groundspeed information on the EHSI. As flight plan waypoints are reached, the active
leg symbol automatically shifts to the appropriate from-and-to waypoints.
11.3
AIRBORNE PROCEDURES – SYSTEM NAVIGATION
10.
Systems Navigation Hack Procedure. The procedure for hacking during systems
navigation is similar to the procedure used during visual navigation, described in Chap-
ter 10. During visual navigation, you identified the hack prior to commencing either an
offset or racetrack hack procedure. With GPS assistance, you may commence the hack
run-in procedures prior to visual identification of the hack. This permits a longer and
more stable run-in to the hack.
11.
En-route to the hack, follow the procedures set out in Chapter 10. During visual
navigation, departure routes from the runway to the hack were included to provide a
means of navigating to the hack without the use of the GPS. Ideally, in systems naviga-
tion, departure legs will still be included to provide a means of verifying time and fuel to
the hack. When operating at aerodromes other than Moose Jaw however, the departure
legs on the map may be omitted, as the runway-in-use or local departure procedures
are unpredictable. In this case, follow the issued departure instructions, and when able,
proceed to the hack using the GPS. Ensure the GPS is set up to provide the most SA
during the hack run-in and follow-on low level navigation legs. One recommended set
up is as follows:
a.
EADI: set to composite mode in order to have CDI bar information.
b.
EHSI: set to map mode in order to provide a birds eye view which aids in
manoeuvering the aircraft for the run-in.
c.
Primary navigation source: GPS.
d.
Course: with the hack as the active waypoint, select OBS on the GPS and
with the EFIS course knob set the track for the first leg between the hack and
the first turn point.
e.
Scale: set the range on the EFIS panel to 10 NM scale.
12.
With the kit set up, commence the run-in procedure. Position the aircraft on an ap-
propriate run-in to the hack such that the run-in avoids any restricted areas, and provides
time to stabilize on heading, altitude, and airspeed (HAA).

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 30 =====
13.
Once established on HAA, visually identify the hack and reset your clock. De-
select OBS mode. Crossing the hack, call over the intercom “PREPARE TO HACK,
HACK NOW”, and start the clock. Conduct a 5T check once past the hack. Verify that
the GPS is functioning as expected. A common error is to leave OBS mode selected so
that the hack remains the active waypoint.
Figure 11.1: Systems Navigation Hack GPS EHSI Indications
14.
GPS-Map-Ground. Once airborne complete the mission using the approved meth-
ods of map reading (i.e. Orientation, Anticipation, Confirmation, Pinpoint) and employ-
ing sound navigation procedures. The watch portion of Watch-Map-Ground remains
virtually the same, but can be modified based upon the information available to you.
GPS will be your primary means of anticipation. This is accomplished by verifying
your elapsed time with distance to go. Since low-level navigation is flown at 240 knots
groundspeed (4 NM per minute), timing is easily verified as distance decreases through
multiples of 4. Extrapolate your position to your map and confirm the GPS accuracy us-
ing visual references. If the position is accurate continue, anticipating follow-on points
based upon GPS information available. If the position is inaccurate (highly unlikely),
fall back onto basic navigation skills until the nature of the discrepancy can be deter-
mined.
15.
Enroute Procedures. Fly the pre-determined IAS for the planned groundspeed,
but adjust as soon as you see a variance from the desired groundspeed on your EHSI/GPS.
This will virtually eliminate any timing errors almost immediately. If any errors do de-
velop, correct them using the approved method previously used. Adjust your speed to
attain the desired groundspeed once the error has been corrected. Once a turn is com-
plete, adjust your IAS to attain the desired groundspeed for the next leg.
16.
Heading control is just as important as previously emphasized. Fly your pre-

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 31 =====
Figure 11.2: Systems Navigation GPS EHSI Indications
determined heading, but monitor your track as indicated on the GPS. Adjust your head-
ing to maintain the desired track as soon as a deviation occurs and attempt to confirm
the accuracy of the GPS as soon as able. Once GPS accuracy is determined, continue to
fly the heading required to maintain the pre-determined and actual track as displayed on
your GPS and/or EHSI. Continue to use visual corrections to large, easily identifiable
features as before, reinforcing the need for accurate map reading skills.
17.
As you become proficient at systems navigation you will find that you have more
time to devote to the low-level crosscheck. Take this opportunity to refine and improve
your crosscheck (lookout, altitude control, etc.). Never forget the basics of visual navi-
gation and continue to employ these skills throughout all aspects of systems navigation.
You may be required to rely solely on basic navigation procedures if a malfunction
occurs with your aircraft equipment.
11.4
DEVIATION AND CORRECTION TO TRACK
18.
There are multiple reasons why you may decide to deviate from the planned track.
Situations may arise such as weather, unplanned targets of opportunity, or time correc-
tion doglegs that would necessitate a deviation from track. Being able to take advantage
of the kit to accurately and easily accomplish this task is a desired skill set of military
pilots, and can be accomplished at any flight altitude.
Figure 11.3: Parallelogram
19.
Whenever you make the decision to deviate from track you must ensure that you
consider all threats to your plan. When at lower levels, this will include items such as

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 32 =====
towers, sensitive citizens, fur farms, etc. The following procedures will expand your
capabilities while flying visual navigation.
a.
Weather Deviations: if you have to manoeuvre around upcoming weather,
an effective procedure is to create a parallelogram. At some point along the
current leg you will deviate off-route taking up a heading that will equate to
the track of the next leg. When the weather obstacle has passed return to a
heading that will equate to the track of the previous leg. Upon achieving the
original track line, resume normal navigation. Understand that you need to
eliminate the timing error that was induced due to the extra turn.
b.
Dogleg: another method to avoid weather is to use a dogleg as per the Manual
of Pilot Navigation. Unfortunately, this will induce a timing error as per
Table 11.1. It can also be used to remove a timing error if you find yourself
early on your nav route. The rules of thumb in Table 11.1 can be used.
Degrees Off Track
Time Error Induced
60 deg
Same as one leg
45 deg
½ time of one leg
30 deg
¼ time of one leg
Table 11.1: Dogleg Time Errors
e.g. if you deviate off track by 30 degrees for one minute and then turn back
to track with a 30° intercept, when you get back to track you will have a 15
second error.
c.
Targets of Opportunity: the parallelogram can also be used to good effect
to quickly deviate off-track to attack a target of opportunity. Use the same
procedure as above but select your time/DME to deviate based upon flying a
parallel track to the original track to over-fly the target. By using the parallel-
ogram you will be able to quickly resume your navigation while minimizing
time errors.
11.5
SYSTEMS INTEGRATION MDR
20.
During basic navigation the MDR was conducted visually using the procedures
described in Chapter 10 and without use of any GPS assistance. During Phase 3 you
will have opportunity to augment your MDR with GPS information in the following
manner.
21.
An MDR or diversion can become simplified when the GPS is incorporated into
the planning phase. As in low-level systems navigation, the GPS does not replace tra-
ditional navigation. You will employ conventional MDR procedures using the GPS to
supplement, thus minimizing planning time and complexity. Your instructor will pro-
vide you with a scenario and a target for the MDR. You will be required to analyze the

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 33 =====
situation (current position, nature of the scenario, restriction/weather en route to target,
etc.) and plan your MDR accordingly. You may employ any technique taught in using
the GPS for assistance, but remember that aircraft control and lookout are paramount
during the planning portion of the MDR. Extra vigilance and caution must be exercised
when programming the GPS airborne.
22.
If planning an MDR to a point that is not in the navigation database, you may create
a user-defined point. This can be entered in any number of ways; including RAD/DME
from a nav-aid or from any other defined waypoint in the database or manually enter-
ing a Lat/Long. Another technique which may be used is selecting direct to a defined
waypoint and flying an offset.
23.
En route, conventional MDR procedures shall be employed with the GPS being
used to improve accuracy and SA. You can choose the airspeed/groundspeed and al-
titude at which to fly the MDR (based upon your scenario) and the time update can
be completed via the GPS time-to-go or conventional means. At all times you will be
expected to use the GPS as a guide while continuing to employ map reading skills to
confirm en route tracking and positive identification of the target.

===== SMM part 4 PDF page 34 =====
CHAPTER 12: BASIC FORMATION FLYING
12.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
This chapter describes Harvard II formation flying; however, the principles ap-
ply equally to all forms of formation flying (i.e. helicopter, transport, or fighter-type
aircraft). Formation flying provides mutual support in a combat scenario and enables
a maximum number of aircraft to be launched or recovered. This chapter is devoted
to basic two-plane sequences with three- and four-plane formation covered in a later
chapter.
2.
Formation flying, more than any other type of flying, provides the best environ-
ment for building confidence, teaching self-reliance and self-discipline, and teaching
the proper application of aggressiveness in military flying. As a formation member, you
must be disciplined and alert, and you must trust your leader. Your reactions must be
quick and precise. Formation flying is comparatively simple once you have mastered a
few fundamentals.
3.
Formation flying can be tiring; you must be relaxed and at ease in order to fly
formation well. In the early stages, make a conscious effort to relax. If you find yourself
over-controlling, move out, relax, re-trim, and then move back in to the correct position.
12.2
FORMATION VISUAL CUES
4.
There are certain visual cues that are foundational for an effective formation cross-
check, whether flying in close formation, tactical formation, or rejoining. Understand-
ing the following cues as both concepts and terminology will improve your awareness
in formation and allow you to communicate clearly with your instructor.
5.
Closure. Closure is simply a measure of how fast you are reducing or increasing
the range to another aircraft. The highest closure rate you could have on another aircraft
would be when both are approaching head on. When pursuing the target, assess closure
by monitoring the rate of change of the size of the target aircraft. If the aircraft is getting
big quickly then closure is high. If the aircraft is not changing in size very quickly then
closure is low. Unfortunately, when assessing closure in this way the transition from
low closure rates to high closure rates is often subtle, so closure must constantly be
176

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 1 =====
monitored. In close formation, early recognition of subtle closure can greatly assist in
preventing position errors from developing and growing.
6.
Aspect Angle (AA). Aspect Angle indicates your relative position from a line that
extends through the other aircraft’s longitudinal axis. The aspect angle always relates
to the target aircraft and does not depend on your heading. If the target is heading away
from you — low aspect. If the target is heading towards you — high aspect. When
discussing rejoins, a slightly higher aspect than ideal is referred to as “hot” where a
lower than ideal aspect is called “cold”.
7.
Range. Range is simply the distance to the other aircraft. You can assess range
by considering the size of the other aircraft in your canopy. Note the size of the aircraft
compared to features in the cockpit (e.g. distance between warning lights) to help assess
range against known distances.
8.
Line of Sight (LOS). From Chapter 2, line of sight is a measure of how fast the
target aircraft is moving across your windscreen. If the target is moving relatively slowly
then there is a low line of sight. If the target is moving relatively fast then there is a high
line of sight. If maintaining a constant range while remaining on the turn circle of the
target, then the line of sight is zero.
9.
Heading Crossing Angle (HCA). HCA is the angular difference between your
aircraft’s heading and the other aircraft’s heading.
Figure 12.1: Aspect Angle
12.3
BASIC FORMATION
10.
The basic formation positions described here are:
a.
echelon right;
b.
echelon left;
c.
line astern; and
d.
route.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 2 =====
Figure 12.2: Heading crossing Angle
NOTE
All references and figures are relative to a pilot occupying the front seat.
12.4
ECHELON LEFT AND RIGHT
11.
In the echelon left position, number two (also referred to in this publication as the
wingman) is on the left wing of the lead aircraft. The proper position for echelon left is
illustrated in Figures 12.3 and 12.4 and the references are as follows:
a.
Lateral. The wingman lines up the leading edge of the lead’s wing tip with
the propeller spinner of the lead’s aircraft.
b.
Longitudinal. The wingman aligns abeam the rudder hinge line of the lead’s
aircraft.
c.
Vertical. The wing-tip on the spinner provides a good vertical reference.
12.
In the echelon right position, number two is on the right wing of the lead aircraft.
The references are the same as echelon left.
12.5
LINE ASTERN
13.
Number two is directly behind and below the lead aircraft. Nose–tail longitudinal
clearance is approximately 10’, with number two’s vertical stabilizer below lead’s prop
wash and exhaust. The proper position for line astern is illustrated in Figures 12.5 and
12.6 and the references used are:
a.
Lateral. The wingman lines up with the centerline of lead’s aircraft.
b.
Longitudinal. The lead’s wings should fill the windscreen to ensure nose–
tail longitudinal clearance of approximately 10’ (Figures 12.5 and 12.6).

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 3 =====
Figure 12.3: Front Seat Echelon References
Figure 12.4: Rear Seat Echelon References
c.
Vertical. The top of the windscreen cuts the lead’s vertical stabilizer ap-
proximately one third above the empennage; this should coincide with the
placement of the tip of the VHF Antenna flush with the underbelly of the
aircraft. (This view may vary somewhat due to seating position.)

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 4 =====
Figure 12.5: Front Seat Line Astern References
Figure 12.6: Rear Seat Line Astern References
12.6
ROUTE
14.
The purpose of the route formation is to allow the wingman a sufficient margin
of safety to complete cockpit checks. With the formation in route, the lead will have
restricted manoeuvring, and consequently, if greater manoeuvrability is required, a dif-
ferent position should be selected.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 5 =====
15.
Route formation is flown on the extended wing-tip reference line at a distance of
one to three wingspans (Figures 12.7 and 12.8).
Figure 12.7: Front Seat Route References
12.7
INTRAFLIGHT COMMUNICATIONS
16.
When flying formation, the key to success is effective communication between all
formation members. Both formation signals and R/T may be used for communication.
While formation signals provide a tactical advantage of maintaining radio silence, the
use of radios normally provide the most accurate means of communication. In the early
stages of training, R/T will likely be used more often to promote clarity. As experience
is gained, formation signals should be used to the greatest degree practical.
17.
There is limitless information that can be relayed within the formation (i.e. current
weather at a destination aerodrome or conflicting traffic in the area). The intent of this
section is not to discuss all the possible calls, but rather the desired procedures when
using the radios. A table containing a list of formation signals can be found in Section
12.40.
18.
R/T procedures used during formation flights are designed to minimize radio traf-
fic while ensuring all necessary information is passed on. This requires clear and con-
cise R/T calls using a standardized format. Several sections within this chapter contain

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 6 =====
Figure 12.8: Rear Seat Route References
examples of the standard R/T procedures to be used during training. While these ex-
amples provide guidelines, they do not address all potential situations. As a formation
member you will be required to make radio calls “unrehearsed”. When in this situation,
remember to be brief while ensuring the intent of the message is clear.
19.
The formation call signs and numbering used in Harvard operations are similar
to that used within the fighter force. The formation will be named (e.g. “YODAS”)
and a two digit series will be assigned to each formation. The lead will assume the
first number (such as 41) and each wingman thereafter will be numbered sequentially.
When R/T calls require a response from other aircraft within the formation, such as
a formation change outside close formation, the initial call will include the formation
call-sign, however, the aircraft responding will only acknowledge with their position,
i.e. Lead — “YODAS ECHELON LEFT”, Wingman — “42”. This specifies which
formation is being directed, yet avoids repetition, thus freeing up the radios.
20.
Directive calls within the formation will normally only be initiated by the lead
aircraft. Directive calls made by lead may be to the formation or to individual aircraft.
In most cases, the call will simply include the action to be taken. i.e. “BLACKS,
ECHELON LEFT”. Commands for ancillary selection will contain the command “GO”
and may be repeated in order to allow the wingman to ensure speed restrictions are
adhered to for their aircraft prior to ancillary selection, i.e. “BLACKS, LANDING
GEAR, LANDING GEAR — GO”. When directed to perform a task, the wingman

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 7 =====
is expected to display acknowledgement of the direction. During formation changes
or ancillary selections, this is accomplished by the movement of the aircraft or by the
ancillary movement. Only when directed to perform a task which is not apparent to
lead, will the wingman acknowledge over the radios.
21.
Although directive calls are normally initiated by lead, other aircraft in the forma-
tion may become directive if required for flight safety reasons (traffic, etc.). If a wing-
man is in a situation which requires a directive call, it shall also include a description of
what necessitated the transmission (e.g. “BLACKS, PULL UP, PULL UP, TRAFFIC 1
O’CLOCK LEVEL 1 MILE”).
12.8
GROUND HANDLING
22.
The formation should start up and taxi together. This maintains formation integrity
and keeps the fuel endurance of participating aircraft relatively equal. If all aircraft in the
formation are parked together, engines may be started on a visual signal; if the aircraft
are parked separately, a start time may be given. The lead should be advised as soon
as possible if any start delays are anticipated. Do not omit checklist items or attempt to
rush start procedures if you are delayed. If you are unable to join the formation before
take-off, proceed to the briefed rendezvous or abort the mission.
12.9
RADIO CHECK-IN
23.
Once all aircraft have started, and after receiving ATIS, each pilot should switch
radios to the pre-briefed formation frequencies. Lead will initiate a check-in on the
Primary radio and after the wingman responds lead will initiate a check-in on the Aux-
iliary radio. Follow the procedure set out in the 2 CFFTS Flying Orders to accomplish
the radio check-in.
24.
If a change from a memory or pre-set to a manual frequency must be made, lead
will make the appropriate call, i.e. “YODAS PUSH 283.7” and allow an adequate inter-
val before checking the formation in on the new intraflight frequency. Always go to the
directed frequency even if it may be the wrong one, and let the lead make any required
corrections.
12.10
TAXIING
25.
The formation taxies to the take-off position as a unit. The wingman maintains five
aircraft-length separation in the ramp area directly behind lead and three aircraft-length
separation on taxiways in a staggered formation with each aircraft occupying one side
of the taxiway (Figures 12.9 and 12.10). Lead will normally be on the downwind side
of taxiways to minimize the wingman’s exposure to the lead’s exhaust.
26.
The lead obtains any clearance for the formation required for crossing runways
and lining up for take-off.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 8 =====
Figure 12.9: Ramp Spacing
Figure 12.10: Taxiway Spacing
27.
The over-speed governor check will be accomplished as a unit as follows: lead
will ensure he has turned enough to allow following aircraft to park beside him during
their checks. Each aircraft will perform individual checks in a normal manner and once
complete, number 2 aircraft will give a head nod at which point lead will continue
taxiing.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 9 =====
28.
During the pre-take-off check, select anti-collision lights off as you turn the land-
ing light on. The anti-collision lights are very distracting during formation and should
remain off until after landing, at which point they are selected on during the post-landing
check.
12.11
FORMATION TAKE-OFF
29.
During your training you will be performing two-plane formation take-offs. During
a formation take-off varying degrees of lateral separation may be used dependent on the
situation. During your training you will normally line up centered on your half of the
runway abeam the hinge reference on the lead aircraft. As you gain experience, you
may be shown a tighter line-up using echelon references.
12.12
LINE-UP
30.
After obtaining clearance, the lead taxies onto the runway with the wingman lining
up as briefed (normally on the upwind side of the lead). On a 150-foot wide runway,
lead will normally line up on the downwind half of the runway. The wingman will
then move into position lining up on their half of the runway and abeam lead’s hinge
reference. The wingman should ensure that, as the aircraft moves into position on the
runway, the nose wheel is straight by using the nose-wheel steering.
12.13
RUN-UP
31.
When in position, the aircraft are to be held with the brakes and the nose-wheel
steering must be disengaged. The wingman will give a head nod to lead when ready for
run-up. When the lead gives the run-up signal, both aircraft slowly advance their PCL to
30% torque and complete their take-off check. After the take-off check is complete, the
lead will look at the wingman. The wingman nods, signifying he is ready for take-off. If
the runway is slippery and the aircraft cannot be held stationary at 30% torque, reduce
torque and inform the lead. This may require that the formation complete an interval
take-off, joining up in the climb.
12.14
INTERVAL TAKE-OFF
32.
An interval take-off may be performed when desired or when crosswinds, runway,
or weather conditions make the wing take-off hazardous. It is simply a normal take-
off performed by each aircraft in interval. Lead will signal the run-up check, and once
completed, the wingman will confirm that the check is done by directing a head nod
towards lead. The formation lead will then commence their take-off roll. Unless other-
wise briefed, the second aircraft delays the take-off roll for a minimum of five seconds
behind the lead aircraft. Lead flies a normal take-off, and after achieving 180 KIAS,
reduces torque to approximately 80%. Alternatively, after completing the post-take-off

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 10 =====
check, lead reduces torque to approximately 80%, and continues to accelerate to 180
KIAS. This combined torque setting and airspeed will allow the wingman to achieve
sufficient overtake to accomplish the join-up as described in Section 12.23.
12.15
TAKE-OFF ABORT
33.
It is impossible to consider all potential emergency situations that may occur dur-
ing take-off, or even to state when a pilot should abort or continue the take-off. The
decision to abort rests with the pilot and depends upon the nature of the problem, speed,
position, and other aircraft on the runway. The same factors apply to individual aircraft
in formation as for a single aircraft. In formation, however, there will be less time to
assess the situation. The lead aircraft also has the additional responsibility of deciding
if and when to abort the entire formation.
34.
If lead requires the formation to abort, the radio call will be directive, i.e. “BLACKS,
ABORT, ABORT, ABORT”. In this situation, all aircraft must abort their take-off roll
immediately and bring their aircraft to a stop, again maintaining their section of the
runway if able. If performing an interval take-off, an abort by an aircraft requires all
trailing aircraft to abort also.
12.16
FORMATION CLIMB
35.
The lead should attempt to climb with as high a torque setting as possible while
continually monitoring the wingman to ensure that there is sufficient torque to maintain
the echelon position (approximately 85–90%). Adjustment of the power during the
climb should be smooth to allow the wingman to maintain position.
12.17
IN-FLIGHT CHECKS
36.
When directed to complete a cockpit check, unless otherwise briefed, the wing-
man acknowledges the instruction by moving to the route position. After the check is
completed, the wingman will not assume a close echelon position until directed to do so
by the formation lead. While in the route position during in-flight checks, the wingman
is still responsible for maintaining formation. This position should be cross-checked
between each item of the cockpit check. The lead should not plan to make turns or
initiate climbs or descents during in-flight checks. If the lead is forced to turn while the
formation is carrying out a check, the lead shall call the turn: “BLACKS — TURN-
ING RIGHT”. The wingman will resume the check when the formation is once again
established wings level.
37.
As a general practice, cockpit checks will not be completed while a formation is in
cloud. The formation lead will delay checks until clear of cloud. If this is not practical,
the wingman must maintain echelon position and accomplish check items individually
while devoting as much time as possible to maintaining the correct formation position.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 11 =====
During the RTB, it is also the wingman’s responsibility to prepare for the intended
approach by setting up the appropriate navigation aids and approach plates in the event
of a requirement for lost wingman procedures.
38.
During any station change, there exists an opportunity for the wingman to quickly
complete an abbreviated in-flight check while in the “corners.” Given that the formation
is not manoeuvrable during the station change, it is important that if this opportunity is
taken that the checks are timely in fashion and do not increase the overall duration of
the task.
12.18
STATION KEEPING
39.
To help you maintain station, standard terms to correct errors in position will be
used. They are:
a.
Longitudinal: “Forward” or “Back”
b.
Vertical: “Up” or “Down”
c.
Lateral: “In” or “Out”
d.
Line Astern: “Right” or “Left”
40.
In essence, formation flying is station keeping. As you know, the Harvard II is
a stable aircraft which, when properly trimmed, will remain in a given attitude with-
out deviation. (Note: due to wing tip vortices, a lateral trim change will be felt when
moving in or out of echelon. Also, longitudinal trim will vary when in line astern.)
Remember that there is a slight lag between a PCL movement and the corresponding
aircraft response; therefore, you must anticipate this lag. Initially, you will likely find
that you will be making almost continuous torque changes to maintain position on the
lead. As you become more experienced, you will be able to anticipate many of the lead’s
movements and you will find yourself making small adjustments to maintain position.
Make all adjustments as soon as you detect the need, so that the change will be small;
the longer the delay, the greater the amount of adjustment, thus the longer the time to
regain position. Once you are in the correct position, no control movements are nec-
essary, and with experience the control movements will become control pressures. Pay
close attention to the tips your instructor will give you regarding station keeping. If you
are in doubt about any point, ask questions.
12.19
TURNS
41.
When the lead turns away from the wingman while in echelon formation, the turn
is “stepped up”. When the direction of turn is into the wingman, the turn is “stepped
down”. While maintaining position relative to the lead, the wingman initially climbs or
descends slightly as bank is changed.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 12 =====
42.
When your aircraft is on the outside of a turn (stepped up), an increase in torque
is required on the entry, and a decrease in torque will be necessary on the rollout. Con-
versely, on the inside of a turn (stepped down) you must decrease torque on the roll-in
and increase it on the rollout. These PCL changes are normally quite small, and as
your proficiency increases, you will be able to anticipate both the rate and amount of
adjustment required.
43.
The most common error during a turn is failing to match the lead’s rate of roll. In
almost all cases, the wingman lags behind the lead’s rate of roll, and therefore tends to
slide wide during a stepped up turn, and to slide towards the lead during a stepped down
turn.
Figure 12.11: Echelon Right Stepped Up
12.20
STATION CHANGES
44.
Wingmen change station only on command from the lead, either by visual signal
or R/T. When changing station, the following rules apply to all wingmen:
a.
when moving fore and aft, always maintain adequate wing-tip clearance; and
b.
when moving across the lead’s flight path, always cross below and behind
the lead while maintaining the prescribed vertical and nose–tail minimum
clearance.
NOTE
Make the change at a controlled rate to prevent overshooting the re-
quired position.
CAUTION
In close formation, never overlap the wing of another aircraft. Keep in
mind that wing-tip vortices cause a circular flow of air around the wing-
tips and a downward flow of air behind the trailing edge of the wings.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 13 =====
45.
Echelon to Echelon. To make this change, reduce torque sufficiently to move back
and down, so that the nose of your aircraft is a minimum of 10’ behind the tail of lead,
and the tail of your aircraft is low enough to pass below the prop-wash. Increase torque
to stabilize in this position. Anticipate this power increase to not fall aft of the ideal
position. Initiate the crossover by putting on some bank in order to generate a small
change in heading and then take the bank off. It is the heading differential that results
in the crossover and a slight torque increase is required to compensate for the extra
distance traveled. A further torque increase is required as the downwash of the lead
aircraft is encountered to prevent falling further back. Pass through or slightly aft of
the line astern position, and when the cross-over is completed, stop the aircraft (change
the heading to that of the lead). Stabilize directly behind the echelon position, increase
torque and move forward and up to the new echelon. When in position, torque will have
to be reduced in order to stabilize in the new position (Figures 12.12 and 12.13).
Figure 12.12: Front Seat Corner References
46.
Echelon to Line Astern. This change is identical to the first half of the echelon-
to-echelon move except that you stop directly astern of the lead aircraft. Adjust torque
to move into position.
47.
Line Astern to Echelon. The lead will normally call this change on the R/T. The
mechanics of the change are the same as the latter portions of the echelon-to-echelon
change. A slight increase in longitudinal spacing is acceptable.
48.
Common faults during station changes are:
a.
failure to advance the torque far enough or soon enough for the torque in-
creases required on the echelon-to-echelon change;

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 14 =====
Figure 12.13: Rear Seat Corner References
b.
applying too much bank initially for the cross-over and not removing it; and
c.
failure to keep the relative movements of the aircraft at a controlled rate.
12.21
BREAK-OUT
49.
A break-out is an intentional and rapid manoeuvre away from the last known posi-
tion of either the lead or another aircraft in the formation. During this manoeuvre several
things must be considered: G loading, other aircraft in the formation, ground proximity,
traffic, and weather. A breakout is required when any of the following occurs:
a.
loss of visual contact;
b.
hazardous situation develops;
c.
directed to do so by the lead; or
d.
if ahead of or under the lead.
50.
As soon as possible after commencing a break-out, the wingman must advise the
lead (i.e. “BLACK TWO OFF”) and continue with the break-out manoeuvre until ade-
quate separation is assured or until direction is received from lead. In all cases after a
breakout, a rejoin will not be attempted until clearance is received from the lead.
51.
After a breakout has occurred, lead will immediately attempt to gain visual con-
tact with their wingman. Also, when adequate separation is assured, the wingman will
attempt to regain visual contact with lead. If the lead or wingman cannot get visual
contact, or when visual contact is made, an R/T call must be made, i.e. “BLACK LEAD

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 15 =====
BLIND” or “BLACK LEAD VISUAL”. This call shall be made as soon as possible to
ensure that all aircraft in the formation are aware if visual separation is being main-
tained. If lead is visual with two, radio calls shall be used to direct two’s attention in
order to gain visual contact with lead. When two has visual contact on lead, lead can
continue and coordinate the rejoin, however, if both aircraft are blind, lead must become
directive ensuring positive separation and coordination of the subsequent rejoin.
12.22
PRACTICE BREAK-OUT
52.
During your training you will practice an emergency break-out manoeuvre. This
will provide you with practical exposure to the separation generated from lead in the
event of an actual breakout. This manoeuvre will be briefed to you by your instructor
and will be flown in the following manner:
a.
Lead will establish the formation in straight-and-level flight and ensure there
are no traffic conflicts.
b.
Lead will clear the wingman for the breakout procedure using R/T, e.g. “BLACK
72, CLEARED BREAKOUT”.
c.
Two performs a break-out by initiating a climbing turn away from leads last
known position. This is an aggressive turn away using approximately 3 G
to ensure positive separation and should be performed with PCL MAX to
maximize your energy state. Two informs lead of the break-out using R/T
in the following manner: “BLACK 72 BLIND”. Lead will immediately at-
tempt to gain visual contact with two and become directive to ensure positive
separation.
d.
If lead gains visual contact, direction is given to ensure separation and coor-
dinate the rejoin e.g. “BLACK 71 VISUAL, 72 REVERSE”. After two has
reversed the turn, he must transmit to lead whether he has visual contact with
lead or if he is blind. If visual, the R/T call would be “BLACK 72 VISUAL”.
If two is not visual, lead must be informed i.e. “BLACK 72 BLIND”. Lead
should then transmit their position and distance relative to two, i.e. “BLACK
72, 71 IS AT YOUR 10 O’CLOCK POSITION SLIGHTLY LOW FOR 1
MILE” in order to aid two in gaining visual contact. Once two is visual, lead
will normally direct two to rejoin straight ahead. Employ the procedures
listed in Section 12.26.
e.
If lead cannot gain visual contact, information on current position will nor-
mally be provided which is used by two to ensure positive separation, i.e.
“BLACK 71 BLIND, HEADING 090, MAINTAINING 10,000 FEET”. Pos-
itive separation is achieved through altitude and/or heading de-confliction.
Lead will then coordinate and direct the rejoin over the radio.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 16 =====
12.23
REJOINS
53.
There are several situations that necessitate having to perform a rejoin on another
aircraft. These include moving out of formation to complete aircraft checks, lost lead,
from fighting wing, from interval take-offs, etc. Whether joining formation in straight-
and-level flight or in a turn, the join-up should be completed expeditiously but safely.
Turning rejoins are the most expeditious and therefore, should be the primary method
of rejoining. The straight-ahead rejoin will be used when a turning rejoin is impractical.
For both rejoins, aim to stabilize in route position prior to closing into the echelon
position. For turning rejoins, this should ensure sufficient clearance to be able to pass
safely behind and below the lead aircraft in the event of an overshoot. For straight-ahead
rejoins, this should ensure sufficient lateral clearance to pass beside lead in the event of
an overshoot. This safety margin will reduce the danger of collision, which could result
from misjudging closing speed. During your training you will practice both types of
rejoins. Unless otherwise briefed, all rejoins will be conducted with the lead aircraft
stabilized at 200 KIAS.
12.24
TURNING REJOINS
54.
Lead will advise the wingman to commence a rejoin using R/T, e.g. “BLACKS,
ECHELON RIGHT”, or by using the visual signal, a rocking of the wings. Unless
previously briefed, a 30-degree bank turn will be established, indicating the echelon to
which the rejoin is to be accomplished, and a pre-briefed or announced airspeed will be
maintained. During the rejoin, the lead will monitor the wingman while continuing to
clear the area.
55.
The two factors that are used to accomplish the rejoin are angular cut-off and
airspeed overtake.
56.
As the wingman, if you maintain the same bank as the lead throughout a 180-
degree turn, you will, at the same airspeed, roll out in front of the lead with the same
spacing you had on entry (see Figure 12.16) Therefore, you will need to vary your bank
throughout the rejoin. To rejoin, use the same or more bank as the lead in order to
place him at your 10:30 or 1:30 clock position, depending on the direction of turn, and
slightly above the horizon. The initial reference is to line up the lead’s aircraft so that
roughly half of the upper wing is visible aft of the trailing edge of the vertical stabilizer.
The rejoin line offers a safe transition by resulting in a slow convergence with the angle
of the spinner-wing-tip line, while aiming at the aft most portion of the lead’s aircraft.
Torque is normally added at this point if an airspeed overtake is desired. In the early
stages of training little to no overtake will be used to establish a more controlled closure
and maintenance of the rejoin line. In later stages it is recommended you establish an
airspeed 10 to 20 KIAS greater than lead’s. Ensure that the torque is reset once the
desired speed is reached to prevent large, unwanted airspeed changes in overtake.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 17 =====
Figure 12.14: Turning Rejoin Cockpit View
57.
Once on the rejoin line, bank must be varied to maintain it, occasionally glancing
at your airspeed indicator to monitor airspeed overtake. If the lead is being held at a
constant position on the windshield, an intercept/collision course has been established.
Maintaining the desired rejoin line can initially be difficult due to the distance of the
lead aircraft and subtle differences in the detail of the aforementioned references. As
the trailing edge of lead’s vertical stab approaches the upper wing tip, the rejoining
aircraft has drifted ahead of the ideal line and a correction towards the outside of the
rejoin circle is required. If a large amount of the upper wing tip is extending aft of
the trailing edge of the vertical stab, the rejoining aircraft is aft of the ideal line and
a correction into the rejoin circle is required. Depending upon the size the error, the
amount of angle of bank used will vary, but in most cases the amount is quite subtle.
Keep in mind that whatever amount of bank is used to start the correction, a similar
amount of change will be required to re-stabilize on the ideal line.
58.
Continue on the rejoin line and maintain the horizontal plane just slightly below
lead. As you approach the lateral spacing of the route position, adjust torque to remove
airspeed overtake and adjust your bank to align your fuselage with the lead. Once the
closure has been stabilized, transition to the appropriate echelon reference. A stabilized
rate of closure is similar to the rate of movement used during station changes and move-
ment to and from route. This transition from the rejoin line, to the spinner-wing-tip line
may take place at a lateral separation similar to the route position or as late as the ‘near’
corner of the station change from echelon to echelon. Regardless of exactly where the

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 18 =====
transition takes place, it is imperative that until the closure is stabilized, a safe escape
path is maintained by remaining on the rejoin line. This line uses airspeed as well as
cut-off to enable a rejoin with the lead; however, if properly executed, a rejoin can be
affected with little or no airspeed advantage. Rejoin lines other than described above
(10:30 or 1:30) will allow effective rejoins, however, difficulties may be encountered in
aligning with the lead during the final stages of the manoeuvre.
CAUTION
Excessive bank can result in losing sight of the lead. Do not attempt to
arrest the rate of closure with lead by executing a steep turn inside lead’s
circle.
59.
Always join to the inside of the turn unless otherwise instructed. If instructed to
join on the outside of the turn, plan your rejoin to pass one to two aircraft lengths behind
and below the level of the lead.
Figure 12.15: Turning Rejoin
12.25
PRACTICE REJOINS
60.
During training you will practice several rejoins to allow you to learn the tech-
niques employed. In order to achieve maximum training value, a procedure, termed the
Break and Rejoin, has been established to position the aircraft for rejoin as quickly and
safely as possible. The Break and Rejoin is accomplished in the same manner as the
Overhead Break.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 19 =====
Figure 12.16: Hot Line and Cold Line Rejoins
61.
Lead will ensure the area is clear and call for the break using R/T or hand signals,
for example, “BLACKS, BREAK AND REJOIN”. Lead then executes a 180 degree
turn at 60 degrees of bank and 2 G. The wingman will delay the pre-briefed amount
of time, after which they initiate a 60 and 2 turn to roll out behind the lead. Although
lead has cleared the area for the break and rejoin, the number two aircraft can assist in
traffic de-confliction and overall safety of flight by using this opportunity (while not in
close formation) to clear the turn as well. Lead will delay after rolling out to allow the
wingman to complete their turn, and will then indicate to the wingman to rejoin. The
wingman then rejoins as described above.
12.26
STRAIGHT-AHEAD REJOINS
62.
A straight-ahead rejoin is initiated by either an R/T transmission or wing rock from
the formation lead. The call should specify the formation desired and only if the lead’s
airspeed differs from the pre-briefed rejoin airspeed, should an airspeed be specified.
For example “BLACKS, ECHELON RIGHT.” If a wing rock is used, the wingman is
expected the return to last formation flown.
63.
As the wingman, turn to position your aircraft in the lead’s extended 6 o’clock
position. Use full torque to generate overtaking airspeed. When you are approximately
500’ behind lead, turn slightly towards the desired echelon. This will produce a vector
slightly away from lead. Monitor your overtake and begin to reduce the torque setting
as you approach lead. As you get closer, use the speed brake and/or torque to control
the rate of overtake, aiming to stabilize in the route position. Because closure rates are
difficult to judge at long range, be prepared to reduce torque to idle and use the speed
brake if it becomes obvious that you are overtaking at an excessive rate. During the final
stage of the rejoin, the wingman should continue to approach with a flight vector that is
slightly away from the lead aircraft prior to establishing position.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 20 =====
Figure 12.17: Straight Ahead Rejoin
12.27
OVERSHOOTING
64.
Overshooting the lead aircraft during rejoins may occur for various reasons. The
most common error is misjudging the rate of closure, which can be a result of high air-
speed or a large cut-off angle. If you are uncertain of being able to stabilize in a route
position, overshoot. An overshoot is not an indication of a poor rejoin, it is a deci-
sion made at the latter stages of a rejoin in the interest of flight safety. When time and
conditions allow, inform lead of your decision to overshoot i.e. “BLACK 72 OVER-
SHOOTING”. This call is made for information purposes and no follow-on action by
lead is required.
65.
Turning Rejoin. If reducing torque and extending speed brake will not prevent
an overshoot, safe separation is maintained by passing behind and below the lead. In-
creasing bank in an attempt to salvage a rejoin may cause the wingman to lose sight of
the lead at a critical phase of flight. Once the decision to overshoot has been made, the
collision course must be broken by rolling the wings level and reducing torque. Ensure
that the aircraft does not climb as you cross below and behind the lead aircraft. At no
time can you allow your aircraft to climb to or above lead’s altitude. By ensuring ver-
tical separation at all times, the possibility of collision is greatly reduced. Once on the
outside of the lead’s turn, stabilize your aircraft in relation to lead and, after ensuring
that there is at least one aircraft length clearance and you do not have any overtake,
cross over to the appropriate echelon and complete the rejoin as normal. After having
completed an overshoot, do not sacrifice safety in order to expedite your cross over to
