continue with the rejoin. Ensure you are completely stable on the outside of the turn
prior to continuing (See Figure 12.18).
66.
Straight-Ahead Rejoin. If, despite reduced torque and the use of the speed brake
an overshoot develops during your straight ahead rejoin, establish vertical separation,
turn away from the lead and make an advisory call on the radio, i.e. “BLACK 72
OVERSHOOTING LEFT/RIGHT”. The lead will direct further action as required. If at

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 21 =====
Figure 12.18: Overshoot from a Turning Rejoin
any time you lose sight of the lead, break out immediately.
CAUTION
When overshooting straight ahead, there is a tendency to move the con-
trol column in the direction in which you are looking, that is, towards the
lead. This will cause your aircraft to close on lead, which could result in
a collision.
12.28
LEAD CHANGES
67.
Lead changes are normally made with the formation in straight-and-level flight. As
the lead change requires the attention of both formation members, neither will be able to
direct their full attention to clearing the formation of potential conflicts while the aircraft
are exchanging positions. It is paramount that the initial formation lead methodically
clear the area of all potential conflicts before commencing the lead change. To signal
for the change, the lead may use visual signals or R/T. If the lead uses a radio call, for
example, “BLACK 72 YOU HAVE THE TAC LEAD”, number two acknowledges “72”.
Number two then moves laterally approximately one wing span, advances the PCL and
moves promptly and positively ahead of the lead. Both pilots mutually monitor the
change. The new formation lead becomes the tac lead and assumes responsibility for
the formation as the two aircraft pass abreast of each other. The new lead then looks
ahead and reduces torque smoothly to maintain manoeuvring airspeed. Once the change
is completed, the tac lead shall perform a comprehensive lookout. If lead is exchanged
via a visual signal, the wingman shall acknowledge with a head nod and follow the same

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 22 =====
procedures as above with the change still taking effect as number two passes abreast of
the lead aircraft. Upon completion of the lead change, the formation as a whole will
conduct an ops check.
12.29
FIGHTING WING
68.
Fighting Wing is primarily an offensive formation utilized in many communities,
including the tactical fighter, tactical helicopter, and transport communities. Its prime
tactical advantage is that it is an extremely fluid and manoeuvrable formation that pro-
vides a measure of mutual support. In addition to its tactical advantage, Fighting Wing
is also useful during departure, enroute, and arrival domestic manoeuvring as it provides
each formation member with the opportunity to relax and maintain a good lookout while
still maintaining formation integrity.
Figure 12.19: Fighting Wing References
69.
In the optimum Fighting Wing position, number two maintains a 30 to 60 degree
sweep from the lead at a distance of 500–1000’. Once the lead begins to manoeuvre,
number two is free to manoeuvre as required to stay behind the lead and maintain sep-
aration. This may include collapsing to lead’s 6 o’clock, flowing to the opposite side of
the formation, utilizing the vertical, adjusting power, or any combination thereof. Once
manoeuvring has ceased, the wingman moves back into the swept position (not neces-
sarily on the same side of lead as prior to manoeuvring). In order for the wingman to
efficiently manoeuvre behind the lead, he must have an understanding of and employ
the principles of lead, lag, and pure pursuit. When these principles are effectively em-
ployed, lead is free to manoeuvre in any plane with minimal impact on Fighting Wing
positioning.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 23 =====
70.
Prior to being exposed to any aggressive Fighting Wing manoeuvring, you will be
introduced to the concepts of lead, lag, and pure pursuit.
Figure 12.20: Fighting Wing Turn Entry (in position)
Figure 12.21: Fighting Wing Turn Entry (stretched)
12.30
PURSUIT CURVES
71.
To maintain separation from a manoeuvring aircraft in a dynamic environment
is not an exact science. Because most parameters are constantly changing, a trailing

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 24 =====
Figure 12.22: Fighting Wing Turn Entry (tight)
Figure 12.23: Fighting Wing Turn Exit
aircraft must employ various skills and tools in order to maintain and regain separation.
The basic tools are the concepts of lead, lag, and pure pursuit.
72.
Lead Pursuit. Projecting your flight path in front of a preceding aircraft is called
lead pursuit. In simple terms it equates to putting your nose in front of the preceding
aircraft, or putting the preceding aircraft in the bottom of the windscreen. This will
normally generate closure and will enable a trailing aircraft of equal performance to
maintain or reduce the separation on a preceding aircraft. Pulling lead will normally
increase aspect angle and change the line of sight.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 25 =====
Figure 12.24: Lead and Lag Pursuit Curves
Figure 12.25: Pursuit Curves Cockpit Perspective
73.
Pure Pursuit. Projecting your flight path onto the preceding aircraft is called pure
pursuit. In simple terms it equates to putting your nose on the other aircraft, or putting
the preceding aircraft in the middle of the windscreen. This will normally generate
closure and enable a trailing aircraft of equal performance to maintain or close the sep-
aration on a preceding aircraft, but not to the extent that lead pursuit will. Pure pursuit
will normally cause an increase in aspect angle and will generate zero line of sight.
74.
Lag Pursuit. Projecting your flight path behind a preceding aircraft is called lag
pursuit. In simple terms it equates to putting your nose behind the preceding aircraft,

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 26 =====
or putting the preceding aircraft in the top of the windscreen. Lag pursuit is used to
decrease closure or increase separation as well as decrease the higher aspect angles that
result from lead and pure pursuit. The amount of lead, lag or pure pursuit that is required
is based solely on the severity of the manoeuvring. Remember that the prime objective
at this stage of your training is to maintain separation, which is accomplished by flying
on, or around, the preceding aircraft’s turn circle. Therefore, in a dynamic environment,
lead or lag as required to remain on, or around, the defined turn circle.
12.31
DESCENT PROFILES
75.
Before commencing descent, the lead will call or signal for the pre-descent check.
Formation position during descent is at the discretion of the lead (i.e. fighting wing,
echelon right/left) and will vary according to the anticipated weather conditions. To
provide some measure of PCL response for the wingman, a minimum torque setting
of 20% is recommended. Power changes should be smooth such that the wingman
can maintain position. Significant power changes should be prefaced with the power
reduction signal to increase predictability. (It is the wingman’s responsibility to prepare
for the intended approach by setting up the appropriate navigation aids and approach
plates in the event of a requirement for lost wingman procedures or taking the lead if
necessary).
12.32
TRAFFIC PATTERN
76.
The dimensions and procedures for joining and flying the traffic pattern are the
same as during normal clearhood flying. In formation, turns are normally flown with
the wingman on the outside of the turn. Try to avoid the tendency of over-controlling
during turns in the traffic pattern. This is due, in part, to the sensation of speed and
proximity to the ground at traffic pattern altitudes.
77.
The ultimate goal of the traffic pattern is to place the formation at initial, in prepa-
ration for the overhead break and landing. To ensure that the formation is in the proper
echelon for the break, the lead may have to initiate a station change at some point in the
traffic pattern. In this case, follow the procedures outlined in Section 12.20. Make the
change as expeditiously, but safely, as possible. There is little room for extending the
parameters of the traffic pattern while waiting for the completion of a station change.
12.33
OVERHEAD BREAK AND FINAL TURN
78.
The mechanics of the overhead break and final turn for a formation are identi-
cal to single-plane procedures. The break will always occur away from the wingman;
therefore, be prepared for a station change as late as inside the Initial Point in order to
have the formation in the appropriate echelon for the break. The normal spacing for
an overhead break is three seconds (i.e. number two delays their break for three sec-
onds after the lead). This spacing may be changed for various reasons (environmental

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 27 =====
conditions, runway conditions, etc.), and the lead will normally cover the details of the
overhead break during the formation briefing. The following paragraph describes the
“three-second” break, but may be modified as required to accommodate the necessary
spacing.
79.
The Overhead Break. The lead initiates the break in the normal manner by turn-
ing away from the wingman. The wingman maintains heading and starts a 3-second
count as the lead breaks. Avoid looking in the direction of the lead while delaying for
the break as this may lead to a tendency to turn into the break. The wingman initiates
the roll-in once the count is completed. As 60 degrees of bank is established, torque
is reduced as per a normal break. The turn should be coordinated so that the wing-
man remains level with the lead and rolls out on downwind outside the lead’s wash
on a slightly wider track than the lead aircraft. This procedure provides the formation
with separation and allows formation members to start the final turn from essentially the
same position. The pre-landing check is now initiated. All formation members should
maintain approximately 120 KIAS until final turn is started.
80.
The Final Turn. Lead initiates the final turn from a normal perch point, based
upon the day’s conditions. Lead will fly a normal approach maintaining 120 KIAS
during the turn until crossing the window. Landing clearance will be requested at the
same time as for a single-ship final turn. At this point lead will transition for a normal
landing. The wingman will initiate their final turn from approximately the same point
used by lead and will monitor the preceding aircraft. Modifications to the normal final
turn should be made to maintain desired spacing. The wingman should avoid low final
turns; if spacing is questionable or excessive wake turbulence is encountered, go around.
The minimum landing separation is 2000’, but may be extended if required.
12.34
LANDING
81.
Lead will normally land on the centreline of the runway, and number two will land
directly behind. A minimum of 2000’ separation must be maintained between aircraft
until touchdown. Once slowed to a safe speed, each aircraft moves to the turn-off side
of the runway.
82.
After Landing Procedures. After landing, unless otherwise briefed, the Lead air-
craft will clear the active runway and stop at approximately 45 degrees off the taxiway
centerline facing into wind providing enough space for the rest of the formation. The
following aircraft will stop to match lead’s angle into wind at a suitable distance (ap-
proximately one wing span). Following the post-landing checks, the wingman will give
a head nod to lead to indicate that they are ready to taxi. Lead will then continue as per
normal ground/taxi operations.
83.
A formation mission is normally not complete until all aircraft are in the chocks
and the engines are shut down. The same considerations apply for ground handling as
they did during the taxi to the runway.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 28 =====
12.35
TOUCH AND GO
84.
During formation overhead breaks, you can perform full stop landings as described
in the preceding paragraphs or the formation may decide to perform a touch and go.
Normally, a touch and go is accomplished for practice purposes in order to maximize
traffic pattern training. In all cases, a thorough briefing on the procedure to be flown
must be accomplished prior to flight to include as a minimum the formation to rejoin to
and the track lead will fly after the touch and go. A touch and go can be performed from
an overhead break and final turn approach as described below.
85.
All aircraft fly a normal approach as previously described until touchdown. After
touchdown, each aircraft performs a touch and go as per clearhood training. Once air-
borne each aircraft raises gear and flaps individually and performs a post-take-off check.
Lead will normally reduce torque to approximately 80%, accelerate straight ahead to
180 KIAS and then continue climbing to pattern altitude or as briefed. The wingman
continues to accelerate in order to effect a rejoin on lead as briefed.
12.36
FORMATION LEADING
86.
A successful formation mission depends largely on the lead — effective formation
flying can only result from good leadership. On an operational squadron, a great deal of
experience is normally required in order to become an element lead. It is not intended
to fully train you as a formation lead during your training. Rather, the intent is to expose
you to some of the aspects of leading that you could be faced with, even as a brand new
wingman on an operational squadron. To this end, the following paragraphs detail some
of the responsibilities and considerations that a formation lead is faced with.
87.
The lead’s responsibilities begin in the flight room before a mission and extend
through to the post-flight debriefing. The lead must plan the mission according to the
weather, the experience level of the pilots and the mission requirements. The lead should
pay particular attention to the following points (your instructor will assist you in devel-
oping these points to suit the requirements of your formation training):
a.
Lookout. Formation flying requires a particularly regimented lookout. For-
mations are slow manoeuvring and sometimes require a large amount of
airspace to complete exercises (e.g. break and rejoin, line abreast, fighting
wing, fluid manoeuvring). Formation leads must not sacrifice good lookout
or early traffic deconfliction in order to fly smoothly or to complete mission
items.
b.
Pre-Flight Briefing. A thorough pre-flight brief is required, with the major-
ity of time spent on critical areas such as formation changes, breaks, rejoins,
etc.
c.
Controlling and Manoeuvring.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 29 =====
(1)
All manoeuvres should be carried out smoothly and accurately, and
should be within the capabilities of all the formation pilots.
(2)
Aircraft farthest from the lead in large formations (particularity in ech-
elon) must make relatively large changes in pitch and torque during
turns. Entries and recoveries should be made accordingly.
(3)
Full power and idle power settings should be avoided by the lead when
in close formation.
(4)
Very low speeds must be avoided, otherwise aircraft on the inside of
turns may encounter control difficulties.
(5)
High-G manoeuvres require smooth and deliberate control inputs to
preclude the wingman from exceeding G limitations while attempting
to maintain formation position.
(6)
Ensure that the wingman is in the proper position before starting the
next manoeuvre.
NOTE
Never become so overly conscious of smoothness that ma-
noeuvring precision or safety are compromised. It is far
more important to fly your aircraft safely with minor ex-
cursions from perfect performance, than to fly “super smooth”
and be a liability to the flight.
d.
Airmanship.
(1)
Lookout. In close formation, the attention of the wingman is concen-
trated on the lead, who must assume the responsibility of look-out for
the whole formation. During the lookout, the formation lead must be
aware of the greater airspace taken up by, and the reduced manoeuvra-
bility of, the formation.
(2)
Navigation. The lead is responsible for the navigation and use of radio
aids for the formation.
(3)
Fuel State. This must be obtained periodically. The lead must appreci-
ate that the wingman will have higher fuel consumption than the lead.
(4)
Position of the Sun. Whenever possible, the lead should avoid flying
directly “up-sun” of any member of the formation.
(5)
Weather Conditions. You must continually monitor the weather condi-
tions and anticipate cloud penetration by ensuring that all checks, for-
mation changes and speed-brake calls are made before entering cloud.
Ensure that frequency changes are timely.
(6)
Emergencies. Be mentally prepared to cope with any emergency that
may arise.
(7)
Wingman Experience. The lead must be aware of the abilities of the
wingman and restrict manoeuvring accordingly.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 30 =====
88.
Above all, the formation lead should set an example of good air discipline where
unnecessary R/T should be avoided, briefed manoeuvres and flight profile should be
adhered to, and safe and efficient conduct of the mission should be ensured.
89.
During your training on the Harvard II, you will be leading the formation in a
number of sequences of which you will be graded. Those sequences include: the for-
mation take-off (interval or wing), wing work, instrument/visual approach, line abreast,
overhead break and stream landing, and TAC RTB. Although you will be given further
tips and techniques on each sequence by your instructor, the basics are outlined in the
individual sequences outlined earlier.
12.37
RESPONSIBILITIES OF THE WINGMAN
90.
To perform wingman duties, you must understand and strictly adhere to several
fundamental principles.
Although procedural differences exist between operational
units and from aircraft to aircraft, the following principles, which underlie basic wing-
man responsibilities, do not change. Regardless of mission or aircraft:
a.
maintain a constant awareness of the collision potential associated with for-
mation flying;
b.
maintain accurate station keeping;
c.
keep the lead in sight at all times. If you do lose sight of the lead, follow
briefed break-out procedures;
d.
anticipate required corrections and think ahead of the aircraft. This is of
particular importance during high performance manoeuvres;
e.
if anticipating IMC conditions, have all your navigation equipment set up so
you will be able to change immediately to instruments should you lose sight
of the lead;
f.
listen closely for R/T that may affect your flight;
g.
avoid laziness as proficiency increases; and
h.
keep your lead informed of any abnormalities in your aircraft or any other
flight safety aspects.
91.
For Joker/Bingo calls while in formation, the wingman (i.e. Zulu 22) will transmit
a fuel call over the discrete radio frequency when the fuel state is from one hundred to
zero pounds above the planned Joker/Bingo fuels annotated on the formation mission
card. The flight lead (i.e. Zulu 21) will make the fuel call within the cockpit only when
these fuel states are met. Regardless of tactical lead changes, the original wingman (i.e.
Zulu 22) will always transmit the fuel call over the radio.
92.
For formations of three aircraft or more, the first member of the formation (exclud-
ing the flight lead) who reaches the depicted Joker/Bingo fuel state first will transmit
the call over the radio. If at that point any other formation member (excluding the flight

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 31 =====
lead) has a lower fuel state, then that formation member will transmit their fuel state
over the radio in order to provide SA to the whole formation as to which aircraft has the
lowest fuel remaining.
93.
As a wingman, your primary attention is devoted to maintaining proper position;
however, this does not mean that you cannot clear for the formation. While referencing
lead, it is possible for you to clear the area that you can see beyond the lead. You do this
by using all of the lead’s aircraft as a reference; do not attempt to fly using just one spot
on the lead’s aircraft as a reference.
12.38
LOST-WINGMAN PROCEDURE
94.
Before any flight, the formation leader will brief lost-wingman procedures. Im-
mediately upon losing sight of the lead aircraft under visual flight conditions, the wing-
man must break out. The lead must be immediately advised by R/T, e.g. “BLACK 72
BLIND.” If, as the wingman, you lose sight of the lead in cloud, immediately transition
to instrument flight and make no attempt to find the lead. Execute the appropriate lost-
wingman procedure, calling the lead and later calling the controlling agency, advising
them of the situation. In both cases the lead aircraft will continue on the planned flight
profile and coordinate further actions with the wingman and the controlling agency. No
amount of legislation can cover every circumstance in which a wingman might lose
the lead. Intelligent reaction must be your response in such an event; however, lost-
wingman procedures listed below should be followed in IFR flight:
a.
Straight Flight (Climbing, Descending or Straight and Level). Simulta-
neously inform the lead and turn 10 degrees away for 10 seconds, using 10
degrees of bank. If safe to do so, reduce torque slightly (10%), then resume
course and obtain separate clearance.
b.
Turns (Climb, Descent or Level).
(1)
On the outside of the turn, roll out to wings-level and inform the lead.
Continue straight ahead for 20 seconds to ensure separation before re-
suming the turn. Obtain separate clearance.
(2)
On the inside of a turn, reduce torque to ensure nose-to-tail clearance,
and inform the lead to roll out of the turn. Maintain angle-of-bank to
ensure lateral separation and obtain separate clearance. The lead may
resume the turn only when separation is ensured.
c.
Approach (Initial and Intermediate). Transition to straight-and-level in-
strument flight, inform lead, and then begin a level 360-degree turn away
from the lead. Resume the penetration at the completion of the turn if cleared
to do so by the controlling agency.
NOTE
Always be aware of minimum IFR altitudes.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 32 =====
d.
Approach (Final, Precision and Non-Precision). The wingman momentar-
ily turns away to ensure separation, informs the lead, initiates the published
missed-approach procedure and obtains a separate clearance from the ap-
proach control.
12.39
COLLISION AVOIDANCE RESPONSIBILITIES
95.
It is incumbent upon all formation members to anticipate and be conscious of the
mid-air collision potential associated with formation flying. It is not within the scope of
this manual, nor is it possible to address every possible situation that, if mishandled or
poorly flown, could result in an accident or incident. Throughout your training, every
effort will be made to instill in you those principles that develop sound airmanship and
cultivate pilot judgment. The procedures outlined in this training manual are designed to
accomplish course objectives and maximize safety. Bear in mind that nothing precludes
aircrew members from taking whatever action is deemed necessary to avoid in-flight
collisions; therefore, the ultimate responsibilities for safe flight must rest with each
pilot.
12.40
FORMATION SIGNALS
96.
Formation signals are used by lead to alert the formation of a particular action or
manoeuvre. These signals may be given visually or over the radio. This requires the
wingman to be attentive to not only the lead aircraft, but the lead pilot as well. For
signals given over R/T, each call is prefaced by the formation call sign, followed by
the order (repeated in some cases) and an executive command, i.e. “YODAS, SPEED
BRAKE — GO!” When using hand signals for ancillaries, the lead shall ensure that
the wingman has received the signal prior to selection — an affirmative head nod is
sufficient. The executive signal is then the lead’s head nod. The hand signals for each
manoeuvre are shown in Figures 12.27, 12.28, 12.29 and Tables 12.1 & 12.2.
NOTE
In the case of selecting landing gear and/or flaps using hand signals, if
the airspeed requirement is not met, the wingman shall not acknowledge
the request with a head nod. The lack of acknowledgment will indicate
to lead that the wingman’s airspeed is not below 147 KIAS and therefore
lead shall wait until the head nod is given.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 33 =====
Figure 12.26: Take-off Hand Signals
NOTE
When using hand signals to indicate number values, extend fingers ver-
tically for numbers 1 to 5 and horizontally for numbers 6 to 9. Use a
closed fist to indicate zero and pull you hand out of sight between each
digit.
12.41
FORMATION EMERGENCY PROCEDURES
97.
Airborne Emergency. No amount of legislation can cover every conceivable pos-
sibility. In the event of an airborne emergency, the emergency (sick) aircraft should try
to inform the formation lead (or wingman) of the problem as soon as conditions permit.
Further action will be as dictated by circumstances and as directed by the formation or
element lead. During emergency handling mutual support should be maintained. The
following are guidelines in reaction to an emergency while in formation:
a.
Mutual Support: formation integrity and visual support shall be maintained
except when unable to do so. Lead may take over de-confliction contract/responsibilitie
to alleviate workload of the sick aircraft by stating “Supporting” and manoeu-
vring as required. The supporting aircraft may be used as required to assist
with checklist actions and troubleshooting;
b.
Critical Emergencies — Handle and Advise: the sick aircraft should ensure
separation (break-out if required), carry out immediate actions (red pages)

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 34 =====
Figure 12.27: Formation Hand Signals
associated with the emergency, and then communicate their situation/status
to other aircraft (lead/wing);
c.
Non-Critical Emergencies — Advise and Handle: the sick aircraft should
maintain formation if able, communicate their situation/status to other air-
craft (lead/wing), and then carry out non-critical emergency checklist items
(yellow pages) when the formation is stable and/or the sick aircraft has the
lead;
d.
Emergencies in Tactical Formation: if in tactical formation or tactically ma-
noeuvring at the time of the emergency, the sick aircraft should call “Knock It
Off” and then proceed as per Critical or Non-Critical emergency paragraphs
above. Knock it Off implies that an RTB will be required. If the pilot is

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 35 =====
Figure 12.28: Formation Landing Hand Signals
unsure if the aircraft problem will require an RTB, the pilot may call “Ter-
minate” and subsequently communicate their situation/status to other aircraft
when time permits; and
e.
RTB: the sick aircraft should be escorted for the RTB to best provide mutual
support.
98.
Emergency Hand Signals. Emergency hand signals are regulated by NATO agree-
ment and are to be used for communication when there is radio failure in flight. To
attract attention during the day, rock the aircraft wings and, at night, flash the navigation
lights. A thumb-up/down signal is used to indicate satisfaction or dissatisfaction, as ap-
propriate. The emergency hand signals that are to be used during the day are as shown
in Table 12.3

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 36 =====
Figure 12.29: Formation Misc Hand Signals
99.
NATO Fuel Status. The fuel-status hand signal is classed as an “information
signaled in the NATO agreement. The lead shall make a drinking motion with a closed
hand having the thumb extended towards the mouth when fuel status is being requested.
The pilot who is reporting the aircraft fuel status shall give the estimated flying time
remaining by extending fingers as appropriate. Each finger shall represent 10 minutes,
while a closed hand shall indicate one hour. (For example, a clenched fist followed by
three fingers extended represents 1.5 hours.)

ADVANCED FLYING TRAINING
1.
The remainder of this book describes several advanced handling sequences that
will help prepare you for follow-on flying in jet aircraft in the Canadian Forces. These
sequences will allow you to explore the outer reaches of the Harvard II flight envelope
and, in doing so, will enhance your airmanship and overall flying skills.
2.
By adapting the skills and techniques that you have already learned during your
basic flying training, you will find that the advanced sequences employ the same con-
siderations and, for the most part, are simply variations of the basic manoeuvres you
have mastered to date.
218

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 43 =====
CHAPTER 13: FORCED LANDINGS
13.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
A successful forced landing depends on skills acquired through practice, good
judgment, and adequate pre-landing considerations. There is only a remote possibility
of experiencing an engine failure/malfunction in flight that will require you to com-
plete a forced landing. A thorough understanding of the Harvard II flight performance,
emergency procedures, and forced landing pattern coupled with an appreciation of the
ejection system, will assist you in dealing with the situation should the need arise.
13.2
FORCED LANDING OR EJECTION
2.
The decision to force land or to eject is a choice that the pilot must make when
faced with a serious engine malfunction. The time available to make this decision will
depend upon the phase of flight during which the engine problem occurs. This can
range from a few seconds during the take-off phase to a few minutes while flying at
higher altitudes. Landing the Harvard II on an unprepared surface is not recommended
so ensure this is factored into your decision.
13.3
ENGINE FAILURE/MALFUNCTION
3.
Usually fuel starvation, FOD, mechanical malfunction, or PCL/aircraft mishan-
dling causes an engine failure/malfunction. Indications of an engine failure are: loss of
thrust and airspeed; a drop in N1, torque and ITT; propeller movement towards feather
due to lack of oil pressure; illumination of various CWS panel lights; and a lack of en-
gine noise. Before initiating any action in response to a possible engine malfunction, the
pilot must take the time to analyze the symptoms in order to avoid an improper response
during a possible critical phase of flight. Checking the indications of the N1, torque,
and ITT gauges will ascertain whether the engine has failed or not.
4.
The malfunctions described above are covered in the flight manual/Checklist under the
heading “Engine Failure In Flight”. Your immediate response to a suspected engine
failure/malfunction is to maintain positive aircraft control while analyzing the situation.
219

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 44 =====
Once the problem has been identified, the correct response can then be carried out as
detailed in the flight manual/Checklist.
5.
At low altitudes, if there is no immediate indication of a re-light, attempt the air-
start procedure as detailed in the checklist if time permits. It will take 15 to 20 seconds
for the propeller to un-feather after the N1 reaches 45%. If the air-start is unsuccessful,
the decision to eject or to force land must be made as soon as possible to ensure that
successful ejection parameters exist. The flight manual recommends ejection above 2000 ft
AGL in level flight with an optimum speed between 125 and 180 KIAS. During any
re-light procedure, you must monitor your altitude and airspeed. If, at any time, doubt
exists in achieving a successful re-light within acceptable altitude limits, discontinue the
re-light and eject or force land. At higher altitudes you will have more time to carry out
the air-start procedure according to the checklist and decide whether an airfield is within
a safe gliding distance.
13.4
PROCEDURE FROM ENGINE FAILURE TO HIGH KEY
6.
After completing a zoom, establish the aircraft in a glide at 125 KIAS. If the de-
cision is made to attempt a forced landing, turn towards the nearest suitable airfield and
carry out the following steps while maintaining an effective lookout:
a.
determine if you have sufficient altitude to reach High Key maintaining a
minimum of 125 KIAS;
b.
declare a MAYDAY and advise ATC of your position, altitude, nature of the
emergency, and your intentions;
c.
time permitting, carry out appropriate checklist procedures if required;
d.
monitor the glide profile while proceeding to the airfield to ensure that you
can still reach High Key. Strong headwinds could adversely affect your glide,
making it necessary to proceed directly to Low Key, Final Key, or to eject if
the landing surface cannot be reached; and
e.
initiate the Forced-Landing Check immediately upon deciding that a forced
landing will be attempted. It is critical, after determining that the engine will
not re-light, to place the PCL in the CUT-OFF position so that the propeller
will feather. Also be sure to pull the emergency firewall shut-off handle. This
will cut off fuel flow and hydraulic fluid flow to the engine as well as cut off
air flow and hydraulic fluid flow from the engine reducing the risk of fire.
13.5
GLIDING PERFORMANCE
7.
The Harvard II glides approximately 2.0 NM for each 1000’ of altitude; therefore,
because many of your exercises are flown in the local area at high altitude, you may be

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 45 =====
Figure 13.1: Steps Leading to High Key
able to glide to an aerodrome in the event of an engine malfunction. To determine the
maximum gliding distance, calculate the distance to fly using the best available means
(GPS, DME, RADAR, and visually) and divide this number by 2. Multiply the result
by 1000 and add to your key altitude. For example, if you are 5 NM from desired
aerodrome (Moose Jaw):
a.
5 / 2 x 1000 + High Key altitude = Minimum altitude required to reach High
Key
b.
5 / 2 x 1000 + 5000’ MSL (ideal High Key altitude at Moose Jaw) = 7500’
MSL
8.
This tells you that at 5 NM from High Key you should be at an altitude of at least
7500’ MSL in order to glide to High Key at 125 KIAS. You can vary the airspeed, rate-
of-descent and flight path to arrive at High Key, which is over the button of the runway,
between 3000–4000’ AGL. The ideal situation is to arrive at High Key at an airspeed
of 120 KIAS with the landing gear down. Excess altitude can be lost, but altitude can
never be regained.
CAUTION
Never reduce your airspeed below the recommended gliding speed in
order to stretch your glide. Below this speed, the aircraft is in the area
of reverse command (behind the power curve) and the increased drag
causes a higher rate of descent.
9.
If you are unable to determine your distance, you may use the following method to
assess whether you can glide to the airport. This method is based on judging movement
of the runway relative to a fixed position on the windscreen. Over a short period of time,

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 46 =====
you should be able to determine if the runway appears to move up or down from that
position. If the runway stays in the same place, you should be on a glide profile that will
take you to the runway. If the runway appears to move up the windscreen, it is likely that
you are below the required glide profile. With practice, you will be able to visualize the
High Key position on the windscreen and apply the same method to assess your ability
to glide to that point. This technique is effective until you are within approximately 5
NM from High Key, at which time the runway threshold will begin to disappear from
view under the nose of the aircraft. If you have maintained the proper sight-picture you
should be able to reach High Key close to the ideal altitude.
10.
If it becomes apparent that High Key is not attainable, assess if you can make Low
Key or Final Key. This can be determined in the same manner as that used for High Key
described in the paragraph above of this chapter, except you need to add Low or Final
Key altitude instead of High Key altitude.
11.
If it becomes apparent that you are above the ideal glide profile, gliding at a speed
above 125 KIAS can increase the rate of descent in order to arrive at High Key. You
will need to monitor your altitude closely in order to ensure that you re-establish the 125
KIAS glide before you descend below the ideal glide profile. If you still have excess
altitude as you are approaching the High Key position, you may choose to lower the
landing gear to increase your rate of descent. If you are at High Key with 2000’ excess
altitude, one orbit at 125 KIAS and 30 degrees of bank in a clean configuration will lose
approximately 1700’. An orbit at 120 KIAS and 30 degrees of bank with the landing
gear down will lose approximately 2600’. If you were to arrive at High Key 10,000’
high, the most effective and expeditious technique to complete the forced landing would
be to make a rapid, but controlled descent. Adjust the turning radius, rate of descent and
airspeed to enable the aircraft to arrive over the ideal High Key.
12.
As you approach High Key, you will have some difficulty seeing the threshold of
the runway. In order to establish an accurate High Key position, you should extend an
imaginary line out from the upwind end of the runway and select the Low Key position
prior to reaching High Key. By aligning the aircraft fuselage with this imaginary line
and waiting until you are abeam the Low Key position, you are able to fix yourself over
the threshold of the runway. It is important to fly to a precise High Key position because
the forced-landing pattern is designed to be initiated from High Key on speed, heading,
and within the 1000’ window (3000–4000’ AGL).
13.6
THE FORCED-LANDING PATTERN
13.
The Forced Landing Pattern is a descending circular pattern approximately 1 NM
in diameter (Figures 13.2 and 13.3). The Harvard II will lose approximately 2600’ in a
360-degree turn at 120 KIAS with 30 degrees of bank and the landing gear down. With
a High Key altitude of 3000’ AGL an extra 400’ is provided to the pilot to use drag
devices and roll the wings level on short final.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 47 =====
14.
When applied properly, this pattern will allow you to fly an accurate engine out
approach to any airfield. In order to fly an accurate pattern, you must strive to eliminate
variables under your control which affect either your aircraft performance or the forced
landing pattern. For example, if you do not maintain your airspeed, this will directly
affect your descent profile and make it very difficult to assess your pattern. Do not
alter the size of the pattern or the airspeed at which it is flown as this renders all key
information invalid and requires very fine judgment. The Harvard II is designed to fly
the pattern as described and can easily lose any excess altitude if required.
15.
The pattern has three reference points named High Key, Low Key, and Final Key.
These keys are used as checkpoints during your pattern to assess your glide profile and
to aid you in determining if you are high, low, or on profile. Always fly to your key
points and ensure you cross over them with the correct orientation. As you cross over
your key points, check your altitude and compare it to the desired. This can help you
quickly determine how your profile is doing. In general, if you are high at a key point,
you can correct this by selecting drag devices, or you can continue with excess altitude
if you know it is created by outside factors such as wind. If you are low at a key point,
delay drag device selection and fly direct to your next key point.
Figure 13.2: PFL pattern RWY 29L no wind
13.7
HIGH KEY
16.
Plan to reach High Key with 120 KIAS and the landing gear down. On reaching
High Key, which is situated over the button of the runway, if you are at the ideal altitude,

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 48 =====
Figure 13.3: PFL pattern RWY 11R no wind
start a turn using approximately 30 degrees of bank in the direction of the selected Low
Key position. If the aircraft is higher than ideal at the High Key position, continue along
the landing path until you have lost one half of the excess altitude, then begin the turn
to Low Key (Figure 13.4). This technique is very effective as long as you have reached
High Key between 3000–4000’ AGL, but it is not recommended if you are outside these
parameters. Do not increase the radius of the orbit to lose extra altitude. This requires
fine judgment and renders the information gathered from your ensuing keys invalid.
13.8
LOW KEY
17.
Your next check-point is Low Key, which is 180 degrees around the pattern from
High Key, at approximately 1700’ AGL (3700’ MSL in Moose Jaw). Ensure you cross
over Low Key with 180 degrees of turn remaining and check your altitude. Do not
extend if you are high. Continue your turn towards your Final Key position and make
any necessary corrections to glide path. T/O flap is normally selected between Low Key
and Final Key. If time permits during the forced-landing pattern, transmit to the tower
at each of the key positions.
13.9
FINAL KEY
18.
Your last checkpoint is the Final Key position. It is located 270 degrees around
the pattern from High Key, and the aircraft should be approximately 1000’ AGL (3000’

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 49 =====
Figure 13.4: PFL Pattern with False High and Low Key
MSL in Moose Jaw). Ensure you cross Final Key with 90 degrees of turn remaining to
the runway and check your altitude. After crossing this point, select an initial touchdown
point one-third of the distance along the runway. Alter your bank angles slightly to roll
wings level prior to the runway. When attaining the selected touchdown point is assured,
it can be brought closer to the runway threshold by selecting flaps as required. Maintain
120 KIAS throughout the entire pattern.
13.10
LANDING TRANSITION
19.
Once the aircraft is aligned on final the transition to land is commenced. This tran-
sition should be completed in a two-stage round-out; the first stage reduces (checks) the

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 50 =====
rate of descent and resets the attitude to a normal landing transition. The second stage
is simply a normal round-out/flare. Touchdown will normally occur at 80-90 KIAS.
Throughout the landing transition, maintain 120 KIAS until the first stage flare, and the
minimum threshold crossing speed for your flaps setting until the second stage flare.
13.11
SIDE-SLIPPING
20.
The Harvard II has the capability of performing full rudder side-slips; however,
in the training environment, side-slips with the landing gear down shall be avoided.
This manoeuvre can be used as a means of increasing rate of descent without increasing
airspeed, configuration, or power setting. Rates of descent in excess of 4000 fpm have
been accomplished when side-slipping. It is not intended that this manoeuvre should
be used as a normal means to lose altitude but rather as a last solution to lose excess
altitude. To execute a side-slip effectively, apply rudder and use opposite aileron as
required to control the angle of bank so that you will track toward the desired point. The
rate of descent will vary proportionally with the amount of rudder input. It is extremely
important to note that your pitch attitude still controls your airspeed and that you will
have to lower the nose to compensate for the increased drag in an effort to maintain the
same gliding airspeed. Do not trim the elevator to maintain this new attitude. To turn
the aircraft while side-slipping always use top rudder then bank as required to perform
the turn. However, never use more than 45 degrees of bank or let the nose rise above
the horizon. When the decision is made to stop side-slipping smoothly remove the cross
control inputs and adjust the pitch attitude to control the airspeed. This attitude should
already be trimmed for if the aircraft was properly trimmed prior to beginning the side-
slip.
13.12
WIND EFFECT
21.
The forced-landing pattern is designed to be circular under zero or light wind con-
ditions. A strong wind has considerable effect on the aircraft’s ground track, and the
pattern will be shifted downwind unless there is some compensation made.
22.
There are two methods used to compensate for wind in the PFL. Both methods are
effective; however, you must always strive to fly the aircraft to the desired key points
and have the aircraft properly oriented. Although the pattern is designed to be flown at
30 degrees of bank, alter your bank as required to be on heading over the key.
a.
Method one is accomplished by varying the bank to fly over the normal key
positions. You will then, in effect, fly a greater time through the air during
half of the pattern which will cause you to lose more altitude into wind;
conversely, during the other half of the pattern, you will spend less time and
lose less altitude while flying with a tailwind component. For this reason,

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 51 =====
you must consider wind effect when you check your altitude at the keys to
accurately decide when to select flap.
b.
Method two is accomplished by moving each key position into wind so that
you can fly a normal pattern to the touchdown point. This technique is used
primarily for strong winds however it is difficult to judge precisely. It is
recommended that you combine both methods in strong winds by moving
the keys into wind and adjusting the pattern by varying bank as required to
land on the runway. For example, if there is a strong wind straight down
the runway, delay the turn at High Key so that the aircraft arrives upwind of
the ideal position. If you do not delay the turn, you will arrive at Low Key
altitude past the end of the runway.
Figure 13.5: PFL Pattern in Strong Headwind
23.
A strong crosswind has an even more adverse effect. If you use 30 degrees of
bank, a strong crosswind from the direction in which you orbit will cause Low Key to
be too close to the runway. If this is not corrected, the shorter path remaining to the
end of the runway will cause the aircraft to overshoot the centerline of the runway, or
be unacceptably high on final. Thus, you must vary the turn by using less bank so that
the aircraft ends up at the correct Low Key position. Also, you must use more bank
on the final turn so that the aircraft does not overshoot the extended centerline. Should
the crosswind be from the other side, thus causing the aircraft to drift away from the
runway, use more bank in the first half of the turn and less in the second half. Avoid the
requirement to use excessive bank on final, at low airspeed, in an attempt to line up on
the runway centerline.
13.13
PATTERN VARIATIONS
24.
You cannot always fly the ideal pattern. There may not be sufficient altitude to
allow the aircraft to glide to High Key by 3000’ AGL. Remember that the pattern is

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 52 =====
Figure 13.6: PFL Pattern in Strong Crosswind
designed to allow you to be reasonably sure of making a successful forced landing,
provided that the aircraft reaches one of the key positions, at the correct altitude and on
the correct heading. If you cannot reach High Key, fly directly to Low Key and delay
lowering the landing gear if necessary. If you are doubtful about reaching Low Key, go
directly to Final Key. If possible, avoid making large heading changes when aligning
yourself with the appropriate key position; instead, go to the reachable key position that
is most directly aligned with your current heading.
13.14
PRACTICE FORCED LANDINGS
25.
All Practice Forced Landings (PFLs) will be conducted to an aerodrome. To simu-
late the glide performance of a failed and feathered engine, set the PCL to 4–6% torque.
The pattern is identical to that described above, except that you must transmit to the
tower so that ATC can regulate traffic. Before beginning a PFL, ask for clearance and
remember to transmit at High Key, Low Key, and/or at Final Key, as required by local
flying orders.
26.
Many diverse scenarios may be forced upon you due to unforeseen circumstances.
Your instructor will discuss these with you and have you practice variations to the ideal
pattern. Remember that the best gliding speed is 125 KIAS prior to High Key and 120
KIAS with the landing gear extended. Never attempt to gain more distance by raising
the nose and reducing the airspeed: this is termed “stretching” the glide. Should you
be low on final because of an error in judgment, maintain 120 KIAS until the first-stage
flare. Airspeed may then be reduced to cross the threshold at the appropriate threshold

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 53 =====
crossing speed for your configuration.
WARNING
When conducting a practice forced landing, the following requirements
shall be met by 200ft AGL:
a.
runway will be reached
b.
minimum 120 KIAS until initiation of 1st stage flare
c.
landing gear is down
d.
flaps selected to minimum take-off position (may be travelling)
e.
less than 45 degrees angle of bank
f.
aircraft heading is within +/- 30 degrees of the runway head-
ing.
If any of the preceding requirements are not met, a go around shall be
initiated.
13.15
LANDING ON AN UNPREPARED SURFACE
27.
Forced landing on an unprepared surface is not recommended. The ejection system
in the Harvard II is very reliable and you must not sacrifice aircrew safety. If circum-
stances are severe enough that a forced landing to an unprepared surface is deemed to
be of less risk than ejecting, the following paragraphs provide some useful information
on unprepared surface selection.
28.
If you cannot reach an airfield and circumstances are such that you decide to force
land rather than eject, the best area for a forced landing is a level, hard-packed, long,
smooth field with no high objects on the approach path nor, preferably, anywhere near
it. However, since there is no guarantee that you will be near a suitable field when you
have an engine failure, you must be able to pick out the best available field. Cultivated
fields are good, but some fields used for pasturing animals are of doubtful value because
they usually contain tree stumps or large boulders. On the prairies, the best criterion for
deciding if the field is flat is its color. Usually, fields of one color are flat. Avoid fields
which are crossed by power lines, ditches, or have trees and other natural hazards. If
you have a choice, select a field near habitation or at least near a road.
29.
When choosing a suitable forced-landing field, take length into account. If there is
a strong headwind, the distance needed for the landing is comparatively short; however,
in a calm wind, the landing distance is extended, and the field must be proportionally
longer.

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 54 =====
30.
If possible, land into wind. Nature has many ways of disclosing the direction and
strength of the wind, smoke being the most reliable. If smoke rises slowly and drifts
away, the wind is light, if it rises and then drops down close to the earth — the wind
is strong or gusty. Grass and grain ripples in the direction of the wind and dust drifts
with the wind. If you are unable to decide which way the wind is blowing, use the wind
direction at the time of take-off. If you are unable to land into wind because of a lack of
altitude or a lack of suitable field into wind, do a crosswind landing or, as a last resort, a
downwind landing. Aim to touch down as soon as the aircraft crosses the approach end
of the selected field.
31.
When you have selected a field, stick to your choice and prepare the aircraft for
a forced landing. Time permitting, squawk emergency, transmit MAYDAY on Guard
and complete the forced-landing check. Fly the forced-landing pattern as if you were
landing on a runway. It is not recommended to extend the gear or flaps if landing on an
unprepared surface.
32.
On touchdown, the deceleration may be rapid but, provided that you are prop-
erly strapped in, you should suffer no adverse effects. As soon as the aircraft stops,
carry out a ground egress. If there is no evidence of fire, remove and comply with the
forced-landing instructions. If there is a fire, do not attempt to save any equipment, but
ensure the safety of yourself and anyone else in the vicinity. Inform your unit of your
whereabouts as soon as possible.
13.16
DITCHING
33.
Ditching the Harvard II is not recommended because the aircraft will sink quickly;
therefore, eject when it becomes obvious that you are to be forced down over water.
Remember to use your radio to your full advantage and transmit the standard emer-
gency calls. Give yourself plenty of time to deploy the parachute manually should the
automatic feature not work. Never delay ejection below 2000’ above the water.
13.17
FORCED LANDING FROM THE TRAFFIC PATTERN
34.
Except under extreme wind conditions and in the early part of the departure leg,
the airspeed and altitude flown in the traffic pattern are such that the aircraft should be
able to glide to the runway-in-use from most points in the traffic pattern. If you suspect
an engine malfunction, take time to analyze the situation, but remember a more rapid
response is required when in the traffic pattern than when at altitude. Your immediate
response to a confirmed loss of power should be:
a.
zoom straight ahead to capture 125 KIAS;

===== SMM part 5 PDF page 55 =====
b.
when able, commence a turn towards the runway and assess the situation. If
the decision is made to attempt a forced landing, choose the closest suitable
key position for the most suitable runway; and
c.
if a scan of the instruments indicates an engine failure, the PCL must be
moved to OFF as soon as possible as the un-feathered propeller will greatly
reduce glide distance.
NOTE
The zoom is a basic technique to exchange airspeed for altitude; how-
ever, depending on your position in the traffic pattern and actual weather
conditions, remember that unnecessarily delaying your turn towards the
runway prior to reaching the apex of the zoom could severely jeopardize
your chances of successfully landing the aircraft.
35.
Apply the same considerations as per the forced landing procedures described
above when making the decision whether to eject or carry out a forced landing. The
decision must be made quickly because of the close proximity to the ground. Remem-
ber that the safety of the crew is paramount in any abnormal situation. Don’t discount all
your options early. Remember that you may have the option of landing in the opposite
direction on the runway in use, or utilizing another runway. Winds and traffic should be
factored into your assessment.
36.
Once the decision has been made to attempt a forced landing determine the ap-
propriate checklist response. Checklist actions should be taken as time permits; how-
ever, the air-start procedure is not recommended below 2000’ AGL, as primary attention
should be to eject or safely recover the aircraft
37.
If there is time available, attempt a MAYDAY call, giving position and intentions.
38.
Do not attempt to fly to a key position if you will arrive there off the required
heading but rather, join the pattern at a tangent at the most suitable point. Be cognizant
of any potential traffic conflicts that may arise by doing so. You are extremely limited
in manoeuvrability and other traffic may not see you if you are not in the regular flow.
39.
Do not lower the landing gear or T/O flap until you are sure that the aircraft will
reach the runway. Concentrate on making a safe approach and landing. Do not stretch
the glide in an attempt to reach the runway. If you are approaching inside Final Key,
and are not in a favorable position to make the runway, it would be wise to eject. The
longer you delay, the closer you put yourself to the limits of the ejection envelope.
40.
Under certain circumstances, such as during the final turn or on straight-in final, a
zoom is not possible. Attempting to raise the nose rapidly could result in a stall. In such
a situation, you will have very little time to decide whether to eject or forced land. It is

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 1 =====
essential that you maintain flying airspeed and, if your decision is to eject, do so as soon
as possible. Although not recommended, if your decision is to attempt a landing on an
unprepared surface, apply the unprepared surface considerations detailed earlier.
13.18
PRACTICE FORCED LANDINGS FROM THE TRAFFIC PATTERN
41.
Carry out a PFL from the traffic pattern according to the guidelines given in this
chapter applying the considerations and techniques described above. Make the initial
request for the exercise. Concentrate on flying the aircraft while making the appropriate
key transmissions.
CAUTION
When practicing forced landings, ensure that you:
a.
do not move the PCL to OFF; and
b.
do not pull the emergency firewall shutoff handle
Figure 13.7: Simulated PFL from the Pattern

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 2 =====
CHAPTER 14: ADVANCED TRAFFIC PATTERN AND
AIR HANDLING
14.1
AOA APPROACH AND LANDING
1.
Most approaches in the Harvard II are normally flown at a pre-determined airspeed
that is more or less independent of fuel weight. This airspeed combines a margin of
safety for student training while maintaining an acceptably short after-landing roll. An
AOA approach is flown using the same patterns as a normal straight-in approach while
maintaining optimum AOA to touchdown. This technique reduces approach and landing
speed and will minimize after-landing roll. Many circumstances or combinations of
circumstances may require a pilot to want to minimize their after landing roll. The
following are examples:
a.
short runway;
b.
icy or contaminated runway surface; and
c.
brake failure.
2.
An AOA approach is normally accomplished from a straight-in approach. Dur-
ing the approach, transition your cross-check from the airspeed indicator to the AOA
gauge/indexer. The AOA approach speed is approximately equivalent to 90 KIAS plus
fuel in hundreds of pounds (e.g. with 700 lbs of fuel onboard, AOA approach speed
should be approximately 97 KIAS). Select LDG flap, adjust pitch to prevent ballooning
and reduce your power. As the aircraft slows, adjust pitch to maintain the glide path.
Continue to allow the AOA to increase until the desired AOA is achieved (amber donut
on the AOA gauge/indexer). At this point readjust PCL to maintain the AOA and the de-
sired aim point. The aircraft should be configured and stable “on AOA” prior to passing
through the window.
233

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 3 =====
3.
Continue the approach as per normal approach using the AOA and runway as your
sources of information. Due to the higher drag associated with the slower approach
speeds, simultaneous pitch and power changes may be required to maintain a stabilized
approach to a pre-determined aim point. Maintain your cross-check through final ap-
proach up to the round-out. At this point reduce PCL to idle and allow the aircraft to
land. Due to the reduced airspeed, touchdown will occur quickly and may be firmer
than for a normal landing.
CAUTION
A go around shall be considered if the approach is not stabilized on AOA
anytime inside the window. Three conditions where you must consider
going around include: excessive AOA, steeper than normal glide path,
or grossly undershooting the intended aim point.
14.2
FLAPLESS FINAL TURN
4.
After you have gained proficiency at flying a straight-in flapless approach, your
instructor will have you practice flapless final turns from the overhead break or closed
pattern. Follow the normal procedure up to the point where you would normally config-
ure. With the flaps remaining up, select the torque to approximately 30% and maintain
120 KIAS on downwind. Commence the final turn at the normal perch point, but set a
slightly shallower sight picture than with flaps. Maintain 120 KIAS in the final turn. Be-
cause of the reduced drag without the flaps, the torque setting will be slightly less than
normal (approximately 10–15% torque). Remember that the minimum speed during the
final turn is final approach speed plus 10 (120 KIAS). Thus the normal final turn speed
and minimum final turn speed are coincidental and must be monitored closely. Roll out
on final as you would in a normal final turn, reducing your speed to flapless threshold
crossing speed (110 KIAS). Proceed for the flapless landing transition as previously
learned during your phase II training.
14.3
AIR HANDLING
5.
The purpose of conducting advanced handling exercises is to learn the advanced
handling characteristics of the Harvard II. During basic aerobatics the emphasis was
placed on rate control at pre-determined airspeeds in order to complete the actual ma-
noeuvre. This advanced phase allows the pilot to learn the basics of energy management
and to fly the Harvard II in the maximum performance regime by properly using its Lift
Vector (LV).
6.
Advanced air handling is subdivided into two parts: advanced aerobatics includ-
ing the Barrel Roll, the Vertical Eight, the Vertical Roll, and Off-speed aerobatics; and
advanced handling exercises including Maximum Performance Turns, the Pitch Back,

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 4 =====
the Slice, the Split-S, and the Immelmann. The advanced handling manoeuvres have a
practical application in future fighter pilot manoeuvring. The emphasis in this type of
flying is placed on max performing the aircraft and learning how to achieve the desired
exit parameters regardless of the entry parameters. For training purposes, max perfor-
mance parameters for the Harvard II have been selected as 160 kts and 17 AoA. Exiting
the advanced handling exercises at 160 kts enables the pilot to enter a max performance
turn in relation to a simulated threat.
7.
Advanced handling manoeuvres can be performed at different entry speeds. To
provide a starting point for these manoeuvres, the following paragraphs describe entry
speeds and techniques to simplify the learning process. However, during the advanced
handling manoeuvres, power, speed brake, angle of bank, and AoA will be varied to
achieve the desired exit parameters as described in the paragraphs to follow. Energy
management requires the pilot to be aware of the energy state of the aircraft throughout
the manoeuvre. If the desired parameters are not being achieved then you are expected
to vary the control inputs to improve the placement of your LV in order to achieve the
proper exit parameters.
14.4
ENERGY MANAGEMENT
8.
The emphasis while performing max performance aerobatic manoeuvres in the
Harvard II is placed on AOA control. During most manoeuvres in the Harvard II, the
best turn rate will be achieved at max power and 17 Units of AOA. Being able to fly at
high AOA is an important skill that is required for your follow on training as a fighter
pilot.
9.
Entry airspeeds, load, and power settings for advanced aerobatics are included in
Table 14.1. It must be stressed that these are intended as entry parameters only. Once
the manoeuvre has been initiated, AOA control becomes the primary aim throughout
the exercise with the exception of the barrel roll. Developing the ability to effectively
control AOA will be paramount in advanced handling exercises.

14.5
CONSIDERATIONS
10.
Altitude. The aircraft’s performance during aerobatics varies considerably with
altitude. It is recommended that aerobatics be carried out below 16,000’ MSL due to
the loss in performance at greater altitudes. The SMM recommended entry speeds are
predicated on altitudes of approximately 10,000’ MSL.
11.
Control Effectiveness. Speed plays an important part in control effectiveness. The
aircraft stall margin is reduced at lower speed and/or higher G loading. At high AOA,
the aircraft will be on the verge of a high speed stall during most of the manoeuvres. At
higher speeds, it is easier to overstress the aircraft due to the increased sensitivity of the
flight controls. Regardless of the condition of flight, the pilot must develop a feel for the
aircraft that helps prevent a stall or overstress situation from developing. Smooth and
coordinated flight control handling will ensure the success of each manoeuvre.
12.
Trim. Before conducting any aerobatic manoeuvres, the aircraft should be trimmed
to a selected speed, normally the entry speed. This trim setting should not be re-adjusted
during aerobatics, as this will ensure that the control pressures will feel the same every
time a particular aerobatic manoeuvre is performed.
13.
Ballistic. Ballistic is the term used to describe the projectile state flight behavior
of your aircraft. When ballistic, it is implied that while the airplane is still flying, you
are unable to control the direction of your platform and are therefore unable to predict
your exact flight path. Flight controls are ineffective due to the lack of energy and the
airplane is being directed mostly by potential energy and inertia. The term “ballistic”
also warns other aircraft about your inability both to maintain positive control of your
flight path and to effectively avoid traffic. Once ballistic, the inadvertent departure from
controlled flight red page response should be carried out.
14.
Lookout. The type of lookout performed to clear the area will vary according to
the complexity of the aerobatic manoeuvre. Although the area should be cleared in all
directions, special emphasis should be placed on those directions in which the aircraft
will be moving. Aerobatics are clearing manoeuvres in themselves so it is essential to
maintain a good lookout throughout each manoeuvre. Avoid channelized attention and
maintain proper lookout.
15.
Pre-Spin, Stall, Aerobatics Check (PSSA). Prior to conducting any aerobatic ma-
noeuvres, carry out a thorough PSSA Check. If conducting multiple manoeuvres, it is
not necessary to complete additional PSSA Checks. Provided the interval between ma-
noeuvres is relatively short, only a few of the items need to be rechecked. These include
as a minimum, but are not restricted to, fuel, area suitability, altitude considerations, and
lookout.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 6 =====
14.6
HARD DECK
16.
The Harvard II hard deck in the Moose Jaw training areas is set at 3000’ AGL
(roughly 6000’ MSL). The hard deck simulates the ground during training while pro-
viding several thousand feet safety margin from the real ground. Any time you go below
the hard deck in training simulates that you have impacted the earth while attempting to
manoeuvre. Introducing the hard deck concept during advanced handling will help you
build altitude awareness within your cross-check which will pay off during your future
training. Once you have breached the hard deck you shall immediately discontinue any
manoeuvring while setting a climb attitude and calling “Hard Deck”.
14.7
SOFT DECK
17.
The soft deck concept is similar to the hard deck one however it is primarily used
to raise altitude awareness of the impending hard deck breach if your airplane’s nose is
below the horizon. The soft deck should never be lower than 1000’ above the hard deck
and is directly proportional to your aircraft’s turning performance. Therefore, it could
be set higher depending on the manoeuvres you want to perform. The soft deck altitude
should be an altitude sufficient for you to recover from a significant diving attitude
providing that max G is used. Anytime you go below the soft deck, awareness must
be displayed through appropriate manoeuvring away from the deck combined with an
appropriate “Soft Deck” call.
14.8
THE BARREL ROLL
18.
The barrel roll is a coordination manoeuvre in which the aircraft nose, as seen
from the cockpit, appears to describe a circle about a point on the horizon. The actual
flight-path of the aircraft is seen in the Figure 14.1.
19.
The barrel roll is started with PCL set to MAX and the aircraft aligned with a ref-
erence line. At 230 KIAS, the nose of the aircraft is raised for the start of the manoeuvre
and aileron is blended in to begin the roll. The aileron and back pressure are regulated
so that the aircraft reaches a position 45 degrees off the original reference line with 45
degrees of pitch up and 90 degrees of bank. Coordinated roll and back pressure is con-
tinued until the aircraft reaches a position 90 degrees off the original reference line in a
level flight, inverted attitude. As the airspeed decreases, the aileron deflection must be
increased to maintain a constant rate of roll. The back pressure must be reduced to keep
the nose moving at a constant rate and from dropping too quickly. As the nose drops
through the horizon the airspeed will begin to increase. Coordinated aileron and back
pressure are continued until the aircraft returns to a position 45 degrees from the origi-
nal reference line, but this time with 45 degrees pitch down and 90 degrees of bank. As
the airspeed continues to increase, the amount of aileron deflection must be decreased to
keep the same rate of roll. The back pressure must be increased to keep the nose moving

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 7 =====
Figure 14.1: Barrel Roll
at a constant rate. The manoeuvre is completed when the aircraft reaches the level flight
attitude at 230 KIAS and aligned with the original reference line.
14.9
OFF-SPEED AEROBATICS
20.
For simplicity, an off-speed aerobatic manoeuvre is defined as any aerobatic ma-
noeuvre that is entered at an airspeed and/or power setting other than those listed for the
standard manoeuvres. To further develop your overall aerobatic proficiency, aerobatics
may be performed throughout the entire range of recommended entry airspeeds. In gen-
eral, the minimum airspeed for looping and rolling manoeuvres is 200 or 180 KIAS,
respectively, and a maximum entry speed of 250 KIAS.
21.
For higher airspeed aerobatics, the entry airspeed may range from the normal to
the maximum. The power may be the same as for the standard manoeuvre or it may be
varied as desired. The back pressure will have to be adjusted to obtain the desired result
of completing the manoeuvre and recovering with the desired airspeed.
22.
Slower speed aerobatics are entered at lower than standard airspeeds. The back
pressure will be adjusted according to the conditions. When a maximum power setting
is used, normal G-loading may be sufficient to perform the manoeuvre; however, at
standard PCL settings and/or lower entry airspeeds, maximum obtainable G may be
required.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 8 =====
23.
Performing aerobatics at slower airspeeds requires certain considerations. On en-
try, the aircraft is closer to the basic stalling speed; therefore, the possibility of a high
speed stall is increased. There will be less G available during slow-speed aerobatics
because of the increased stalling speed at the higher G-loading. The slower airspeed
will result in a smaller turning radius. As the altitude increases, the reduced thrust and
the IAS–TAS relationship will further complicate the success of the manoeuvres.
24.
During off-speed aerobatics, exit airspeeds are generally derived from the entry
airspeed. If the entry and exit airspeeds are the same in a standard manoeuvre, then,
generally, both speeds will be the same in an off-speed manoeuvre. If the entry and exit
airspeeds are different in the standard manoeuvre, then, generally, the same difference
will be applied to the speeds of an off-speed manoeuvre.
14.10
SLOW SPEED LOOP
25.
A slow speed loop in the Harvard II is any loop entered below the normal entry
speed of 230 kts. Regardless of the entry airspeed, the aim is to max perform the aircraft
by flying at max PCL and in stick shaker (ideally 17 AOA). Considerations must be
given to the following:
a.
aircraft is closer to the basic stalling speed; therefore, the possibility of a high
speed stall is increased;
b.
there is less G available;
c.
the aircraft has a smaller turn radius;
d.
the torque effect is much greater at slow speed with max PCL and;
e.
as the altitude increases, the reduced thrust and the IAS–TAS relationship
decreases therefore reducing the overall aircraft performance.
26.
Enter the slow speed loop from level flight above the hard deck and at no less
than 200 KIAS. Select PCL to MAX and smoothly apply backpressure to capture stick
shaker (ideally not to exceed 17 AOA). Eliminate any roll using the same techniques
as per the normal loop. Caution must be exercised to avoid a high-speed stall as the
aircraft approaches the inverted position. The airspeed at the inverted position will be
significantly less than for a normal loop; thus it will be difficult to maintain 17 AOA
over the top of the slow speed loop. Extreme caution must be exercised to avoid stalling
the aircraft. Back pressure shall be applied appropriately to exit at the desired speed.
CAUTION
The minimum recommended looping speed for the Harvard II is 200
KIAS.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 9 =====
14.11
LOW LEVEL AEROBATICS
27.
Manoeuvres are flown the same manner at low level as at higher altitudes. Avoid
the tendency to increase the G on the back half of looping manoeuvres during low level
aerobatics. This is caused by the increased sensation of ground rush at lower altitudes.
14.12
LIFT VECTOR (LV)
28.
Lift vector is generally defined as the direction of lift generated by the aircraft’s
wings. The LV acts perpendicular to the wings, or more appropriately, you can judge
and therefore manoeuvre your LV by looking directly up through the canopy MDC. In
a high G turn, your LV defines your plane of motion and will always point at the centre
of your turn circle.
14.13
TURN PERFORMANCE
29.
Turn rate is the ability to change nose position over time and is normally measured
in degrees per second. In simple terms, it is a measure of how quickly an aircraft can
generate a turn. In the fighter world, there are two types of rates that are important.
Instantaneous rate and sustained rate. Instantaneous rate is what the aircraft can generate
with its energy available at the beginning of the turn. Sustained turn rate is the rate the
aircraft can maintain without bleeding airspeed. This rate can be maximized by trading
potential energy for kinetic energy (i.e. descending to increase airspeed). The most
important factor in a combat engagement is whoever can change their nose position the
fastest (highest turn rate), will be able to point the nose of their aircraft at the bandit first
and therefore win the fight. Turn rate (W) is determined by the following formula:
W = g Gr
V
,
where V is TAS in units of ft/sec, Gr is radial G, and constant g = 32.2ft/sec2.
30.
Turn radius provides the baseline for defining a manoeuvring aircraft’s turn per-
formance. Speed and G are the two basic parameters that define an aircraft’s turn radius.
A change in either of these parameters will immediately change the turn radius. Turn
radius (R) is determined by the following formula:
R = V 2
g Gr
,
where V is TAS in units of ft/sec, Gr is radial G, and constant g = 32.2ft/sec2.
31.
The optimum turn rate in the Harvard II occurs at approximately 160 KIAS and
17 units AOA. Most advanced handling exercises require the pilot to manage energy to
capture or maintain this optimum turn rate, regardless of the entry parameters.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 10 =====
14.14
MAX PERFORMANCE TURNS
32.
The concept of max performing the aircraft is of the utmost importance to the
fighter pilot. Depending on the flight path geometry and performance of your aircraft
relative to your adversary, you will need to manoeuvre for either maximum turn rate
or minimum turn radius. In a jet aircraft, due to the relationship of power production
and airspeed, the maximum turn rate is normally achieved at a higher airspeed than the
minimum turn radius. The Harvard II, being a turboprop powered aircraft, is subject to
a different aerodynamic relationship between turn rate and radius. Both maximum turn
rate and minimum turn radius are achieved at the aerodynamic lift limit (i.e. just below
the critical AOA). Consider the complete stoppage of turn rate during a high speed
stall. Any momentary advantage in turn rate is quickly lost if the aircraft is mishandled
resulting in a stall. The aspiring fighter pilot must learn to fly smoothly and aggressively
in order to get the maximum performance from their aircraft.
33.
In order to prepare trainees for future fighter manoeuvring, the focus in advanced
handling is to achieve maximum performance by managing energy and AOA within
the stick shaker range of the Harvard II. The aircraft should not be flown at an AOA
high enough that the aircraft stalls (i.e. 18 AOA) because stalling will negatively affect
performance.
34.
Level MPT (LMPT). This manoeuvre simulates manoeuvring at the hard deck.
To obtain the maximum rate of turn in level flight, the angle of attack must be as high as
possible without inducing a high speed stall. The rate of turn increases as the angle of
attack increases and, for a given airspeed, the radius of turn decreases at the same time.
As the load factor increases, drag and stall speed also increase. Once the stick shaker
activates, full power must be used during the turn in order to overcome the deceleration
due to increased induced drag. In a level (constant altitude) maximum performance turn,
the airspeed will stabilize at approximately 150 KIAS minus the altitude in thousands
of feet.
35.
To maintain a level MPT, adjust stick back pressure to maintain the AOA within
stick shaker range without stalling. When stabilized, the nose attitude is slightly higher
than for a steep turn and the AOB is approximately 75 degrees. Level flight is always
maintained by varying the bank rather than pitch. If the nose is too high, maintain back
pressure, increase bank, and allow the nose to fall to the correct attitude. If the nose
is too low, maintain back pressure, decrease bank, and allow the nose to climb to the
correct attitude. Any attempt to control the attitude with the elevators will cause the
aircraft to either stall or fly below the optimum angle-of-attack. All control movements
must be coordinated.
36.
The level MPT may be entered from a variety of energy states. Always perform a
thorough lookout prior to entry. An academic entry involves slowing to the anticipated
stabilized speed, rolling into the turn, smoothly applying sufficient back pressure to
activate the stick shaker, and applying full power. The level MPT may also be entered

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 11 =====
from a descending CSMPT (defined below) by raising the attitude once at the desired
altitude and allowing the speed to decay. To enter the level MPT from a higher airspeed,
simultaneously roll into the turn and set the PCL to mid-range (i.e. ‘stand up the PCL’).
Smoothly apply back pressure and positively increase the G (up to approximately 4 G)
and allow the speed to bleed off. Once the stick shaker activates, advance the PCL to
MAX and maintain the level MPT as described above.
37.
Constant Speed MPT (CSMPT). The CSMPT simulates manoeuvring above the
hard deck in a two circle (rate) engagement, where potential energy (altitude) is lost
in order to maintain optimal turn rate. The CSMPT is flown at a simulated maximum
rate turn speed of 160 KIAS. The basic principles are the same as for the level MPT,
except that the pitch attitude is adjusted to achieve and maintain 160 KIAS rather than
level flight. Continually strive to find the appropriate attitude and bank angle required
to achieve and maintain 160 KIAS within the stick shaker range without stalling. The
stabilized attitude will be slightly lower than that for a level MPT.
38.
The CSMPT may be entered from a variety of energy states. Always perform
a thorough lookout prior to entry. An academic entry involves slowing to 160 KIAS,
rolling into the turn, applying sufficient back pressure, and applying full power. To
enter the CSMPT from a higher airspeed simultaneously roll into the turn and set the
PCL to mid-range. Apply back pressure to smoothly and positively increase the G, up
