to approximately 4 G, and allow the speed to bleed off. Once the stick shaker activates,
advance the PCL to MAX and maintain the CSMPT as described above.
14.15
IMMELMANN
39.
The immelmann (Figure 14.2) is a max performance roll off the top. Enter the
manoeuvre from level flight at 200–250 KIAS. Apply full power and, while maintaining
wings level, apply back pressure to achieve and maintain approximately 4 G until stick
shaker activates. As airspeed bleeds and the stick shaker activates, manage back pressure
to maintain in stick shaker without stalling. Approaching the inverted position, relax
stick pressure and roll out to wings level. At slow speed and high power, significant
rudder coordination will be required to maintain balanced flight. Airspeed on roll out
will vary depending on environmental conditions and entry parameters. As training
progresses, transition to follow on manoeuvres as desired.
14.16
SPLIT-S
40.
The split-s (Figure 14.3) is a max performance half roll pull through with full
power. The result is a loss of altitude and an increase in airspeed. The altitude loss will
be approximately 2,000’. Initially, in the Moose Jaw MTCA, start practicing from at
least 10,000’ MSL until you determine how much altitude you will consistently lose.
Once your proficiency increases you may be asked to conduct it at a lower altitude.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 12 =====
Figure 14.2: Immelmann
41.
Enter the Split-S from level flight at 100–120 KIAS. Apply full power and raise
the attitude to approximately 20 degrees nose up. Roll to the inverted attitude at approx-
imately 0.5 G. Upon reaching the inverted position adjust back pressure to maintain in
stick shaker without stalling. As training progresses, transition to follow on manoeuvres
as desired.
14.17
PITCH BACK
42.
The pitch back (Figure 14.4) is a full power climbing entry into a maximum per-
formance turn. During your training on the Harvard II, the aim of the pitch back will be
achieve the maximum performance turn parameters prior to completing 180 degrees of
turn.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 13 =====
Figure 14.3: Split-S
43.
Enter the manoeuvre from level flight between 160–220 KIAS. Apply full power
and, considering the entry speed, roll to place your lift vector as required. Apply back-
pressure to achieve approximately 4 G. As airspeed bleeds and stick shaker activates,
maintain the turn in stick shaker without stalling. Approaching the max performance
airspeed, adjust the lift vector to capture and maintain a max performance turn attitude.
Roll out upon completing approximately 180 degrees of turn or, as training progresses,
transition to follow-on manoeuvres.
CAUTION
When flying at speeds greater than 190 KIAS, it is relatively easy to pull
beyond the max allowable asymmetric G.
14.18
SLICE
44.
The slice (Figure 14.5) is a full power descending entry to a maximum perfor-
mance turn. During your training on the Harvard II, the aim of the slice will be to
achieve the maximum performance turn parameters prior to completing 180 degrees of
turn. The lift vector placement during the slice will determine the amount of altitude
lost and how quick the airplane will accelerate.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 14 =====
Figure 14.4: Pitch Back
Figure 14.5: Slice

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 15 =====
45.
Initially, start practicing from at least 10,000’ until you determine how much alti-
tude you will consistently lose. Once your proficiency increases you may be asked to
conduct it at a lower altitude.
46.
Enter the slice from level flight between 100–160 KIAS. Apply full power and
roll to place the lift vector past horizontal (approximately 90–135 degrees AOB) as
appropriate depending on the entry airspeed. Apply backpressure smoothly to the onset
of stick shaker and maintain the turn within stick shaker without stalling. Approaching
the max performance airspeed, adjust the lift vector to capture and maintain a max
performance turn attitude. Roll out upon completing approximately 180 degrees of turn
or, as training progresses, transition to follow on manoeuvres.
CAUTION
Exercise caution as this dynamic manoeuvre can easily induce a high
speed stall.
14.19
THE VERTICAL EIGHT
47.
The vertical eight (Figure 14.6) is a manoeuvre in which the aircraft flight path
follows the outline of an upright figure eight.
48.
The vertical eight is commenced with the PCL set to MAX and airspeed of 280
KIAS. An off-speed immelmann is then executed, using 4 to 5 G on the pull up. A
slow-speed loop is then completed. Do not commence the slow speed loop below 200
KIAS. From this position, an off-speed split-s is executed with a maximum entry speed
of 160 KIAS while max performing the aircraft. Due to the higher entry speed, more
back pressure is required earlier to prevent an excessive airspeed build up. If excessive
airspeed occurs during the exit due to mismanagement of AOA, do not attempt to regain
max performance: execute a nose low U/A recovery.
WARNING
At higher altitudes, the air is less dense which results in decreased per-
formance and less engine thrust. Both of these factors make it more
difficult to execute a vertical eight. If excessive altitude is lost, airspeed
build-up is too rapid, or G build-up is anticipated on the final pull-out,
the PCL may be retarded to IDLE and speed brake extended, if re-
quired, to prevent airframe over-speeds and/or overstress, as per the
nose low U/A procedure.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 16 =====
Figure 14.6: Vertical Eight
14.20
THE VERTICAL ROLL
49.
The vertical roll (Figure 14.7) is a manoeuvre in which the aircraft rolls through
360 degrees while in a vertical nose up attitude. The vertical roll commences when the
nose of the aircraft is approximately 90 degrees above the horizon and zero G has been
established.
50.
The manoeuvre is commenced with the PCL set to MAX and airspeed of 280
KIAS. Enter the vertical roll in the same manner as for a vertical eight. As the aircraft
reaches the vertical attitude, the control column is checked forward to set 0 G in order
to stop further pitch movement. Sufficient aileron is then applied to rapidly roll the
aircraft. Once the aircraft has rolled through 360 degrees in the vertical, the aileron
input is neutralized to stop the roll. Recovery to straight-and-level flight is accomplished
by either completing a loop or a roll off the top from the vertical position. The aircraft
should not be subjected to negative G during the manoeuvre.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 17 =====
Figure 14.7: Vertical Roll

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 18 =====
CHAPTER 15: ADVANCED INSTRUMENT FLYING
15.1
AIRCRAFT PERFORMANCE
1.
In order to safely fly any aircraft during IMC conditions it is extremely important
to consider all aspects of your aircraft performance to ensure adequate obstacle clear-
ance and performance under adverse conditions. This includes, but is not limited to,
a detailed study of climb performance in conjunction with applicable departure proce-
dures, consultation with aircraft charts for landing and T/O distances, and fuel/altitude
considerations for the conditions of the day.
15.2
HIGH APPROACH & TRANSITION
2.
A high VOR/DME approach, as published in the GPH 200, is used exclusively
by military aircraft. Most high approaches are associated with military airfields. A
high approach commences at the Initial Approach Fix (IAF) and enables the aircraft to
descend to final approach within the confines of the terminal control airspace. A high
approach can culminate in a VOR/DME, VOR, ILS, LOC only, LOC/BCRSE, or radar
final approach.
3.
Possible routings to the High IAF include point-to-point or radar vectors. At the
IAF, ensure checks are complete (e.g. 5-T Check), as outlined in the Manual of Instru-
ment Flying. A common profile used to descend from the IAF is a penetration descent,
which is started at a distance published on the appropriate high altitude approach chart.
This is normally flown at 220 KIAS, PCL set 4–6%, and pitch approximately 10 degrees
nose down. The descent rate will stabilize at approximately 4000 fpm.
4.
High approaches are designed for aircraft flying 300 KIAS; therefore, turn radii
and lead radials are based on that speed. Due to the slightly slower speed of the Harvard
II, the resulting turn will be tighter. One technique is to use approximately 20 degrees
AOB (adjusted for wind) for a teardrop penetration turn to intercept the on-course as
depicted. If a higher AOB is used, be sure to roll out with an adequate intercept angle
to ensure interception of the on-course prior to the IF.
5.
If a turn is required it is normally flown using approximately a rate half turn. If a
higher airspeed is desired, a steeper angle of bank, up to 30 degrees will be necessary.
249

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 19 =====
If you anticipate icing, select prop de-ice ON and ensure the probes anti-ice is ON.
Complete the 18,000’ descending check to ensure that the altimeter is not misread.
15.3
NO COMPASS PAR
6.
The no compass PAR is used during electrical failures where the main compass is
not functioning or cannot be relied upon. Perform a standard rate one turn not to exceed
30 AOB in the radar square and standard rate-half turns on final when commanded by
the controller. The possibility for requiring this type of approach in the Harvard II is
very remote.
15.4
MIN FUEL APPROACHES
7.
A min fuel PAR is used to conserve fuel. The controller will normally plan to have
the aircraft intercept the final approach course closer to the runway in order to minimize
fuel consumption. As a result of intercepting the glideslope sooner, one must maintain a
good air picture and be vigilant of airspeed in order to be ready for configuration; timing
will be critical.
15.5
SHUTTLE TRANSITION/ DEPARTURE
8.
A shuttle procedure is defined as a manoeuvre involving a descent or climb in a
holding pattern. In the approach phase, it is normally prescribed where a descent of more
than 2000’ is required during the initial or intermediate approach segments. It can also
be required when performing a missed approach or departure procedure from certain
airports. A shuttle procedure shall be executed in the holding pattern as published unless
instructions contained in an ATC clearance direct otherwise. Refer to the GPH 204A
and A-GA-148 for further information.
15.6
CROSS COUNTRIES
9.
During your training you will be expected to safely and effectively fly the Harvard
II aircraft throughout Canada and the USA. You will be exposed to a wide variety of
instrument procedures and, more specifically, on how US procedures differ from ours.
10.
You have already taken the first steps of planning enroute missions including de-
parture, enroute, and arrival considerations. In Phase III, you will be expected to take
this to the next level and learn about the entire process involved with taking an air-
plane away from home base. The very first task to be completed before cross country
missions will be for you to carry out the Daily Inspection (DI) check so that you can

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 20 =====
properly conduct Pre/Thru/Combo/Post flight inspections and secure the aircraft while
away from your home base.
11.
Planning. During the planning stage you will be required to review and plan, as a
minimum, for the following:
a.
Personal Effects/Environment. Due to the limited cargo capacity, you will
be restricted on the amount of personal items you can bring and this will
be further restricted by the number of FLIPs you will have to store in the
cargo bay. Weight and balance must be calculated and be within operating
limits prior to departure. During long distance transit, your luggage will be
submitted to high altitudes and cold temperatures therefore be cautious about
liquids that could freeze and/or leak as well as pressurized bottles that could
explode as the bay is unpressurized.
b.
ALSE. A thorough personal ALSE inspection will be required to ascertain
that your equipment will not become expired while en-route; this should in-
clude unexpected delays. Additionally, flying clothing may vary while on
X/C due to warmer/colder operation environments.
c.
Applicable Weather/NOTAMs/Preferred IFR routes. This includes look-
ing at the general forecast for all of the planned locations en-route to ensure
a good understanding of any potential issue you may encounter at these loca-
tions. In addition to destination and alternate airport NOTAMs, FIR enroute
NOTAMs must be checked. GPS NOTAMs and GPS RAIM prediction must
be considered. The GPH 205 is an invaluable tool to determine preferred IFR
routings between locations in Canada; well worth a look.
d.
Aerodrome Information. Consider all of the potential alternates and emer-
gency airports en-route to assess which, if any, of the airfields are unusable.
An extensive review of documents such as the IFR flight supplements (both
Canadian and US), the GPH 204, and the GPH 200 is required. A work-
ing knowledge of the GPH 270 (DOD), the primary US IFR document, is
also required prior to deploying to the US. Consider the following: what ser-
vices are offered such as fuel type, flight planning, customs, hangar capabil-
ities, etc. If you are going to a military airfield a Notice of Visiting Aircraft
(NOVA) message or Prior Permission Required (PPR) may be needed. As
well, if customs is required, determine if the airport is an Aerodrome of En-
try (AOE) or a Landing Rights Airport (LRA), and if Customs is actually
available at the aerodrome, otherwise you will need to wait until they arrive.
It is during this pre-planning stage that you will discover if there are specific
procedures particular to an aerodrome such as VFR/IFR restrictions and/or
noise abatement.
e.
Aircraft Performance. You must be aware of the performance of your air-
craft and be able to plan for adverse conditions. You will calculate take-off,
landing, and climb performances for the conditions of the day and insert the

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 21 =====
results on the Take-Off and Landing Data card (TOLD) published in Figure
4-10-2 from the flight manual. This will become part of your take-off brief.
f.
Departure. Carefully review the aerodrome layout and departure procedures
in order to select the most appropriate one for your pre-calculated aircraft
performances. Have a thorough knowledge and understanding of problematic
departures such as SIDs without a plan view and/or spec vis departures. Pay
specific attention to the following:
(1)
Canada. Assessed vs non-assessed aerodromes and the implications
during either a take-off or touch and go (spec vis). An ATC Missed Ap-
proach Instruction (MAI) should not be accepted without careful review
of the published MAI. The pilot is ultimately responsible for obstacle
clearance; if in doubt do not accept the clearance.
(2)
USA/ICAO. The terminology differs from Canada as all aerodromes
issued with an IFR approach have been assessed and will either have
a Diverse Departure (which is very similar to ½) and/or a Non-Diverse
Departure which requires specific departure instructions to be followed.
When departing on a non-diverse departure be aware of such restric-
tions, especially if the approach plate indicates a “Trouble T”.
(3)
Some airfields do not have specific STARs and SIDs. Some are assessed
while others are not. This does not preclude you from going into that
airfield but will require more extensive planning on your part. Your QFI
will ask you to plan to go into such an aerodrome which may require
you to look at VFR charts and assess for yourself if there are any ob-
stacles which will impose restrictions or preclude you from using that
aerodrome under actual IFR conditions.
g.
En-Route. Have at your disposal the required publications for available di-
version airfields as you could be re-routed anytime.
h.
Approach. A thorough approach brief is expected, especially when flying
into an unfamiliar aerodrome. Be aware of potential threats such as short run-
ways, high obstacles (towers), terrain (contour lines), approach lights (more
specifically AA), circling restrictions, PAPI vs VGSI, noise abatement, ar-
restor cables, RWY DA, and slope. Be extremely cautious in determining
which nav aid is used to depict MSA.
i.
Post-Landing. You should know where you are going on the aerodrome
and already have planned for the ideal taxiway to exit the runway and the
best routing to the FBO. You should also contact the FBO on their specific
frequency to inform them of your arrival. If arriving at an airport where you
are required to clear customs, you must remain at the aircraft until cleared by
customs officials.
12.
Execution.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 22 =====
a.
Ground Handling. Expect the unexpected as your IFR clearance could be
changed anytime; do not hesitate to delay your taxi to eliminate any confu-
sion regarding the clearance. During your emergency take-off brief actual
weather conditions must be applied to your recovery plan unless your in-
structor has given you simulated conditions.
b.
En-route. During your transit time, your instructor will introduce you to a
variety of tasks such as planning a diversion, responding to an emergency,
interacting with external agencies (Base OPS, FSS), position reports (if re-
quired), uncontrolled IFR transitions, systems knowledge/operations (GPS),
fuel management, etc.
c.
Arrival. At busy airports, the ground controller will expect you to carry out
their instructions promptly. Asking for progressive taxi is always a solution
but could also result in delays.
d.
Shut-down. While away, you are responsible for the aircraft. Conduct a thor-
ough inspection, secure the aircraft properly and clearly communicate your
intentions and requirements with ground crew. As long as the aircraft is prop-
erly secured, ensure the parking brake is released to allow the ground crew
the ability to move your aircraft if required. Your instructor will introduce
you to the administrative duties involved with cross country operations.
13.
In summary, you will be responsible for all aspects of planning and executing the
cross country.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 23 =====
CHAPTER 16: ADVANCED FORMATION
16.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
This chapter describes advanced Formation sequences, including Dynamic Station
Changes, BVR Rejoin, Fighting Wing, Fluid Manoeuvring, Line Abreast, Flat Turns,
formation landings, and multi-element formation. Basic formation sequences and tech-
niques discussed in Chapter 12 of the SMM continue to apply. However, the manoeuvres
discussed in this chapter will require that you refine the skills learned in basic formation
to attain a higher degree of precision, energy management, and situational awareness.
16.2
WING TAKE-OFF
2.
During your training you will be performing two- or three-plane formation take-
offs. During a formation take-off varying degrees of lateral separation may be used
dependent on the situation. During your training, for a two-plane take-off, you will
normally line up centered on your half of the runway abeam the hinge reference on
the lead aircraft. As you gain experience, you may be shown a tighter line up on the
echelon references. This tighter echelon line up will be important for three-ship or four-
ship take-offs.
3.
Line Up (Standard 2-Ship Wing Take-Off). After obtaining clearance, the lead
taxies onto the runway with the wingman lining up as briefed (normally on the upwind
side of the lead). On a 150’ wide runway, lead will normally line up on the centre of the
downwind side of the runway. The wingman will then move into position in the centre
of the upwind side of the runway. Depending on the runway width, this will normally be
slightly wider than standard echelon formation spacing. Although the wing-tip reference
changes between formation take-offs, depending on runway width, the wingman always
stays aligned with the rudder hinge line of lead’s aircraft. Overall, the aim is to stay
with lead throughout the formation take-off and to avoid falling behind lead in order to
prevent any chance of encountering wake turbulence. Alternatively, the wingman may
line up with standard echelon references. This will result in a tighter spacing, which
may be beneficial if low IFR conditions exist, or when conducting a vic takeoff (a 4-
plane takeoff sequence). When lining up both aircraft must ensure that, as the aircraft
254

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 24 =====
moves into position on the runway, the nose-wheel is straight by using the nose-wheel
steering.
4.
Run-Up. When in position, the aircraft are to be held with the brakes and the nose-
wheel steering must be disengaged. The wingman will give a head nod to lead when
ready for run-up. When the lead gives the run-up signal, both aircraft slowly advance
their PCL to 30% torque and complete their take-off check. After the take-off check is
complete, the lead will look at the wingman. The wingman nods, signifying he is ready
for take-off. If the runway is slippery and the aircraft cannot be held stationary at 30%
torque, reduce torque and inform the lead. This may require that the formation complete
an interval take-off, joining up in the climb.
5.
Take-Off. After receiving the wingman’s head nod, the lead will direct their atten-
tion forward and, with little delay, signal commencement of the take-off roll. The signal
for take-off is preceded by a precautionary indication of two taps on the helmet. Brakes
are released on completion of a head nod by the lead. The lead will slowly advance
the PCL to a maximum of 90% allowing the wingman to maintain position through the
judicious use of torque. In the event that the wingman is unable to stay in position due
to limited power, a “Gimme 1” call can be stated over the radio. At this point, lead will
reduce torque slightly, but not below 80%, to give the wingman extra power authority.
6.
As an alternative technique, following brake release, the lead advances the PCL
smoothly to MAX then immediately cracks the PCL back to 80–90%. As briefed, the
wingman will also similarly advance the PCL, using minor torque changes to remain
in position. In the event that the wingman is unable to stay in position due to limited
power, a “Gimme 1” call can be stated over the radio. At this point, lead will reduce
torque slightly, but not below 80%, to give the wingman extra power authority.
7.
Lateral Separation. The aim is for each aircraft to maintain its respective position
during the take-off roll. The wingman’s primary reference during the take-off roll is the
lead aircraft; however, periodic quick glances down the runway may be useful to ensure
you are maintaining proper alignment. The rudder is the primary means of maintaining
proper lateral separation. If the wingman moves closer than the desired references dur-
ing the take-off roll, a positive correction is required to return to the ideal reference. In
the event that the aircraft moves laterally wide, stop the error, stabilize, and commence
a slow controlled correction back to the ideal.
8.
Longitudinal Separation. The wingman shall use small, timely PCL movements
to maintain relative position on lead longitudinally. If the wingman moves off of the
reference, smoothly change the PCL setting as required to correct to the ideal.
9.
The first indication of rotation is the extension of the lead’s nose-wheel oleo. The
wingman will rotate with the lead. If the wingman becomes airborne before the lead,
the aircraft must be held level at a safe altitude until the lead is airborne.
10.
Once above flap retraction speed and after ensuring that the wingman is safely
airborne, the lead will select the landing gear UP and, after a momentary pause, retract

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 25 =====
the flaps. Upon seeing the landing gear doors of the lead start to open, number two will
raise gear and flaps. Each pilot is responsible for completing their respective post-take-
off check. When completing checks in close formation, the wingman must still maintain
position on the lead aircraft. To this end, it is advisable to complete each item in the
post-take-of check individually while devoting as much time as possible to maintaining
the correct formation position.
NOTE
If the take-off was completed in a wider than echelon position, the wing-
man should complete the post-take-off check and then move to the pre-
briefed position (i.e. fighting wing or echelon). If IMC conditions are an-
ticipated on departure, the wingman should close to echelon references
prior to cloud penetration.
16.3
TAKE-OFF ABORT
11.
It is impossible to consider all potential emergency situations that may occur dur-
ing take-off, or even to state when a pilot should abort or continue the take-off. The
decision to abort rests with the pilot and depends upon the nature of the problem, speed,
position, and other aircraft on the runway. In formation, however, there will be less
time to assess the situation. The lead aircraft also has the additional responsibility of
deciding if and when to abort the entire formation.
12.
During a formation take-off, if an individual formation member aborts after be-
ginning the take-off roll, the other aircraft should normally continue with their take-off.
The most important factor if aborting is to maintain aircraft control and stay on your
section of the runway until the other aircraft are clear. After all aircraft are clear you
are free to manoeuvre as required to deal with whatever situation necessitated the abort.
When time allows, the other aircraft in the formation must be notified with an advisory
call, i.e. “YODA 41 ABORTING”.
13.
If the lead aborts out of a formation, the wingman must immediately carry out the
best course of action.
14.
If lead requires the formation to abort, the radio call will be directive, i.e. “YO-
DAS, ABORT, ABORT, ABORT”. In this situation, all aircraft must abort their take-off
roll immediately, again maintaining their side of the runway. Once under control, wing-
man should call “42 CLEAR” over the radio, advising lead that they can manoeuvre
to the cold side if required. If performing an interval take-off, an abort by an aircraft
requires all trailing aircraft to abort also. After all aircraft are clear, you are free to
manoeuvre as required to deal with whatever situation necessitated the abort.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 26 =====
16.4
DYNAMIC STATION CHANGE
15.
Station changes up to this point have been done while straight and level. Now,
with the basics mastered, we will begin to introduce station changes in a dynamic state.
The process by which to do so does not change, however, required power and G will
differ.
16.5
FLAT TURNS
16.
A flat turn is a turn where all aircraft in the formation are maintained on the same
horizontal plane as lead and is an ideal coordination manoeuvre that requires judicious
use of all three flight controls. The flat turn is entered from echelon and can only be
performed in a direction away from the wingman. The faster roll rates utilized in flat
turns are effective in manoeuvring the aircraft around the limited space available in the
traffic pattern. The following references are used (see Figures 16.1 and 16.2):
a.
Lateral. The wingman lines up the trailing edge of the lead’s up-going wing
with a point halfway along the lead’s canopy.
b.
Longitudinal. The wingman aligns along the elevator hinge line of the lead’s
aircraft. In this position the hinge line will appear to be above number two.
c.
Vertical. The wingman positions their aircraft vertically to keep the spinner
of the lead aircraft on the horizon.
17.
A flat turn is normally initiated using R/T, i.e.
“YODAS, STAND-BY FLAT
TURN—LEAD ROLLING IN, NOW”. On the word “NOW”, the lead will enter a turn.
The wingman will match the lead’s roll-rate, using coordinated control inputs to main-
tain level with, and at a constant distance from, the lead. The turn may also be initiated
by a visual signal. In this case, begin the turn as the lead commences the roll-in. Antic-
ipate a positive increase in torque as the bank angle is increased. The back pressure is
also increased to maintain the flat turn position.
18.
Once established in a flat turn, use the above references to maintain position.
NOTE
Horizontal separation is slightly greater than one wing span.
19.
The rollout from the flat turn is accomplished in the reverse order of the roll-in. At
the completion of the roll-out, number two should be in the appropriate echelon position.
The roll-out can be initiated using R/T, i.e. “YODAS, LEAD ROLLING OUT, NOW”
or the lead can simply begin rolling out of the turn. Anticipate a positive reduction in
torque as the bank angle is decreased. The back pressure is also decreased in order to
roll out in the echelon position.

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 27 =====
Figure 16.1: Flat turn front seat references
Figure 16.2: Rear seat flat turn references
16.6
INSTRUMENT/VISUAL APPROACH PROCEDURES
20.
lnstrument/visual approach procedures do not vary significantly from single air-
craft approaches. In formation, however, smooth aircraft handling is a critical compo-
nent of leading. During the initial and final phases of the approach, lead should attempt
to maintain a minimum of 20% torque. Selection of ancillaries shall be completed
in sufficient time to permit the wingman to stabilize before manoeuvring and the lead
must take into account the minimum recommended torque settings when planning the
approach because the flight controls are less effective at slower speeds with the aircraft

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 28 =====
configured. During setup for instrument or visual straight-in approaches, take-off flap
will be automatically selected following the gear selection. Land flap selection will be
directed by standard R/T or formation signal. Lead will request landing clearance for
the formation the same as for single aircraft operations and when clearance is received,
lead will acknowledge and confirm the gear for the formation.
NOTE
To maintain mutual support throughout an instrument approach, and
if time and weather permit, the wingman should complete all required
checks, briefing items, and navaid set-up for the planned approach.
16.7
DROP LANDINGS
21.
The lead shall brief or signal at what point during the approach they will drop
off the wingman. The signal will be for the lead aircraft to point at the wingman and
then proceed to point at the runway with the same hand – finger extended. Once lead
signals the wingman to continue as a single aircraft, the wingman shall transition to a
normal approach, planning to land on the centre of the runway, while lead initiates a
go around. The wingman is still responsible to ensure separation during this transition.
Lead will normally drop off the wingman, select PCL to MAX, and go around. This
ensures positive separation between aircraft in the event that the wingman needs to go
around on short final.
16.8
LOW APPROACHES AND GO AROUNDS
22.
The lead will call for a go around via R/T: for example, “BLACKS GO AROUND”.
Torque is advanced smoothly and a climb is initiated. Landing gear and flaps are re-
tracted as per a normal take-off. Although recommended, the “GO AROUND” R/T call
is optional and not required; you will be aware of the go around when significant power
is required to stay in position.
16.9
FORMATION LANDINGS
23.
The lead shall plan to land on the center of the downwind side of the runway.
A call or signal for landing flaps shall be made, normally somewhat earlier than for a
single aircraft landing. At this time the wingman shall select LDG flap, step-up, and
move laterally (Figures 16.3 and 16.4). The aim is to be on the same plane as lead while
acquiring at least 10’ of wing-tip (lateral) separation. Vertical position is achieved by

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 29 =====
stepping up sufficiently to place the leading edge of lead’s wing-tip on the longitudinal
white stripe of lead’s fuselage. Wing-tip (lateral) separation is accomplished by aligning
the leading edge of lead’s wing-tip with lead’s nose wheel strut (oleo). Although exact
references are not desired during this transition to landing, it is important to remember
that the rudder hinge line reference remains the same. Although the wing-tip reference
changes during a formation landing, the wingman stays aligned with the rudder hinge
line of lead’s aircraft. Overall, the aim is to stay with lead throughout the formation
landing and avoid falling back on lead in order to reduce any chance of wake turbulence,
or of landing prematurely.
Figure 16.3: Wing Landing References- Front Seat
24.
As the lead approaches the landing surface, the wingman will cross-check the run-
way environment while still referencing lead. The lead will flare, reduce torque to idle
smoothly, and land normally; number two continues to reference lead and in antici-
pation of lead’s torque reduction, sets PCL to IDLE during the flare, thereby landing
slightly before lead. Once on the runway, both aircraft lower nose wheels, exercising
care to maintain a straight-ahead rollout, and when below 80 KIAS apply the necessary
braking. If carrying out a formation landing and the lead must manoeuvre in front of
the wingman to reach the turn-off side of the runway, the wingman will advise the lead
once slowed to a safe speed and certain that no possibility of conflict with lead exists,
i.e. “YODA 42 CLEAR”. This will allow the lead to move to the turn-off side of the
runway. If there is any doubt about landing safely, go around immediately and inform
the tower. If unable to remain behind lead with moderate braking, the wingman should

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 30 =====
Figure 16.4: Wing Landing References – Rear Seat
pass the lead and maintain this position until clear of the runway and stopped.
CAUTION
It is imperative that both aircraft maintain their respective sides of the
centerline in order to maintain safe separation if the wingman over-
shoots lead once on the runway.
NOTE
The above procedure does not require any calls/signals other than the
selection of LDG flap. This requires the lead to operate their aircraft
and “telegraph” their intentions to their wingman correctly, i.e. gradual
PCL, airspeed, and pitch changes.
16.10
FORMATION TOUCH AND GO
25.
Straight-in. As discussed for a full stop landing, both aircraft will perform their
own flare and touchdown. Once the main wheels are on the ground, lead will set PCL
to MAX and perform a normal touch and go remaining over the downwind side of

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 31 =====
the runway. After becoming airborne, lead will select gear and flaps UP and perform a
post-take-off check. Lead will normally reduce torque to approximately 80%, accelerate
straight ahead to 180 KIAS, and then continue climb to pattern altitude or as briefed.
The wingman will set PCL to MAX after lead begins to pull away on touchdown. At
this point, the wingman also flies a normal touch and go over the upwind side of the
runway. After becoming airborne, the wingman selects gear and flaps UP, performs a
post-take-off check, and accelerates, at which point transition to the lead aircraft is made
in order to effect a rejoin.
16.11
BEYOND VISUAL RANGE REJOIN (BVR)
26.
The Beyond Visual Range (BVR) rejoin is used to put a split formation back to-
gether when neither the lead nor the wingman have visual contact. A formation split
can happen for a number of reasons such as loss of visual contact in fluid manoeuvring,
turning the wrong way in line abreast, or it can be done for training purposes to prac-
tice the rejoin coordination. When flown for training, it may occur that the lead and/or
wingman is visual with the other aircraft throughout the exercise; however, to ensure
maximum training value, the procedure below should be followed to the maximum ex-
tent possible. This skill set is critical for a lead to ensure safe operation of the formation
when an unplanned or unexpected split happens.
27.
The first priority after a formation has been split is to establish a safe contract for
deconfliction. As in the break out or practice break out procedure, the wingman will call
“OFF, BLIND” and shall also advise lead of their current altitude, i.e. “BLACK 71 OFF
BLIND, HEADING 090, MAINTAINING 10,000 FEET”. It then becomes the lead’s re-
sponsibility to ensure deconfliction, usually using altitude to ensure vertical separation.
Separation by means of radial and/or DME is also effective but less commonly used.
After lead directs the deconfliction over the discrete frequency, the wingman shall read
back the deconfliction contract to ensure both pilots are aware and have acknowledged
the safety measure before a rendezvous can be organized.
28.
The lead’s next responsibility is to coordinate a rendezvous. This can be done in
a variety of ways including but not limited to sending the wingman to a radial/DME to
orbit, intercepting a radial inbound/outbound to create closure between the two aircraft,
or having the wingman circle over a unique ground feature. The lead and wingman will
then begin looking for one another while maintaining their respective safety contract. If
a radial and/or DME contract was first established, then visual contact or an altitude con-
tract must first be established prior to generating any closure between the two aircraft.
Ideally the lead should find the wingman and, using clock positions, distance approxi-
mation, and relative altitude, the lead will talk the wingman onto their aircraft in order
to acquire visual contact. The lead can then use any combination of geometry, rejoin
techniques, and known formations to coordinate an efficient rejoin into a known position
(including but not limited to fighting wing, line abreast, and/or close formation).

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 32 =====
29.
Unless done for training purposes, once back in a known formation the sequence
should be debriefed airborne to ensure both pilots are comfortable continuing the flight
or if the mission should be terminated and discussed further on the ground. The for-
mation is considered back together once the wingman is in a known position and both
pilots are comfortable and ready to continue with the mission.
16.12
TACTICAL FORMATION
30.
The purpose of any tactical formation is basically two-fold: firstly to provide se-
curity against attacks by the enemy through mutual support, and secondly to provide
maximum firepower in offensive operations against the enemy. To achieve security, the
formation must be able to detect the attackers and be able to manoeuvre against the threat
before the attacker(s) can gain tactical advantage. When choosing a tactical formation,
one must carefully weigh the benefits of mutual support with those of manoeuvrability.
Unfortunately, it is not possible to have one formation that provides excellent mutual
support coupled with an equal measure of manoeuvrability.
16.13
FORMATION CONSIDERATION
31.
The two basic tactical formations you will learn at this stage of your training are
fighting wing and line abreast. Fighting wing is characterized by simplicity, manoeu-
vrability, and good forward look-out but poor visual coverage of the rear hemisphere.
For these reasons, it is generally employed when attacking as an offensive formation.
Line abreast is characterized by good visual coverage of the rear hemisphere, but de-
graded manoeuvrability. As a defensive formation, line abreast is normally employed
to provide mutual protection, but it also provides some measure of offensive posture.
Although the wingman is primarily responsible for making the miss and maintaining
position, both fighting wing and line abreast formations also allow the wingman the
opportunity to clear the area for the formation. A minimum 300’ crossing separation
(vertical and/or horizontal) shall be maintained for all line abreast manoeuvres.
32.
Considerations when choosing a formation include:
a.
simplicity;
b.
manoeuvrability;
c.
flexibility;
d.
visual coverage;
e.
detectability;
f.
mutual support;
g.
size (number of aircraft);

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 33 =====
h.
aircraft characteristics (blind area, performance);
i.
threat; and
j.
atmospheric conditions.
16.14
LOOKOUT AND REPORTING
33.
The areas of responsibility for visual search and the lookout sectors are shown be-
low. Aircrew should spend about ¾ of their time searching their primary area and the
rest searching the remainder of the sky. Breaking down the secondary and tertiary look-
out sectors into several smaller sectors using a scan time of 3–5 seconds will ensure an
efficient and effective lookout. Although the following examples illustrate the lookout
technique employed for line abreast, lookout during fighting wing is no less important.
Despite poor visual coverage of the rear hemisphere during fighting wing, this lookout
technique should still be employed to cover the remainder of the sky.
Figure 16.5: Lookout Technique
Figure 16.6: Secondary Lookout Technique

===== SMM part 6 PDF page 34 =====
34.
Cover the entire sky, vertically as well as level. Try to avoid staring at the lead
aircraft, although it is the wingman’s job to maintain position. Search the briefed area
and report any sightings.
35.
Normally, all aircraft seen should be reported on the intraflight frequency. Sighting
reports should be reported in the manner mentioned in Chapter 2.
16.15
FIGHTING WING
36.
As described in Chapter 12, fighting wing provides a measure of manoeuvrability
and mutual support unattainable in close formation. It is particularly useful during the
departure, enroute, and arrival phases of domestic operations, as each formation member
has the opportunity to increase situational awareness and maintain a good lookout.
37.
Maintaining fighting wing requires an understanding of pursuit curves and angular
relationships (lead, lag, and pure pursuit; aspect angle; line of sight; closure; etc.) as
introduced in Chapter 12. This chapter will go into further detail with these concepts,
as they will come into play during advanced formation manoeuvring.
38.
Rejoins to echelon from fighting wing may be conducted either via a straight ahead
rejoin or by closing through route to echelon. When rejoining through route, the wing-
man will establish level or slightly below lead (for safe separation below and behind
in the event of misjudged closure) and proceed to route spacing. Once at route spacing
with safe closure, the wingman may remove the stack and proceed from route to echelon
in the normal manner.
16.16
PURSUIT CURVES AND ANGULAR RELATIONSHIPS
39.
When pursuing another aircraft, one cannot simply point at the preceding aircraft
and hope to maintain separation. Depending on the severity of the lead aircraft’s ma-
noeuvring, this could generate either excessive closure rates or flight path crossing an-
gles well in excess of 90 degrees, requiring dynamic manoeuvring to maintain any pre-
defined separation. The key in maintaining separation rests in being able to assess the
lead aircraft’s turn circle and being able to fly in relation to it. In an ideal world, if
a pursuing aircraft were to match performance while flying on the same turn circle as
the preceding aircraft, the separation would remain constant. However, in a dynamic
manoeuvring environment it is virtually impossible to match performance exactly as the
preceding aircraft’s turn circle is constantly being redefined. Thus you must constantly
assess the preceding aircraft’s turn circle and manoeuvre accordingly. In order to assess
the lead’s turn circle, it is important to understand the factors that define the turn circle.
At this stage of your training the factors of prime importance are: turn radius, flight
path, plane of motion, lift vector, turn rate, total G, and pursuit curves.

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 1 =====
a.
Turn Radius. Turn radius provides the baseline for defining a manoeuvring
aircraft’s turn performance. The turn radius is the radius of the circle flown
by a turning aircraft and is determined by TAS and G. A change in either of
these parameters will immediately change the turn radius. Any aircraft with
the same TAS and pulling the same G will have the same turn radius. Aircraft
that sustain a higher G for a given TAS will have a smaller turn radius.
b.
Flight Path. This is merely the three-dimensional path that an aircraft traces
through the air.
c.
Plane of Motion (POM). Due to the fact that flying is three-dimensional, the
flight path of an aircraft defines in which plane of motion the turn circle is.
As an example, if changing from a level turn to a vertical/oblique manoeuvre,
an aircraft’s turn radius may remain constant, but its POM changes, thus a
pursuing aircraft will no longer remain on the same turn circle unless its flight
path also changes. POM is the plane in which the aircraft is traveling and will
vary with the given lift vector (LV) placement, amount of yaw present, angle
of attack (AOA), G, and airspeed.
d.
Turn Rate. Normally expressed in degrees per second, turn rate is a measure
of how quickly an aircraft can generate a turn. Theoretically, if a pursuing
aircraft were to maintain the same turn rate and remain on the same turn
circle as a preceding aircraft it should be able to maintain a constant range.
In a dynamic environment, the rate of turn rarely remains constant, making
it next to impossible for two aircraft to match performance exactly. Addi-
tionally, turn rate dictates how fast an aircraft can re-define its turn circle;
i.e. a sudden increase in turn rate of 10 degrees per second will drastically
re-define an aircraft’s turn circle, making it harder for a pursuing aircraft to
maintain separation.
e.
Lift Vector. The lift vector (LV) is an imaginary line, extending straight up
through the top of your canopy, perpendicular to the wings. It is where the lift
from the wings is directed and indicates where, if manoeuvring, your aircraft
will end up. In essence, when you look through the top of the canopy, you
are looking in the direction that the lift force is acting.
Figure 16.7: Lift Vector
f.
Total G. Total G is the vector sum of G as read off the G meter and the
earthward 1 G of gravity. In a 3 G level turn, the total G is very near 3 as the
1 G of gravity is nearly perpendicular to the aircraft’s LV. In a 3 G loop, the
total G is constantly changing. At the bottom of the loop the LV opposes the

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 2 =====
1 G of gravity so total G is approximately 2 (3 G minus 1 G). At the vertical
points (nose up and nose down) the total G is approximately 3 for the same
reason as the level turn. At the inverted point the LV aligns with the 1 G
of gravity so the total G is approximately 4 (3 G plus 1 G). Because of this,
the rate and radius of turn during a loop or any other vertical manoeuvre is
constantly changing.
Figure 16.8: Total G
40.
Pursuit Curves: Lead, Lag, and Pure. The concepts of lead, lag, and pure pur-
suit were introduced in Chapter 12, and will be used extensively throughout Advanced
Formation.
a.
Assessing Turning Performance. While pursuing another aircraft there are
a number of tools that will enable you to assess your aircraft’s position in
relation to the preceding aircraft and its defined turn circle. The ones of prime
concern at this stage of your training are aspect angle, heading crossing angle,
line of sight, and closure. Although these concepts were introduced in Basic
Formation (Chapter 12), they will be looked at in greater depth in Advanced
Formation.
b.
Aspect Angle (AA) or Angle of Tail (AOT). The concept of AA was intro-
duced in Chapter 12. The aspect angle is the angle from the target’s tail to
the fighter. The aspect angle always relates to the target and does not depend
on the fighter’s heading. It is effectively the fighter’s view of the target. If the
target is heading away from the fighter – low aspect. If the target is heading
towards the target – high aspect. A position directly behind the target aircraft
tail, extended through the 6 o’clock position, is 0° AA. A position directly in
front of the target aircraft is 180° AA regardless of your heading. If you are
on the right side of the target, that is right aspect. If you are on the left side,
that is left aspect.
c.
Heading Crossing Angle (HCA). The concept of HCA was introduced in
Chapter 12. HCA is the angular difference between your aircraft’s heading
and the other aircraft’s heading, as indicated in Figure 16.9.

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 3 =====
Figure 16.9: Aspect Angle and Heading Crossing Angle
d.
Line of Sight (LOS). The concept of LOS was introduced in Chapter 12.
Remember, line of sight is a measure of how fast another aircraft is moving
across your windscreen. If the aircraft is moving relatively slowly then there
is a low line of sight. If the aircraft is moving relatively quickly across your
windscreen then there is a high line of sight. If flight paths are matched
between two aircraft, of the LOS is zero.
e.
Closure (Vc). The concept of Vc was introduced in Chapter 12. Remember,
closure is a measure of how fast you are reducing or increasing the separation
to the other aircraft. The highest closure rate you could have on another
aircraft would be when head-on: 180 degrees HCA, 180 degrees AA, and
zero LOS. When pursuing another aircraft, assess closure by monitoring the
rate of change of the size of the preceding aircraft. If the aircraft is getting
big quickly then closure is high. If the aircraft is not changing in size very
quickly then closure is low. Unfortunately, when assessing closure in this
way the transition from low closure rates to high closure rates is often subtle,
so it must constantly be monitored.
41.
Putting it all together. It becomes obvious that attempting to maintain separation
from a manoeuvring aircraft in a dynamic environment is not an exact science. Because
most of the above parameters are constantly changing, a trailing aircraft must employ
various skills and tools in order to maintain and regain separation. Remember, your
visual cues such as closure, aspect angle, range, and line of sight, will dictate your
actions as a pilot (lead, lag, and pure pursuit) to maintain and/or regain position. Fluid
manoeuvring will allow you to practice the application of these concepts in a dynamic
environment.

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 4 =====
Figure 16.10: Maintaining Separation in a Turn
16.17
FLUID MANOEUVRING
42.
Fluid manoeuvring (FM) on the Harvard II is defined as an exercise that allows
for the practice and development of pursuit curves and angular relationships in a dy-
namic environment. It involves a series of manoeuvres which will be incorporated into
a non-regimented sequence whereby the wingman is expected to react to the preceding
aircraft, and employ lead, lag, and pure pursuit in order to maintain separation within
the confines of a defined manoeuvring area behind the other aircraft. Initially during
training, FM will consist of a level turn, a loop, two wingovers (described below), and
a barrel roll. This sequence may be modified as your training progresses. The aim of
the wingman during FM is to maintain position within a 60 degree cone behind the pre-
ceding aircraft at a distance of 500–1000’. This position is similar to the fighting wing
position except that, during FM, both aircraft maintain the same power setting and there
is never any requirement for the wingman to utilize the 30 to 60 degree swept position
as this would put the aircraft outside the FM parameters.
43.
Entry into Fluid Manoeuvring. During the initial exposure to FM, you will
normally use an academic entry from an echelon position using a 2-second break for
spacing. As your training progresses, you may also enter from fighting wing or line
abreast. In order to ensure both aircraft are matched in performance, a constant power
setting will normally be maintained throughout the exercise. When the lead is ready to
commence FM they will provide an advisory call using the standard intraflight comm
procedures. Once the wingman acknowledges, the lead will begin manoeuvring. If the
wingman is not quite in in the ideal FM positioning, they should use the first planned
manoeuvre to establish the required FM parameters.
44.
Manoeuvring. As the manoeuvring progresses, the wingman must employ the
concepts of lead, lag, and pure pursuit in order to remain within the FM parameters. In
doing so, certain restrictions apply. They are:
a.
in addition to the structural limits of the aircraft, a 5 G limit applies at all
times;

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 5 =====
b.
at no time is the wingman to generate more than 90 degrees of aspect coupled
with more than 90 degrees of HCA and low line of sight while employing
lead, lag, or pure pursuit; and
c.
A 500’ bubble shall be maintained at all times during fluid manoeuvring.
45.
If either of these parameters is exceeded then a “TERMINATE” call must be made
on the radios. Any formation member can initiate the call, at which point all manoeu-
vring will cease and all formation members will acknowledge the call in succession i.e.
“LEAD TERMINATE” ...“TWO TERMINATE”. A rejoin will then be coordinated and
lead will ascertain why the call was made. If no structural limits were exceeded and no
other flight safety implications apply, FM may be set up and re-entered as described
above. Otherwise the nature of the terminate call will dictate subsequent actions.
46.
If, on the other hand, the wingman finds themself outside the FM parameters and
is not in a position to regain those parameters in an expeditious manner, they shall
make a “TERMINATE FOR POSITION” call. Lead will acknowledge the call and
enter a predictable turn. The wingman will then reposition their aircraft within the FM
parameters and then transmit a “Cleared to Manoeuvre” call at which time lead will
re-commence manoeuvring.
47.
During the CH phase, you have learned all the skills required to lead FM, such
as the loop and barrel roll. The wingover is also useful and can be flown as follows.
The initial wingover is entered at approximately 230 KIAS. Commence the pull up with
a pitch rate similar to the cloverleaf, blending aileron as the nose comes through the
horizon. You should aim to use approximately 45 degrees of pitch (above and below
the horizon), up to 120 degrees of bank, and approximately 3 G to exit the wingover
heading approximately 180 degrees from the direction of entry. Ideally, you will com-
plete another wingover in the opposite direction of roll to complete the manoeuvre.
Predictability remains the primary focus of lead to allow the wingman to employ proper
pursuit curves throughout the exercise.
NOTE
Use of pursuit curves may require adjustments to the academic manoeu-
vre speeds, amount of G, and/or bank angle to maintain FM parameters.
Ensure that all flight manual limits are respected while making these adjust-
ments.
48.
Fluid manoeuvring is normally ceased with a terminate call, acknowledged by both
aircraft as described above. Lead will recover to level flight and will normally signal for
a rejoin to close formation (turning or straight ahead).

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 6 =====
16.18
LINE ABREAST (LAB)
49.
Line Abreast is a formation designed to optimize rear hemisphere look-out and
mutual support. The basic element in line abreast is a two-ship formation. In the Harvard
II, the element flies line abreast with 4000–6000’ lateral separation, 0–10 degrees sweep,
± 2000’ vertical separation, and at 220 KIAS (Figures 16.11, 16.12, 16.13). This allows
each pilot to visually clear the wingman’s six o’clock blind area well back behind the
formation. The most vulnerable area is the “mutual blind area”, mid-way between the
two airplanes, where the two individual blind areas intersect (Figure 16.14). To ensure
adequate protection, the two pilots must fly far enough apart to move the “mutual blind
area” as far as possible behind the formation. At spread distances greater than 9000’, it
becomes extremely difficult for pilots to provide adequate visual coverage and warning
of an enemy attacking from the beam area outside the formation.
Figure 16.11: Line Abreast
Figure 16.12: Front seat LAB references

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 7 =====
Figure 16.13: Rear seat LAB references
Figure 16.14: Line Abreast Mutual Blind Area
50.
It is always the wingman’s responsibility to remain within the prescribed LAB pa-
rameters. If any manoeuvring occurs which puts the wingman out of position, they must
manoeuvre accordingly to correct the error, regardless of how it developed. Turns may
be initiated by a radio call, a wing flash, or some other briefed signal. Most turns will be
performed promptly using a 3 G level turn with PCL set to MAX. This will result in a

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 8 =====
minor loss in airspeed which must be regained after rolling wings level. This minimizes
the time spent in the turn, where the formation is most vulnerable to attack, and still
maintains both aircraft at a combat airspeed throughout the turn. The procedures are
fairly simple, allowing both pilots to concentrate on visual look-out before, during, and
after, the turn.
51.
Line abreast may be entered from echelon, route, or fighting wing. The simplest
entry from echelon is for lead to establish the formation on the desired heading before
directing the wingman to LAB. The wingman then manoeuvres into position while lead
flies straight and level. Lead may direct a more dynamic transition into LAB where both
aircraft manoeuvre simultaneously onto a new heading. Lead must ensure the integrity
and safety of the formation during these more dynamic transitions.
16.19
LINE ABREAST TURNS
52.
Delayed 90-Degree Turn. Delayed turns allow each pilot in the formation to com-
plete their turns rapidly, without interference from the other aircraft in the formation, and
in view of the wingman through most of the turn. Once the turn is initiated, the pilot
on the outside of the turn (the side opposite the direction of turn) immediately begins
a 3 G, energy sustaining level turn with PCL as required to maintain airspeed into the
wingman, while clearing the inside of the turn. After 90 degrees of turn, the pilot rolls
out, and immediately begins clearing outside the completed turn, where the wingman
will shortly appear. The inside pilot continues straight ahead, clearing the outside of the
turn until the wingman reaches the 5 or 7 o’clock position, depending on the direction
of the turn. At this point, a 3 G, energy sustaining level turn with PCL as required to
maintain airspeed is commenced, clearing the inside of the turn. After 90 degrees of
turn, the pilot rolls out, back to LAB, but on the opposite side of the formation.
NOTE
Wingman must crosscheck lead during turn to potentially adjust G and/or
anticipated heading to help with fixing spacing errors prior to roll out.
However, any remaining spacing errors must be corrected upon roll out.
53.
Delayed 90-Degree Turn into Wingman. This manoeuvre is performed in the
manner described above with the lead initiating the turn into the wingman. If R/T is
used to commence the turn, the appropriate call shall be: “BLACKS, 90 LEFT/RIGHT”
(see Figure 16.15).
54.
Delayed 90-Degree Turn away from Wingman. Lead initiates the turn with
a wing flash.
The wingman commences a 90-degree turn into lead and then rolls
out. If R/T is used, the wingman will commence their turn on the call: “VIPERS,

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 9 =====
90 LEFT/RIGHT”. The wingman immediately completes a 90-degree turn into the lead
and then rolls out. Once the wingman has reached the 5 or 7 o’clock position, the lead
will then initiate a 90-degree turn in the direction called (see 16.15).
Figure 16.15: LAB Delayed 90 turns
NOTE
The lateral width of the formation will play an important part in decid-
ing when the inside aircraft should start their turn. The general rule for
this is: CLOSER ...TURN EARLY — WIDER ...DELAY
55.
Delayed 45-Degree Turn. Delayed 45-degree turns are executed in a manner
similar to the delayed 90-degree turns. Once the procedure required to accomplish this
particular turn is understood then you will have little difficulty carrying out turns ranging
from 30 degrees to 90 degrees.
56.
Delayed 45-Degree Turn Into Wingman. The lead initiates the turn into the
wingman, as in the delayed 90-degree turn. The wingman seeing the lead start the turn
should assume that a 90-degree turn is about to be performed. When the lead rolls out on
the new heading, which in this case is only 45 degrees, the wingman continues in front
of the lead to the other side and picks up the new heading in LAB. If the turn is called on
the radio, the lead will transmit “VIPERS, 45 LEFT/RIGHT” and the wingman will now
be aware of the new heading (see Figure 16.16). Upon lead initiating the turn, a check
turn may be conducted by the wingman to adjust geometry if required (Fig 16.17).
57.
Delayed 45-Degree Turn Away From Wingman. In this manoeuvre, the lead
initiates the turn with the standard wing flash. When the wingman sees this, a 90-degree

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 10 =====
turn is started towards the lead. However, instead of completing the entire 90 degrees
of turn, the signal to roll out at the approximate heading (unless the lead has called a
particular heading) is given by the lead providing a wing flash. The lead will cross to
the wingman’s other side and then turn to the new heading to regain LAB. Again, if the
turn is called, the lead will transmit “BLACKS, 45 LEFT/RIGHT” (see Figure 16.16).
Figure 16.16: Delayed 45º LAB Turn
Figure 16.17: 45 Degree LAB with Check

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 11 =====
58.
Check Turns. Check turns are short in-place turns used to turn the formation 30
degrees or less. Check turns may also be used to help a wingman gain a line abreast
position. When the turn is initiated, both pilots simultaneously conduct an energy-
sustaining turn, rolling into the direction indicated, pulling through 30 degrees of turn
or less, and rolling out. Check turns of more than 30 degrees of turn are often called
“in-place” turns (see Figure 16.18) and are used to transition from LAB to fighting wing
formation or vice-versa, and manoeuvring through the G-Awareness. All check turns
will be completed with comms, except when the turn is expected over a specific point
(i.e. TACNAV).
Figure 16.18: Check and In-place Turns
59.
In-Place Turns. In-Place turns may be used to turn the formation into a trail posi-
tion with normal LAB spacing. When Lead calls “VENOMS IN PLACE 90 LEFT/RIGHT”
an energy sustaining turn with PCL as required to maintain airspeed is completed. On
rollout, the trailing aircraft should be in-line with the leading aircraft, adjusting into po-
sition if not. This manoeuver can be carried out either into or away from the wingman
and can only be conducted with radio comms. In some cases, an in-place turn can be
used to bring a formation back into LAB.
60.
Hook Turn. Hook turns require both pilots to turn simultaneously in the direction
indicated for a 180-degree turn. Normally the turn is initiated with a radio call specifying
the direction of turn. “BLACKS, HOOK LEFT/RIGHT” At this time, both pilots begin
a 3 G, energy-sustaining level turn with PCL as required to maintain airspeed. Both
pilots clear the inside of the turn — one pilot looking level to low, and the other looking
level to high — until both roll out in LAB after 180 degrees of turn (see Figure 16.19).
To initiate a hook turn with comms out, the lead initiates the turn with a wing flash;
number two commences a turn into lead. As the lead sees this, he will commence a turn
away from number two to start the 180-degree turn. When number two sees the lead
turning away, a 180-degree turn is assumed and the turn is continued to roll out in LAB.
61.
Shackle Turn. Shackle turns are crossing turns that do not change the formation’s
direction of flight. Lead will call for the turn over the radios “BLACKS, SHACKLE”.

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 12 =====
Figure 16.19: LAB Hook Turn
Figure 16.20: Shackle

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 13 =====
Both aircraft begin a prompt 3 G, energy-sustaining level turn with PCL as required
to maintain airspeed into each other and roll out after approximately 45 degrees of
turn. While lead remains level, the wingman must immediately telegraph nose position
to ensure vertical separation is achieved, while simultaneously turning approximately
45 degrees towards lead (adjusting rollout heading to fly directly above/below lead).
Both aircraft continue toward each other, with the wingman ensuring positive separa-
tion, clearing the “inside”, or rear area of the formation.
62.
After the two aircraft cross, lead will indicate the start of the 45-degree reversal,
back to the original direction of flight, timing the turn to arrive back in LAB. The wing-
man must ensure to crosscheck lead to visually recognize the timing for the reversal.
63.
Shackle turns have several tactical applications. They may be used to increase vi-
sual coverage of the rear hemisphere, when checking for a possible threat at six o’clock.
They may also be used to place the wingman on the proper side for an upcoming attack.
The disadvantage of a shackle turn is that it increases the formation’s detectability. If an
enemy has seen one aircraft, and watches it through a shackle, the second aircraft will
very likely be seen when they cross. Furthermore, the wing flash of both aircraft also
increases the likelihood of visual pick-up as they begin the turn and while in proximity
to each other after crossing (see Figure 16.20).
64.
Cross Turn. Cross turns may also be used to reverse the formation’s direction
of flight. This turn is initiated by a radio call from lead who will transmit “BLACKS,
CROSSTURN”. Both pilots simultaneously begin a 2 G, energy-sustaining turn toward
each other, and maintain it for approximately 90 degrees of turn. Upon initiation of the
turn, the wingman must immediately telegraph nose position to ensure vertical separa-
tion while turning towards lead (PCL MAX will likely be required to maintain airspeed).
The wingman may also need to adjust G to fly directly above/below Lead. After cross-
ing, both pilots increase to a 3 G energy-sustaining turn, and use bank as required to roll
out with 180 degrees of heading change. It is important to keep in mind that energy is
either lost or gained while climbing or descending to “make the miss” with lead during
the turn. Compensating for this loss or gain in energy will help to minimize geometry
errors at the conclusion of the manoeuvre. For example, if a climb is used by the wing-
man at the beginning of the cross turn to achieve safe separation with lead, a descent
can be used by the wingman after the aircraft cross to replenish the energy that was lost
at the beginning of the manoeuvre. As experience is gained and your comfort level dur-
ing line abreast is increased, you will learn to safely and efficiently manage your energy
state to make the geometry and spacing work. Just as in the shackle, cross turns increase
the potential of formation detectability. In the tactical environment, detectability may
be reduced somewhat by ensuring additional altitude separation between aircraft as they
cross (see Figure 16.20). In the training environment, the wingman will manoeuvre as
required to ensure positive separation and 180 degree HCA as both aircraft merge.

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 14 =====
Figure 16.21: Cross Turn
16.20
REJOINS FROM LINE ABREAST
65.
There are two types of rejoins from line abreast: the straight-ahead rejoin and
the turning rejoin. Regardless of the type, unless otherwise directed, the wingman will
initially rejoin to fighting wing.
a.
Straight Ahead Rejoin From LAB. As the name implies, the lead is ex-
pected to maintain direction of flight while number two rejoins to the di-
rected position. In this case, the lead calls “BLACKS ... FIGHTING WING”
or rocks the wings once and maintains direction of flight. Number two turns
into lead slightly to establish a rejoin line to a fighting wing position, and on
the same side as the line abreast position that was just left. Number two’s fi-
nal vector prior to attaining the fighting wing position must clearly aim away
from the lead. If called to echelon right from line abreast, the wingman will
proceed to the wing via a fighting wing position and then aim to establish a
route echelon position while aiming away (to the right) from lead on the final
vector as per a normal straight ahead rejoin. This allows for misjudging the
overtake during the rejoin.
b.
Turning Rejoin From Line Abreast. During a turning rejoin from line
abreast, the lead has the option of turning either into, or away from, number
two. In either case, the lead will maintain a constant AOB and IAS during the
rejoin. To initiate, the lead calls “BLACKS ... FIGHTING WING” or rocks
the wings once, then enters a 30-degree bank turn while setting a briefed air-
speed. Number two then should manoeuvre the aircraft to rejoin safely in the

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 15 =====
Figure 16.22: Straight Ahead Rejoin to Fighting Wing
Figure 16.23: Straight Ahead Rejoin to Echelon
fighting wing position.
(1)
If the lead turns away from number two it is a simple matter of crossing
lead’s turn circle to set up a normal rejoin line with ample closure.
Figure 16.24: Turning Rejoin (Away)
(2)
If lead turns towards number two, it is best for the wingman to ag-
gressively turn to point at the lead. At this point, roll out of the turn
and monitor the lead. Once a definite increase in the line of sight is
detected, reverse the turn to capture the fighting wing position. The
wingman needs to anticipate reversing this turn in order to align the

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 16 =====
aircraft’s fuselage with the lead’s upon intercepting the fighting wing
position, while continuing to monitor closure rate.
66.
If joining directly to the echelon position, the wingman must remember to arrive at
the rejoin station through a flight path that passes through the fighting wing position to
capture normal rejoin references (for the turning or straight-ahead rejoin respectively).
This will provide an overshoot lane if it becomes needed. Figure 16.25 illustrates the
turning rejoin when cleared directly to echelon (known as the hot turning rejoin).
Figure 16.25: Hot turning rejoin from LAB
67.
The lead’s responsibility in a turning rejoin is to tailor the manoeuvre to the expe-
rience level of the wingman. For example, a small check-turn away from the wingman
prior to initiating the rejoin may be used to help a novice wingman learn the basics of
the manoeuvre and increase the safety margin while doing so. More experienced pilots
may not need such a turn for the rejoin, but may still require a correction to the proper
line abreast position if the wingman is too far ahead of the normal position. The lead
must always assess the situation prior to calling the rejoin.
16.21
FLIGHT SAFETY
68.
Situational Awareness. Situational awareness refers to your understanding of
what is happening right now and what is about to happen should the manoeuvre, what-
ever it happens to be, be continued. Obviously then, a loss of situational awareness can
range from temporary uncertainty to total confusion which, if allowed to progress, may
result in a dangerous situation. The keys are to anticipate events about to happen, and

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 17 =====
to then avoid them. If, at any time during the trip, you lose situational awareness or find
yourself in a dangerous situation, you must take immediate corrective action; the action
to be taken depends on the current situation.
69.
Advanced formation, including fluid manoeuvring and line abreast, shall conform
to the following training rules. The training rules given in Table 16.1 must be briefed
prior to any mission involving these exercises.
16.22
TWO-SHIP G-AWARENESS
70.
Prior to commencing any manoeuvring above 3 G, a G-awareness exercise shall be
carried out. As shown in Figure 16.26, the sequence will be flown from LAB formation
with a minimum airspeed of 220 KIAS. In order to commence the G-awareness the lead
will call “VENOMS, STAND-BY FOR G-WARM”. At this point all formation mem-
bers select PCL to MAX, crosscheck fuel quantity, and complete the required checks.
Once ensuring 220 KIAS, formation will call ready with fuel in sequence “VENOM 21,
READY WITH 800 AND BALANCED” followed by “VENOM 22 READY, SAME,
BALANCED”.
71.
At this time lead will call “VENOMS IN PLACE 90 (L/R)” normally towards the
wingman, which is to be flown at 3 G. Following the turn, both aircraft will commence
a 5 second ½ G push over. Once lead sees the wingman level, they will then call “VEN-
OMS, HOOK (L/R).” Both AC will fly a 4 G energy sustaining hook turn with PCL
set to MAX, which may require a slight descent. Once both crews feel satisfied to con-
tinue (should only take a few seconds), the wingman will call “22 COMPLETE.” This
means all players in number two aircraft are feeling ready to move on. The lead aircraft
will complete the G-awareness by calling “VENOMS IN PLACE 90 (L/R)” to original
heading, which will be flown as a standard 3 G energy sustaining turn.
16.23
THREE AND FOUR PLANE FORMATION
72.
Three- and four-plane formations are an integral part of your training. A three-
plane is flown with two wingmen under the direction of one lead. A four-plane is flown
with two elements under the direction of one lead. This section deals with the concepts
of two-element formation and their complexities; therefore, a three-plane formation as
described in the following is primarily about a four-plane without the number four air-
craft.
73.
The positioning of each aircraft in all of the formations shown can be directly
related to basic two-plane positions. Thus, number four in the box position is simply
flying the line astern position on lead, while number two and number three are flying
normal echelon. In all of the other four-plane formations, number four flies one of the
basic positions on number three; number three flies one of the basic positions on either

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 18 =====

horizontal
Altitude restrictions:
•
Hard deck 3000’ AGL or 2000’ above cloud
•
6000’ AGL with solo students
Manoeuvring Considerations:
•
Wingmen makes the miss (high if LL)
•
Lead remain predictable
•
Transmit your intended flight path
•
Head-on — Clear right
•
Nose high goes high if able
•
No crossing flight paths
•
Fluid maneouvring/high-aspect — 500’ bubble
•
LAB crossing — 300’ min safe separation
Flight Safety:
•
Wingmen losing visual or SA will manoeuvre away from lead’s last known position and call BLIND
with altitude
•
Lead losing sight or SA of wingmen will manoeuvre predictably
•
G-limits — Lead +4 G / Wingmen +5 G
•
Out of control / stall / spin recovery
•

•
Inadvertent cloud penetration
•
Ejection — OOC 6000’ AGL
•
Rescue cap responsibilities
Terminate: Ceases the current event or manoeuvre, is indicated by R/T or wing rock, is acknowledged
by all players in sequence and will be called when:
•
BINGO fuel is reached
•
Desired learning objectives are either met or unattainable
•
Breach of a training rule or other limits are recognized
•
Both lead and wingmen lose visual contact
•
Loss of comms with formation members
Knock-It-Off: Ceases all maneouvres, ends the overall mission, is indicated by R/T or wing rock, is
acknowledged by all players in sequence and will be called when:
•
Safety of flight is a factor
•
A dangerous situation develops (e.g. FM with 90 aspect, zero LOS, and high closure)
•
Weather deteriorates below minimums

lead. A three-plane formation may be referred to as if in a four-plane formation with
