number four not present, i.e. in three-plane finger right, number three flies echelon right
on lead while number two flies echelon left.
16.24
BRIEFING
74.
The lead will brief the formation using the format as discussed previously in the
Basic Formation section. It cannot be over-emphasized that the success of a formation
trip often depends upon the thoroughness of the briefing. This is especially important in
three- or four-plane formation.
16.25
GROUND HANDLING
75.
The start, taxi, and radio procedure shall be conducted in the same manner as for
the two-plane formation. Keep in mind that there are three or four aircraft manoeuvring
in proximity to one another; therefore, care shall be exercised to ensure that each for-
mation member is taxiing in the correct sequence and that no other aircraft cuts into the
formation. The lead shall brief taxi positions to minimize prop wash.

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 20 =====
Figure 16.27: Basic Four-Plane Formations
16.26
LINE-UP
76.
The main consideration for the line-up is flight safety. Some of the factors in-
fluencing flight safety are: the runway length in the event of an abort, the crosswind
component, the runway condition, the turnout direction (all aircraft should be able to
see the aircraft they are joining on), etc. The formation leader must consider all these
factors and establish the line-up accordingly.
77.
A four-plane formation take-off is normally accomplished in elements with lead

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 21 =====
and two as the first element, and three leading four in the second element. Both elements
line up as per the two-ship procedures, but with 500’ separation. It is important that each
element employ the same two-plane take-off technique to maintain separation between
elements. Normally, the second element will line up in the opposite echelon of the
first element as this allows for a quick rejoin into a finger reference. Five hundred feet
between elements normally provides the second element with sufficient separation so
that significant wake turbulence is not encountered. As the line-up does not permit the
lead to see all aircraft, number four will transmit when in position and ready for run-up
(i.e. “VENOM 34, IN”).
NOTE
During three-plane operations, the lead has the option of taking off with
both wingmen in echelon (Vic) (minimum runway width of 150’) or with
number three following four-plane procedures. This option may also be
applied to a four-plane take-off; number four will line up behind the
Vic with 500’ of separation and delay their roll for a minimum of five
seconds after the Vic. This type of take-off is commonly referred to as a
Vic + 1 take-off.
Figure 16.28: Four-Plane Line-Ups
16.27
TAKE-OFF
78.
The formation take-off in the Harvard II is a normal procedure that allows for up
to three aircraft to conduct the take-off roll together. The lead will normally call for
the engine run-up using the radio, i.e. “YODAS RUNNING UP”. No call is required

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 22 =====
from the second element unless there is an abort (during the briefing, the lead should
have indicated the procedure in the event of an abort prior to brake release). All aircraft
advance their torque to 30% and individual element take-offs are performed as for a
two-plane formation. The second element commences its take-off roll a minimum of 5
seconds after the lead element. This type of take-off is commonly referred to as a 2 + 2
take-off in operational jet flying.
NOTE
The spacing of five seconds for element take-offs is based on 500’ of sep-
aration on line-up. If this separation is reduced, the interval must be
increased. Care must be taken in reducing the 500’ separation as the
turbulence experienced in the second element will increase.
16.28
ABORT ON TAKE-OFF ROLL (FOUR PLANE)
79.
Special consideration should be given to situations which may cause you to abort
in a four-plane takeoff, as they may differ from a two-plane formation or single-plane.
In the event of an abort by an aircraft in the lead element, the non-aborting aircraft will
action as per normal two-plane abort procedures. The aborting aircraft shall attempt to
call the abort on the radio and continue to roll out straight ahead. The second element,
upon hearing or observing an abort in the lead element, must also abort and call it on
the radio i.e. “VENOM 33/34 ABORT ABORT ABORT”. Extreme caution must be
exercised if attempting an abort with two aircraft in close proximity. An abort by a
member of the second element is handled as per two-plane procedures. The formation
commander will handle further conduct of the mission. Once under control, number
four should call “Clear” followed by number three, and number two over the PRI radio
to advise all formation members that they can manoeuver to the cold side if required.
16.29
INTERVAL TAKE-OFF
80.
Under extreme wind conditions, a stream (four-plane interval) take-off may be
performed. The same procedures are utilized as for a two-plane interval take-off. The
formation lead will brief the required interval spacing between aircraft however a mini-
mum of 5 seconds must be used. Aborts during four-plane interval take-offs require all
aircraft behind the aborting aircraft to abort also.
16.30
JOIN-UP
81.
The join-up can be accomplished by having the second element join from a fight-
ing wing position or with the second element maintaining close formation. Normally

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 23 =====
the lead element will accelerate to 180 KIAS and set approximately 80% torque. Alter-
natively, when briefed, following the post take-off check, lead may set approximately
80% torque and accelerate to 180 KIAS. The second element will take-off and perform
the post-take-off check as per normal.
82.
Fighting Wing Join-Up. Once the post-take-off checks are complete and condi-
tions safely permit number three signals number four to move to fighting wing. This
allows the second element to be highly manoeuvrable. Both aircraft will then rejoin
to the pre-briefed positions using the procedures detailed in the rejoin section of this
chapter.
83.
Close Formation Join-Up. Once the post-take-off checks are complete, the sec-
ond element continues to accelerate beyond 180 KIAS in order to generate closure with
the lead element. The procedures used by three to rejoin are the same as those detailed
for single element formations. It is three’s responsibility to assess closure rates and plan
to reduce closure in order to stabilize in a route position on the lead aircraft. Three,
however, must also ensure that PCL, speed brake, and bank changes are not beyond the
capability of number four.
16.31
FORMATION CLIMB
84.
When the formation has rejoined to the required position, the lead shall advance
torque as required allowing the wingmen to maintain station (normally approximately
85%). During the climb, or shortly after the level off, the lead shall signal the climb
and level off checks to be completed. Individual aircraft shall move to the route position
and complete the required checks. If in fighting wing formation, a signal to complete
checks is not required. After completion of the checks, the formation will reform or
change position on the lead’s command.
16.32
FORMATION CHANGES
85.
Most three-plane and all four-plane formation changes shall be called by the lead
on R/T. If, for some reason, you do not understand the call made by the lead, do not
attempt to guess what it was, but rather remain in your present position until the lead
repeats the transmission, or ask for clarification. If at any time during a four plane for-
mation change you are unsure of the actions to be taken or feel that an unsafe condition
exists, call FREEZE. At any time that this call is made, all aircraft in the formation stop
in their current position and wait. Lead will then be directive, request who called the
freeze, discern the nature of the problem, then take the necessary steps to rectify the
situation. Remember, if in doubt, do not hesitate to call FREEZE. An extra freeze call
will not harm anyone; however the lack of one due to uncertainty may prove hazardous.
If able, the formation member that calls FREEZE should add a descriptor for the reason
e.g. “VENOMS FREEZE, VENOM 32 FREEZE AREA BOUNDARY”.

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 24 =====
86.
Extra care and attention must be exercised by all wingmen during formation changes.
Aircraft control should be smooth and positive, but not rushed. Before completing a sta-
tion change, ensure that the preceding aircraft has established the correct position, e.g.
finger to line astern – number three drops back but does not move laterally until number
two has moved into the line astern position. Numbers two and three shall bear in mind
that they may have an aircraft on their wing and must fly accordingly.
87.
Finger-Right to Echelon-Right. When the lead has given the echelon-right di-
rective, i.e. “YODAS, ECHELON RIGHT”, number three, with number four on their
wing, moves out, back, and slightly down to provide sufficient room for number two to
assume the new formation. When number two sees that sufficient space has been made,
the move to the new formation can be initiated, during which number two crosses be-
hind and below the lead. Once number two is in position, numbers three and four will
regain normal formation spacing. The same procedure is used to change from finger-left
to echelon-left.
88.
Echelon-Right to Finger-Left. Numbers three and four move slowly back and
down to pass safely behind and below number two and the lead. As number three passes
behind the lead aircraft, number four should pass lower than, and behind, number three
and begin to take up a position of echelon left on number three. Once in position,
normal spacing is maintained. To change from finger-left to echelon-right the procedure
is simply reversed. The same procedures apply from echelon-left to finger-right or vice-
versa.
89.
Finger to Line Astern. When changing formation from either finger to line astern,
numbers two, and three with four on the wing, will begin to move back and slightly
down. Number three will move back far enough to allow number two to take up position
before starting to move into position. As number three moves across, number four
moves into position on number three. Numbers two and three should make every effort
to be as smooth as possible in order to prevent any errors from compounding.
90.
Line Astern to Finger. Number two will begin to move to the appropriate side and
up into position. When number two has moved out of the line astern position, number
three will move to the appropriate side and up into the appropriate echelon position on
lead, and number four will regain echelon position on number three.
91.
Finger to Box. When the lead calls for box formation, numbers two and three
maintain their positions on lead. Number four will move back and down slightly to pass
behind number three and stabilize in a loose line astern behind lead. Once stabilized,
torque is applied to move into a normal line astern on lead. Lead must ensure that the
formation reforms in the same finger that was used before going to box.
92.
Finger to Fighting Wing/Echelon to Fighting Wing. When entering fighting
wing formation from either finger or echelon, the wingmen slowly drop back from the
lead and simultaneously increase separation from each other. This separation should

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 25 =====
gradually increase until each aircraft is stabilized in a fighting wing position behind the
preceding aircraft. A lateral move into position can then be made.
16.33
PROHIBITED CHANGES IN FOUR-PLANE FORMATION
93.
The only change not authorized for four-plane formations is finger to finger.
16.34
REJOINS
94.
Four-plane rejoins, whether in elements or single aircraft, follow the same proce-
dures as in two-plane, with the exception that numbers three and four will normally join
to the outside of lead on a turning rejoin. Specific overshoot procedures are detailed in
the following paragraphs; however, once committed to an overshoot, the wingman must
advise the formation lead (this also warns the other formation members), and execute
the procedure as briefed.
95.
Straight Ahead. Straight ahead rejoins can be either to finger or echelon posi-
tion. The lead initiates the rejoin by making a radio call for the desired formation, e.g.
“VENOMS FINGER LEFT”. The wingmen use the same procedures as for a two-plane
rejoin, but each aircraft must maintain safe separation until the preceding aircraft is sta-
bilized in position. In the event of an overshoot, the same techniques as for two-plane
overshoots are to be followed.
96.
Turning Rejoins. The lead initiates the rejoin by rocking the wings (number of
wing rocks depend on starting point and desired finish point) and then starting a turn
(normally 30 degrees of bank); the airspeed is maintained as pre-briefed or as called.
Number two (unless briefed otherwise) joins to the inside of the turn using the proce-
dures described in the Basic Formation chapter. Numbers three and four establish the
same cut-off line as number two and continue to close on lead, aiming to pass approx-
imately two aircraft lengths behind and slightly lower than lead. Overshoot procedures
are the same as in two-plane rejoins, but all formation members must be aware of the
overshoot procedure for each aircraft in the formation and ensure safe separation is
maintained to allow for any manoeuvring required. Number two’s overshoot path is
to the outside of the turn. Only after number two has stabilized and is entering their
new position can number three cross behind the lead to join echelon on the outer wing.
Number four must not cross behind lead until number three is stabilized. The overshoot
procedure for number three or number four is to roll wings-level and go outside of lead’s
turn.

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 26 =====
16.35
LEAD CHANGE
97.
If a change of lead is required during the mission, it shall be carried out as briefed.
Once the new lead has moved into position, a call for a re-number and fuel status will
be made. The new numbers two, three, and four will state their new positions in the
formation, in sequence, followed by fuel remaining i.e. “VENOMS RE-NUMBER” —
“VENOM 21 – 500, BALANCED, VENOM 22 – SAME, BALANCED, VENOM 23 –
SAME, BALANCED, VENOM 24 – SAME, BALANCED”.
16.36
MANOEUVRING THE FOUR-PLANE FORMATION
98.
Four-plane manoeuvring should be smooth and positive. The lead shall think ahead
and make every effort to ensure that instructions are understood. Wingmen should try
to anticipate torque changes and make them as smoothly as possible. Remember that
someone may be flying off your wing. The most important consideration in any ma-
noeuvring is the experience level of the wingmen.
99.
Turns in finger formation and in line astern are the same as in two-plane forma-
tions.
100. While turns in fighting wing may be more aggressive, the lead shall avoid hard
turns into a wingman that could cause abrupt manoeuvring throughout the formation.
101. Echelon will be primarily used to set up for the overhead break. If the occasion
arises to manoeuvre the formation, turns in echelon are normally flat and away from the
formation. Gentle turns into and away (stepped up) from the formation are acceptable,
but due to the slow roll rate, these are not useful manoeuvres. Four-plane flat turns
are not formally taught during your advanced training. If required, follow the same
procedures as for two-plane, but be extremely vigilant and attempt to avoid any abrupt
corrections to the ideal position. Remember, there may be someone flying directly be-
neath you.
102. The box formation is excellent for manoeuvring, since each wingman is flying off
the lead.
16.37
STATION KEEPING IN FINGER AS NUMBER FOUR
103. Station keeping in finger as number four is done off of number three the same as
if flying directly off the lead. Since number three will be moving around in position
(relative to lead) to some extent, attempting to maintain an exact position as number
four may lead to excessive movement on your part. Attempt to maintain a more fluid
position, striving to average out the movements of number three and to minimize your

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 27 =====
movements in relation to the formation as a whole. The recommended method of doing
this is to take your primary references for the appropriate echelon position off of number
three, while trying to take your references for the formation (pitch and bank) off of the
lead. This is commonly referred to as “flying through” number three. Initially, this is a
difficult task to accomplish, as you must always honour number three’s movements.
16.38
FIGHTING WING FORMATION
104. Three- or four-plane fighting wing is a very manoeuvrable semi-tactical formation
that allows all formation members to maintain an active lookout. As in two-plane fight-
ing wing, all formation members fly a 60 degree sweep off the preceding aircraft. For-
mation members are free to manoeuvre within their assigned area, with number three
and four normally alternating on the side of the cone opposite to that being flown by
number two.
Figure 16.29: Four-Plane Fighting Wing
105. Fighting wing is normally entered from a finger position or echelon. If entered
from echelon, number two will normally move out to the same side occupied during
close formation. If desired by the lead, it may be employed immediately after take-
off. In all cases with a three- or four-plane formation, a radio call will be used to
indicate a change in formation to the fighting wing formation, i.e. “YODAS, FIGHTING
WING”. To resume close formation, lead will make a radio call, i.e. “YODAS, FINGER
RIGHT”.

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 28 =====
106. If lead wants to rejoin comms out then they must pre-brief that a wing rock fol-
lowed by a 30 degree bank turn means rejoin to finger. The direction of the turn indicates
the finger formation desired, in that number two always joins to the inside of the turn
unless otherwise briefed, i.e. right turn means join finger left.
16.39
FOUR PLANE FIGHTING WING
107. During four-plane fighting wing, wingmen must, while maintaining position with
reference to the aircraft ahead, keep all preceding aircraft in sight and ensure separa-
tion. As in two-plane fighting wing, if any member of the formation wishes the lead
to discontinue manoeuvring for any reason, a call of “BLACKS TERMINATE” can be
made. If this call is made, all formation members must acknowledge in succession (i.e.
BLACK 71 TERMINATE, 72 TERMINATE, 73 TERMINATE, etc.) and anticipate that
the lead shall transition to a level turn. Manoeuvring shall be recommenced on the
lead’s direction. The general rule of thumb is that four misses three, who misses two,
who misses lead.
16.40
FLUID MANOEUVRING
108. The concepts of three- and four-plane fluid manoeuvring are the same as two-plane
manoeuvring however there are certain restrictions as outlined in the 2 CFFTS Orders.
16.41
4-SHIP OFFSET BOX
109. Offset Box (Figure 16.30) is a four-plane formation flown with two elements flying
in LAB with the second element trailing by 6000–8000’ (1.0–1.2 NM) behind the first
element with an offset. In box formation, elements use the LAB manoeuvring and
lookout principles. The objective of the spacing is to give separation to avoid easy
visual detection of the whole formation and to allow the rear element to protect the front
element. Because the Harvard II is difficult to see from a direct trail position, an offset
will facilitate keeping sight of the lead element.
110. Offset box can be established from fighting wing or finger. On the command,
“VENOMS, OFFSET BOX EAST”, element leaders initiate formation manoeuvres as
briefed. The number three aircraft manoeuvres to achieve pre-briefed spacing on the
lead element.
111. Collision Avoidance. The trailing element is responsible for maintaining separa-
tion from the front element. This is especially important if the box becomes over-square.
Ensure offset to overcome this problem.
112. Offset Box Manoeuvring. (Figures 16.30, 16.31, and 16.32)

===== SMM part 7 PDF page 29 =====
a.
Delayed (45/90) and Hook Turns. Number 3 and number 4 delay turn by
10–15 seconds and miss numbers one and two. This is to ensure that the
second element will flow to trail in the correct position.
b.
In place, shackle, and check turns will have no delay.
Figure 16.30: Offset Box + Delayed 90 Right
Figure 16.31: Offset Box Delayed 45 Right

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 1 =====
Figure 16.32: Offset Box Hook Turn
16.42
4 SHIP SPREAD 4
113. Spread-4 (Figure 16.33) is a four-plane LAB formation. In the formation, number
three will be flying LAB off lead and the wingmen will position themselves at LAB off
their respective leads.
Figure 16.33: Spread 4 LAB

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 2 =====
114. The formation will normally only move into spread-4 via finger or fighting wing.
When initiating the move from finger to spread-4, care must be taken by number three
to move out slower than usual to allow sufficient manoeuvring space for number four.
As such, number three will wait for number four to begin moving out before starting to
create distance.
115. The responsibility of number four is to maintain visual with number three and
ideally lead and number two throughout. This can be established by using a reduced
heading change for gradual positioning and by using a slight altitude stack. For number
two nothing changes, and they can move into position as per SOP.
116. To maintain proper formation integrity and ideal LAB, respective positions will fly
as per the following: number three and number two fly LAB off lead, number four flies
LAB off number three.
117. Lookout Responsibilities. Lookout must be increased to include scanning for the
remaining airplanes.
16.43
SPREAD 4 MANOEUVRING
118. Approved formation manoeuvring in spread-4 includes: delayed 90s, delayed 45s,
in-place, hook, and check turns. Delayed turns are depicted in Figure 16.34. Timing
the turns remains standard as with two-plane LAB; however, extra care must be taken
to fly accurate headings and keeping a lookout for the aircraft you are turning towards.
Precision during the turns is of increased importance due to the fact that spacing, timing,
and corrections all affect the remaining aircraft in the section.
119. All turns will be initiated with comms.
NOTE
During spread-4 manoeuvring, where aircraft are likely to cross flight
paths, altitude separation is the main means of ensuring safe separation
between all aircraft.
16.44
SPREAD 4 G-AWARENESS
120. Spread-4 G-awareness (Figure 16.35) is largely carried out the same way as in
two-plane formation, however, differences in lookout and accuracy exist. Primary re-
sponsibility of all players will be to fly an accurate platform and maintain separation of
the aircraft ahead. This includes flying accurate G and headings during the turns and on
rollout.

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 3 =====
Figure 16.34: Spread 4 delayed turns
Figure 16.35: Spread 4 G-Warm
16.45
SPREAD 4 HOOK TURNS
121. Spread-4 hook turns (Figure 16.36) are again carried out as per two-plane forma-
tions; however, extra vigilance is required to ensure accurate manoeuvring in respect
to the other players. The aim is to have all players roll out 180 degrees from original
heading in LAB formation. In order to accomplish this, aiming for aligned fuselages at

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 4 =====
the 90 degree mark should be done for all aircraft on the outside of the turn.
Figure 16.36: Spread 4 Hook Turn
16.46
RETURN TO BASE
122. Circuits and Landing. As in two-plane formation, the overhead break is executed
from echelon formation with the move to echelon normally being made after completing
the last turn in the traffic pattern. The break and subsequent circuit and landing are the
same as in two-plane formation, with each aircraft taking separation off of the preceding
aircraft. During the final approach and landing, as described in two-plane procedures,
the lead aims to line up and land on the centerline of the runway with the other formation
members landing directly behind. Ensure that a minimum of 2000’ separation is main-
tained between aircraft. If wake turbulence is anticipated, greater separation should be
established. After landing, each aircraft, when at a safe speed, moves to the clear side
of the runway.
123. Formation Landing. Procedures are the same as in two-plane landings. As only
two aircraft normally land together, a three- or four-plane formation intending to land in
formation usually split up into separate elements. This will be coordinated by the lead.
124. IFR. For planned IMC flights, the lead shall consider several factors including de-
parture and destination weather, icing, turbulence, tops of cloud, and possible greater
fuel consumption. Separate element climbs to a rejoin “on top” is recommended, en-
suring separation through an altitude and/or heading split between the two elements,

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 5 =====
but may not be authorized by civil ATC. Departure to “on top” may be carried out in
separate two-plane formations or a join-up to four planes “under the weather” may be
possible, but this is the least recommended option. Once level off has occurred “on top”,
the formation members shall ensure that the briefed enroute position is maintained (nor-
mally fighting wing). If IMC weather is encountered, close formation shall be recalled
by the lead, and must be formed without delay, prior to entering the weather. Four-plane
instrument approaches should not be attempted. Rather, the approach should be made
in separate elements. The procedure for splitting into elements must be fully covered
in the pre-flight briefing and must conform to base flying orders. The remainder of the
approach and landing is the same as for a two-plane formation.
125. During formation, only the lead is required to squawk. If the formation is split into
element pairs, then both element leads shall squawk.

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 6 =====
CHAPTER 17: TACTICAL NAVIGATION (TAC NAV)
17.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
Once you have mastered single-plane low-level systems navigation and medium
level LAB, the next step is to fly two-plane, low-level LAB missions. LAB low-level
navigation is designed to maximize lookout and survivability of the flight through mu-
tual support.
2.
The mechanics of the various turns are performed in the same way as you learned
at medium level altitude. There is always a contract between lead and the wingman;
this clearly establishes division of responsibility within the formation to simplify LAB
in the low-level environment.
17.2
PLANNING
3.
Normally lead will take charge of the mission planning with the assistance of the
wingman. The completed maps should be identical for both the wingman and the leader.
The planning process is the same as for single-ship navigation with the following ex-
ceptions.
a.
Take-off to Hack. The formation lead is expected to brief the formation po-
sition the wingman will be expected to fly enroute to the hack. Typically,
fighting wing will be flown as it is extremely fluid while maintaining forma-
tion integrity, which is desirable during this phase of flight.
b.
Hack. The hack should be positioned so that the formation can be lined up
with it early enough to complete all necessary checks and to position the
wingman in LAB. As a general rule, a 10 NM offset run-in to the hack will
provide sufficient time for the wingman to move to the ideal LAB position,
for the formation leader to align the formation with the first leg track, to
execute a formation descent, and to be ready to cross the hack on heading,
altitude, and airspeed.
c.
1:500,000 Map. Take the width of the formation into account to avoid the
wingman flying through restricted areas and towns, etc. Only the lead’s route
is marked on the map; other non-standard markings such as the wingman’s
300

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 7 =====
position, etc. should only be placed on individual maps and not disseminated
to other cockpits to avoid confusion.
d.
Routes. The route should be planned to allow the formation maximum ex-
posure to different types of turns covering heading changes which are not
always exactly 45 or 90 degrees. Take into account that both aircraft must
have the opportunity to fly as lead and wingman; a tactical lead change proce-
dure should be planned with an adequate amount of time to minimize impact
to other tasks.
e.
RTB. The target should be located in a position and distance from the aero-
drome that allows for sufficient time to carry out all the necessary post-target
checks, procedures, and duties.
17.3
BRIEFING
4.
The briefing is a vital part of mission preparation. The lead should brief all do-
mestic aspects of the mission (i.e. those items normally covered during close-formation
trips), followed by tactical domestics including the transit from and to the airport, the
type of recovery, the descent into the low level environment, and any other pertinent
aspects of the mission. All pilots should come to the briefing room prepared, having
spent time studying the route and any aspects of the mission which may pose challenges
to the formation, such as sensitive citizens, obstacles, restricted areas, etc.
17.4
TAC NAV PROCEDURES
5.
General. The aim of tac nav will be to hone tactical formation flying skills in
preparation for air-to-surface tactics, which you will learn during future training. Task
prioritization is vital during this type of mission, the order of which will be: adherence to
contracts through individual low-level crosscheck, effective mutual support, and precise
navigation.
6.
Departure Procedures. Enroute to the hack, in addition to carrying out all checks
done during single aircraft low-level navigation, you will be required to carry out G
awareness and the FENCE-IN check.
7.
G-Awareness. G-awareness is normally conducted in LAB as described in Chap-
ter 16. In your future training this turn will most often be done towards the wingman,
allowing lead to complete their weapons checks.
8.
Time-On-Target (TOT). Your previous navigation training involved arriving at
an elapsed time. In tac nav, you may be required to arrive at a given TOT. When given
a TOT, work backwards from the TOT and add any other required mission task times to
determine your hack time. Crosscheck of your elapsed time or your EFB may be used
to assess your enroute timing.

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 8 =====
9.
Marshall. The marshall is a means by which the formation can hold prior to
the hack. The marshall is usually conducted at or prior to the hack and will generally
be flown in LAB or fighting wing, at 200 KIAS. Lead will marshall the formation to
make good their hack time. Once lead requires the formation to accelerate to 240 knots
groundspeed (in order to make their hack time) they will normally call “HAMMERS,
PUSH IT UP”. Upon crossing the hack or appropriate hack time, the formation lead will
call the hack over the radio “HAMMERS, PREPARE TO HACK, HACK NOW” and
both aircraft will start their clocks.
10.
At some point during the marshall or prior to the hack, the formation lead will call
for the FENCE-IN check to be completed.
11.
The FENCE In check is used to ensure the formation is ready to enter the low level
tactical environment.
12.
The standard R/T for initiating FENCE In/Out checks is:
a.
Lead: “YODAS FENCE IN / OUT”
b.
Wingman: “42 FENCE IN / OUT”
13.
The following is the FENCE IN check:
a.
Fuel — determine fuel quantity above joker fuel
b.
Emitters — squawk 1200 (both aircraft sqwk ALT)
c.
Navigation checks:
(1)
Nav source GPS, turn anticipate disabled;
(2)
Ops check, harness, and loose articles secure; and
(3)
Reset clocks
d.
Comms — set appropriate frequency in VHF2 (e.g. Gravelbourg MF) if
required
e.
EW — Set the anti-collision lights ON.
14.
Line Abreast Descent. The two-plane descent profile will be performed in the
same manner as during single-plane low-level navigation operations. Strict adherence to
the low-level cross-check, primarily to terrain avoidance and lookout, is paramount. The
descent will normally be carried out straight ahead and the wingman shall wait for lead
to initiate the descent. Each pilot shall brief terrain considerations and the 500’ altitude
(expressed as an altitude MSL) prior to descent. Initially the wingman should ensure
that they maintain lead at or slightly below the horizon. When reaching 1000’ AGL,
both pilots will focus mainly on terrain clearance and levelling at the pre-determined
altitude.
15.
Line Abreast Station Keeping.
It is the wingman’s responsibility to maintain
appropriate LAB position. The wingman will normally position lead on or slightly
below the horizon in the low-level environment.

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 9 =====
16.
LAB Turns. In the tactical environment, turns are seldom exactly 90 degrees or
45 degrees. Turns of 30 degrees or less will normally be flown as check turns with the
wingman repositioning to minimize loss of mutual support. Check turns on a low-level
route may be completed comms-out (as briefed). As a general rule, for turns greater than
30 degrees up to 70 degrees, a modified delayed 45 should be used. For turns greater
than 70 degrees but less than 110 degrees, a modified delayed 90 should be used. For
heading changes greater than 110 degrees, the turn may be flown as a single delayed
turn or a combination of several delayed turns.
17.
For turns less than 90/45 (i.e. 75 degrees for a delayed 90), the second aircraft
will turn later. For turns greater than 90/45, the second aircraft will turn earlier. With
experience, the geometry solution will become easier to identify.
18.
When leading the formation, determining when to initiate the turn is important for
maintaining formation integrity. Whenever you are on time in your navigation, timing
can be used to determine when to initiate the turn. For a delayed 90, execute the wing
flash approximately 17 seconds before the turn point. For a delayed 45 execute the wing
flash approximately 35 seconds before the turn point. If you have a minor timing error,
use DME to determine when to initiate the turn. As a general rule of thumb this wing
flash should be done 1.1–1.3 NM prior to a turn point for a delayed 90 and 2.1–2.3 NM
prior for a delayed 45.
19.
As a principle of mutual support, the wingman must maintain situational awareness
on timings while en route. This allows them to anticipate upcoming turns and be ready
for the lead’s wing-flash. Additionally, the wingman must strive to regain their line
abreast position as soon as possible after rolling out. It is also vitally important that the
wingman achieves the correct line abreast position prior to a turn in order to avoid a
conflict with lead, especially with 45 degree turns.
20.
It is the wingman’s responsibility to ensure they make the miss with the lead air-
craft. For safety reasons, the wingman will miss lead by going high while operating in
the low-level environment
21.
Before turning, visually clear the area into the turn, evaluate the terrain for slope
and look out for other aircraft and hazards. In a tactical environment you will also have
to check the formation’s old six o’clock, new six o’oclock, and new 12 o’clock position
(i.e. clearing the area both ahead of and behind the present and anticipated position).
This will prepare you for potential threats in your future training.
22.
Mutual protection is achieved by keeping a constant lookout while maintaining
accurate line abreast formation. During your training you will learn to develop and
maintain an effective work cycle throughout the missions in order to achieve these goals.
23.
Look out is vital. Do not stare at the other aircraft but clear your areas of respon-
sibility. You can only look away from clearing the front for 3–5 seconds at a time and
you MUST NEVER LOOK BEHIND THE 3—9 LINE WHILE TURNING. Monitor

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 10 =====
the upcoming terrain in the turn and if a descent is detected, correct immediately by first
decreasing the bank and then increasing the G. Once rolled out, clear the terrain ahead,
lookout, assess position relative to lead, and carry out any mission specific tasks that
may be required.
24.
Track Corrections. Formation integrity and mutual protection should be the num-
ber one priority even when off track. Any heading corrections using check turns should
be kept small to prevent loss of formation integrity. If delayed turns are used, a shackle
may be necessary to reposition the wingman on the appropriate side, based on threats,
or if LAB geometry requires immediate adjustment. If track errors are large, it does not
matter if you are not able to get to the next waypoint, the route can be adjusted (tactical
situation permitting) to maintain formation integrity and get back on time.
25.
Time Corrections. As with track corrections, any time corrections should be done
with minimum impact on formation integrity.
26.
During your training you will use the following for timing corrections:
a.
Small error (less than 30 sec): adjust airspeed to correct time error.
b.
Medium error (greater than 30 sec, but less than 1 minute): use the principle
of turning on time. By turning on the planned time and adjusting the heading
to the next waypoint (using DCT TO on the GPS) it is possible to take out
inaccuracies of up to 60 seconds on a 90 degree turn. Larger errors will
require an additional time allowance on top of the planned turn-on-time to
account for the increase in distance on the next leg. Threat analysis must
be a consideration when using this type of correction. For example, if you
are planning on turning prior to the turn point, you must ensure that your
new track does not go over a sensitive citizen or some other simulated threat
presented by your QFI.
c.
Large error (greater than 1 minute): due to the route being built with approx-
imately 90 degree angles between most of the legs, shortcut options exist
for deviations larger than 1 minute. Before directing their wingman (using
comm-out turns where possible) lead must ensure applicable NOTAMs and
other restrictions off the planned route will be respected and avoided.
27.
Target Run-In. On the final leg of the route, there will be an Initial Point (IP)
prior to the target. This is designed so that if the formation is significantly off-time they
can flow directly to the IP to manage time within the TOT window range. This IP will
generally be built approximately 8 NM back from the target but may also be the last turn
point prior to the target. Immediately after crossing the IP, the formation lead will call
“VENOM 21 AIR-TO-GROUND GO” to which the wingman will respond “VENOM
22”. At this point both formation members will carry out their target brief, which will
consist of the following items:
a.
Action — Action point and direction, including wind considerations to main-
tain 240 knots groundspeed

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 11 =====
b.
Target — Specific target
c.
TOT — Within or outside of allotted time window
d.
Egress — Egress heading, altitude, and rejoin
e.
Fuel — Fuel required at target point and current joker delta
28.
Action on the target run-in. (Figure 17.1) After completing the target brief, the
wingman will monitor the distance remaining for the Action which will be conducted at
approximately 4 NM (or 1 minute) prior to the target.
a.
At 4 NM (1 minute) prior to target, the wingman conducts an energy-sustaining
90 degree turn (setting PCL to maintain 240 knots groundspeed) perpendic-
ular to track and calls “22 ACTIONING”.
b.
After approximately 20 seconds (including “actioning” turn and approxi-
mately 5–10 sec after passing tail of lead) the wingman turns direct to the
target. This will place lead at your approximate 8 o’clock or 4 o’clock po-
sition. Groundspeed must be maintained at 240 knots to ensure adequate
separation between formation members.
c.
Once lead passes overhead the target, they will call “YODA 41, ONE AWAY”.
The wingman will follow with “YODA 42, ONE AWAY” when overhead
their target.
Figure 17.1: Target Run-in Procedure
29.
Post Target Manoeuvring: (Figure 17.2)

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 12 =====
a.
Lead will commence a full power climb with a turn approximately 90 degrees
to the egress heading, to the post-target/egress altitude, gain lateral separation
(approximately 1 NM), and then turn fully to the egress heading.
b.
The wingman will commence a full power climb and turn to the egress head-
ing, climb to the post-target/egress altitude (normally 1000’ below lead), and
look to visually acquire lead. Lead should be at roughly the 11 or 1 o’clock
high position. Once visual with lead, the wingman will call “22 VISUAL”
and then can automatically rejoin to standard LAB or a planned egress po-
sition. Once the wingman is visual and closing in as confirmed by lead, the
lead will call “TERMINATE, TERMINATE, YODA 41 TERMINATE”. The
wingman will respond with “YODA 42 TERMINATE”.
Figure 17.2: Post Target Manoeuvring
30.
Communication Procedures. As a general rule, tactical low-level navigation will
be flown using comms-out procedures therefore communications shall be kept to a min-
imum. The formation leader will normally brief the calls to be made along the route.
31.
Summary of Responsibilities. All formation members are responsible for the safe
and effective operation of their own aircraft, and lookout to maintain mutual support.
Individual formation responsibilities can be summarized as follows:
a.
Lead:

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 13 =====
(1)
low-level tasks;
(2)
lead the formation;
(3)
navigate; and
(4)
lookout.
b.
Wingman:
(1)
low-level tasks;
(2)
maintain formation integrity;
(3)
lookout; and
(4)
backup navigation.
17.5
DESTINATION PROCEDURES
32.
Normally, plan to recover via tac initial or battle break to the applicable runway. If
low on fuel, an overhead break may be the best option. The RTB is generally conducted
in fighting wing until the formation is set up for the final run-in.
33.
Enroute to destination. Once the formation has climbed to a safe and appropriate
altitude you will be required to conduct the FENCE Out and the battle damage checks.
a.
Fuel –– determine fuel quantity above bingo fuel
b.
Emitters –– squawk 0200 (lead – alt / wingman – stby)
c.
Navigation checks –– set up kit for RTB, climb/level off checks
d.
Comms —- reset frequencies as directed by lead
e.
EW –– Set the anti-collision lights OFF.
34.
Rejoins. During TACNAV training, rejoins from line abreast or fighting wing can
be accomplished by simply collapsing to close formation through the route position.
35.
Battle Damage Check. Following the rejoin to echelon, lead will give the check
signal to the wingman. As shown in Figure 17.3 the wingman then moves out to a loose
echelon position, then up on plane to check the upper side of lead’s fuselage, then down
on plane until in a loose low-echelon position, then quickly transiting underneath lead
through a position that is lower than the line astern position checking the underside of
lead’s wings and fuselage. The wingman will then move up through the loose echelon
position on the other side of lead to a loose high-echelon position to inspect the other
side of lead’s fuselage. After moving back into the loose echelon position they give the
thumbs up signal to lead that the check is complete. After this, a lead change will be
done and the checks will be repeated by lead on the wingman. Once the second battle
damage check is completed, another lead change will normally be done.
36.
Tac Initial. The formation lead should aim to be at initial in a closer than normal
line abreast position with an approximate spacing of 1000’ and at 220 KIAS. As shown

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 14 =====
Figure 17.3: Battle Damage Check
Figure 17.4: Tac Initial in Moose Jaw
in Figure 17.4, both aircraft break at the same time; the wingman should conduct a
normal overhead break and follow lead around the break aiming to roll out behind and
slightly outside lead’s aircraft.
37.
Battle Break. As shown in Figure 17.5, the battle break is similar to the tac
initial except the run-in is to be flown at low-level and is flown as a climbing turn to
circuit altitude similar to a closed pattern. Close attention should be given to lookout
and awareness of traffic on final as the formation approaches the airfield. The downwind
portion will be flown as per the taciInitial with the number two aircraft following behind
and slightly outside lead’s aircraft.

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 15 =====
Figure 17.5: Battle Break
17.6
TACTICAL NAVIGATION TRAINING RULES
38.
As in low-level navigation and advanced formation, prior to any TACNAV mission
the low-level nav training rules (Table 10.1) and the advanced formation training rules
(Table 16.1) shall be briefed.
17.7
SUMMARY
39.
The tactical navigation techniques learned during phase III are the foundation of all
low-level surface attack missions. During your follow-on training, the missions will be-
come more and more complex, with different types of attacks, accurate TOTs to achieve,
and bandits to evade. Efficiency at these techniques is essential for any ground attack
pilot. Although there is no bandit at this stage of training, you should work on increas-
ing lookout as your capacity increases; this will help prepare you for more advanced
training and the actual tactical environment.

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 16 =====
CHAPTER 18: NIGHT FLYING
18.1
INTRODUCTION
1.
Night flying is an important part of pilot training. The handling of the aircraft is
no different from ordinary day flying, and the transition from day flying to night flying
is a pleasant experience. However, you may have some initial difficulties adapting your
night vision and becoming accustomed to flying with fewer outside references. That
being said, it is important to remember that a constant and vigilant lookout at night
is just as important as it is during the day to ensure flight safety. Night flying is not
a separate or difficult skill, but merely a combination of the techniques of visual and
instrument flying previously described in this manual. Turbulence is generally less at
night thus night flying is normally an enjoyable part of the course.
2.
This chapter deals primarily with the visual procedures during night flying. In-
strument flying is also part of the night syllabus during your initial training, but the
techniques used for night instrument flying are the same as during the day. Your in-
structor will review pertinent instrument exercises with you prior to each flight. Refer
to Chapters 8 and 9 for applicable instrument flight procedures and techniques.
18.2
PRE-FLIGHT
3.
The pre-external and external checks can be completed normally using a flashlight
as required. Ensure that the area surrounding the aircraft is clear of chocks and other
equipment and that the canopy and windshield are clean.
4.
For the strap-in, select the battery switch to ON, and then turn on the instrument,
console, and flood lights to the desired level for your cockpit. This will aid you during
your strap-in procedures. Rheostats are provided to control the lighting independently
in each cockpit. The instrument (INST), console (SIDE) and flood (FLOOD) lights can
each be adjusted, as required.
5.
Complete the pre-start check normally, paying particular attention to the park brake
ensuring it is on properly as it is more difficult to detect forward movement at night.
During the start check, the anti-collision lights will not be turned on in order to prevent
distractions. Ensure you have the ground crew’s attention as you give the start signal.
310

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 17 =====
6.
Use the taxi light while the aircraft is on the ground at night, unless there is a
danger of blinding someone. At night there is a tendency to taxi too fast; therefore,
constantly check your taxiing speed by referring to the ground area adjacent to the aero-
drome lights. If ever you are in doubt of a taxi routing or of current position, stop and
ask the tower for assistance. Always taxi in the centre of the taxiway. The strobe lights
are extremely bright and in order to prevent distraction, will not be turned on until after
take-off clearance is received. Fog and frost are difficult to detect at night in winter
therefore consider using the defog while taxiing prior to take-off.
7.
It takes about 30 minutes for the eyes to fully develop night vision, but it can
be destroyed in as little as one second. Avoid looking directly at bright lights and be
careful not to damage the night vision of others through careless use of the landing and
taxi lights. Reduce the cockpit light intensity during taxiing as your eyes become more
accustomed to the dark. Remember that the GPS, RMU, and EFIS displays have their
own independent lighting controls on their individual displays. The lights should not
be brighter than is necessary to read the instruments and displays comfortably, prevent
eyestrain, and keep canopy reflections to a minimum.
18.3
IN FLIGHT
8.
Using the taxi light, line up in the centre of the runway and then switch the landing
light on. The landing and taxi lights can normally remain on throughout the mission,
as they will only illuminate when the landing gear is down and locked. At night you
must transition to instruments before the aircraft reaches the end of the runway because
of a lack of visual reference beyond the end of the runway. Remember to crosscheck
the EADI with the VSI during this period to make sure that there is a positive rate
of climb and, as an additional precaution, make the initial climb slightly steeper than
normal. Frequency changes, if required, should be done after the post-take-off check is
completed and the aircraft properly trimmed.
9.
The degree of darkness and horizon clarity determines how you should divide your
attention between the flight instruments and the outside references. On bright, moonlit
nights, you can do most of the flying visually, with occasional glances at the instruments
to confirm your visual impressions. On dark nights, when there is little or no horizon,
concentrate on the instruments and occasionally glance outside to confirm the aircraft’s
position in relation to known points on the ground. Whether the night is light or dark,
the transition from instruments back to outside references should be gradual and should
not normally be started until the aircraft is 500’ AGL after take-off.
10.
Visibility at altitude often exceeds 100 NM, which makes the judgment of dis-
tances difficult. Also, there are fewer outside references, and you must use the flight
instruments more frequently to maintain the desired condition of flight. Ensure that the
defog is turned on before you descend.

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 18 =====
11.
Lights displayed by other aircraft are often very easy to detect; however, their
direction of flight, distance and closure rate are difficult to determine. The navigation
and strobe lights displayed by other aircraft may help to determine their direction of
flight.
12.
High cirrus cloud, St Elmo’s Fire, aurora borealis and the use of the strobe lights
or taxi/landing lights during precipitation can all contribute to pilot disorientation. Turn
the strobe lights and/or taxi and landing lights off if you are distracted while flying in
cloud or precipitation, and concentrate on the flight instruments.
18.4
TRAFFIC PATTERNS AND CIRCUITS
13.
The traffic pattern and circuits flown at night are the same as those flown during
the day. On initial take-off (or after a go around), climb straight ahead and level off at
pattern altitude with an airspeed of 220 KIAS. When able, turn to the crosswind leg.
You must avoid a tendency to make the pattern larger when flying at night. A glance at
the EHSI will show you whether you are flying the pattern correctly. Keep a vigilant
lookout and fly by combining outside references with the instrument indications. It is
even more important at night to use the runway as your primary reference for pattern
orientation. Because of the limited night phase, it is imperative that you develop skills
and references that can be applied to any aerodrome. Geographical references are not
as readily visible at night and they are very airport specific.
14.
When the aircraft is lined up at initial, carefully assess drift in order to apply
proper corrections during the overhead break, final turn, landing, and any subsequent
traffic patterns and circuits. Transmit to tower and pick up your sequencing. Remember
that there may be a strong wind shear at circuit altitude, which could lead you to make a
false estimate of drift. Be aware of this possibility, because it is important at night that
you make an accurate allowance for drift. Fly a normal break while maintaining altitude
and track parallel to the runway downwind.
15.
Roll out on downwind and continue to track parallel to the runway. Initiate the
pre-landing check in preparation for the final turn and complete the final turn as you
would during the day in similar weather conditions. Avoid the common error at night
of rolling out of the final turn lower than normal. Aim to roll out on final ¾ NM from
the threshold while transitioning to a 3-degree glide path. Do not hesitate to use any
available visual/instrument aids to assist in obtaining the proper sight picture (i.e. PAPI
or ILS).
16.
Request landing clearance at the normal position during the final turn. If necessary
select the landing lights ON when the aircraft is on final and continue a normal approach
and touchdown in the centre of the runway. Look ahead, watching both rows of runway
lights, to assist you in judging your proximity and rate of descent to the runway. This
will also assist in keeping the after-landing roll straight. You will be taught to land with
and without the landing lights.

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 19 =====
17.
At the end of the landing roll, when the aircraft has slowed sufficiently clear the
runway, stop the aircraft, and then do the post-landing check. Ensure that the anti-
collision lights are turned OFF prior to taxiing to the ramp. Maintain a vigilant lookout
throughout.
18.
Taxi at a safe speed. Use the taxi light as required; however, before turning into
line on the tarmac, turn the taxi light OFF in order not to blind the marshaller.
18.5
STRAIGHT-IN
19.
Procedurally, the straight-in at night time is no different than one during the day.
That being said however, with a lack of outside visual aid, an increased awareness to
crosschecking instruments and making full use of PAPI/VASI lights for glide path inter-
ception and maintenance is required.
18.6
SPIN
20.
The spin recovery at night shall be carried out as per the “instrument recovery
from a spin” procedure that is outlined in Section 8.19. This will be practiced in the
FTD only.
18.7
PRACTICE FORCED LANDINGS
21.
The PFL at night will be carried out as per the procedures outlined in Chapter 13.
However, due to the lack of daytime visuals, extra awareness is required to crosscheck
airspeed and altitude to maintain parameters.
18.8
MEDIUM LEVEL NAVIGATION
22.
Medium level navigation will be practiced using the GPS with the procedures out-
lined in Chapter 11.
18.9
AERODROME LIGHTING
23.
The DATCO will have explained the lighting system in use at your home base.
Note that lighting systems are not standardized and that the most noticeable difference
lies in the pattern of the approach lights.
24.
Approach lighting systems provide visual aids for orientation on the final approach
and are particularly helpful when there is a low ceiling with poor visibility. Several sys-
tems are used and, in some cases, different presentations are used at the same aerodrome.
The VASI is an invaluable aid in judging the correct approach path at night as well as
during the day. The landing charts in the GPH 200 depict the lighting systems in use
at various aerodromes. It is advantageous to know in advance what type of lighting is

===== SMM part 8 PDF page 20 =====
Figure 18.1: Aerodrome, Approach, and Runway Lighting
available, particularly when there is restricted visibility. Remember to check the lighting
presentation during your pre-flight planning.
25.
You may notice various white lights and red obstruction lights in the vicinity of
the runway. The white lights usually depict ceilometers and transmissometers. Know
the lighting system in use at your destination.
